<meta>This page begins the preface to Thomas Stanley's "The History of Philosophy." Stanley transitions from the personal dedication of the previous page to a theoretical discussion on the nature and utility of history and biography.</meta>

# PREFACE

<image-desc>A large ornamental woodcut initial letter 'H'. The letter is white on a dark, hatched background decorated with scrolled acanthus leaves and a stylized face or mask at the bottom center.</image-desc>

*History*—which, by explaining past actions, teaches us how to regulate the future and provides us with wisdom at the cost of other men's experience—is not unlike *Painting*. Their purpose is the same. Just as in painting it shows a lack of skill to look upon the entire drawing with an indifferent eye rather than selecting and focusing on some chief detail (which is the mark of an *Artist*), so it is with history. He who is satisfied with a general account of affairs, without focusing on some prominent *actor* in that story, loses its greatest benefit. This is because what is most specific, through its closer connection to us, has the greatest influence upon us.

Because of this, there are two kinds of *History*. One represents the general affairs of *State*. The other gives an account of specific persons whose lives have made them eminent. *Homer* has provided an example <note>original: "essay"</note> of each: the first in his *Iliad*, an account of a war between different *nations*; the second in his *Odyssey*, which is focused on the person of *Ulysses*.

Now, since the life of man is either *practical*—busy with the civil affairs of peace and war—or *contemplative*—retired from public business for speculation and the study of wisdom, whether divine or human—it follows that this personal history <note>i.e., biography</note> is likewise twofold. It describes either the actions of people who are entirely involved in affairs of state (who are properly compared to the characters of a dramatic design, whose individual *characters* and parts serve only to make up one joint plot; such are most of those whose lives are related by *Plutarch* and the *Twelve Caesars* of *Suetonius*), or the lives of those who have been excellent in some kind of learn—

<sig>a</sig>
<meta>catchword: ing;</meta>

<summary>Thomas Stanley introduces the concept of history as a tool for learning from the past, specifically distinguishing between general state histories and individual biographies. He categorizes biography into two types: those focusing on men of action and those focusing on men of contemplation.</summary>
<keywords>history, biography, Homer, Plutarch, Suetonius, practical life, contemplative life, historiography, Ulysses</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface of Thomas Stanley’s "The History of Philosophy," specifically continuing a sentence from the previous page regarding the history of specific professions.</meta>

including; thus *Antipho* wrote of *Poets*; *Eudemus* of *Astrologers*; *Cicero* and *Plutarch* of *Orators*; and *Suetonius* of *Grammarians*. Those who wrote about *Philosophers* far exceeded the rest in number. It would be unnecessary to give a specific account <note>original: "accompt"</note> of them because their works no longer exist <note>original: "are not extant"</note>; therefore, we shall only name them: *Aetius*, *Anaxilides*, *Antigonus*, *Antisthenes*, *Aristocles*, *Aristoxenus*, *Callimachus*, *Clitomachus*, *Diocles*, *Diogenes Laertius*, *Eunapius*, *Heraclides*, *Hermippus*, *Hesychius* <note>original: "Pefychius"</note>, *Hippobotus*, *Ion*, *Idomeneus*, *Nicander*, *Nicias*, *Panaetius*, *Porphyrius* <note>original: "P or-rius"</note>, *Plutarch*, *Sotion*, and *Theodorus*.

Of almost all these authors (which is much to be deplored), there remain no traces <note>original: "footsteps"</note>. The only author of this kind remaining for the more ancient *Philosophers* is *Diogenes Laertius*, and for the later philosophers, *Eunapius*. To make the misfortune even greater, the information *Laertius* gives us is so much less than what he might have done, that there is much more to be found regarding the same people dispersed among other authors—which I have here collected and organized <note>original: "digested"</note> with as much diligence as I could.

It is also not untimely <note>original: "unseasonable"</note> at this point to examine the tenets <note>original: "tenents"</note> of the old *Philosophers*, since such a great variety of opinions spring up daily. Some of these are merely raked out of the ruins of antiquity and ought to be restored to their original owners; others, being recent inventions, will be improved <note>original: "receive addition"</note> when, having advanced to such a height, we look down to the bottom from which *Philosophy* first rose and see how great a progress she has made, though her beginnings are almost impossible to uncover <note>original: "inscrutable"</note>.

Although some *Greeks* <note>original: "Grecians"</note> have claimed for their own nation the origin of *Philosophy*, the more learned among them have acknowledged that it was derived from the East. To omit the obscure <note>original: "dark"</note> traditions of the *Athenians* concerning *Musaeus*, of the *Thebans* concerning *Linus*, and of the *Thracians* about

<meta>catchword: Or-</meta> <note>Likely "Orpheus"</note>

<summary>Stanley lists ancient biographers of philosophers whose works are now lost, identifying Diogenes Laertius as his primary remaining source. He argues that studying ancient philosophy is necessary to understand the origins of modern ideas and credits the East, rather than Greece, with the birth of philosophical thought.</summary>
<keywords>biography, Diogenes Laertius, Eunapius, historiography, Greek philosophy, Eastern origins, Musaeus, Linus</keywords>

# Preface.

~~Orpheus~~ <note>This name appears to be a fragment or a heading from a related section.</note> It is clear that the origin <note>original: "original"</note> of Greek Philosophy can be traced back to Thales. After traveling to the East, he first introduced natural science <note>original: "Naturall learning"</note>, geometry, and astrology from there into *Greece*. Because of this, the title <note>original: "attribute"</note> of "wise" was given to him, and at the same time to six others for their excellence in morality and politics. In the most ancient times, learning was called *Sophia* (wisdom) by the Greeks, and its practitioners, who elevated their souls to a high level of knowledge, were called *Sophos* (wise). *Pythagoras* was the first to name it Philosophy (the love of wisdom) and himself a Philosopher, claiming that no man is truly "wise," but only *God*.

<margin>Diogenes Laertius, *Life of Thales*.</margin>
Regarding those who were honored with this title of "wise," *Damon* of *Cyrene* <note>original: "the Cyrenean"</note> dismisses <note>original: "undervalues"</note> them all, especially the famous "Seven." *Anaximenes* says they were all devoted to *Poetry*. *Dicaearchus* argues they were neither *wise men* nor *philosophers*, but simply upright men and *lawgivers*. *Archetimus* of *Syracuse* wrote about their meeting with *Cypselus* (the father of *Periander*), at which he claims he was present himself. *Ephorus* states they all met with King *Croesus*, except for *Thales*. Some say they also met at the *Panionian* festival, and at *Corinth* with *Periander* at *Delphi*.

There is some controversy regarding their famous sayings <note>original: "sentences"</note>, some of which are attributed to several different people, such as this:

->*The Spartan <note>original: "Lacedæmonian"</note> Chilon made this claim:*<-
->*Nothing in excess; the middle path <note>original: "mean"</note> is best.*<-

There is no less disagreement regarding their number. *Leandrius* replaces *Cleobulus* and *Myson* with *Leophantus* (son of <margin>Not "Leophantus Gorsiades," as the translators <note>original: "Interpreters"</note> suggest.</margin> *Gorsiades*, a *Lebedian* or *Ephesian*) and *Epimenides* the *Cretan*. *Plato* (in his dialogue *Protagoras*) swaps *Myson* for *Periander*, while *Ephorus* swaps *Anacharsis* for *Myson*. Some even add *Pythagoras*. *Dicaearchus* mentions four who were universally acknowledged...

<meta>catchword: ledged</meta>

<summary>This page explores the origins of Greek philosophy, attributing its introduction to Thales and detailing the transition of the term "wise man" (Sophos) to "philosopher" under Pythagoras. It also discusses the historical debates surrounding the identity and number of the Seven Wise Men of Greece.</summary>
<keywords>Thales, Pythagoras, Seven Wise Men, Sophia, philosophy, Chilon, ancient Greek sages, definition of wisdom</keywords>

# Preface.

<margin>
So Suidas <note>original: *Suidas* (a 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia)</note> records: "Acusilaus, son of Caba, the Argive" <note>original Greek: 'Ακουσίλαος Κάβα υἱὸς 'Αργείος</note>. By this, the account by Laertius <note>original: *Laertius* (Diogenes Laertius)</note> is explained in a way that contradicts the usual translators.
</margin>

...acknowledged by everyone as **Thales, Bias, Pittacus**, and **Solon**. He then names six more, from which three are to be selected: **Aristodemus, Pamphilus, Chilon** the Lacedaemonian <note>Lacedaemonian: another name for a Spartan</note>, **Cleobulus, Anacharsis**, and **Periander**. Some also include **Acusilaus, son of Caba** (or **Scabra**), an Argive <note>Argive: a person from the city of Argos</note>. **Hermippus**, in his *Treatise of the Seven Wise Men*, says there were seventeen in total, from which the seven were selected and named differently by various authors. These seventeen were **Solon, Thales, Pittacus, Bias, Chilon, Cleobulus, Periander, Anacharsis, Acusilaus, Epimenides, Leophantus, Pherecydes, Aristodemus, Pythagoras, Lasus** of **Hermione** (the son of **Charmantides**, or, according to **Aristoxenus**, the son of **Simbrinus**), and **Anaxagoras**. In his *Commentary of Philosophers*, **Hippobotus** includes **Linus, Orpheus, Solon, Periander, Anacharsis, Cleobulus, Myson, Thales, Bias, Pittacus, Epicharmus**, and **Pythagoras**. **Laertius** lists them as **Thales, Solon, Chilon, Pittacus, Bias, Cleobulus**, and **Periander**; to these he adds **Anacharsis, Myson, Epimenides**, and **Pherecydes**. He says these were called the wise men, though some also include **Pisistratus** the Tyrant <note>Tyrant: in ancient Greece, this referred to a ruler who seized power by force or held absolute authority, not necessarily a cruel one</note>.

Among the Romans as well, three men held the surname "the Wise" <note>original: *Sapiens*</note>: **Marcus Cato, Gaius Laelius**, and **Lucius Acilius**.

# THALES.

## CHAP. I.

->The Country and Parents of Thales.<-

Great minds, which have been successful in benefiting posterity through their excellent inventions, have not always had the fortune to enjoy their just reward; their glory is often intercepted by some later disguise of alteration or addition. It is therefore an act of gratitude for us, who find ourselves instructed by the ancients, to vindicate the memory of our masters by diligently inquiring who the authors of those labors were from which we reap the harvest. This kind of injury has happened to a significant degree to **Thales**, the wise man of **Miletus**, who first introduced natural and mathematical learning into Greece, from where it was passed down to us. However, the honor of so noble a design has been so obscured by the ambitious opposition of some and the industry of others that little reputation is left for the deserving author. I have therefore considered it worth my effort to organize what I could collect or observe about a person to whom all lovers of learning are so much indebted.

The origin of **Thales** is recorded very obscurely. Some believe he was a Phoenician by birth; since this opinion seems to be strongly founded on **Laertius** and the authorities he cited, it is necessary that we begin with an examination of his words, which are commonly translated as follows:

<margin>From his *Astronomy*. <note>original Latin: *Natione fuit Phoenix, ut Herodotus Milesius dicit.*</note> "By nationality he was a Phoenician, as Herodotus the Milesian says." This is undoubtedly corrupt (for which Herodotus is that?) and should be restored to this effect: <note>original Latin: *Natione fuit Phoenix ut Herodotus, Milesius ut alij dicunt.*</note> "He was a Phoenician by nationality according to Herodotus, but a Milesian according to others."</margin>

> Now **Thales** was born, as **Herodotus**, **Duris**, and **Democritus** affirm; his father being **Examius** and his mother **Cleobulina**, of the **Thelidae**, who were Phoenicians, the most illustrious of all descendants from **Cadmus** and **Agenor**, as **Plato** also says.

The testimony of **Herodotus**, though **Hyginus** and **Suidas** <note>Suidas: refers to the Suda, a 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia</note> seem to understand it according to the common error as if he were born in Phoenicia, expressly confirms the contrary, being as follows: "*Thales, a Milesian, but a Phoenician by distant descent*." From this we may gather that the other two authorities, **Duris** and **Democritus**, meant little more, or at least nothing to the contrary. So likewise the testimony of **Leander**, which is cited by **Clement of Alexandria** along with **Herodotus** to prove him of Phoenician descent.

He was granted citizenship <term>made free: granted the status of a 'freeman' or citizen</term> of **Miletus** when he arrived with **Neleus**, who was banished from Phoenicia. The learned **Casaubon**, in order to reconcile this...

<margin>* Under the entry for **Thales**.</margin>

<summary>This page introduces the biography of Thales of Miletus, discussing his role as the founder of Greek natural philosophy and addressing conflicting historical accounts regarding his Phoenician or Milesian ancestry.</summary>
<keywords>Thales of Miletus, Phoenicia, Herodotus, Diogenes Laertius, Greek philosophy, genealogy, Examius, Cleobulina</keywords>

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Thales' ancestry, specifically addressing potential textual corruptions regarding his connection to Neleus and Phoenicia, and transitions into a chronological debate regarding the exact year of his birth.</meta>

story with that of **Neleus**, who was not banished out of **Phoenicia** when he built **Miletus**. For the text <gloss>when he came with Neleus, who had been expelled from Phoenicia <note>original Greek: ὅτε ἦλθε σὺν Νείλῳ ἐκπεσόντι Φοινίκης</note></gloss> some read <gloss>each of the Phoenicians <note>original Greek: ἕκαστος φοινίκων</note></gloss>; as if **Thales**, having been banished out of **Phoenicia**, had gone with **Neleus** to **Miletus**. This alteration creates a very strange anachronism, for this **Thales** lived more than four hundred years later than that **Neleus**. Therefore, if **Laertius** meant the same **Neleus**, either he was strangely mistaken, or his text is corrupt and should be understood as referring to the ancestors of **Thales**. The text may be restored to that sense with little alteration, thus: <gloss>they were each born in Miletus when they came with Neleus, who was expelled from Phoenicia <note>original Greek: ἕκαστος ἐγενήθησαν δὲ ἐν Μιλήτῳ ὅτε ἦλθον σὺν Νείλῳ ἐκπεσόντι Φοινίκης</note></gloss>. This implies that they, being Phoenicians, went into **Caria** and became citizens of **Miletus** at the time when **Neleus** (the son of **Codrus**), having been pushed aside from the Kingdom of **Athens** by his younger brother **Medon**, led the Ionian colonies there, of which **Miletus** (which he built) was the chief city. Concerning this colony, see **Herodotus**, **Strabo**, and **Aelian**.

<margin>
* Plutarch, *On the Malice of Herodotus*.
* Diogenes Laertius, *Life of Thales*.
</margin>
"But as most say, he was born there, at **Miletus**, and of a noble race." This is how they translate <gloss>noble <note>original Greek: εὐγενὴς</note></gloss>; but in opposition to the first opinion, which only mentions his family and not his country, it may perhaps be understood here in the same context. Some (he says) think he was of Phoenician descent, among those who were incorporated at **Miletus**; others think he was of a Greek family, and a noble one. Of this latter opinion is **Plutarch**, who reproves **Herodotus** for claiming that **Thales** was descended from a distant barbarian stock; and **Hermippus**, who attributes to him the saying that he thanked the Gods he was a Greek, not a barbarian.

## CHAP. II.

->Of the time of his birth.<-

<margin>
* Laertius.
* Laertius.
* Book 3.
</margin>
**Apollodorus** says that **Thales** was born in the first year of the thirty-fifth **Olympiad** <note>Approximately 640 BCE</note>. **Demetrius Phalerius** says that he was honored with the title of "wise" when **Damasias** was **Archon** <note>The chief magistrate of Athens</note>. **Damasias** was Archon, according to **Dionysius of Halicarnassus**, in the second year of the thirty-fifth Olympiad, when **Ancus Marcius**, the fourth King of **Rome**, began his reign.

<margin>
* *On the Times of the Athenians*.
* *On Mathematical Science*, 32. 8.
* In **Eusebius**.
* *Archont.* 1. II.
* *Rationarium Temporum*, 2. 12.
</margin>
Because of this, an anachronism of one year is suspected by very learned men, who would have **Thales** be born in the same year that **Damasias** was Archon. Consequently, **Sigonius**, **Vossius**, and others bring the birth of **Thales** down to the second year of this Olympiad. **Scaliger**, **Meursius**, **Petavius**, and others move **Damasias** up to the first year so that they may make his magistracy agree with **Thales's** birth. Neither is entirely correct, for the office of **Damasias** does not relate to the birth of **Thales**. To clear this up, we must take notice that **Damasias**, in the Athenian *Fasti* <term>chronological records</term>, is said twice

<meta>This page continues the chronological debate regarding the exact year of Thales' birth and his professional timeline, specifically addressing historical inconsistencies and the existence of another individual with the same name.</meta>

# THALES

...is said to have been Archon, first in the second year of the thirty-fifth Olympiad (the year following the birth of *Thales*), and again in the fourth year of the forty-eighth Olympiad, when he obtained the title of *Wise*. We cannot mention the latter date without acknowledging the great clarity that chronology has gained from Mr. *Selden’s* *Arundel Marbles* <note>original: *Marmora Arundeliana*</note>.

<margin>
* Plutarch, *Life of Lycurgus* <note>original: *Vita Lycurg.*</note>
* In *Eusebius*
* *Phlegon* fragments
* *Suidas*
* *Thales*
* See St. Augustine, *The City of God*, Book 18, chapters 24 and 27 <note>original: *de civit. dei, lib. 18. cap. 24. & 27.*</note>
</margin>
It is also worth noting that there was another person of this name, whom *Eusebius* places at the eighth Olympiad, and whom *Laertius* and *Plutarch* make a contemporary of *Homer*, *Hesiod*, and *Lycurgus*. *Scaliger*, who recognized the inconsistency of this account, did not recognize the reason for it; for *Lycurgus* and *Iphitus* established the Olympic games twenty-seven Olympiads before *Coroebus* was the victor (who, according to *Eusebius*, is considered the first). These two men named *Thales* are confused by some writers; *Eusebius* calls the first a natural philosopher, whereas it is certain that this kind of learning was first introduced into *Greece* by the second *Thales*. *Suidas* cites *Phlegon* as saying that the latter *Thales* flourished in the seventh Olympiad, though *Phlegon* undoubtedly meant the first. Those who place *Thales* around the seventh Olympiad and make him a contemporary of *Romulus* confuse the later *Thales* with the first, and the true epoch of the Olympiads with the common one <note>The "common" epoch starts with Coroebus in 776 BCE, while the "true" epoch refers to the earlier institutional dates of Iphitus</note>. For the first *Thales* lived in the seventh Olympiad from the first year of *Iphitus*; *Romulus* lived about the same distance from the Olympiad of *Coroebus*.

<margin>
* Clement of Alexandria, *Stromata*, Book 1 <note>original: *Stromat. 1.*</note>
* *Preparation for the Gospel*, Book 10 <note>original: *Præpar. Evangel. 10.*</note>
</margin>
This timeframe is wrongly used by that learned Father *Clement of Alexandria* to prove that *Thales* was younger than the later Prophets. 
> Thus (he says), it is demonstrated that those who prophesied in the time of Darius Hystaspes, in the second year of his reign—namely, Haggai <note>original: *Aggæus*</note>, Zechariah, and Malachi (who was one of the twelve <note>Referring to the twelve Minor Prophets of the Old Testament</note>)—were more ancient than Pythagoras (who is said to have lived in the sixty-second Olympiad) and Thales (the eldest of the Greek Sages, who lived around the fiftieth Olympiad), since they prophesied in the first year of the forty-eighth Olympiad.

This is as if this were not instead an argument to prove these Prophets were contemporaries of Thales, which Eusebius permits.
> He says that around the time of Cyrus, King of Persia, the Seven Wise Men flourished; this was also the time when the last of the Hebrew Prophets prophesied, more than six hundred years after the fall of Troy and no less than fifteen hundred years after Moses.

But if, along with *Clement of Alexandria*, we consider these Prophets to be contemporaries of *Darius Hystaspes*, they will appear much younger than *Thales*, because *Darius* began his reign in the last year of the sixty-fourth Olympiad.

<summary>This page analyzes the chronological placement of Thales, distinguishing between the philosopher and an earlier namesake while correcting dating errors made by historical figures like Clement of Alexandria.</summary>

## CHAPTER III.

### His Travels.

<margin>* Diogenes Laertius</margin>
He spent the first and largest portion of his time in Crete to learn the mysteries of their religion (since that island was famous as the birthplace of Jupiter), as he himself acknowledged in an epistle to Pherecydes.
   The same epistle affirms that he also traveled to Asia; some say he went to Phoenicia, basing this on his knowledge of astronomy <term>Astrology: used here in the archaic sense of astronomy</term>, which he is believed to have learned from the Phoenicians, who were the masters of that science. This is especially noted because he is said to have been the first to observe the constellation of the Lesser Bear, by which the Phoenicians navigated. <margin>* On the Mathematical Sciences, chapter 32.</margin> Vossius attempts to prove the word *Cynosura* is Phoenician, rather than being derived from the Greek *kynos oura* <note>original: κυνὸς οὐρὰ, meaning "dog's tail"</note>. He suggests it comes from the Hebrew words *kanash* and *ur*, meaning a "collection of light," or *kunsh orah*, meaning a "fiery navel" <note>original: *Umbilicus igneus*</note>.
<margin>* Plutarch, *On the Sentiments of the Philosophers*, Book 1, chapter 1.</margin>
   His final journey (undertaken when he was advanced in years) was to Egypt to consult with priests and astronomers, as he acknowledges in his letter to Pherecydes. <margin>* Iamblichus, *Life of Pythagoras*, Book 1.</margin> There he was instructed by the priests at Memphis, particularly those of Jupiter (according to Iamblichus). Diogenes Laertius affirms he learned geometry from them, and Plutarch implies the same regarding his philosophy.
<margin>Plutarch, *The Banquet of the Seven Sages*</margin>
   *He lived there during the reign of King Amasis, by whom he was greatly favored and admired for many achievements, especially for measuring the height of the pyramids using their shadows. Eventually, however, he was accused before the king of having a dislike for monarchs and that form of government; many bitter sayings of his regarding tyrants were cited as evidence. For instance, when Molpagoras, a prominent man from Ionia, asked him what was the strangest thing he had ever seen, he answered, "An old tyrant." On another occasion, at a feast where the question was asked, "Which beasts are the most dangerous?" he replied, "Among wild animals, a tyrant; among tame ones, a flatterer." Plutarch notes that although princes claim to be very different from tyrants, they take no pleasure in such maxims <term>Apothegmes: concise, pithy sayings or maxims</term>. Consequently, he lost the favor of Amasis.* <margin>* Cyril</margin> Having thus studied philosophy in Egypt, he returned to Miletus and brought back that vast store of learning to his own country.

<margin>Diogenes Laertius; Suidas in the "Life" [of Thales].</margin>
...married and made his *sister’s son (whom *Plutarch calls *Cybisthus*) his heir. <margin>Life of Solon; Diogenes Laertius; Plutarch, Symposiacs, 3. 6; Stobaeus, Sermon 66.</margin> *He put his mother off when she first urged him to marry by telling her it was not yet time; and when he was older and she solicited him again, he answered, "Nor is it now time," meaning it was then too late. Being asked why he took no steps to have children, he answered <term>dia philoteknian</term> <note>original: διὰ φιλοτεκνίαν</note>, which is pronounced the same as <term>di' aphiloteknian</term> <note>original: δι’ ἀφιλοτεκνίαν</note>, and may be interpreted either as "because he loved children" or "because he did not love them," as <margin>In Laertium.</margin> *Casaubon conjectures. But perhaps his meaning may be better understood from another answer he gave to the same question: <margin>Stobaeus, Sermon 66; Life of Solon.</margin> *that he did not intend to bring voluntary worries into his life and disturb its peace; or from this story related by *Plutarch.

Solon, coming to Miletus to visit him, told him that he wondered why he completely neglected marriage and children. Thales at that time answered nothing, but some few days later he suborned a stranger to pretend that he had arrived from Athens within the last ten days. Solon asked what news there was from there. "Nothing," answered the other as he was instructed, "except the burial of a young man attended by the whole city, being, as was said, the son of the most eminent person in the city, who at that time was traveling abroad." "Unhappy man," cries Solon, "what was his name?" "I heard it," answered the other, "but I have forgotten; I only remember he was very famous for wisdom and justice." Solon's fear increasing with every answer, he at last asked him if the father’s name were not Solon. When the other confirmed this, Solon beat his own head and performed other actions accompanied by speeches typical of those overcome with grief. Thereupon Thales, smiling and interrupting him, said, "These things, Solon, deterred me from marriage, for they distress even you, who are a most constant person. But do not be troubled by this news; it is a trick."

<margin>The Banquet of the Seven Sages.</margin>
In this private life, he was sought after and contacted by many princes whose invitations and friendships (*Plutarch* says) he refused; yet he was visited by many eminent persons.

<margin>Diogenes Laertius.</margin>
*He is said to have lived for some time with *Thrasybulus* (a man of excellent wit and judgment) who was king, or (according <margin>Aristotle, Politics, 5. last; Herodotus, 1.</margin> to the Greek word) tyrant of Miletus, *though his reign lasted only eleven months; *this was around the time that the *Milesians* entered into an alliance with *Alyattes* II, who was then King of *Lydia*.

<margin>Salmasius, *Plinian Exercises*, page 843.</margin>
Salmasius was unaware of this when, to make the words of Laertius agree with the first Damasias, he misinterpreted Eusebius and Clement of Alexandria and disrupted all other accounts of the birth and death of Thales, while this account fortunately agrees with the timelines of all the seven.

The first was Thales, who was justly preferred above the rest because of his great learning, which he did not owe to any teacher; the time when this honor was conferred on him occurred in the fifty-ninth year of his age.

The second was Pittacus of Mytilene, who flourished in the forty-second Olympiad and died in the third year of the fifty-second.

The third was Bias of Priene, a contemporary of Pittacus, living under the reigns of Alyattes and Croesus.

The fourth was Solon, who was Archon at Athens in the third year of the forty-sixth Olympiad. He died in the 55th Olympiad.

The fifth was Cleobulus of Lindus, a contemporary of Solon.

The sixth was Myson of Chene.

The seventh was Chilon of Lacedaemon, who was an <term>Ephorus</term> <note>One of the five senior magistrates in ancient Sparta.</note> in the 56th Olympiad.

The credit and glory of these seven were much increased (says Plutarch) by a Tripod sent around from one to another by a mutual, noble, and modest yielding to one another. The occasion is related thus by Diogenes Laertius and Valerius Maximus:

<margin>Diogenes Laertius.</margin>

->Do you come, Milesian, to consult my shrine?<-
->The Tripod to the wisest I assign.<-

In prose, it reads: "Thales the Milesian, son of Examius, offers this twice-received prize of excellence to the Delphian Apollo of the Grecians." The one who carried the cup from one person to another was Thyrion, the son of Bathycles; to this, these verses by Phoenix of Colophon allude:

At last, it came to Solon, who sent to the Pythian Oracle to ask who was the wisest and was answered: "Myson." Eudoxus substitutes Myson for Cleobulus, and Plato substitutes him for Periander. The Oracle concerning Myson was this:

> Myson of Oeta I declare
> Wiser than those who wisest are.

The person sent to make the inquiry was Anacharsis. Daedacus the Platonist and Clearchus affirm that the cup was sent by Croesus to Pittacus and so was carried around. Andron, in his book *The Tripod* (which seems to have been a discourse entirely on this subject and is likewise cited by Clement of Alexandria to prove that Thales and the other six flourished around the 50th Olympiad), writes that the Argives proposed this tripod as a prize for the wisest of the Greeks, and that it was awarded to Aristodemus, a Spartan, who resigned it to Chilon. Aristodemus is mentioned by Alcaeus:

> This speech we to Aristodemus owe:
> "Money makes the man; none is poor and honest too." <note>An English rendering of the Greek proverb *Chrēmata anēr*, meaning wealth defines a person's status.</note>

There are those who report that a ship richly laden, sent by Periander to Thrasybulus, the tyrant of Miletus, was shipwrecked in the Coan Sea, and the tripod was taken up by some fishermen. Phanodius affirms it was lost in the Athenian Sea and afterwards brought to the city; upon consultation, it was voted to be sent to Bias. Others say this tripod was made by Vulcan, who gave it to Pelops as his wife’s dowry; from him, it came to Menelaus, and afterwards, being taken away with Helen by Paris, it was thrown into the sea by the Lacedaemonian [Helen], calling to mind...

After this, some Lebedians fishing thereabouts drew it up, and quarreling with the fishermen about it, it was brought to **Kos**, but the controversy was not decided. The business was told to those of **Miletus**, which is the chief city of that country; they sent a messenger to demand it, and finding themselves slighted, made war upon the **Coans**, in which many were slain on both sides. The Oracle declared that the **Tripod** should be given to the wisest, whereupon both parties with joint consent presented it to **Thales** <margin>Plutarch, *Life of Solon*</margin> [\* **The Coans** being willing to grant that to a private person, for which they before contested with all the **Milesians**] who dedicated it to **Apollo Didymaeus**; the effect of the Oracle to the **Coans** was this:

Finally, it was sent from *Miletus* to **Thebes**, and dedicated to **Ismenian Apollo**. *Theophrastus* says it was first sent to *Bias* at *Priene*, then by *Bias* to *Thales* at *Miletus*, so passing through all, it came again to *Bias*; and finally was sent to **Delphi**. This is most generally reported, except that instead of a **Tripod**, some say it was a Cup sent from *Croesus*, others that it was left there by *Bathycles*.

Thus was the priority of *Thales* confirmed by the Oracle, for which reason he is styled by *Cicero* and *Strabo* as the *Prince of the wise men*, to whom the rest yielded the preeminence.

* *On False Religion*, Book 1, Chapter 5.</margin> Thales (says *Laertius*) is affirmed by many to be the first who made inquiries into nature. \* *Cicero* (who first taught Greek philosophy to speak Latin) acknowledges *Thales* to be its first author. \* *Strabo* says that he was the first of the Grecians to make inquiries into natural causes and mathematics. \* *Plutarch* calls him the inventor of philosophy; *Justin Martyr*, \* the most ancient of philosophers; *Tertullian*, the first of natural philosophers; \* *Lactantius*, the first who made inquiry after natural causes.

<header>THALES.</header>

<margin>
* Opinions of the Philosophers, 1. 3.
* Physical Extracts, 1. 13.
</margin>
### Section 1. That Water is the Principle of all things.

**I**n his investigation of the natural causes of things, he conceived *Water* to be the first principle of all natural bodies, of which they consist, and into which they eventually dissolve. His reasons (as delivered by *Plutarch* and repeated by *Stobaeus*) are these:

First, because natural seed, the principle of all living creatures, is *humid*; therefore, it is probable that humidity is also the principle of all other things.

Secondly, because all kinds of plants are nourished by moisture; when they lack it, they wither and decay.

Thirdly, because *fire*, even the Sun itself and the stars, are nourished and maintained by vapors proceeding from water, and consequently the whole world consists of the same. For this reason, *Homer*, assuming all things were engendered from water, says:

->Ocean, from whom all things receive their birth.<- <note>original: "Ὠκεανὸς ὅσπερ γένεσις πάντεσσι τέτυκται"</note>

<margin>
* Metaphysics, 1. 3.
* Natural Questions, 6. 6.
* Metaphysics, 1. 3
</margin>
In pursuit of this opinion (*as Aristotle says*), he assigned water the lowest place, holding (according to *Seneca*) that the whole Earth rests and is carried upon the water—whether we call that the Ocean, the great sea, any simple moisture of another nature, or a moist element. By this water (he says) the earth is sustained like a large ship, which presses upon the water that bears it up; this is because the heaviest part of the world cannot be upheld by the *Air*, which is fine and light. This is how *Aristotle* is to be explained when he says, *Thales held that the Earth, being capable of swimming, rests like wood or something similar; for such things do not swim upon Air, but upon Water.*

<margin>
* Natural Questions, 3. 13.
* Natural Questions, 6. 6.
</margin>
It was upon this ground that he held *Water* (as *Laertius* says) to be the cause of earthquakes. Thus *Seneca* writes: He holds that the globe of the Earth is upheld by water and carried like a boat, and it floats by the movement of that water at the times when it is said to quake. One of his reasons, alleged by *Seneca*, is this: in all extraordinary motions of the earth, new fountains commonly break out. If the earth inclines to one side and shows its keel tilted, it gathers water; if the burden it bears happens to be overweighty, the water raises itself higher toward the right or left side.

<margin>
* Is fire or water more useful?
* Theogony.
</margin>
From the testimony of *Homer*, which *Thales* used to defend this tenet (according to *Plutarch* and *Justin Martyr*), it is manifest that this idea—that water is the principle of all things—was delivered (though imperfectly) by other Greeks before *Thales*. *Plutarch* elsewhere produces this authority from *Hesiod*:

->First of all things, Chaos came to be —<- <note>original: "Πάντων μὲν πρώτιστα χάος γένετ’"</note>

<sig>D</sig>
<meta>catchword: ad-</meta>

<summary>Thales argues that water is the primary substance of the universe, citing biological moisture and meteorological vapors as evidence, while also proposing that the Earth floats upon water like a ship.</summary>
<keywords>Thales, Water as First Principle, Arche, Aristotle, Seneca, Earthquakes, Homer, Hesiod, Natural Philosophy, Cosmology</keywords>

--- Page 32 ---
<meta>This page continues the physical philosophy of Thales, specifically his argument that water is the primary substance of the universe, and links this belief to various ancient traditions, including Phoenician, Hebrew, and Indian thought.</meta>

<header>THALES.</header>

...adds that the majority of ancient philosophers called *water Chaos* <margin>* Argonautica, Book 4.</margin> from the word for *diffusion* <note>original: "παρὰ τὴν χύσιν"</note>. The commentator <term>Scholiast</term> on *Apollonius* says regarding these words:

---

->— The Earth itself sprouted from slime. <-
<note>original: "Ἐξ ἰλύος ἐβλάστησε χθὼν αὐτή"</note>

---

He affirms (citing *Zeno*) that the *Chaos* from which all things were made, <margin>* Cited by Athenagoras.</margin> according to *Hesiod*, was *water*. This water, as it settled, became slime, and the slime condensed into solid *Earth*. To this, add the testimony of *Orpheus*:

->Slime was made from the water:<-
<note>original: "ἐκ τοῦ ὕδατος ἰλὺς κατέστη"</note>

This opinion they borrowed from the *Phoenicians*, with whom the Greeks had a very ancient relationship. *Linus* came from there; *Orpheus* received his learning from there; and *Thales* is believed to have done the same. This appears clearly in <margin>* Porphyry, On the Cave of the Nymphs.</margin> *Numenius*, an ancient philosopher who cites the very words of *Moses* for this opinion: *The spirit of God moved on the face of the waters.* There is a prominent passage in *Eusebius* to prove this: the <margin>* Gospel Preparation, Book 1, Chapter 10.</margin> theology of the Phoenicians asserts that the principle of this world is a dark spiritual air—or the spirit of dark air—and Chaos troubled and enveloped in darkness. This was infinite and for a long time had no boundary. But (they say) the spirit, being moved by the love of its own principles, created a mixture; this connection was called Love. This was the beginning of the production of all things, but the spirit itself was not created. From this connection of the spirit was born *Môt*, which some call slime and others the decay of a watery mixture. From this was made the seed of all creatures and the generation of all things.

The Indians were not ignorant of this either, as *Megasthenes* relates <margin>* Strabo, Book 15.</margin> their opinion: *They are of the same mind as the Greeks in many things: that the world had a beginning and shall have an end; that God, its Maker and Governor, pervades it entirely; that all things had different beginnings, but the substance from which the world was made was water.*

The word <term>Principle</term> <note>original: "ἀρχὴ"</note>, because it includes the "efficient cause" among philosophers and thus—when understood alone—excludes the rest (as being the most noble part), has given some cause to mistake *Thales*. They assume that by acknowledging no other principle, he consequently believed *water* to be *God*. However, we may easily gather from *Plutarch* that *Thales* understands "Principle" only as the material cause. Plutarch condemns *Thales* for confounding <margin>* On the Opinions of the Philosophers, Book 1, Chapter 2.</margin> a *Principle* with an *Element* and for holding them to be the same. *As he says, "There is a great difference; Elements are compounded, but Principles are neither compounded nor are they any complete substance. Indeed, we call water, air, earth, and fire elements; but we call other natures principles because nothing precedes them from which they are generated."*

<summary>This page explores Thales's theory of water as the primary "Principle" of the universe, connecting his views to Phoenician, Egyptian, and Indian cosmogonies. It also clarifies that Thales viewed water as the material substance of the world rather than its divine creator.</summary>

<keywords>Thales, Chaos, water, slime, Phoenicians, Orpheus, Numenius, Moses, Eusebius, Megasthenes, Plutarch, Principle vs Element, cosmogony</keywords>

--- Page 33 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Thales's physical philosophy regarding water as the primary principle and transitions into his theological views on the nature of God.</meta>

<header>THALES.</header>

<margin>Aristotle, *Metaphysics*, Book 1, Chapter 3.</margin>
For otherwise, if they were not the first, they would not be Principles; rather, that should be so termed from which they were made. Now, there are certain things preceding them of which Earth and water are compounded: namely, the first unformed matter, the form itself, and privation. Thales therefore errs in affirming that water is both the Element and the principle of all things. Thus we see through Plutarch that the objection can only be regarding the terminology, not the reasoning behind the names; for since the distinction between "principle" and "element" was not used at that time, Thales, by "principle," meant nothing regarding the efficient cause, which is quite certain from Aristotle. Thales, he says, affirms water to be the principle; therefore, he held the earth to be above the water. Perhaps he conceived it so because he saw that the nourishment of all things is moist, that heat itself consists of it, and that every creature lives by it. He held that the substance of which things are made constitutes the principles of all things; for these reasons, he held this opinion, as also because the seeds of all things are of a moist nature, and water is the principle of moist things.

## Section 2. Concerning God.

<margin>Tertullian, *Apology against the Heathens*</margin>
Tertullian says that Thales, when asked by Croesus concerning the Deity, gave no certain account, but requested several periods of time for deliberation to no effect. He seems to reflect upon the same or a similar story to that which is reported of Simonides and Hiero.

<margin>* Clement of Alexandria, *Stromata*, Book 5.</margin>
But what the opinion of Thales was concerning God may be gathered from two aphorisms <term>Apothegmes</term> cited by Laertius, repeated with this commentary <term>glosse</term> by *Clement of Alexandria*: *And are these not the sayings of Thales derived from this source: "That God is glorified forever and ever"? And he openly confesses that God is called the Knower of Hearts <note>original: "καρδιογνώσης"</note>.* For when Thales was asked what God was, he replied, "That which has neither beginning nor end." When another asked if a man might do evil and conceal it from God, he replied, "How so, when even a man who merely thinks of it cannot?" *Men ought to believe (says *Cicero in his name) that the gods see all things.*
<margin>* Cicero, *On the Laws*, Book 2.</margin>

<margin>* Cicero, *On the Nature of the Gods*, Book 1.</margin>
He acknowledged God as the first of beings and the Author of the world, asserting (according to Laertius) that the most ancient of all things is God, for he is unbegotten; and that the most beautiful thing is the world, for it is his work. This is confirmed by Cicero. "Thales the Milesian," he says, "who first inquired into these things, said that water was the principle of things, but that God was that Mind which formed all things from water." If gods can exist without sense and mind, why did he join the mind to water? And why water to the mind, if the mind can exist without a body? Thus Cicero understands Thales to mean that the material principle was co-eternal with the efficient cause; however, Thales himself does not seem to mean this, since he declared God to be the first of beings. But the words of Cicero confirm that the Mind <note>original: "Mens"</note> of *Anaxagoras*—for the linking of which to matter he was so famous—was nothing more than what he borrowed from Thales.

<meta>catchword: He</meta>

<summary>This page explores Thales's philosophical arguments for water as the primary principle of the universe and details his theological beliefs, identifying God as an unbegotten, eternal mind that formed the world.</summary>
<keywords>Thales, Aristotle, Plutarch, Cicero, God, water, principle, element, creation, theology, Clement of Alexandria, Anaxagoras</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Thales's theological views, specifically his belief in divine providence, his theories on dæmons, and his definition of the soul as a self-moving entity.</meta>

<header>THALES.</header>

He affirmed that God, by the immutable decree of his providence, governs the world. Thales (says Stobæus), when asked what was strongest, answered "Necessity," for it rules all the world. Necessity is the firm judgment and immutable power of providence. To this we must likewise refer what is cited under his name by the same Stobæus: that the first mover is immovable. <margin>\* Aristotle, *Physics*, Book 8, Chapter 7.</margin> \* Aristotle borrowed this from him without acknowledging the author.

<margin>
\* Clement of Alexandria, *Exhortation to the Greeks*.
\* Herodotus, Book 2.
</margin>
Something concerning God was previously delivered, though imperfectly, by Orpheus, as alleged by \* Clement of Alexandria and others; but as Cicero says, Thales was the first among the Greeks who made any search into these things. The Greeks themselves do not deny that he brought this knowledge out of Egypt; \* for they acknowledge that they received the names of their gods from there, and believed the Egyptians to be the first who, looking up to the world above them and admiring the nature of the universe, reflected upon the Deity.

## Section 3. Of Dæmons.

<margin>
\* Plutarch, *Opinions of the Philosophers*, Book 1, Chapter 8.
\* Athenagoras, *Apology*.
</margin>
Thales (says \* Plutarch), along with Pythagoras, Plato, and the Stoics, holds that dæmons are *spiritual substances, and the souls of heroes separated from their bodies. Of these, there are two kinds: good and bad; the good heroes are the good souls, and the bad heroes are the bad souls.* The same order \* Athenagoras attests was observed by Thales, ranking the three degrees thus: first, that of the immortal gods; next, dæmons; thirdly, heroes. This was followed by Pythagoras, who held that the gods were to be preferred in reverence before dæmons, and heroes before men.

<margin>
\* Aristotle, *On the Soul*, Book 1, Chapter 8.
\* Cicero, *On the Laws*, Book 2.
\* Iamblichus, *Life of Pythagoras*.
</margin>
He affirmed (as Stobæus says) *the world to be full of these dæmons.* This is thought to be the meaning of the statement by \* Aristotle, repeated by Cicero, that *Thales thought all things were full of gods.* Diogenes Laertius ascribes the same assertion to Pythagoras: *that all the air is full of souls, which are heroes and dæmons.*

<margin>\* Iamblichus, *On the Egyptian Mysteries*, near the beginning.</margin>
This opinion was asserted by the Greeks before the time of Thales, particularly by Hesiod; but I question whether that is enough of an argument to deny that Thales received it from the Egyptians. We may learn from \* Iamblichus that they held the belief in the same manner. Besides, Pythagoras and Plato (whom Plutarch joins with Thales in this doctrine) drew their learning from the same Egyptian source.

## Section 4. Of the Soul.

<margin>
\* Plutarch, *Opinions of the Philosophers*, Book 4, Chapter 2.
\* Aristotle, *On the Soul*, Book 1, Chapter 2.
\* Plato in *Timaeus*.
</margin>
Plutarch and Stobæus say that *Thales first affirmed the soul to be* <term>αὐτοκίνητον</term> <note>autokineton: self-moving</note>, *a self-moving nature.* \* Aristotle says he calls it <term>κινητικὸν</term> <note>kinetikon: having the power of motion</note> in respect to the motion it gives to other things, which includes both parts of the definition used by the \* Platonists: *a substance*

<meta>catchword: stance</meta>

<summary>This page outlines Thales's beliefs on divine providence, the hierarchy of divine beings (gods, dæmons, and heroes), and his pioneering definition of the soul as a self-moving substance.</summary>

--- Page 35 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Thales's definition of the soul as a self-moving substance and examines the philosophical debate between his followers and Aristotle regarding this definition.</meta>

<header>THALES.</header>
<sig>E</sig>

...substance, having within itself a power to move itself and other things; which *Plato* argues to this effect:
> *The first of motions is that by which a thing moves itself; the second, that by which it moves another. Everything that moves itself lives; every living thing lives because it moves itself. Therefore, the power of self-motion is the essence of that substance which we call the soul. This soul is the cause of the first generation and motion of things which are, were, and shall be—and of all their contraries—as well as of all transformation. It is the principle of motion, and therefore more ancient than the body, which it moves by a secondary motion.*

And afterwards, he declares these to be the names of the soul's motion: *to will, to consider, to take care, to consult, to judge rightly and wrongly, to rejoice, to grieve, to dare, to fear, to hate, to love, and the like.* These, which are the primary motions and the recipients of the secondary bodily motions, bring all things into growth and decrease, change or <note>original: "condmnation," likely a typo for condensation</note> condensation, and separation or thinning.

<margin>
* *Stobaeus, Physical Selections, Book 1.*
* *Aristotle, On the Soul, Book 2.*
* *Cicero, Tusculan Disputations, Book 1.*
</margin>
This opinion, first raised by *Thales*, was entertained in the schools with the agreement of *Pythagoras, Anaxagoras, Socrates*, and *Plato*, until it was rejected by *Aristotle*, whose chief arguments against it were these: 1. That nothing moves except what is in a place, and nothing is in a place except what has quantity; because the soul lacks quantity, none of the four kinds of motion (namely: *local motion, alteration, decrease, and growth*) are compatible (*in itself*) with it. Secondly, that self-motion is not essential to the soul because she is moved accidentally by external objects. 

The first argument, if understood as physical limitation, not only denies the motion of all things that are definitively located, such as spirits, but even of the highest sphere of heaven, if compared with *Aristotle's* own definition of place. Yet that some of these types of motion—though in a different, extraordinary manner—are appropriate to the soul and not merely accidental, may be argued: 1. From the further spreading of the soul according to the growth of the body. 2. From the intellect, which is acknowledged as a perfection and consequently a kind of change. That *Thales* understood this to be one of the soul's motions is clear from the maxim ascribed to him by *Laertius*: "*The swiftest of things is the mind, for it outruns everything.*" On this basis, *Cicero* (admitting in almost the exact words of *Thales* that "*nothing is swifter than the mind, and no swiftness can compare with it*") would interpret the <term>ἐντελέχεια</term> <note>entelechy: the realization of potential or a state of complete activity</note> of *Aristotle* as a continued and perpetual motion.

The second reason may be questioned by comparing the acts of memory and recollection. The first is occasioned by external things, yet only as objects; thus the motion is within the soul herself. But through recollection, she moves herself from a state of lack to a state of knowledge, without the help of anything external.

<margin>* *On the Soul, Book 3.*</margin>
It is worth noting that among these and other reasons alleged by *Aristotle* to destroy this assertion, one is the possibility of the resurrection of the body; but this is <term>παρὰ λόγον</term> <note>para logon: contrary to reason or logic</note>.

From the second part of the distinction in the definition (namely,

<summary>This page discusses Thales's definition of the soul as a self-moving entity, a view supported by Plato but contested by Aristotle, who argued that the soul lacks the physical quantity necessary for motion.</summary>

<margin>

(Because it moves other things) *Thales* argued that the **lodestone** <term>a naturally magnetized mineral</term> and **amber** had souls; the first because it draws iron, the second straw. He further (says *Laertius*) asserted that those things we count inanimate have souls, arguing this from the lodestone and amber. Regarding the reason for this latter example, *Aldobrandinus* falsely interprets it as its change of color and its "jarring," as it were, at poison. But *Aristotle* explains it more plainly: for of those whom we mentioned, Thales seems to have taken the soul to be something <note>original: "κινητικόν"</note> capable of motion, since he affirmed a stone to have a soul because it moved iron.

He likewise asserted the soul (of man) to be immortal and, according to *Choerilus*, was the first who held this view. *Cicero* ascribes the origin of this opinion to *Pherecydes*, but it seems rather to have been brought by *Thales* from the Egyptians; *Herodotus* attests that they held this belief.

->Section 5. Of the World.<-

<margin>
* Plutarch, On the Opinions of the Philosophers, 2. 1.
* Laertius.
* On the Heavens, 1. 10. 12.

Thales held that there was but one world, and that it was made by God. This truth was followed by all philosophers, as *Aristotle* confesses, until he rejected it to defend, by the contrary, an equally false assertion: that the world is everlasting—which could not be, he says, if it had a beginning.

<margin>
* Laertius.
* On the Opinions of the Philosophers, 2. 1.

That the world, being God's work, is the fairest of things; whatever is disposed in a lively order is a part of it, for which reason Pythagoras (according to *Plutarch*) first called it <note>original: "κόσμον"</note> the **cosmos** <term>the world as an orderly, beautiful system</term>.

<margin>
* Timothy.
* Damascene.
* Tacitus, On the Customs of the Germans.
* Caesar, On the Gallic War, 6.
</margin>
That night is older than day. This circumstance of the creation was held likewise by *Orpheus* and *Hesiod*, who had it from the Phoenicians; for this reason, the *Numidians*, *Germans*, and *Gauls* reckoned time by nights.

<margin>
* Laertius.
* Plutarch, On the Opinions of the Philosophers.
</margin>
That the world is animated <term>alive</term>, and that God is the soul of it, diffused through every part, whose divine moving power penetrates through the element of water. This is explained by the Hermetic philosophers: the divine spirit, who produced this world out of the first water, is infused, as it were, by a continual inspiration into the works of nature. Being diffused widely through a certain secret and continual act, moving the whole and every particular part according to its kind, it is the soul of the world.

<margin>
* Laertius; Plutarch, Dinner of the Seven Wise Men.
</margin>
That the world is contained in a "place." This agrees with the definition of place as space; but those who, with Aristotle, define place as a **superficies** <term>a surface or boundary</term>, though they hold the parts of the world to be in a place, are forced to deny the whole world to be so.

<margin>
Plutarch, On the Opinions of the Philosophers, 1. 18.
</margin>
That in the world there is no **vacuum** <term>a void or empty space</term>, in which (as Plutarch observes) all philosophers agree who affirm the world to be alive and governed by providence. The contrary is defended by those who maintain that the world consists of atoms, is inanimate, and is not governed by providence.

<margin>
* Plutarch, On the Opinions of the Philosophers, 1. 9.
</margin>
That matter is fluid and variable.

<header>THALES.</header>

<margin>Plutarch, *On the Opinions of the Philosophers*, 1. 16.</margin>
That bodies are <term>passible</term><note>subject to change or external influence</note> and infinitely divisible, and continuous, as are also a line, surface, place, and time.

That mixture is made by the composition of the elements.

<margin>Plutarch, *On the Opinions of the Philosophers*, 2. 13; Achilles Tatius.</margin>
That the stars are made of earth, yet are fiery; and the Sun is also made of earth. Those who affirm the stars to be fiery, says *Aristotle*, hold this view because they conceive the entire superior body <note>the heavens</note> to be fire.

<margin>Fragments on Aratus; *On the Heavens*, 2. 7; Plutarch, *On the Opinions of the Philosophers*, 2. 28.</margin>
That the Moon is of the same nature as the Sun, and that she is illuminated by him. *Plutarch* and *Stobaeus* affirm that this was first held by *Thales*, though *Eudemus* (cited by *Theon*) ascribes the idea to *Anaximander*.

That the monthly occultations <note>disappearances</note> of the Moon are caused by the nearness of the Sun shining around her.

<margin>Plutarch, *On the Opinions of the Philosophers*, 3. 9, 3. 10, 3. 11.</margin>
That there is but one Earth, which is round in the shape of a globe and seated in the midst of the world <note>the cosmos</note>. To this relates a speech ascribed to him by *Cleodemus*: that *if the Earth were taken out of the world, a chaos of all things must necessarily follow*.

<margin>*The Banquet of the Seven Sages*; Laertius; *Meteorology*, 2. 6; *On the Opinions of the Philosophers*; Book 1.</margin>
That the overflowing of the Nile is caused by the Etesian (yearly) winds. These rise with the Dog-star after the summer solstice and, beginning to blow from the North, spread into remote quarters (as *Aristotle* describes them). These winds, says *Plutarch*, blow directly against Egypt and cause the water to swell; the sea, driven by these winds, enters the mouth of that river and hinders it so that it cannot discharge itself freely into the sea, but is repulsed. Whereupon *Diodorus Siculus* adds that it overflows Egypt, which lies low and level. 

But this reason, though it seems plausible, is easily disproved. If it were true, all rivers that discharge into the sea opposite to the Etesian winds should have the same overflowing. Thus *Diodorus* concludes in his excellent discourse on this subject, which ends with the opinion of *Agatharchides*: that the flood is instead caused by rain coming from the mountains of Ethiopia.

### CHAPTER VII.

### Of his Geometry.

<margin>Apuleius, *Florida*, book 4; Laertius, *Life of Pythagoras*.</margin>
<margin>*On the Life of Pythagoras*, 1. 2.</margin>
<margin>On Euclid, 2. 4.</margin>
Apuleius, who calls *Thales* the inventor of Geometry among the Greeks, is more just to his memory than <unclear>Anticlides</unclear> and others, who ascribe that honor to <unclear>Moeris</unclear> or to *Pythagoras*. Even *Iamblichus*, a Pythagorean, acknowledges that Pythagoras learned mathematics from *Thales*. The origin and progress of this science, up to the perfection it received from *Pythagoras* (which gave occasion to that mistake), is thus described by *Proclus*:

Geometry was invented by the Egyptians, taking its beginning from the measuring of fields. This was necessary for them because of the inundations of the Nile, which washed away the boundaries of their individual plots. It is not to be wondered at that this science, like others, should have its beginning from utility and opportunity; since, as it is said of generation,

<margin>* Supply the gap in the text: <note>original: "καὶ ἀπὸ τοῦ λογισμοῦ εἰς τὴν θεωρίαν μεταβαίνει"</note> "and from reasoning it passes into contemplation," etc.; so Barocius translates.</margin>
It proceeds from the imperfect to the perfect; therefore, the transition from sensory perception to reasoning, and from reasoning to the mind, is not without reason. Just as the certain knowledge of numbers began among the Phoenicians because of merchandise and trade, so geometry was discovered among the Egyptians for the reasons mentioned previously. Thales, traveling to Egypt, first brought this science over into Greece. He discovered many things himself and taught his followers the principles of many subjects, explaining some generally and others more clearly. <margin>* Read: <note>original: "ὡς ἁψάμενος τῆς γεωμετρίας μνημονεύεται"</note> "as he is remembered for having taken up geometry." After these men, Pythagoras examined its principles more deeply and advanced it into a liberal science.

### Sect. 1. Propositions invented by him.

That he improved the geometry which he learned from the Egyptians with many propositions of his own (as Proclus implies) is confirmed by Laertius. Laertius states that Thales significantly advanced those subjects which Callimachus, in his *Iambics*, attributes to Euphorbus the Phrygian—such as <term>scalenous triangles</term><note>triangles with three unequal sides</note> and others. Nor is it to be doubted that many of these are among the propositions that Euclid later incorporated into his *Elements*. Euclid’s goal was to collect and organize those discoveries made by others, accurately demonstrating those that had been proven more negligently; however, of these, only the following are known to be Thales's:

<margin>* *Elements*, Book 1, Definition 17. * Commentary on Euclid, Book 2, Chapter 14.</margin>
1. [**Every diameter divides its circle into two equal parts.**] Although Euclid makes this proposition part of the definition of a diameter, Proclus asserts that it was first demonstrated by Thales.

<margin>* Euclid, Book 1, Proposition 5. * Book 3, Commentary 9.</margin>
2. [**In all isosceles triangles, the angles at the base are equal to one another; and if those straight lines are extended, the angles under the base are also equal.**] Proclus says that we are indebted to Thales for the discovery of this, as well as many other propositions; for he first observed and stated that in every isosceles triangle, the base angles are equal, and according to the ancients, he called equal angles "similar." There are three passages in the demonstration which contribute nothing toward the final conclusion—there are many of this kind in Euclid—which seem to confirm the antiquity of the proof and suggest it was not thoroughly revised by him.

<margin>* Euclid, Book 1, Proposition 15. Proclus, Book 3, Commentary 19.</margin>
3. [**If two lines intersect, the <term>vertical angles</term><note>opposite angles formed by the intersection of two lines</note> shall be equal to one another.**] Eudemus confirms that this theorem was invented by Thales, but first formally demonstrated by Euclid.

<margin>Euclid, Book 1, Proposition 26. * Proclus, Book 3, Commentary 31.</margin>
4. [**If two triangles have two angles equal to two angles respectively, and one side equal to one side—either the side adjacent to the equal angles or the side that <term>subtends</term><note>is opposite to</note> one of the equal angles—they shall likewise have the other sides equal to the other sides, each to each, and the remaining angle equal to the remaining angle.**] Eudemus

<meta>catchword: attri- [attributed]</meta>

<summary>This page discusses the Egyptian and Phoenician origins of geometry and mathematics, Thales's role in bringing these sciences to Greece, and lists specific geometric theorems attributed to him by ancient sources like Proclus and Eudemus.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Thales's contributions to geometry, specifically analyzing the specific theorems attributed to him by ancient historians like Proclus and Laertius, and comparing his discoveries with those of Pythagoras.</meta>

<header>THALES.</header>

Proclus attributes this theorem to Thales for showing the distance of ships at sea; to do so in the manner he is said to have done, it was necessary for him to perform it by this principle.

Pamphila (according to Laertius) affirms that he was the first to describe a right-angled triangle within a circle. <margin>* Geometry.</margin> Ramus, based on this authority from Laertius, attributes the second, third, fourth, and fifth propositions of the fourth book of Euclid to Thales. These concern the drawing of a triangle and a circle together. Consequently, Ramus interprets the term <term>to describe</term> <note>original: "καταγράψαι"</note> here to include both drawing a shape inside another (inscription) and drawing one around another (circumscription). However, in all those propositions, there is nothing specific to a right-angled triangle. Therefore, if the word <term>circle</term> <note>original: "κύκλω"</note> is retained, it must relate to the 31st proposition of the third book, from which the description of a right-angled triangle in a circle may be deduced. 

But because there is no such proposition in Euclid, and this has only an obscure reference to part of that theorem, it is suspected that the text of Laertius is corrupt. It is likely that the word (or mark) for <term>circle</term> <note>original: "κύκλω"</note> was inserted by accident. Without it, the words <term>to describe a right-angled triangle</term> <note>original: "καταγράψαι τὸ τρίγωνον ὀρθογώνιον"</note> exactly correspond with <margin>* Book 10. chap. 11.</margin> those of Vitruvius: <term>to describe a Pythagorean right-angled triangle</term> <note>original: "Pythagoricum trigonum orthogonium describere"</note>. <margin>* Book 9. chap. 2.</margin> By this, he means (as he expresses at length elsewhere) the forty-fifth <note>The text likely refers to the 47th proposition of Euclid's first book, commonly known as the Pythagorean theorem.</note> proposition of the first book of Euclid: that in right-angled triangles, the square of the hypotenuse is equal to the squares of the sides containing the right angle. 

The fact that Vitruvius, Proclus, and others attribute this invention to Pythagoras confirms it is the same one meant here by Laertius. Laertius adds that Thales, **for the invention of this, sacrificed an ox**, though others (he says), among whom is Apollodorus, attribute it to Pythagoras. In his life of Pythagoras, Laertius cites the same Apollodorus, stating that Pythagoras sacrificed a <term>hecatomb</term><note>a public sacrifice of 100 oxen</note> after finding out that the hypotenuse of a right-angled triangle is equal in power to the two sides including the right angle, according to the epigram:

> That noble scheme Pythagoras devised,
> For which a hecatomb he sacrificed.

Cicero, though he names a different author, agrees with Laertius on the size of the offering, affirming that Pythagoras used to sacrifice an ox upon any <margin>* Proclus in Euclid book 2. def. 4. where the words are perhaps inverted: <note>original: "τρισὶ γραμμαῖς ἐπὶ φωνὴν τομαῖς εὑρὼν τὰς σπείρας εὗρον"</note></margin> new invention. This kind of gratitude, begun by Thales, was imitated by others as well, such as Perseus:

> Finding three spiral lines in five sections,
> Perseus an offering to the gods did give.

### Sect. 2. Of his taking the height of the Pyramids.

<margin>* *Polyhistor*, chapter 25.
* *Idylls*, 2.
* Book 22.
* *Variae*, 7. form 15.</margin>
The Pyramids of *Egypt* are supposed by *Solinus*, *Ausonius*, *Ammianus Marcellinus*, and *Cassiodorus* to cast no shadow at all. However (as Mr. *Greaves* <note>John Greaves (1602–1652), author of *Pyramidographia* (1646).</note> has observed in his excellent discourse upon this subject), this must be understood to mean either during the summer time or—which is closer to the truth—that they have no shadow at midday for three quarters of the year.

<margin>? Laertius.
* Book 36, chapter 12.</margin>
For it is acknowledged that *Thales* measured their height by their shadow. *Hieronymus says he measured the Pyramids by the shadow, observing the time when they are of equal size.* *Pliny* affirms that *he found a way to determine their height, and all similar structures, by measuring the shadow at the moment it is equal to the body.* But *Plutarch* has given a more regular and exact account of his method: *by erecting a staff perpendicularly at the end of the Pyramid's shadow, and using two triangles formed by the Sun's rays, he demonstrated that whatever proportion existed between the shadows also existed between the Pyramid and the staff.* This is a demonstration so rational that it remains the standard way of determining heights by shadows, founded upon this theorem:

<margin>* Euclid, book 6, proposition 4.</margin>
*In equiangular triangles, the sides adjacent to the equal angles are proportional, and the sides that subtend the equal angles correspond to one another.*

If *Proclus* had proceeded as far as the sixth book of *Euclid*, we would in all likelihood have found this ascribed to *Thales*. For the same argument used by *Eudemus* to prove him the inventor of the fourth theorem in the previous section (by which he measured distances) is of equal force in this one, by which he measured altitudes.

The height of the Great Pyramid (which *Thales* measured) is 499 feet vertically (according to Mr. *Greaves*) and 693 feet along its sloped ascent.

## CHAP. VIII

### ->Of his Astronomy.<-

Omitting the fable of *Orpheus's* Harp (which alludes to the seven planets) and the observations of *Hesiod*—which were little more than the rising and setting of some principal stars, and so imperfect that *Plato* calls all those who satisfy themselves with such superficial knowledge "astronomers according to *Hesiod*"—we may affirm, along with *Eudemus* and others, that *Thales* was the first among the Greeks *who was skilled in astronomy.* *Pliny* asserts that this science was brought out of *Phoenicia*, and *Aristotle* claims that the Greeks owe much of it to the *Egyptians*, where it had—

<margin>* book 5, chapter 17.
* *On the Heavens*, 2. 12. <note>original: "de Cœlo"</note></margin>

<header>THALES</header>

<warning>Heavy ink and bleed-through in some areas; Greek text is somewhat blurred.</warning>

<margin>* Letter to Pherecydes</margin>
had been practiced for a long time; thither indeed Thales acknowledged that he traveled to confer with astronomers.

### Sect. 1. Of the Celestial Spheres.

<margin>* On the Opinions of the Philosophers</margin>
Thales and Pythagoras (says * Plutarch, repeated by * Stobaeus), along with their followers, affirm that the celestial sphere is divided into five circles (which they call **Zones**). One is called the *Arctic circle*, and is always in our view; one is the *Summer Tropic*; one is the *Equinoctial*; one is the *Winter Tropic*; and one is the *Antarctic circle*, which is never seen by us. The oblique circle called the **Zodiac** lies under the three middle circles; it touches all three as it passes, and each of them is cut at right angles by the Meridian, which goes from pole to pole. Therefore, the invention of the Zones is unjustly ascribed by <margin>* Strabo, Book 2</margin> * Posidonius to Parmenides; similarly, the discovery of the obliquity of the Zodiac is unjustly ascribed by <margin>* Pliny, Book 2, Chapter 8</margin> * others to Anaximander, Pythagoras, or Oenopides.

<margin>* Diogenes Laertius</margin>
* Eudemus says that he first observed the Tropics; Laertius says that he first discovered the progression of the sun from Tropic to Tropic. The word <term>tropous</term> <note>original: "τρόπους"</note> signifies not only the solstices, but the equinoxes as well, according to * Sextus Empiricus. The tropic signs are those which the <margin>* Against the Mathematicians, 5.1</margin> sun enters, causing the air to change and convert. Such signs include Aries and its opposite, Libra, as well as Capricorn and Cancer; for in Aries the vernal conversion occurs, in Capricorn the winter, in Cancer the summer, and in Libra the autumnal. Laertius confirms this exposition when he says that Thales composed only two treatises: one on the Tropics and the other on the Equinoxes, and that he distinguished the seasons of the year.

### Sect. 2. Of the Sun, Moon, and Stars.

He first observed the apparent diameter of the sun—which is the angle formed in the eye—to be the 720th part of its orbit. This is undoubtedly the meaning of Laertius, whose words are: <note>original: "καὶ πρῶτος τὸ τοῦ ἡλίου μέγεθος τοῦ σεληναίου ἑπτακοσιοστὸν καὶ εἰκοστὸν μέρος ἀπεφήνατο" — "And he was the first to declare that the size of the sun is the seven-hundred-and-twentieth part of the lunar circle."</note> This reading, which implies the sun is 720 times smaller than the moon, is completely ridiculous. Since Thales knew the cause of eclipses (as is granted by all), he must also have known that the sun is larger than the moon. Nor is the text much improved by those who read <note>original: "ἢ πρὸς τὸ τοῦ ἡλίου μέγεθος τὸ τῆς σεληναίης" — "or the size of the moon in relation to the size of the sun."</note> <margin>* In The Sand Reckoner</margin> The text seems rather to require the word <term>zodiac</term> <note>original: "ζωδιακοῦ"</note> instead of <term>lunar</term> <note>original: "σεληναίου"</note>, or something to that effect. Regarding this, * Archimedes says: *“We assume this because Aristarchus says that the sun appears to be the 720th part of the circle of the zodiac.”* For he considered how he might use instruments to measure the angle formed in the eye by the sun’s apparent diameter; but to measure any such thing exactly is not easy, for neither the eyesight, nor the hand, nor the instruments used for the observation are reliable enough to demonstrate it with total precision. Apuleius confirms this correction as follows:

<summary>This page discusses Thales's astronomical discoveries, including the division of the celestial sphere into five zones and his calculation of the sun's apparent diameter as 1/720th of the zodiac. It also addresses historical disputes regarding who first discovered the obliquity of the zodiac and the nature of the tropics.</summary>

<header>THALES</header>

In his later years, he made an excellent demonstration of the proportions of the Sun, which I have not only learned (says *Apuleius*) but confirmed by practice: specifically, how many times the Sun's magnitude is contained within the circle formed by its motion. As soon as he discovered this, *Thales* showed it to *Mandraytus* of *Priene*, who was infinitely delighted with this new and unexpected knowledge. He told Thales to ask for whatever he wanted as a reward for such an excellent invention. "It will be reward enough for me," said *Thales*, "if, whenever you share what you have learned from me with others, you acknowledge me as the inventor."

<margin>* Laertius & Achilles Tatius, Introduction to Aratus</margin>
He first discovered the constellation of the Lesser Bear <note>Ursa Minor</note>. * *Callimachus* writes:

->He sailed to *Miletus*, invited<-
->By *Thales’* glory, who, clear-sighted,<-
->Is said to have marked the Lesser Bear,<-
->The star by which *Phoenicians* steer.<-

*Hyginus* affirms that he first called it *Arktos* <note>original: "Αρκτον"</note>, the Bear.

### Sect. 3. Of Eclipses.

He was the first (says *Laertius*) who foretold eclipses, as *Eudemus* affirms in his *History of Astronomy*; for this, *Xenophanes* and *Herodotus* admire him, as attested also by *Heraclitus* and *Democritus*. *Theon of Smyrna* and *Clement of Alexandria* cite the same passage from *Eudemus*, whose book's purpose was the history of astronomers and their discoveries. Similarly, *Pliny* states, "*Among the Greeks, the first to investigate eclipses was Thales the Milesian.*"

<margin>* On the Opinions of the Philosophers, 2.2</margin>
* *Plutarch* affirms that he was the first to observe an eclipse of the Sun and stated that it was caused by the Moon coming in a direct line beneath it. This can be seen in a basin of water or a mirror.

<margin>* Plutarch, On the Opinions of the Philosophers, 2.28</margin>
* He also taught that an eclipse of the Moon is caused by the shadow of the Earth, which, when placed between these two celestial bodies, darkens the smaller one.

<margin>* Book 1</margin>
The testimony of *Herodotus*, cited by *Laertius*, is this: 
> A five-year war broke out between the Lydians and the Medes. During this war, the Medes sometimes had the advantage over the Lydians, and sometimes the Lydians over the Medes; one battle was even fought at night. With the war equally balanced on both sides, in the sixth year, as the armies were joined in battle, it happened that the day suddenly turned into night. *Thales* the Milesian had foretold this change to the Ionians, predicting the very year in which it would occur. Seeing the day turn to night, the Lydians and Medes stopped fighting and worked to conclude a mutual peace. This peace was mediated by *Syennesis*, King of Cilicia, and *Labynetus* of Babylon (whom *Scaliger* believes to be *Nebuchadnezzar*). It was finalized with the marriage of *Aryenis*, daughter of *Alyattes*, to *Astyages*, son of *Cyaxares*, and ratified by the drinking of blood.
