<meta>This page continues the discussion of Thales's astronomical work, specifically focusing on the varying historical and scientific accounts regarding the date and duration of the solar eclipse he famously predicted.</meta>

<header>THALES. 21</header>

<margin>* Book 2, chapter 12.</margin>
This is the story of that memorable eclipse, the timing of which is uncertain. * Pliny dates it to the fourth year of the forty-eighth Olympiad, 170 years before the building of *Rome*. 
<margin>* chapter 20.</margin>
* Solinus places it in the 49th Olympiad, 604 years after the destruction of *Troy*, which falls in the first year of that Olympiad. 
<margin>* Stromata 1.</margin>
* Clement of Alexandria (citing *Eudemus*) places it around the fiftieth Olympiad, during the time when *Cyaxares*, father of *Astyages*, reigned in *Media*, and *Alyattes*, father of *Croesus*, reigned in *Lydia*. *Eusebius* dates it to the second year of the forty-eighth Olympiad, 1,430 years after *Abraham*. *Cleomedes* says it was a total eclipse in the *Hellespont*, but in *Alexandria* it covered ten digits <note>A "digit" is a unit used to measure the magnitude of an eclipse, representing one-twelfth of the diameter of the celestial body.</note>. 
<margin>* Book 6.
<note>original: "χρόνῳ ὁ ἥλιος ἐξέλιπεν ἐπὶ πολλὰς ὥρας τῆς ἡμέρας, καὶ πάλιν ἐφαίνετο"</note>
At that time, the sun failed for many hours of the day and then appeared again.</margin>
* Johannes Antiochenus says it continued for many hours, but it could not have exceeded three.

Later writers have delivered differing accounts of this event:

Ricciolus places it 585 years before the Incarnation, on May 28th, at about 6 o'clock in the afternoon, with 12.56 digits eclipsed.

<margin><note>original: "Θαλῆ τῶ φιλοσόφω τὴν ἔκλειψιν τῶ ἡλίο."</note>
To Thales the philosopher, the eclipse of the sun.</margin>
Calvisius dates it to 607 years before the Incarnation, in the 4th year of the 43rd Olympiad, differing from Pliny by 18 years.

<margin>The manuscript prepared for the press is in the hands of my honored friend Edward Byshe, Esq.</margin>
The learned Bishop of *Armagh* <note>James Ussher</note> places it in the reign of *Cyaxares*, in the 4th year of the 44th Olympiad, the 147th year of the Era of *Nabonassar*. He dates it to the fourth day of the Egyptian month *Pachon*, which according to the *Julian* account is September 20th, a Sunday <note>original: "feria 1"</note>, beginning 1 hour, 3 minutes, and 25 seconds after sunrise. He records 9 digits eclipsed, lasting almost two hours.

Petavius dates it to the 4th year of the 45th Olympiad, Julian period 4117, 597 years before the Incarnation, and 157 years after the building of *Rome*. He places it on *July 9th*, a Tuesday <note>original: "feria 3"</note>, beginning at 4:45 AM, with 9 digits and 22 minutes eclipsed, and a duration of a full two hours.

Ricci refutes *Petavius* because that eclipse does not fit the circumstances of the story; it began too early in the morning and was insufficient in magnitude in *Pontus* and *Asia Minor*.

Lansbergius dates it to the 3rd year of the 48th Olympiad, the 163rd year of *Nabonassar*, on the 12th day of *Tybi*, which is *May 28th*. He records 12.20 digits eclipsed in the *Hellespont* and 10.12 digits in *Alexandria*.

Kepler, Scaliger, Buntingus, and Salianus follow Pliny’s dating. According to *Buntingus*, 11.30 digits were eclipsed.

It is not easy to determine whether this variety of opinions arises from the uncertainty of the astronomers or that of the chronologists.

The discrepancy between sources like Pliny, Ussher, and Petavius highlights the difficulty in reconciling ancient chronologies with astronomical calculations.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the biography of Thales of Miletus, transitioning from his astronomical discoveries regarding eclipses to his division of the calendar and his practical application of astrological knowledge.</meta>

### Section 4. Concerning the Year.

Diogenes Laertius says that Thales distinguished the seasons of the year and that he was the first to call the last day of every month *triakas* <note>original: "τριακάς"</note>, the thirtieth day. He also divided the year into 365 days.

<margin>* Book 2.</margin>
He seems to have learned this calculation of the year in *Egypt*, where it was in use. This is explained and praised by *Herodotus*: "The Egyptians were the first of all men to discover the year, dividing it into twelve months; they gathered this from the stars and, in my opinion, more wisely than the Greeks. While the Greeks insert an extra month every third year to make up the time, the Egyptians add five days every year to the 360 days that make up the twelve months. In this way, the cycle of time returns to its starting point correctly."

This was called in later times the *Egyptian year* (perhaps because it was used by *Ptolemy*, who lived in *Egypt*) to distinguish it from the *Julian year*, which was then used in all Western regions and includes an additional six hours. The most perfect is the *Gregorian year*, consisting of 365 days, five hours, forty-nine minutes, and twelve seconds.

### Section 5. His Astrological Predictions.

Because Thales was earnestly devoted to astrology, he became subject to the criticism of certain people. *As he was being led outside* <margin>* Plato.</margin> *one night by an old woman, his servant girl (a* <margin>*</margin> *Thracian), to look at the stars, he fell into a ditch (into which she had led him on purpose). When he complained to her, she said, "Thales, do you think you can understand the heavens when you cannot even see those things that are at your feet?"*

He was also reproached by some friends for preferring this study over wealth, which brought shame to the science as if it provided no benefit to its practitioners. Thereupon, he defended himself and the art from that insult. *Aristotle says* <margin>* Politics, Book 7.</margin> *that when they insulted him for his poverty, as if philosophy were unprofitable, it is said that through astrology he foresaw a great harvest of olives for that year. Before the winter was over ("before they had begun to bloom," says* <margin>* On Divination, Book 1.</margin> <note>original: "antequam florere cæpissent"</note> *Cicero), he paid deposits and bought up all the oil presses in Miletus and Chios. He did this with very little money because there was no other buyer* <term>chapman: a merchant or trader</term> *at that time to drive up the price. When the season arrived and many people sought the presses in a hurry, he set whatever rates he pleased. By this means, he gathered a great deal of money and showed that it was easy for philosophers to be rich if they wished, but that wealth was not their goal.* *Plutarch* alludes to this when he says that *Thales is reported to have practiced trade.*

<summary>This page describes Thales's contributions to the calendar, specifically his introduction of the 365-day year modeled on Egyptian practices, and recounts the famous anecdote of how he used his astronomical skills to corner the olive oil market to prove philosophy's practical worth.</summary>
<keywords>Thales, Egyptian year, Julian year, Gregorian year, Herodotus, Aristotle, astrology, olive harvest, Miletus, Chios, philosophy and wealth</keywords>

## CHAP. IX.

### His Moral Sentences.

<margin>* <note>original: "Sept. sap. conv."</note> Banquet of the Seven Sages</margin>
Of his moral sentences, those mentioned by *Plutarch* on this occasion should be remembered first. *Amasis*, King of *Egypt*, entering into a competition with the King of *Ethiopia* regarding wisdom, proposed these questions to be answered by him: what is the oldest of all things? What is the most beautiful? What is the greatest? What is the wisest? What is the most common? What is the most profitable? What is the most harmful? What is the most powerful? What is the easiest?

The Ethiopian’s answers were these: the oldest of things is time; the wisest is truth; the most beautiful is light; the most common is death; the most profitable is God; the most harmful is the Devil; the most powerful is fortune; and the easiest is that which pleases. *Thales* asked *Niloxenus* whether *Amasis* approved of these solutions. *Niloxenus*, who had been sent to *Greece* by *Amasis* with these other questions to be answered by the *Sages*, replied that he was satisfied with some but not others. "And yet," replied *Thales*, "there is not one that is not erroneous and betrays ignorance. As for the first: how can it be argued that time is the oldest of things, when one part of it is past, another present, and the third yet to come? For that which is to come must, by logic, be considered younger than all men or things. Next, to claim that truth is wisdom is like saying that light and sight are the same thing. Furthermore, if he considers light to be beautiful, why does he forget the sun? His answers concerning God and the Devil are bold and dangerous, but his answer regarding fortune is most improbable; for if she were so powerful, how is it that she is so easily changed? Nor is death the most common, for it is not common to the living.

The most ancient of things is God, for He never had a beginning or birth. The greatest is space <term>The original uses "place" to refer to the void or container of the universe</term>, for the world contains all other things, but space contains the world. The most beautiful is the world, for whatever is orderly arranged is part of it. The wisest is time, for it has discovered all things already devised and will discover all that are yet to come. The most common is hope, for that remains even with those who have <margin>* <note>original: "Etiam apud Stob. serm. 109."</note> Also in Stobaeus, sermon 109.</margin> nothing else. The most profitable is virtue, for it makes all things useful and advantageous. <margin><note>original: "Etiam apud Stob. serm. 46."</note> Also in Stobaeus, sermon 46.</margin> The most harmful is vice, for it destroys all good things. The most powerful is necessity, for that alone is invincible. The easiest is that which agrees with nature, for even pleasures are often abandoned when they weary us."

To these apothegms <term>pithy sayings or maxims</term>, *Laertius* adds these: "The swiftest of things is the mind, for it outruns everything." He affirmed that *there is no difference between life and death.* When he was asked why he did not then die, he said, "*Because* there is no difference." To one who asked which was older, night or day, he answered, "*Night, by one day.*" Another inquired *whether a man might do evil and conceal it from the gods.* "Not even in his thoughts," he said. To an *adulterer* who asked him if he might not clear himself by an oath,

It concludes with several of Thales' famous short maxims regarding the mind, morality, and existence.</summary>

Let no words fall from you which may accuse you to someone who has committed something to you in trust. Cherish your parents. Do not entertain evil. What you bestow on your parents, you shall receive from your children in your old age. It is hard to understand well. The sweetest thing is to enjoy our desires. Idleness is troublesome. Intemperance is harmful. Ignorance is intolerable. Learn and teach better things. Do not be idle, even if you are rich. Conceal your domestic troubles. To avoid envy, do not be pitiable. ## CHAPTER 10.
### His judgment in Civil affairs.

Politics was, according to Laertius, his first study, in which his advice was of great authority, although he was the only person (as Cicero observes) among the Seven Wise Men who was not a ruler of the city where he lived.

<margin>* Book 1. "The other inhabited cities should nonetheless be considered as if they were districts," <note>original: "τὰς δὲ ἄλλας πόλεας οἰκευμένας, μηδὲν ἧσσον νομίζεσθαι κατάπερ οἱ δῆμοι εἶεν"</note> which Valla renders thus: "The other inhabited states will nevertheless obey this one, just as tribes of other states obey the laws." <note>original: "Cæteras autem civitates habitatas nihilominus huic parituras, quā aliarum civitatum tribus legibus parerent."</note> Stephanus renders it thus: "nevertheless to be held in the same place as the tribes." <note>original: "nihilominus eodem loco haberi quo tribus"</note> Both (I believe) are mistaken.</margin>

Of his judgment in this matter we have two instances. The first is from Herodotus: *The advice of Thales of Miletus was also good, even before the destruction of Ionia. Though he was of Phoenician descent from long ago, he commanded the Ionians to build one common Council Hall, and to place it in Teos, because Teos is in the center of Ionia. He advised that the rest of the inhabited cities should nevertheless maintain their status, just as the citizens did.*

The other instance, cited by Laertius with no less applause, is this: In the first year of the fifty-eighth Olympiad, Croesus, King of Lydia, feared the growing power of Cyrus. Encouraged by what he believed was an Oracle, he sent ambassadors and presents to the most significant of the Greeks, persuading them to join him in an expedition against Cyrus. The Lacedaemonians <note>Spartans</note> and many others did so, but Thales forbade the Milesians from entering into an alliance with him. *It appears,* adds Laertius, *that his advice in civil affairs was excellent; for when Cyrus gained the upper hand, this decision preserved the city.*

Yet Thales did provide Croesus with personal assistance in moving his army across the River Halys, as the Greeks affirm, although Herodotus is of a contrary opinion and gives this account of both: *When Croesus had come to the River Halys, I believe he moved his army across by the bridges that were already there. However, the common report among the Greeks is that Thales of Miletus was the one who conveyed them across. For Croesus was doubtful about what part of the river his army should cross, as there were no bridges in those days. Thales, who was in the field with him, is said to have caused the river, which ran on the left side of the army, to also run on the right. He accomplished this by starting above the camp and digging a deep trench in the shape of a half-moon, so that the river was diverted into the trench from its former channel behind the army. After passing the camp, it returned to the old channel again. Thus, as soon as the river was di-*

divided (which * Lucian says was done in one night), it became fordable on either side. Some say that the old channel was completely blocked up, but I do not believe that, for then, how could they have crossed over it on their return?

<margin>
* Who translates: "By this trench, having diverted a channel from the ancient streams and passing the camp, he led it back again into the ancient bed once the army had crossed over." <note>original: "ταύτῃ τῇ τάφρῳ διώρυχα ἐκτραπόμενος τῶν ἀρχαίων ῥεώθρων καὶ αὖτις παραμειβόμενος τὸ στρατόπεδον εἰς τὰ ἀρχαῖα ἐσβάλλει, in quam cum introduxisset ex pristino alveo fluvium, iterum cum ubi exercitus trajectus esset in suum alveum refunderet."</note>
</margin>
That this is the meaning of Herodotus—which was misunderstood by * Valla—will appear from the * commentator on Aristophanes, who relates it in the same manner, while praising Thales for his excellence in mechanics.

<margin>
* In *The Clouds*.
</margin>
He was a great enemy to tyrants and considered all monarchy to be little better, as appears by Plutarch, who portrays him speaking thus: *As for taking one for the other (namely, a monarch for a tyrant), I am of the same mind as the young man who, throwing a stone at a dog, hit his stepmother; "It is no matter," he said, "for even so, it does not land badly." Truly, I always esteemed Solon very wise for refusing to be king of his own country; and Pittacus, if he had not taken a monarchy upon himself, would never have said, "How hard it is to be a good man." And Periander, being seized (as if by an hereditary disease derived from his father) by the same tyranny, did very well to endeavor as much as he could to disengage himself from it by frequenting the conversation of the best men, inviting sages and philosophers, and being invited by them—not approving the dangerous advice of Thrasybulus, my countryman, who persuaded him to take off the heads of the most prominent citizens. For a tyrant, who chooses rather to command slaves than free men, is like a husbandman who prefers the gathering of locusts and the catching of fowl over the reaping of good corn. These sovereign authorities have only this good, in recompense for many evils: a kind of honor and glory, if men are so happy that in ruling good men, they themselves prove better. For those who in their office aim at nothing but security, without respect for honor or honesty, are fitter to be set over beasts than men.*

<margin>
Stob. serm. 149
Stob. serm. 148.
Stob. serm. 43.
</margin>
In the same *Symposium*, he gives this account of monarchy, democracy, and economics: *That prince is happy who lives until he is old and dies a natural death. That commonwealth is best ordered where the citizens are neither too rich nor too poor. That house is best wherein the master may live most at ease.*

Some affirm (says Laertius) that he left nothing behind him in writing. Others say that he wrote:

<margin>
* City of God.
</margin>
Of natural philosophy: Saint * Augustine says that Thales, to propagate his doctrine to successors, searched into the secrets of nature and, committing his opinions to records and letters, grew famous.

<margin>
* Laertius.
</margin>
Of nautical astrology (mentioned by * Simplicius), which is by some ascribed to Phocus, a Samian.

Of the Tropics and Equinoxes: which two treatises Laertius says he composed, judging the rest easy to be understood. These seem

# THALES.

They seem to be those astrological writings which Lobon of Argos (who wrote concerning the poets) affirms to have extended to two hundred verses.

*On Meteors*: A treatise in verse mentioned by Suidas.

<margin>Book 8. The most wise authors Thales, Castor, and Polybius recorded these things in their writings, and after them, Herodotus the historian. <note>original: "ταῦτα δὲ ἱστορήσαν οἱ σοφώτατοι Θάλης καὶ Κάστωρ καὶ Πολύβιος συγγραψάμενοι καὶ μετ' αὐτούς Ἡρόδοτος ὁ ἱστοριογράφος."</note></margin>
*The History of His Own Times*: This is mentioned if we may give credit to John of Antioch, who says, "The most wise authors Thales, Castor, and Polybius committed these things to writing, and after them, Herodotus the historian." however, this may be no more probable than the idea that Polybius and Castor lived before Herodotus.

Adages: Those cited by <term>Laertius</term><note>Diogenes Laertius, biographer of the Greek philosophers.</note> we have included among his moral sentences, for that is what they were—intended for the instruction of the common people. They were a kind of loose verse approaching prose; for this reason, <term>Demosthenes</term> makes two kinds of poets: those who write in meter and (if we may use the term) those who write in blank verse <note>original: "τὰ ἔμμετρα καὶ τὰ ἄμετρα"</note>, as <term>Casaubon</term><note>Isaac Casaubon, a renowned Renaissance scholar.</note> observes. Whatever Laertius produces of this kind in his *Lives of the Seven Wise Men* does not seem to have been taken from any other poet, but rather to have been written by the wise men themselves.

Epistles: Of these, only two are still in existence, preserved by Laertius.

---

->Thales to Pherecydes.<-

<margin>But the translators interpret this differently.</margin>
I hear that you, the first of the Ionians, are about to publish a discourse to the Greeks concerning religion. You rightly believe that your work should be placed in a public library rather than handed over to unreliable people. If it would be of any benefit to you, I will willingly confer with you about what you have written; if you wish, I will visit you at Syrus. Neither I nor Solon the Athenian would deserve to be called wise men if we—who sailed to Crete to inform ourselves on matters there, and to Egypt to consult with priests and astronomers—did not also make a journey to see you. Solon will also come, if you think it appropriate. You, who love your home, seldom travel to Ionia or care to enjoy the company of strangers; we, who write nothing, spend our time traveling through Greece and Asia.

---

->Thales to Solon.<-

If you leave Athens, you may, in my opinion, settle yourself at Miletus (along with those you bring with you), for here there is nothing to trouble you. Even if you dislike that we Milesians are governed by a tyrant (for you are opposed to all monarchs, even <term>elective</term><note>Leaders chosen by the people but granted absolute power, often called Aesymnetes.</note> ones), you may still enjoy the society and conversation of me, your friend. Bias has also sent word to invite you to Priene; if living at Priene pleases you better, we will also come and live there with you.

## CHAPTER XII.

### His Students and Scholars.

The first eminent person among those who heard Thales and professed his philosophy was **Anaximander**, son of Praxides, a Milesian, who flourished in the time of Polycrates, the tyrant of Samos.

Next is **Anaximenes**, also a Milesian, the son of Euristratus, who (according to <term>Eusebius</term><note>A Roman historian and Christian polemicist known for his chronological accounts of the ancient world.</note>) flourished in the second year of the 56th Olympiad. He was a scholar of Anaximander and Parmenides. 
<margin>* Laertius, *Life of Anaximenes*.</margin>
But that he also heard Thales, he acknowledges in an epistle to Pythagoras.

We may (as Anaximenes does in that epistle) count among the disciples of Thales **Pythagoras** the Samian, founder of the <term>Italick Sect</term><note>The school of philosophy founded by Pythagoras in Southern Italy.</note>. Being from his youth particularly dedicated to the investigation of religious mysteries, he addressed his first journey to Thales at Miletus, as to one who could best further his design. 
<margin>* *On the Life of Pythagoras*, Book 2.</margin>
He was (according to <term>Jamblichus</term><note>A Neoplatonist philosopher and biographer of Pythagoras.</note>) not quite 18 years old. If we follow the account of Eusebius for his birth (the fourth year of the 70th Olympiad) and that of Sosicrates for his age of eighty years (for other accounts, the further they exceed 그hat time, are so much the more impossible to reconcile), this would fall about the second year of the 54th Olympiad, which is the 82nd year of Thales' life. From Thales, he received the basic elements of that excellence which he afterwards attained. This is acknowledged 
<margin>* *Life of Pythagoras*, Book 2.</margin>
by Jamblichus. Thales, he says, 

> *received him very kindly, admiring the difference between him and other youths, which exceeded the reputation he had heard of him. After he had instructed him as well as he was able in mathematics—pleading his own old age and infirmity as an excuse—he advised him to go to Egypt and to converse with the Memphian priests, especially those of <term>Jupiter</term><note>Likely referring to the Egyptian god Amun, whom the Greeks and Romans identified with Zeus/Jupiter.</note>. Thales himself had 
<margin>* *Life of Pythagoras*, Book 3.</margin>
learned those things from them during his own travels, for which he was esteemed wise by many. Furthermore, among other things, Thales chiefly advised him to manage his time well; in respect of which, Pythagoras abstained from wine and meat, eating only such things as are light and easy to digest. By these means, he achieved short sleep, alertness, purity of mind, and constant health of body.*

## CHAPTER XIII.
# On His Death.

<margin>* Diogenes Laertius.</margin>
Thales, having now lived to a great age and being full of honor and wisdom, died in the first year of the fifty-eighth Olympiad (when, according to *Pausanias*, *Erxyclides* was Archon). He passed away while he was watching the Olympic games, overcome by heat, thirst, and the weight of his years, which amounted to ninety-two. *Laertius* underestimates his age, stating he lived only eighty-seven years, despite having previously acknowledged that his birth was in 
<margin>* Petavius, *The Rational of Time*, Book 12. <note>original: "Rationar. temp. l. 12."</note></margin>
the first year of the 35th Olympiad. *Petavius* overestimates, claiming he lived to the end of the 58th Olympiad, which could not be the case because 
<margin>* Lucian, *On the Long-Lived*. <note>original: "De longævis"</note> * Syncellus, *Chronology*.</margin>
he died while attending the Olympic Games. *Lucian* and *Syncellus* claim even more, stating that he lived 100 years. *Sosicrates* comes closest to the truth, allowing him to have lived 90 years and to have died in the 58th Olympiad; for from the first year of the 35th <note>The OCR text reads "38," but the calculation of 23 Olympiads requires the starting year to be the 35th, as mentioned previously.</note> Olympiad, there are 23 entire Olympiads.

The manner of his death gave *Laertius* the occasion to honor him with this epigram:

->*Viewing the Olympic games, Elean Jove,*<note>Zeus, the chief deity honored at the Olympic games in the region of Elis.</note><-
->*You took wise Thales from that race*<-
->*Nearer to yourself; and it was well done, for being old,*<-
->*He could not well from Earth the stars behold.*<-

<margin>Plutarch, *Life of Solon*.</margin>
He was buried according to his own instructions in a poor, obscure part of the Milesian field, where he predicted that in future times their *Forum* <term>Forum</term><note>The public marketplace and center of civic life in a Greek city-state.</note> would be located. Upon his tomb was this couplet:

->*Narrow is this tomb, but the fame—wider than heaven—*<-
->*Of wisest Thales, whom this earth does hide.*<-

There was also a statue erected in his honor bearing this inscription:

->*This represents Milesian Thales,*<-
->*Who surpassed all others in wise astrology.*<term>Astrology</term><note>In this historical context, "astrology" refers to what we now call astronomy.</note><-

<margin>* Diogenes Laertius.</margin>
There were five others of this name mentioned by *Demetrius* of Magnesia: an orator of Calatis, who was an affected imitator; a painter of Sicyon, who possessed a great spirit; a third who was very ancient, a contemporary of *Hesiod*, *Homer*, and *Lycurgus*; a fourth mentioned 
<margin>* Life of Socrates.</margin>
by *Duris*; and a fifth of later times, mentioned by *Dionysius* in his critical works <note>original: "in Criticis"</note>. *Laertius* names *Pherecydes* as a critic of *Thales* the philosopher.

# SOLON.

## CHAPTER I.

### Solon’s Parents, Country, and Condition.

<margin>Plutarch.</margin>
Philocles, cited by *Didymus*, affirms that *Solon’s* father was named *Euphorion*; but by the unanimous consent of all other writers, he was called *Execestides*. He was a person who, though of small fortune and status among the citizens, was yet of the most noble family in *Athens*, being descended <margin>* Laertius. Plutarch.</margin> from *Codrus*. *Solon* traced his own descent from *Neleus*, the son of *Codrus*, and from *Neptune*. <margin>* Laertius, *Life*; Proclus in *Timaeus*.</margin> His mother was a close relative of the mother of *Pisistratus*. His parents had another son named *Dropides*, who was Archon the year after *Solon*; from him, *Plato* was descended.

*Solon* was born (according to *Laertius*) at *Salamis*, which is why he requested at his death that his body be carried there; however, because of his parents and his place of residence, he was surnamed *the Athenian*.

<margin>* Plutarch.</margin>
His father, through his generosity and liberality, reduced his estate so much that he even lacked necessities. *Solon* (being ashamed to receive help from anyone, as he came from a house that used to support others) took up <term>merchandising</term> <note>engaging in trade or commerce</note>. Others say he traveled instead to improve his knowledge and experience, for he was a devoted lover of wisdom, and even until his final days used to say, "*I grow old while learning.*" He did not value riches much, except to grow wealthy like:

->— him who abounds, <-
> *In heaps of gold, as in the grain of his fields,*
> *In mules and horses, while his abundant wealth*
> *Is made pleasant by uninterrupted health;*
> *If to complete these joys, he is possessed*
> *Of wife and children, he is truly blessed.*

And elsewhere:
> *Riches I desire, but not riches gained*
> *Through unjust means, for vengeance comes at last.*

That he was extravagant and refined, and more luxurious in his verses than is appropriate for a philosopher, is attributed to his career in <term>merchandising</term>, as such people require more than ordinary

<summary>This page introduces Solon of Athens, detailing his noble ancestry (descending from Codrus and Neptune), his family connections to Pisistratus and Plato, and his early life as a merchant driven by his family's lost fortune.</summary>
<keywords>Solon, Athens, Execestides, Codrus, Pisistratus, Plato, Salamis, merchandising, philosophy, lineage</keywords>

delicacies and freedoms in compensation for their many and great dangers. That he was numbered among the poor rather than the rich is apparent from these verses of his:

> Many unjust men grow rich, while the pious remain poor;
> Yet we would not trade our virtue for their wealth.
> For constant virtue is a solid base,
> While riches pass uncertainly from man to man.

<margin>* Aristotle, *Politics* 4. 11. * In Scythia. * Oration 8.</margin>
\* *Aristotle* ranks *Solon* among the lower class of citizens, which (he says) is manifest from his elegies—referring, perhaps, to some of those which *Plutarch* cites. \* *Lucian* says he was extremely poor; *Palaeologus* writes that he neither possessed nor valued wealth.

## CHAPTER II.

### How by his means the Athenians took Salamis, Cyrrha, and the Thracian Chersonesus.

<margin>* Demosthenes, *Speech on the False Embassy*.</margin>
Many (says \* *Demosthenes*) who were once obscure and humble have become illustrious by the profession of wisdom. Solon flourished in extraordinary glory both while living and after death, and he was not denied the highest honors. He left a monument of his valor in the *Megarian Trophy*, and of his wisdom in the recovery of *Salamis*; the occasions were as follows.

<margin>* Pausanias. * Plutarch.</margin>
\* The island of *Salamis* revolted from the *Athenians* to the *Megarians*. \* The *Athenians*, after a long and troublesome war with the *Megarians* for its recovery, eventually grew so weary of the conflict that they gave it up. They passed a law forbidding anyone, upon pain of death, to speak or write anything to persuade the city to attempt to retake it. *Solon*, bearing this disgrace with much reluctance and seeing many young men in the city eager to renew the war (though they did not dare to propose it because of the edict), faked madness. He allowed news of his "condition" to spread through the city. Having privately composed some elegiac verses and memorized them, he came rushing into the *Forum* <note>The marketplace or Agora</note> wearing a cap (or as *Laertius* says, a garland). While the people flocked around him, he went up to the herald’s stand and sang his elegy, beginning like this:

> I come as a herald from the fair island of Salamis,
> To declare this message in verse:

<margin>* Pausanias.</margin>
\* The lines that most excited the people were these:

> I would rather have been born
> In the lands of Pholegandros or Sicinus than in Athens;
> For men will say, wherever I go:
> "This is one of those Athenians who lost Salamis."

<unclear>And, how years</unclear>
> Then let us go to Salamis, to renew our claim,
> And with the island recover our lost fame.

<margin>* Plutarch</margin>
* This poem was entitled *Salamis*; it consisted of a hundred very elegant verses. When he had finished singing, it was much applauded by his friends, particularly by Pisistratus, who urged the citizens to follow his advice. By this means the law was repealed and the war recommenced, in which Solon was made General. The common report is that, taking Pisistratus along with him (which is why some, such as Frontinus, Aeneas, and <note>Justin: Marcus Junianus Justinus, a Roman historian.</note> Justin, attribute the whole glory of the action to Pisistratus), he sailed to Colias. There, finding all the women celebrating the festival of Ceres <note>The Thesmophoria, a festival in honor of Ceres (Demeter) and Proserpine.</note>, he sent a trustworthy messenger to Salamis. This man, pretending to be a runaway, told the Megarians that if they wished to surprise the principal women of Athens, they should go with him immediately to Colias. The Megarians, believing what he said, manned a ship and sent it with him. As soon as Solon saw the ship coming from the island, he commanded the women to retire and ordered as many beardless young men to put on their gowns, headdresses, and shoes, while hiding daggers under their garments. They danced and played by the seaside until the enemy had landed and their ship was at anchor. By this time the Megarians, deceived by their outward appearance, landed in great haste and came upon them, thinking to take them away <margin>* Polyaenus, Book 1.</margin> by force; * but the Athenians suddenly drawing their swords, showed themselves to <margin>* Plutarch</margin> be men, not women. * The Megarians were all slain, with not one escaping, and the Athenians, going immediately to the island, captured it.

<margin>* Plutarch</margin>
* Others deny it was taken in this manner, but claim that Solon first received this answer from the Delphian Oracle:

> Let sacrifice be paid to those heroes,
> Who are laid beneath the Asopian ground,
> And, being dead, are surveyed by the setting sun.

Solon sailed to the island by night and sacrificed burnt offerings to the heroes, Periphemus and Cichris. Then he received five hundred Athenian volunteers on the condition that, if they gained the island, the supreme government of it should belong to them. Shipping his men in fishing boats, attended by one ship of thirty oars, they cast anchor by Salamis near a point opposite to Euboea. The Megarians who were in Salamis, hearing an uncertain rumor of this, rushed confusedly to arms and sent out a ship to bring them more certain intelligence of the enemy. Solon captured this ship as soon as it came near and, after killing the Megarians, manned it with choice Athenians whom he commanded to make directly for the city with all possible secrecy. In the mean time, he, with the rest of the Athenians,

### SOLON.

...assaulted the Megarians by land; and while they were in the fight, those who were in the ship made haste and took possession of the town. This account is confirmed by their solemnity: an Athenian ship first approaches in silence, then attacks with cries and shouts, as an armed man leaps forth and runs directly toward the Scirradian Promontory against those coming from the land. Nearby is the Temple of Mars built by Solon, for he defeated the Megarians and released without ransom all those who escaped the misery of the war. <margin>* Aelian <note>Claudius Aelianus, a Roman author and teacher of rhetoric.</note> says that he captured two Megarian ships, in which he placed Athenian officers and soldiers, ordering them to put on the enemy's armor. By this deception, he killed many of the Megarians while they were unarmed. * *Various History* 7. 19.</margin>

<margin>* Plutarch.</margin>
* But as the Megarians persisted in their obstinacy, resulting in the loss of many lives on both sides, the matter was referred to the Lacedaemonians <note>The Spartans.</note> to be decided. Many affirm that Solon alleged the authority of Homer, inserting a verse into his catalogue of ships, which he recited thus at the trial:

> *Ajax brought twelve vessels to Salamis,*
> *And stationed them where the Athenians stood.*

(By this second verse of his own making and addition, he demonstrated that Salamis anciently belonged to the Athenians.) But the Athenians consider this account fabulous, affirming that Solon demonstrated to the judges that Phylaeus and Eurysaces, the sons of Ajax, were made citizens by the Athenians and delivered this island to them. They lived, one at Brauron in Attica and the other in Melite, which is why there is a tribe named Philaidae after Phylaeus, to which Pisistratus belonged.
<margin>* Aelian, *Various History* 7. 19. * Plutarch. * Laertius.</margin>
* He defeated the Megarians in an oration, getting the better of them not with flattering words but with the weight of argument. To convince them more clearly, he cited the burial of the dead and the inscription of the names of the townships used by those of Salamis. He proved this by digging up several graves, showing they were after the manner of the Athenians and not the Megarians; for in Megara they buried their dead with their faces to the east, but in Athens to the west. However, Hereas of Megara denied this, affirming that the Megarians also buried with their faces toward the west. For further confirmation, Solon alleged that the Athenians had a separate coffin for each man, whereas the Megarians buried three or four in the same. It is also said that Solon was greatly helped by certain oracles of Apollo, in which the god calls Salamis "Ionia." This case was decided by five Spartans: Critolaidas, Amomphoretus, Hypsechidas, Anaxilas, and Cleomenes.

<margin>* Plutarch. * Pausanias in *Phocis*.</margin>
* By this action, Solon grew into great esteem and honor, but not long after, he became much more admired and celebrated by the Greeks for speaking on behalf of the Temple at Delphi. The Cyrrhaeans had committed many impieties against Apollo and cut off...

<margin>

* In the life of Solon

part of the land belonging to him.] Solon declared that they were obligated to provide relief and not allow the Cyrrheans to profane the Oracle, but rather that they should defend the cause of the Gods. The Amphictyons <term>A league of Greek tribes responsible for the protection of the temple at Delphi</term>, urged by him, undertook the war with great eagerness. Aristotle confirms this, crediting Solon with the honor of that enterprise. Aeschines says that the motion made by Solon was confirmed by the Oracle. Some claim Solon was made General, while others name Alcmaeon; however, according to Pausanias, the entire Greek army was led by Clisthenes, the Tyrant of Sicyon, and Solon was sent from Athens to serve as his counselor. Suidas states that he was chosen as a counselor by those who were selected for the service of that war. While Clisthenes besieged Cirrha, they inquired about the victory and received this answer from the Pythian Oracle:

> You shall not take this city’s fort before
> Blue Amphitrite’s <note>A sea goddess, personifying the sea</note> swelling billows roar
> Against my water-washed grove and hallowed shore.

<margin>* Pausanias, ibid.</margin>
Based on this, Solon advised them to consecrate the Cyrrhean field to **Apollo**, so that the sea would then touch sacred land. He also employed another stratagem against the Cyrrheans. He diverted the River **Plistus**, which ran through the city, another way. While the town held out against the besiegers, some inhabitants drank well water and others drank rainwater they had saved in cisterns. He ordered roots of hellebore <note>A poisonous plant used as a purgative</note> to be thrown into the **Plistus**. Once he found the water was fully poisoned, he turned the river back into its original channel. The Cyrrheans, drinking greedily of that water, were struck with a constant flux <note>Severe diarrhea or dysentery</note> and were forced to abandon the defense of their fortifications. Having taken possession of the city, the Amphictyons punished the Cyrrheans and avenged the Gods. These two stratagems were attributed to Clisthenes—the first by Polyaenus and the second by Frontinus—but the reason is clear: he performed them under Solon's direction.

<margin>
* Book 3
* Book 3, Chapter 7
* Diogenes Laertius
</margin>
Solon also persuaded the Athenians to bring the Thracian Chersonesus under their control.

### CHAP. III.

->How he settled differences and seditions at home and was appointed Archon.<-

<margin>* Plutarch</margin>
The Cylonian impiety <note>A blood-guilt incurred by the slaughter of Cylon's followers after a failed coup around 632 BC</note> had long troubled the city. It began when the accomplices of Cylon, who had taken sanctuary, were persuaded by Megacles the Archon to stand trial. They held onto a thread that was tied to the statue of **Pallas** <note>Athena</note>. When they came near the statues of the Furies, the thread broke by itself. At this, Megacles and the other Archons attacked them, claiming they had been disowned by the Goddess. Those who were outside the Temple, they stoned,

<meta>catchword: those</meta>

<summary>This page describes Solon's strategic leadership in the First Sacred War, including his poisoning of the Cyrrhean water supply, and begins describing his efforts to resolve the internal Athenian strife caused by the Cylonian massacre.</summary>
<keywords>Solon, Cyrrhean War, First Sacred War, Amphictyons, Clisthenes of Sicyon, Oracle of Delphi, Plistus River, Hellebore, Cylonian Impiety, Megacles, Archonship</keywords>

--- Page 59 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Solon, focusing on his resolution of the Cylonian conspiracy's aftermath, the purification of Athens by Epimenides, and the rising social and economic tensions between the rich and poor.</meta>

# SOLON.

Those who fled to the altars were murdered; only those who appealed to the wives [of the leaders] escaped. Because of this, they were called "impious" and were considered hateful. The survivors of the *Cylonians* grew very rich and were in perpetual conflict with the family of *Megacles*. At the time when this dissension was at its peak and the people were divided into factions, *Solon*, having great authority among them, took the city's leaders with him and intervened. Through persuasion and advice, he convinced those called "impious" to submit to the judgment of three hundred leading citizens. *Myron* was their accuser, and they were condemned: the living were banished, and the bones of the dead were dug up and cast beyond the country's borders.

<margin>* Diogenes Laertius * Plutarch * Diogenes Laertius * Plutarch</margin>
During these disturbances, the *Megarians* took *Nisaea* and recovered *Salamis* from the *Athenians*. The city was full of superstitious fears and apparitions. The priests declared that the entrails of sacrificed animals indicated great crimes and impieties that required purification. * There was also a great plague; * the Oracle advised them to purify the city. To this end, they sent (*Nicias*, son of *Niceratus*, with a ship) * to bring *Epimenides* from *Crete*. When he came to *Athens*, he was welcomed as *Solon’s* guest and lived with him as a friend. He instructed Solon in many things and set him on the path to making laws. *Eusebius underestimates the date of this purification of the city, placing it in the second year of the 47th Olympiad, whereas Solon’s term as Archon—which certainly happened after this—was in the third year of the 46th. Suidas seems to overestimate it, ranking it in the 44th.* <margin>* In the life of Epimenides.</margin> The opinion of * *Diogenes Laertius* agrees best with the circumstances of the story: that it occurred in the 46th Olympiad.

<margin>* Plutarch</margin>
* Once the disturbances of the *Cylonians* were settled and the offenders removed, the people returned to their old dispute regarding the government of the commonwealth. They were divided into as many factions as there were regional distinctions among the people: the city-dwellers favored a *democracy*, the country-dwellers preferred an *oligarchy*, and the coastal people supported a mixed kind of government, preventing either of the other parties from ruling. At the same time, the city was in a dangerous condition because of the conflict between the rich and the poor, arising from their inequality. The situation seemed impossible to resolve except by a *monarchy*. The common people were generally oppressed by the money they had borrowed from the rich; they either farmed their land while paying a sixth part of the crop to the owners—because of which they were called *Hectemoroi* <term>Six-parters; tenant farmers who paid one-sixth of their produce as rent</term> and *Thetes* <term>The lowest social class of citizens, often manual laborers</term>—or they pledged their own bodies to their creditors. Some of these debtors served at home as slaves, while others were sold abroad. Many (as there was no law to the contrary) were even forced to sell their children and leave the city to escape the cruelty of these usu-
->rers,<-

<summary>Solon resolves the internal strife caused by the Cylonian conspiracy and assists Epimenides in purifying Athens. The page also describes the severe economic inequality and debt slavery that pushed Athens toward the brink of revolution or monarchy.</summary>
<keywords>Solon, Cylonian conspiracy, Epimenides, Athens, class conflict, debt slavery, Hectemoroi, Thetes, Olympiad chronology</keywords>

--- Page 60 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Solon, focusing on the social unrest in Athens and Solon's appointment as Archon to resolve the conflict between the debtors and the creditors.</meta>

# SOLON.

The debtors, for the most part (specifically those with the most courage among them), assembled together and encouraged one another not to endure these things any longer. Instead, they planned to choose a trustworthy man to be their leader, to release those who had not paid their money by the set day, to redistribute the land, and to completely restructure the government of the commonwealth. The wisest among the Athenians, looking upon *Solon* as a person free from any crime—being neither involved in the oppressions of the rich nor entangled in the needs of the poor—entreated him to take charge of the commonwealth and to resolve the differences of the people. *Phanias* of *Lesbos* claims that, for the preservation of the state, *Solon* deceived both parties by secretly promising the poor a division of the land and promising the rich that he would uphold their contracts. However, he initially hesitated to undertake the business, deterred by the greed of the one side and the insolence of the other.

<margin>* Diogenes Laertius</margin>
He was chosen Archon immediately after *Cleombrotus* (in the third year of the forty-sixth Olympiad <note>594–593 BC</note>), at which time he also established his laws. He served at once as a peacemaker and a lawmaker, acceptable to the rich because of his wealth, and to the poor because of his virtue. The people often repeated a saying of his: "equality breeds no strife," which pleased both parties alike; one side understood it in terms of number and measure, while the other understood it in terms of merit and virtue. Encouraged by this hope, the most powerful of both factions courted him intensely and desired him to take upon himself the <term>tyranny</term> <note>absolute rule, not necessarily oppressive in the Greek context</note> of the commonwealth, which was now in his power, offering themselves to assist him. Many of the more moderate citizens, seeing how laborious and difficult it would be to reform the state by reason and law alone, were not unwilling to have a prince created—specifically one who was prudent and just. Some affirm he received this Oracle from *Apollo*:

> Sit at the helm of state, their pilot be;
> The commonwealth is glad to be steered by thee.

But he was most of all criticized by his close friends for being discouraged by the name of "Tyranny," as if the virtue of a King were not spread throughout the kingdom. They cited the examples of *Tynondas*, long ago the tyrant of *Euboea*, and *Pittacus*, who was then the tyrant of *Mytilene*. Nothing they alleged could move him; he told them that a tyranny was a beautiful possession, but it had no exit. Writing to *Phocus* in verse, he said:

> That I preserved free my native soil,
> Nor did with bloody tyranny defile
> My honor; I do not blush at this deed;
> In this, all that was done by others I exceed.

<summary>Solon is elected Archon of Athens to resolve a looming civil war between debt-stricken commoners and wealthy creditors; despite pressure from friends and the public, he refuses to become an absolute tyrant.</summary>
<keywords>Solon, Archon, Athens, social reform, debt relief, tyranny, Phanias of Lesbos, Apollo's Oracle, Pittacus, Mytilene</keywords>

--- Page 61 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Solon, describing his refusal to become a tyrant and his initial major economic reform, the Seisachtheia, aimed at relieving the debt crisis in Athens.</meta>

From this, it appears he held great authority before he wrote his laws. He expressed the insults of those who criticized him for refusing to take absolute control of the government in these verses:

> *Solon is neither wise, nor does he know good counsel,*
> *For he rejects the good that God bestows.*
> *He saw the prey within his power,*
> *But would not pull the net; his thoughts were meanly cold.*
> *Had his soul been fired with noble aims,*
> *He would have desired the kingdom for just one day,*
> *Even if he were then destroyed, and his whole family perished.*

## CHAP. III.

### *What alterations he made during his government, and first of the Seisachtheia.*

<margin>* Plutarch</margin>
**A**lthough he refused to be a tyrant, he did not behave weakly in his government. He did not simply comply with the powerful, nor did he make laws merely to please those who had elected him. Where things were tolerable, he neither corrected nor altered anything, fearing that if he should overhaul and throw the entire commonwealth into confusion, he would lack the strength to stabilize it again and to balance it with reason. However, in those matters where he believed he could persuade the willing and compel the stubborn, he enacted laws, joining (as he said) force and justice together. Because of this, when he was later asked if he had given the Athenians the best laws, he replied, "The best that they would receive."

<margin>* Plutarch. * Laertius. * Laertius.</margin>
The first change he made in the government was this: he introduced the *Seisachtheia* <note>Greek: "shaking off of burdens"</note>, which was a release of persons and property. Or, as Hesychius <note>an Alexandrian grammarian</note> defines it, it was a law for the forgiveness of private and public debts, so called because it "shook off" the oppression of usury. At that time, people used their own bodies as collateral for payment, and many were forced into servitude to their creditors because of their poverty. He therefore ordained that all past debts should be canceled, and for the future, no security should be taken against a person's body. He called this reform by the moderate term *Seisachtheia*. There are some (including Androtion) who claim he satisfied the poor not by a total cancellation of debt, but by reducing the interest rate, which he called *Seisachtheia*. To this he added an increase in weights and measures and a revaluation of money. For the *mina*, which previously equaled 73 *drachmas*, he increased to 100. By this means, the poor paid a larger nominal sum using less actual silver, which provided great relief to the debtor without doing legal wrong to the creditor. However, most historians hold that it was an absolute cancellation of debt. This agrees best with the verses of Solon, in which he boasts that he had removed the boundary stones <note>markers of mortgaged land</note> throughout the land.

<summary>Solon defends his refusal to become a tyrant through poetry and initiates the Seisachtheia, a major reform that canceled debts and ended the practice of using one's body as collateral for loans.</summary>
<keywords>Solon, Seisachtheia, debt relief, Athens, usury, economic reform, coinage devaluation, Androtion, Plutarch, ancient Greek law</keywords>

--- Page 62 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Solon, specifically detailing the implementation and immediate fallout of his economic reform, the Seisachtheia (the shaking off of burdens).</meta>

<page-num>38</page-num>
<header>SOLON.</header>

<margin>* Book 1.</margin>
Solon freed those who were under oppression and called home those who, having been forced to travel, had forgotten their native language; he also set at liberty others at home who were in bondage. Diodorus Siculus observes that this same law existed among the Egyptians, believing that Solon (though he had not yet been there) derived it from them.

<margin>* Plutarch</margin>
* But in this plan, a great misfortune befell him. While he endeavored to remedy the oppression of usury <term>the practice of lending money at unreasonably high interest rates</term> and was studying how to begin a speech suitable to the matter, he informed his intimate friends in whom he placed the most confidence—Conon, Clinias, and Hipponicus—that he intended not to interfere with the land, but to cancel all debts. These friends (anticipating the Edict) borrowed great sums of money from the rich, with which they purchased a great deal of land. When the Edict was published, they enjoyed their purchase without paying back their creditors. Solon was much blamed, as if he had not been cheated along with the rest, but was instead a cheater himself and a partner in their deception. However, this accusation was immediately cleared by the five talents <note>a unit of currency</note> he himself had out on loan at interest, which he was the first to cancel according to the law (Diogenes Laertius says it was six talents, and that he persuaded others to do the same; others, including Polyzelus the Rhodian, say it was fifteen). But his friends were ever after called *Chreocopidae* <note>Greek: "debt-cutters"</note>.

<margin>* Plutarch</margin>
* This pleased neither party. He discontented the rich by cancelling their bonds, and the poor even more by not establishing an equal distribution of property, which they expected—just as Lycurgus had done. Lycurgus, being the eleventh descendant from Hercules and having reigned many years in Lacedaemon <note>Sparta</note>, possessed great authority, friends, and wealth. This enabled him to enact what he thought best for the state through force rather than persuasion—even to the point of losing an eye in the process—achieving as a result a thing most beneficial to the preservation and peace of the commonwealth: that none of the citizens were either rich or poor. But Solon did not achieve this in the commonwealth; he was one of the common people and of a middle rank. Yet he omitted nothing within his power, guided by his own judgment and the trust the citizens had in him. That he displeased many who expected other things is acknowledged by Solon himself in these verses:

> Before, they looked upon me kindly; now,
> With eyes of anger and a furrowed brow.
> Had any other my power, he would extort
> Their riches, and their richest milk extract.

But both parties soon found how much this contributed to the general good; and laying aside their private differences, they sacrificed together, calling the sacrifice *Seisachtheia* <note>Greek: "shaking off of burdens"</note>.

<header>SOLON.</header>

### CHAP. 5.

->How he divided the people into classes and established<-
->courts of justice.<-

<margin>* Plutarch</margin>
**H**ereupon they chose *Solon* as the reformer and lawgiver of the Commonwealth, not limiting him in any way, but submitting everything to his power: the magistracies, assemblies, judgments, and the courts that held them to account. He was empowered to prescribe whatever number and times he pleased, and to annul or ratify any existing laws as he saw fit.

<margin>
* Plutarch
* Aristotle's Rhetoric, Book 1, Chapter 23.
* Aulus Gellius, Book 11, Chapter 18.
</margin>
* First, he completely abolished all the laws of *Draco*, except for those concerning murder, because of their rigidity and severity. *Draco* punished almost all offenses with death; for instance, those caught in idleness were put to death, and those who stole herbs or apples underwent the same punishment as those who had committed murder or sacrilege. Because of this, *Demades* wittily said that *Draco* wrote his laws not in ink, but in blood. When *Draco* was asked why he punished all offenses with death, he answered that he conceived the least offense deserved that much, and he knew of no greater penalty for the greatest crimes. *Herodicus*, alluding to his name, said his laws were not those of a man, but of a dragon <note>The name "Draco" or "Drakon" means dragon or serpent in Greek</note>, they were so rigid. *Aristotle* says there was nothing extraordinary or memorable in them except for that severity and greatness of penalty, which was so excessive that they were laid aside—not by any official edict or command, but by the silent and expressed consent of the Athenians. Afterwards, they used the milder laws made by *Solon*, which even differed in name: the first being called *nomoi* <note>original: "νόμοι", meaning laws</note>, and the latter *thesmoi* <note>original: "θεσμοί", meaning ordinances or statutes</note>.

<margin>
* Tatian, Clement of Alexandria, Suidas.
* In Demosthenes against Timocrates.
</margin>
* The laws of *Draco* were made in the 39th Olympiad, forty-seven years (as *Ulpian* calculates) before those of *Solon*.

<margin>
* Plutarch
* Pollux
* Pollux
* Pollux
* Pollux
* Pollux
</margin>
* Next, *Solon*, desiring that all high offices might remain as they were in the hands of the rich, but that other privileges of the Commonwealth—from which the common people were previously excluded—might be distributed more broadly, took an account and valuation of the people [and divided them into four orders]. Those whose stock of dry and liquid fruits amounted to 500 measures he ranked in the first class and called *Pentacosiomedimni* <note>Greek: "five-hundred-measure men"</note>; [these paid one talent to the public treasury]. In the second class were those who were able to maintain a horse or received 300 measures; these he called [for that reason] *Hippeis* <note>meaning horsemen or knights</note>; they paid half a talent. The third class were the *Zeugitae* [so called because] they had 200 measures of both types of goods; these paid ten minae. The rest were all called *Thetes* <note>laborers or serfs</note>, whom he did not allow to be eligible for any magistracy, nor did they pay any taxes. However, they had a stake in the Commonwealth insofar as they had a vote in the public assembly and at trials. This seemed like nothing at first, but it later proved to be of great consequence;

<summary>Solon is appointed as the supreme lawgiver of Athens, leading him to abolish Draco's infamously bloody legal code and reorganize Athenian society into four distinct economic classes.</summary>

<page-num>40</page-num>
<header>SOLON.</header>

For in whatever case was brought before the Judges, he gave the people leave (if they wished) to appeal to the common assembly. Moreover, by writing his laws obscurely and confusingly, he increased the power of the assembly; since the people were unable to settle controversies by the strict letter of the Law, they were forced to turn to the Judges as masters of the law. This equality he expressed himself in this way:

> *I gave the common people sufficient power,*
> *Taking no honor from them, nor bestowing too much;*
> *Those who outshined the rest in power or wealth,*
> *I confined within the bounds of moderation;*
> *To both sides I was a firm defense,*
> *And allowed neither to have preeminence.*

<margin>
* Seneca, Epistle 90.
* Justin, Book 2.
* Aeneas Gazaeus, In Theophrastus.
</margin>
Alluding to this, *Seneca* says, "*Solon founded Athens upon equal rights*," and *Justin* states, "*he carried himself with such moderation between the common people and the Senate that he gained equal favor from both*." He allowed no man (says *Aeneas Gazaeus*) "*to have a private law, but made all men subject to the same.*"

<margin>* Plutarch.</margin>
He also (continues *Plutarch*) established the court of the **Areopagus**, consisting of the annual **Archons**, of which he himself (being the chief) was one. Perceiving the people to be greatly emboldened and uplifted by the cancellation of their debts, he ordained a second Court of Justice, selecting one hundred persons from each tribe (of which there were four in all). These members were to decide upon all decrees before they were reported to the people; nothing could be brought before the general assembly until it had first passed this Senate. He appointed this supreme Senate as the judge and preserver of the laws, believing the city would be less likely to drift aimlessly, and the people would become more stable by relying upon these two courts as two anchors. Thus, most writers consider *Solon* the founder of the Court of the **Areopagus**,

<margin>* Cicero, On Duties, Book 1.</margin>
(among whom is *Cicero*). This seems to be confirmed by the fact that *Draco* never mentions the **Areopagites**, but in criminal cases always names the **Ephetae** <term>A body of 51 judges who handled cases of homicide</term>. However, the eighth law of the thirtieth table of *Solon* contains these words: "*Those who were branded with infamy before* Solon *was Archon shall have their reputations restored, except for those who were condemned by the Areopagites or by the Ephetae, etc.*" It is certain, then, that the Court of the *Areopagus* existed long before *Solon's* time; until then, it consisted of various people prominent for their nobility, power, or riches. But *Solon* reformed it, ordaining that no one should be a member except for those who had first served in the office of *Archon*. See **Meursius, *Areopagus*, chapter 3.**

<margin>
* Pollux, Book 8, chapter 6.
* Scholiast on Aristophanes' *The Clouds*.
</margin>
* *Pollux* says that *Solon* ordained one thousand men to judge all accusations; * *Demetrius of Phalerum* says that he established the **Demarchs** <term>Heads of the "demes" or local districts</term>, who were first called **Nauclari** <term>Local administrative officers responsible for funding ships</term>.

<summary>Solon establishes the "two anchors" of the Athenian state—the Areopagus and the Senate of Four Hundred—to balance the power of the common people and the nobility. He also reforms the judiciary to allow appeals to popular courts, ensuring all citizens are subject to the same laws.</summary>

<header>SOLON</header>
<sig>M</sig>

# CHAPTER VI.

->His Laws.<-

Having thus organized the commonwealth and the courts of justice, he next applied himself to making laws. He performed this so excellently that he is generally remembered for this achievement, just as *Minos* of *Crete* and *Lycurgus* of *Lacedaemon* are. *Solon's* laws exceeded theirs
<margin>* Annals, Book 3.</margin>
(as *Tacitus* says) both in their precision and their number.
<margin>* Manuel Paleologus in *Exhortation to Instruction*.</margin>
*How much more highly they were esteemed than all laws before them may be judged by this: they were the last laws made, and they always remained in use in the city. The people for whom they were made thought them more illustrious than their public ornaments—which surpassed those of all other cities—and more impregnable than their Tower <note>referring to the Acropolis</note>, which they considered the strongest on earth, and far better than those things in which they gloried most.* Nor were they held in less esteem among foreign nations, so much so that the
<margin>* Livy, Book 3.</margin>
Romans—agreeing on the need for laws in general but differing over who should give them—sent ambassadors to *Athens*: *Spurius Posthumius Albus*, *Aulus Manlius*, and *Publius Sulpitius Camerinus*. They commanded them to transcribe
<margin>* Aurelius Victor, *On Illustrious Men*, chapter 21.</margin>
the renowned laws of *Solon*. Once these were transferred out of the books of *Solon*, the *Decemviri* <term>a commission of ten men appointed to codify Roman law</term> explained them in the Twelve Tables. Hence
<margin>* Ammianus, Book 22.</margin>
*Ammianus Marcellinus says that Solon, assisted by the teachings of the Egyptian priests, having framed laws with just moderation, also provided the Roman state with its greatest foundation.*

Of his laws, these have been preserved by *Plutarch* and others:

<margin>* Plutarch.</margin>
> *If any man were beaten, hurt, or treated with violence, whoever had the means and the will to do so might sue the offender.* Thus (says *Plutarch*) he wisely brought the citizens to a mutual feeling for one another's hurts, as if they were done to a limb of their own body.

<margin>* Plutarch.</margin>
> *Regarding infamous persons <term>those deprived of civil rights</term>: let all who were considered infamous before the government of Solon be restored to their status, except for anyone condemned by the Ephetae <note>a court of fifty-one judges who tried cases of homicide</note>, or in the Prytaneum <note>the seat of government and a court for specific crimes</note> by the Magistrates, or those banished for murder, theft, or aspiring to become a tyrant.*

This was the eighth Law of the thirteenth Table. There were two kinds of infamy: by the lesser, a man was degraded and made incapable of holding any honor or office in the commonwealth; by the greater, he and his children were liable to be killed by any man, and the killer would not be questioned for it.

<margin>* Plutarch.</margin>
> *Of his laws, those seem most unique and paradoxical which declare a man infamous if he takes neither side during a rebellion:* 

<margin>* Book 2, chapter 12.</margin>
it is cited from *Aristotle* by *Aulus Gellius* <note>The original text uses "Agellius," a common archaic spelling for the Roman author Aulus Gellius</note> in these words: *If through discord and dissension, any rebellion and disagreement divides the people into two factions, leading both parties to take up arms and fight with angry minds; he, who at that time, and upon that occasion of civil discord...*

<summary>This page discusses the immense historical reputation of Solon's laws, noting their influence on the Roman Twelve Tables and describing specific statutes regarding victim rights, amnesty, and civil neutrality.</summary>
<keywords>Solon's Laws, Tacitus, Roman Law, Twelve Tables, Decemviri, infamy, atimia, legal reform, Plutarch, Aulus Gellius</keywords>

--- Page 66 ---
<meta>This page continues the catalog of Solon’s specific legislation, moving from the general establishment of his legal code to his specific regulations regarding civil duty during times of unrest and the laws governing marriage and inheritance.</meta>

<page-num>42</page-num>
<header>SOLON</header>
<meta>catchword: men-</meta>

...anyone who does not take a side during a civil discord, but remains solitary and separated from the city's common trouble, shall be deprived of his house, country, and goods through banishment.
<margin>* Plutarch</margin>
* He did not want anyone to keep themselves safe while being indifferent to the common calamity, or to boast that they had no share in the public grief. Instead, he wanted them to immediately join the better and more just side, involving themselves in the common danger and offering assistance, rather than waiting safely to see which side would prevail. When we read (says Aulus Gellius <note>original: "Agellius"</note>) this law of Solon—a man endowed with singular wisdom—we were at first in great suspense and wonder, asking why he judged those worthy of punishment who withdrew from sedition <term>civil unrest or insurrection</term> and civil war. Then one whose insight pierced more deeply into the purpose and meaning of the law affirmed that its intent was not to increase but to appease sedition. And so it is indeed; for if all good people—who are too few in the beginning to restrain a sedition—do not deter the distracted, raging populace but instead divide themselves and adhere to either side, it follows that as participants in both factions, the parties might be tempered and governed by them as people of the greatest authority. By this means, they might restore them to peace and reconcile them, governing and moderating the side they are on, and desiring much more that the opposing party be preserved
<margin>
* Cicero, *Letters to Atticus*, Book 10, Letter 1.
</margin>
rather than destroyed. * Cicero, citing this law, asserts that the punishment was capital—perhaps understanding "infamy" here in its most severe form <note>likely referring to *atimia*, the loss of all civil rights</note>.

Absurd and ridiculous (says Plutarch) seems that law which allows *an heiress <note>original: "inheritrix"</note>, if the man who possesses her by law as her lord and master is impotent, to admit any of her husband’s nearest kinsmen.* But some argue it is just toward those who, though they are impotent, marry rich heiresses for their money and use legal privilege to wrong nature. For when they see it is lawful for the heir to admit whomever she pleases, they will either refrain from such marriages or undergo them with the reproach of greed and dishonesty. It is also well ordered that she may not admit just anyone, but only those of her husband’s kindred, so that the offspring may be of his family and race.
<margin>* Plutarch</margin>
<margin>* In *Conjugal Precepts*.</margin>
* To this end, it is also required *that the bride be shut in a room with the bridegroom and eat a quince with him.* (Intimating, according to Plutarch’s interpretation, that the first grace of her lips and voice should be agreeable and sweet.) Furthermore, *he who marries an heiress is obliged to visit her at least three times a month.* For even if they have no children, this demonstrates the respect due to a chaste wife and prevents or reconciles unkindness and dissension.

Those words of the former law, *He who possesses her by law as her lord and master*, have reference to another law of his...

<summary>This page describes Solon's laws requiring citizens to take sides during civil strife to promote reconciliation, and his regulations ensuring that heiresses in marriages to impotent men could produce heirs through the husband's kin.</summary>

<header>SOLON</header>

<margin>* Book 12.</margin>
As mentioned by * Diodorus Siculus, the law required that the next of kin to an heiress could demand her in marriage. Likewise, she could demand that her next of kin marry her, and he was obliged to do so—no matter how poor he was—or else pay 500 drachmas <term>an ancient Greek silver coin</term> for her dowry. * Terence alludes to this:

<margin>* Hecyra, Act 1, Scene 2.</margin>
> The law commands an heiress to marry
> Her husband's next of kin, and for him to take her.

<margin>* Phormio, Act 2, Scene 3.</margin>
And elsewhere, regarding the option of dismissing her with a dowry of 500 drachmas (which is five minas):

> Though I am injured this way, yet rather than
> Being argumentative or forced to endure you;
> Since she is my kinswoman, take her from here and
> Include the dowry the law requires: five pounds. <note>The author equates five minas with five pounds in 17th-century currency.</note>

<margin>* Plutarch.</margin>
<margin>* Book 12. 15.</margin>
<margin>* Book 3, Epistle 243.</margin>
* In all other marriages, he forbade dowries. He ordained that a bride should bring with her no more than three gowns and some modest household goods of small value. The specific items were listed, as * Pollux seems to imply; for Solon did not want marriage to be mercenary or for sale. Instead, he intended for a man and woman to live together for the sake of children, love, and friendship. * Isidor alludes to this, noting that among the Athenians, legal marriage was said to be contracted for the purpose of having offspring.

<margin>* Oration against Leptines.</margin>
<margin>* Plutarch.</margin>
Another of his laws was highly commended, as * Demosthenes and Plutarch attest: the law that forbade speaking ill of the dead. Let no man revile any dead person, even if provoked by the insults of that person’s children.

> * To honor the deceased as holy is pious, to spare the absent is just,
> and to end eternal hatred is civilized.

<margin>* Plutarch.</margin>
* He also forbade anyone to insult a living person at sacred ceremonies, in courts of law, or at public performances, under penalty of paying three drachmas to the insulted person and two more to the public treasury. He believed that failing to moderate one's anger anywhere was rude and disorderly, and while difficult for everyone, it was impossible for some. He felt a law must be adapted to what is possible, aiming to punish a few as an example rather than trying to reform many to no purpose.

<margin>* Plutarch.</margin>
* His law concerning wills <term>testaments</term> is also highly regarded. Previously, no man had the power to make a will; instead, his goods and lands remained within the deceased person's family. Solon made it legal for a man who had no children to give his estate to whomever he pleased. He prioritized friendship over kinship and personal favor over legal necessity, ordering that wealth should be at the disposal of its owner. Yet, he did not permit this recklessly or without limits, but only conditionally: if the man was not influenced by sickness, drugs, imprisonment, or the flatteries of a wife. He rightly judged that being seduced by indirect means is the same as being violently forced; thus, he compared deceit to force, and

<margin>* Oration against Leptines</margin>
...pleasure with pain, as both have equal power to distract a man from his right mind. This law is also mentioned by *Demosthenes*.

<margin>* On the Laws, Book 2</margin>
* He also limited the visits, mourning practices, and feasts of women through a law that restrained their previous lack of discipline. *He did not permit any woman going abroad to carry more than three gowns with her, nor more food and drink than could be bought with one obolus <term>an ancient Greek coin of small value</term>, nor a basket larger than one cubit <term>roughly 18 inches</term> in size. He also forbade them from traveling at night, unless they were in a chariot by torchlight. He prohibited them from scratching or scarring their faces to provoke mourning and lamentation at the funerals of those to whom they were not related. He forbade the sacrifice of an ox at funerals and the burial of more than three garments with the dead body. He also forbade people from approaching the monuments of strangers except during their funeral rites <term>exequies</term>.* *Plutarch* says our laws are full of these regulations. *Cicero also confirms that the laws of the Twelve Tables <term>the foundation of Roman law</term>, meant to reduce the extravagance of funerals and mourning, were transferred from those of *Solon*. *Phalereus* writes that as soon as funerals began to be performed with excessive pomp and mourning, Solon abolished those practices. The *Decemviri* <term>the ten men who drafted Roman law</term> included this in the Tenth Table, using almost the exact same words. The laws regarding the proximity of buildings <note>The original text refers to "neighbourhoods," likely meaning property boundaries.</note> and most other regulations are Solon's, including the specific rule on mourning in his exact words: *Let not women scratch their faces, nor make loud lamentations at a funeral.*

<margin>
* Plutarch
* Preface, Book 6
* Exhortation to the Arts
* Epistle 7
* Book 7
* Book 1
</margin>
* Considering that the city was becoming very populous—as many people from all parts of *Attica* came there for liberty and security—and that the land was mostly barren and poor, Solon realized that sea traders would not bring goods to those who had nothing to trade or exchange. Therefore, he encouraged the citizens to take up trades and crafts. He made a law stating that a son was not obligated to support his father in old age if the father had not raised him to a trade. (This is also mentioned by *Vitruvius*, *Galen, *Theophylact, and others.) He commanded the court of the *Areopagus* <note>the high court of Athens</note> to investigate how every man supported himself and to punish idle persons. He made such people liable to legal action by anyone; upon a third conviction, they were punished with infamy <term>atimia, the loss of civil rights</term>. *Herodotus* and *Diodorus Siculus* claim this law was used by the Egyptians, established by *Amasis* <note>Amasis II, Pharaoh of Egypt</note>, and brought from them by *Solon* to the Athenians.

<margin>* Plutarch</margin>
* Even more severe was the law mentioned by *Heraclides* of *Pontus*, which released the sons of concubines from the duty of supporting their fathers. He argued that a man who violates the boundaries of marriage does so not out of a desire for children, but for pleasure; therefore, he has already received his reward and cannot expect to have any further claim on the children he fathered, whose birth is their shame.

However, anyone who raped a free woman was fined only 100 drachmas, and anyone who acted as a pimp was fined 20 (*Aeschines* says the penalty was death), except in cases involving...
