# SOLON.

...women as were common prostitutes. He also forbade any man to give his sister or daughter to that profession unless he himself first caught her with a man. This (says *Plutarch*) seems absurd: to punish the same offense sometimes severely with death, and sometimes with a financial fine—unless, because money was very rare in *Athens* at that time, the scarcity of it increased the severity of the punishment.

<margin>* Plutarch</margin>
* He assigned five hundred drachmas to the victor of the *Isthmian* games, and *a hundred to the victor of the Olympic games*; this is also attested by *Laertius*, who says Solon reduced the rewards of the athletes, judging them to be dangerous victors, and that they were crowned more in opposition to their country than for its benefit.

<margin>* Plutarch</margin>
* *Whoever brought a male wolf was to receive five drachmas, and for a female wolf, one*; according to *Demetrius Phalereus*, the latter was the price of a sheep, and the former the price of an ox. It is customary with the Athenians that those who have lands better suited for pasture than plowing make war against the wolves.

<margin>* Plutarch</margin>
* Because there is such a scarcity of rivers, lakes, and springs in the country that the people are forced to dig wells, he made a law that *where there was a common well within a hippicon, they should use it.* (A *hippicon* <term>an ancient Greek measure of distance</term> is the distance of four furlongs <term>about half a mile</term>.) *Those who lived further away should procure their own water; however, if they dug ten fathoms deep and found none, they were allowed to fill a six-gallon pitcher twice a day at their neighbor’s well.*

<margin>* Plutarch</margin>
* He prescribed these exact rules for planting: *Whoever planted any young tree in his ground had to set it five feet away from his neighbor's land; if it was a fig tree or olive tree, it had to be nine feet away.* This is because the roots of these trees spread far, and their presence is not harmless to all; they suck away nourishment and, for some plants, their proximity is prejudicial.

<margin>* Plutarch. * Gaius, Book 4 on the Law of the 12 Tables.</margin>
* *Whoever digs any hole or ditch must make it as far from his neighbor's property as it is deep.* These rules are confirmed by *Gaius* <note>Gaius was a famous Roman jurist whose works formed the basis of later Roman law.</note>, who added: *Whoever makes a hedge to separate himself from his neighbor must not go beyond his own boundary; if it is a wall, he must leave a space of one foot; if a house, two feet; and if a well, one fathom <term>about six feet</term>.*

Whoever places a beehive should observe a distance of thirty feet from those already placed by his neighbor.

<margin>* Plutarch</margin>
* He commanded the *archons* <term>chief magistrates of Athens</term> *to curse anyone who exported any products out of the country*, or else that person *should pay a hundred drachmas to the public treasury*. Because of this, we should not dismiss the claims of those who say that in ancient times the exportation of figs was prohibited, and that the person who informed on an exporter was called a "sycophant" <note>The word "sycophant" literally translates from Greek as "one who shows figs," referring to those who informed on illegal exporters.</note>.

<margin>* Plutarch</margin>
* He made a law concerning those who should be hurt by a dog, in which he ordained that *the dog that bit someone must be bound in a chain four cubits <term>about six feet</term> long.*

<margin>* Plutarch</margin>
* This law concerning denization <term>the process of a foreigner becoming a citizen</term> is difficult: *That no one should be made a freeman of the city except those who were banished forever from their own country, or those who came to Athens with their whole families to exercise some*

trade.

trade. He did this not to drive away foreigners, but to invite them to Athens by the certainty of being admitted into the City. He believed such people would be faithful: the former out of necessity, and the latter out of goodwill.

<margin>* Plutarch</margin>
* Likewise, the practice of feasting in the public hall was a specific institution of Solon, which he called *parasitous* <term>parasites; originally meaning those who ate at the public table</term> <note>original Greek: παρασίτους</note>. He did not permit the same person to eat there frequently; however, if someone who was invited refused to accept, he was punished. Solon considered the refusal a contempt for public honor, and frequent attendance a sign of an inordinate appetite.

Hitherto *Plutarch*; the following are recited by *Laertius*.

<margin>Laertius * Orat. in Timarch.</margin>
* If anyone does not support his parents, let him be infamous, as well as anyone who squanders his inheritance. *Aeschines* alludes to this: "In the fourth place, whom does he have to deal with? Any man who has consumed his patrimony or hereditary goods through prodigality." For Solon believed that he who had managed his own family poorly would take care of the commonwealth in the same manner. Nor did the lawmaker imagine it possible that the same person could be privately wicked and publicly good, or that it was fitting for such a person to take the speaker's chair—one who took more care to craft an oration than to compose his own life.

<margin>* Laertius * In Timarch.</margin>
* He forbade those who frequented common women from pleading in court; this is confirmed by *Aeschines*: "In the third place, whom does he have to deal with? Any man," he says, "who is a haunter of common women or procures money by such means." For he believed that such a person, who sold his own reputation for money, would easily sell the business of the state.

<margin>* In Androt.</margin>
And *Demosthenes* says: "It is worth inquiring and considering, *Athenians*, how great a care *Solon*, the author of this law, had for the commonwealth in all the laws he made, and how particularly solicitous he was in this above all other things. This is evident from many other laws, and also by this one, which forbids those *who have prostituted themselves* <note>original Latin: qui se prostituerunt</note> either to plead or judge in public."

<margin>* Laertius * In Panathen.</margin>
* He increased the rewards for those who died in war, whose sons he ordered to be raised and instructed at the public expense. *Aristides* says: "You alone of all men ordained these three things by law: that those who died for their country should be publicly praised every year at their tombs; that their children should be maintained at the public expense until they are grown men, and then sent back to their father's house with a complete set of armor; and likewise, that disabled citizens should be maintained at the public expense."

<margin>* Menexen.</margin>
* *Plato* adds that the same kindness was allowed to the parents: "You know the care of the commonwealth, which in the laws concerning the children and parents of those who died in the war, commands the supreme magistrate to ensure that the parents of those who died in the war should not suffer any injury, above all other citizens." The state also brings up the children. By this means, says *Laertius*, they became eager for fame and honor in war—like *Polyzelus*, *Cynegirus*, and all those in the battle of *Marathon*. To these may be added *Harmodius*, *Aristogiton*, *Miltiades*, and countless others.

His law concerning theft is expressed by *Laertius* as follows: *Do not take up what you did not lay down; otherwise, the punishment is death.* *Aeschines* adds: "if they confessed themselves guilty." Others affirm the punishment was only to pay double the value; among these are *Agellius and *Hermogenes, who affirm the law made a distinction between sacrilege and theft, punishing the first with death and the latter with double restitution. *Demosthenes clears this up by reciting the law exactly in these words: *If any man steals in the daytime more than fifty drachmas, he may be taken to the eleven officers <note>The Athenian magistrates in charge of prisons and executions</note>. If he steals anything by night, it shall be lawful for anyone to kill him, or to wound him in pursuit and carry him to the eleven officers. Whoever is convicted of such offenses as deserve imprisonment shall not be capable of giving bail for his theft, but his punishment shall be death. If anyone steals a garment, a small vessel of wine, or any other thing of little value from the Lyceum, the Academy, or Cynosarges, or some vessel out of the gymnasiums or harbors, he shall be punished with death. But if any man is convicted privately of theft, it shall be lawful for him to pay double the value; and it shall also be at the pleasure of the prosecutor, besides the payment of money, to put him in chains for five days and as many nights, so that all men may see him bound.* Even those who

### CHAPTER VII.

<margin><unclear>αὐτῶν νόμους</unclear> <note>Greek: "their laws"</note></margin>
->Of the *Axones* and *Kyrbeis*, the Senators' Oath, and other institutions of Solon.<-

<margin>
\* Plutarch.
Agyllius 2. 12.
\* Plutarch, *Etymologicon*.
</margin>
These laws he ratified for a hundred years. They were \* carved on different tables. \* Those which concerned private actions were recorded on oblong, rectangular wooden tables with frames that reached from the ground and turned on a pin like a wheel. Because of this, they were called \*<term>Axones</term> <note>Greek: Ἄξονες, meaning "axles"</note>. They were \* placed first in the citadel and then brought into the Prytaneum <note>the seat of government</note> so that everyone could see them. Some remains of them still existed in Plutarch's time.

<margin>
\* Pollux, 8. 10.
\* Suidas.
\* Ammonius, *On the Difference of Words*.
\* Suidas.
Scholiast on Aristotle’s *Ares*.
\* Didymus in Harpocration and Suidas.
</margin>
Those laws which concerned \* public orders and sacrifices were recorded on \* triangular tables of stone called <term>kyrbeis</term> <note>Greek: κύρβεις</note>. This name came either from \* Cyrbus, who took the account of every man's estate, or <unclear>from the *kyrbas* of the sacred things</unclear> <note>original: ἀπὸ τοῦ τὰς κύρβας τῶν ἱερῶν</unclear>, or from the Corybantes, to whom some attribute their invention. These were placed in the <term>Royal Portico</term> <note>Latin: *Porticus regia*</note>. \* Both the *Axones* and *kyrbeis* were written in the same manner as oxen turn while plowing (<term>writing *boustrophedon*</term>) <note>Greek: βουστροφηδὸν γράφειν; a style of writing where lines alternate from left-to-right and right-to-left</note>. For this reason, *Demosthenes* calls that law the "lowest" which begins on the left side.

<margin>\* Plutarch.</margin>
\* The <term>Senate</term> took a collective oath to uphold the laws of Solon for a hundred years. Each of the <term>Thesmothetae</term> <note>the six junior archons or lawgivers</note> swore an oath in the Forum at the Crier’s stone that if he violated them, he would dedicate a golden statue of equal weight to himself at Delphi.

<margin>
\* Stobaeus, *Sermon* 112.
\* Demosthenes, *Oration on the Crown*.
\* Demosthenes.
</margin>
Some details of the oath imposed by *Solon* are mentioned by various authors. For instance, *Plutarch* mentions the promise not to abolish his laws; \* <term>Stobaeus</term> mentions the promise to admit no young man as a judge; and \* *Demosthenes* mentions the promise to hear both the plaintiff and the defendant impartially. These details suggest it is the same oath that the same \* author provides in these words:

> "I will declare my opinion according to the laws of the Athenians and the Council of Five Hundred. By no assistance from me shall tyranny or oligarchy be admitted. I will never side with anyone who has corrupted the people, or who intends or endeavors to do so. I will never permit new 'tables' <note>referring to the cancellation of debts</note> or any redistribution of lands already accepted, nor a forced equality of goods. I will never recall any banished or confined person. I will consent to the expulsion of any man from the city who denies these laws decreed by the Senate and confirmed by the people. I will never permit—"

</margin>
Considering the irregularity of the months and the course of the moon—which did not always agree with the rising and setting of the sun, but sometimes overtook and passed it in a single day—he called that day the <term>old and new</term> <note>original: ἔνη καὶ νέα (ene kai nea)</note>, attributing the part that precedes the conjunction to the previous month and the rest to the beginning of the next. Thus, *he taught the Athenians to adjust the reckoning of their days to the motion of the moon;* and he was (as it appears) the first who correctly understood that passage of *Homer*:

-> *when one month ended and the next began:* <-

<margin>
* *The Clouds*, act 4, scene 2.

# CHAPTER VIII.

## *How he entertained Anacharsis; his travels to Egypt, Cyprus, Miletus, Delphi, Corinth, and Crete.*

<margin>
* Diogenes Laërtius.
* Plutarch.
</margin>
In the forty-seventh Olympiad (according to *Sosicrates*), *Anacharsis* came to *Athens* while *Eucrates* was Archon. He went immediately to the house of *Solon* and, knocking at the door, said he was a stranger desiring his friendship and hospitality. *Solon* answered, "*It is better to make friends at home*"; there you

<summary>This page details the judicial oaths taken by Athenian magistrates, Solon's reform of the lunar calendar, and his regulations concerning the recitation of Homeric epics. It concludes with the start of the story regarding the Scythian philosopher Anacharsis's arrival in Athens.</summary>
<keywords>Solon, Athenian Law, Lunar Calendar, Homeric Recitation, Venus Pandemos, Anacharsis, Hesychius, Aristophanes, Pisistratus</keywords>

--- Page 75 ---
<meta>This page continues the account of Solon's interaction with the Scythian philosopher Anacharsis, his decision to leave Athens to avoid constant questioning about his new laws, and the beginning of his travels in Egypt and Cyprus.</meta>

# SOLON

"Those who are at home," replies *Anacharsis*, "make me your friend and guest." *Solon*, admiring his sharp wit, entertained him kindly and kept him for some time while he was occupied with public affairs and the ordering of his laws. When *Anacharsis* understood this, he smiled that *Solon* undertook to curb the injustice and greed of citizens by written ordinances. He said they were no different from cobwebs, which hold fast the weak and poor while the powerful and rich break through them. To this *Solon* answered, "Men stand fast to those covenants which it is not convenient for either party to break." He gave the citizens such laws that it was evident to all that keeping them was better than breaking them; however, the outcome agreed more with the conjecture of *Anacharsis* than the expectation of *Solon*.

<margin>* Plutarch</margin>
After his laws were published, various people came to him daily, either to praise or criticize them, or to advise him to add or remove whatever came into their minds. The majority wanted the meaning explained, questioning how everything was to be understood and entreating him to reveal the sense. He considered that not to satisfy them would seem like pride, yet satisfying them would make him liable to criticism. Therefore, he determined to avoid ambiguities, persistent demands, and occasions for blame; for as he said:

> *In matters that are not small,*
> *It is hard to please everyone.*

Disguising his travels under the pretense of being the master of a ship, and having obtained leave from the Athenians to be absent for ten years, he set out to sea, hoping that in that time his laws would become familiar to them.

<margin>* Plutarch</margin>
The first place of his arrival was *Egypt*, where he lived, as he himself says:

> *At the mouth of the Nile, near the Canopian shore.*

He studied philosophy for a while with *Psanophis* of *Heliopolis* and *Sonches* of *Saïs*, the most learned of those priests. By them, *Plato* affirms, he was taught the Atlantic history <note>original: "Atlantick language" — referring to the legend of Atlantis</note>, which he afterward began to explain in verse. When he questioned them about antiquities, the elder priest said to him, "O *Solon*, *Solon*, you Greeks are always children; there is not one Greek who is an old man."

<margin>* Plutarch</margin>
From there he went to *Cyprus*, where he was much favored by *Philocyprus*, one of the kings of that country. This king had a small town, built by *Demophon*, son of *Theseus*, upon the River *Clarius* in a strong but rugged and barren location. *Solon* persuaded him to transfer the town to a pleasant plain lying beneath it, making it more spacious and delightful. Being present during this undertaking, *Solon* took care that it might be populated and assisted the king in designing it for both health and strength.

<summary>This page describes Solon's departure from Athens to avoid the burden of explaining his laws, and his subsequent travels to Egypt and Cyprus, where he encountered ancient wisdom and assisted in city planning.</summary>
<keywords>Solon, Anacharsis, Athens, Egyptian priests, Atlantis, Cyprus, Philocyprus, legislation, Greek history</keywords>

--- Page 76 ---
<meta>This page continues the account of Solon's travels after establishing his laws in Athens, focusing on his time in Cyprus and his interactions with other legendary wise men of Greece.</meta>

# SOLON

strength; because of this, many people came to *Philocyprus*, whom other kings envied. For this reason, he credited the honor to *Solon*, naming the city (which was previously called *Aepea*) *Soli* after him. Solon mentions this founding in his *Elegies*, addressing his speech to *Philocyprus*:

> *May you as King in Cyprus long abide,*
> *And o'er this people and this town preside;*
> *From this harbor, may a swift vessel*
> *Convey me, while Cythera <note>An epithet for Aphrodite, the goddess of love.</note> crowned with violets guides my way.*
> *May she grant her kind favor and happiness*
> *To this town, and a safe return to me.*

He also visited *Thales* at *Miletus*, whose trickery toward him (already related in *Plutarch's* words) is here recounted by *Tzetzes* <note>John Tzetzes, a 12th-century Byzantine poet and grammarian.</note>:

> *Solon's friend Thales led a single life,*
> *Though Solon often urged him to take a wife;*
> *A Milesian (by Thales' own design)*
> *Met Solon, pretending he had just arrived*
> *From Athens. Solon asked inquisitively what news was there;*
> *'A man who is abroad,' he said, 'has lost his heir,*
> *And the city has gathered for his funeral rites.'*
> *'Was it not Solon's son?' Solon replies.*
> *To this the stranger (as he was coached) assents;*
> *Solon, with torn hair, gives vent to his grief in cries;*
> *Whom Thales (tenderly embracing) told, 'Leave*
> *This grief, for I only meant to deceive*
> *You to show why marriage I decline:*
> *It can deject so great a soul as thine.'*

<margin>* Laertius; * Sermon 121.</margin>
Whether it refers to this trickery or to a real loss, * *Dioscorides* and * *Stobaeus* report that while he was weeping for the death of his son, someone told him, "But this does nothing to help," to which he answered, "And that is why I weep."

<margin>* Laertius.</margin>
* At *Delphi*, he met with the rest of the wise men, and the following year at *Corinth* by *Periander's* invitation. This was, as *Plutarch* implies, long before *Pisistratus* began to reign; nor does <margin>* Oration 37. * J. Meursius.</margin> * *Dion Chrysostom* mean the contrary, though he was interpreted that way by * a learned scholar <note>Johannes Meursius, a Dutch classical scholar.</note>. His words mean only this: *Solon* did not flee the tyranny of *Periander*, though he did flee that of *Pisistratus*.

<margin>* To Pherecydes.</margin>
That he also went to *Crete* (perhaps to visit *Epimenides*) is clear from a * letter by *Thales*.

# SOLON

## CHAP. IX.

->*The title of "Wise" conferred on him: his moral sentences.*<-

<margin>* Diogenes Laertius</margin>
<margin>* Themistius, Oration 4</margin>
<margin>* Plutarch, Life of Solon</margin>
<margin>* Plutarch, Life of Themistocles</margin>
<margin>* Macrobius, Commentary on the Dream of Scipio, 2:17</margin>
**W**hen Damasias (the second) was Archon (in the year of the 49th Olympiad), all the seven sages received the title of "Wise." Among these was **Solon**, upon whom (**Themistius** says) it was conferred as "an honorable title full of dignity." **Plutarch** asserts that "all of them (except **Thales**) were so named because of their skill in civil affairs." And again, "In philosophy, **Solon** was primarily interested (as were most of the wise men) in that part of morality which concerns politics." Speaking of **Mnesiphilus**, Plutarch says, "He was not an orator of those philosophers who are called 'natural,' but embraced that wisdom which teaches the government of a State and prudence in public actions, which he maintained as a school of thought passed down by succession from **Solon**." For this reason, **Macrobius** cites **Solon** as being skilled in the kind of learning that draws philosophy deeper and establishes a state.

To this may be added his moral teachings, for which the seven were so famous that some claim the title of "Wise" was given to them only for excelling others in a virtuous way of life and for summarizing moral rules in short sentences. (Although **Socrates** first organized this into a science and was honored as its inventor). They had three types of these: Apothegms <note>Pithy sayings or maxims.</note>, Precepts, and <term>Asmata</term> <note>original: "Ασματα", meaning songs or poems.</note>.

Of his Apothegms, **Laertius** records these: "Speech is the image of action"; "He is a king who has power"; "Laws are like cobwebs which entangle the lesser folk, while the greater break through"; "Those who are in favor with Princes resemble the counters used in accounting, which sometimes stand for a great number and sometimes for a smaller one; likewise, those people are sometimes honored and sometimes cast down." When asked how men might be persuaded to do no wrong, he said, "If those who have received no injury are just as concerned as those who are wronged." "Excess <note>original: "Satiety".</note> comes from riches, and insolence <note>original: "contumely".</note> comes from excess."

<margin>* Plutarch, Life of Solon</margin>
<margin>* Plutarch, Banquet of the Seven Sages</margin>
**Plutarch** and others record these: "He believed that city to be best governed where the people prosecute wrongs done to others as eagerly as those done to themselves." When asked how a city might be best ordered, he answered, "If the citizens obeyed the magistrates, and the magistrates obeyed the laws." He affirmed that a king or tyrant would become most glorious if he converted his monarchy into a democracy. He considered the best family to be one where wealth is earned justly, kept honestly, and spent without regret.

* He defined "the happy" as those who are adequately furnished with outward things, act honestly, and live temperately; a definition which *Aristotle* approves.

<margin>

</margin>
* He said, "A commonwealth consists of two things: reward and punishment."

<margin>

* Seeing one of his friends deeply grieved, he took him to the Acropolis <note>The text refers to "the Tower," likely indicating a high vantage point in Athens.</note> and asked him to view all the buildings below. When the friend had done so, Solon said, "Now think to yourself how many sorrows have lived under those roofs in the past, how many do so now, and how many shall in future ages; and stop being troubled by the misfortunes of mortality as if they were yours alone." He also said that if all men were to bring their misfortunes together into one place, everyone would carry his own home again rather than take an equal share from the common stock.

<margin>
* Stobaeus, *Sermones*, 34.
</margin>
* While drinking, he was asked by *Periander* whether he was silent because he lacked anything to say or out of folly. He

<margin>

</margin>
answered, "No fool can remain silent while drinking." * He said that the city which was best ordered was the one in which the good were rewarded and the bad punished.

<margin>
* John of Salisbury, *Policraticus*, 8.14.
* *Orations against Timocrates*.
</margin>
* He said a man ought to fear nothing except that his death might exclude philosophy.
* *Demosthenes* recounts a speech Solon used to the judges while accusing someone who had proposed a harmful law, to this effect: "It is a law generally accepted in all cities that he who counterfeits money should be put to death." He then asked the judges whether that law seemed just and commendable to them. When they agreed, he added that he believed money was used among citizens for private contracts, but that laws were the "money" of the commonwealth. Therefore, judges ought to punish those who debased the laws of the commonwealth much more severely than those who debase the currency of private individuals. To help them understand that it is a far greater offense to corrupt laws than to counterfeit coins, he added that many cities use silver money alloyed with brass or lead without any harm to themselves; but whoever uses laws so corrupted cannot escape ruin and death.

<margin>
* Diogenes Laertius.
</margin>
* *Mimnermus* writing this:

> Free from trouble and diseases,
> Let death take me at sixty years.

He reproved him, saying,

> By my advice, extend that wish,
> Nor slight your friend for his counsel.
> Alter your song, and let it be,
> At eighty years let death take me.

-># SOLON<-

<margin>Stobaeus, *Sermones*, 3.</margin>
His moral precepts are presented by *Demetrius Phalereus*, some of which are cited by *Laertius*: "Nothing in excess." <note>original: "Nothing too much"</note> "Do not sit as a judge; if you do, the person you condemn will consider you an enemy. Avoid pleasure, for it leads to sorrow. Value honesty in your conversation more strictly than an oath. Seal your words with silence, and silence with the right opportunity. Do not lie, but speak the truth. Reflect on serious matters. Do not say that anything is more just than your parents. Do not gain friends in haste, nor part with those you have gained in haste. By learning to obey, you will know how to command. Whatever penalty you impose on others, undergo it yourself. Advise citizens not on what is most pleasant, but on what is best. Be not arrogant. Do not associate with wicked people. Consult the Gods. Cherish your friend. Revere your parents. Make reason your guide. Do not speak of what you see. Conceal what you know. Be gentle to those who belong to you. Deduce hidden things from what is evident."

<margin>* Clement of Alexandria, *Stromata*, 1.</margin>
His specific maxim, according to *Didymus* and *Laertius*, was "Nothing in excess"; according to *Ausonius*, it was "Know yourself," who also attributes these verses to him:

> *I dare call that man happy whose end I see,* <note>A reference to Solon's famous advice to Croesus: "Count no man happy until he is dead."</note>
> *Match with your equal; mismatched pairs do not agree.*
> *Raise no one to honor who is guided only by fortune;*
> *Rebuke a friend in private; praise them in public.*
> *Honors achieved far exceed those that are merely granted;*
> *If fate is certain, what use is it to be cautious?*
> *And if it is uncertain, there is less need for fear.*

Of his <unclear>Admonitions</unclear>, *Laertius* mentions these:

> *Be wary of every man, lest he carry*
> *A sword hidden within his breast, a clear*
> *Face, a double tongue, and a harsh mind.*

### CHAPTER X.

->How he opposed Pisistratus and rebuked Thespis.<-

<margin>* Plutarch.</margin>
During the absence of Solon, the former dissension broke out again in the city. *Lycurgus* was the leader of the countrymen, *Megacles* led the coastal faction, and *Pisistratus* led the city dwellers, who were most violent against the rich. Solon’s laws were still observed in the city, but the people aimed for innovation and change—not because they thought it was most just, but in hopes of becoming masters of other men’s property and suppressing the opposing party. While things stood thus, Solon returned to Athens, where he was much

<summary>This page records the moral maxims and poetry attributed to Solon by various ancient sources and begins describing the political factionalism that erupted in Athens during his absence.</summary>
<keywords>Solon, Demetrius Phalereus, Laertius, Ausonius, ethics, maxims, Athens, Pisistratus, Lycurgus, Megacles, political factions</keywords>

--- Page 80 ---
<meta>This page continues from the discussion of Solon’s moral maxims into a narrative of his later life in Athens, focusing on his opposition to the rise of the tyrant Pisistratus and his critique of the early theater.</meta>

-># SOLON<-

Although he was much reverenced and honored by all, Solon could not speak or act in public because of the weakness of his body and spirit. Yet, he privately took each of the Commanders aside and endeavored to reconcile their differences. Among them, *Pisistratus* seemed the most willing to be persuaded; Solon had a very ancient friendship with him, grounded both upon their family ties and upon the good qualities of *Pisistratus*. *Solon* used to say that there could not be a person of more worth than *Pisistratus*, if only he were cured of his ambition.

<margin>Plutarch</margin> About this time (according to *Plutarch*), which was in the fiftieth Olympiad, *Thespis* began to present Tragedies. <note>Suidas erroneously accounts this as ten Olympiads later, as noted by Meursius.</note> The people were much taken with the novelty of the performance, for as yet there were no organized competitions in the theater. *Solon*, who was naturally desirous to hear and learn, and who in his old age indulged more in ease, pleasure, feasting, and music, went to see *Thespis* himself act, as was then the custom. When the play ended, he went to *Thespis* and asked him if he were not ashamed to speak so many lies before such a great audience. *Thespis* answered that it was no shame to act or say such things in jest. *Solon*, striking the ground hard with his staff, replied, "But in a short time, we who approve of this kind of 'jest' shall use it in earnest in our contracts and business transactions." 

<margin>Diogenes Laertius</margin> In the end, he absolutely forbade him to teach or act tragedies, believing their falsehood to be harmful. He compared this theatrical fiction to the deception of *Pisistratus*, who soon after, <margin>Plutarch</margin> having wounded himself, came into the *Forum* in a chariot. He pretended to have been treated this way by his enemies because of his support for the commonwealth, and he inflamed the people with great rage. *Solon*, coming near to him, said, "Son of *Hippocrates*, you play the part of *Homer's Ulysses* poorly. You use the same means to deceive your citizens that he used (by whipping himself) to delude the enemy." <note>In the Odyssey, Odysseus/Ulysses disguised himself as a beggar and whipped himself to sneak into Troy undetected.</note> Immediately, the people flocked together to defend *Pisistratus*. *Aristo* moved that he should be allowed a standing guard of fifty men. *Solon* rose up to oppose it, giving speeches that he later expressed in verse:

> *If evil should befall you for your ungodly acts,*
> *Do not blame the Gods for those misfortunes;*
> *You yourselves are the cause, having given power to those*
> *Who, in return, impose servitude upon you.*
> *You are like a lion following the footsteps of a fox;*
> *Your souls are filled with deceit and vanity.*
> *You only pay attention to a man’s smooth tongue and speech,*
> *But you never look deeply into his actual deeds.*

He also foretold their true aims regarding *Pisistratus* in an elegy to this effect:

<meta>catchword: Vapours</meta>

<summary>Solon attempts to mediate political disputes in Athens while warning against the rise of the tyrant Pisistratus, critiquing early theatrical performances by Thespis as a dangerous precedent for public deception.</summary>
<keywords>Solon, Pisistratus, Thespis, Athens, Tragedy, Tyranny, Deception, Aristo, Hippocrates, Ulysses</keywords>

--- Page 81 ---
<meta>This page continues the account of Solon’s later years in Athens, specifically his futile warnings against the rising power of the tyrant Pisistratus and his eventual withdrawal from public life.</meta>

-># SOLON<-

> *Vapors condensed produce hail and snow,*
> *And thunder flows from radiant lightning.*
> *The sea is troubled by the raging wind,*
> *But when not disturbed by that, nothing is kinder.*
> *A city by powerful men is overthrown,*
> *And taught to groan beneath a monarchy.*

But seeing the common people were very devoted to *Pisistratus* and prone to unrest, while the wealthy were afraid and seeking safety in flight, he retired. He said, "Athenians, I am wiser than some and braver than others: wiser than those who do not understand the deceit of *Pisistratus*, and braver than those who understand it yet remain silent out of fear." The Senate, being of the same faction as *Pisistratus*, said he was mad; to which he answered,

> *A short time will make my madness clear*
> *To the people, when the truth shall appear in their midst.*

Having granted *Pisistratus* his request for a guard, the people did not question their number. Instead, they overlooked his recruitment and maintenance of as many soldiers as he pleased, until he finally took control of the towers <note>The Acropolis</note>. At this, the city fell into an uproar, and *Megacles*, along with the rest of the *Alcmaeonidae*, fled. *Solon*, now very old and lacking anyone to support him, went into the Forum <margin>* Laertius</margin> armed with a spear and shield. He made a speech to the people, partly accusing them of folly and cowardice, and partly inciting them not to abandon their liberty. He used this famous speech: "It would have been far easier to suppress this tyranny in its infancy, but it is much more noble to cut it off now that it has reached its height." 

Since no one dared to listen to him, he went home. Taking his arms, he set them in the street before his door (*Laertius* says, "before the armory" <term>Magazine: a storehouse for arms and ammunition</term>), saying, "I have helped my country and the law as much as I could." Or, as *Laertius* records: "O Country, I have assisted you both in word and deed." *Plutarch* adds that from that time on he lived in retirement, dedicated to his studies. When told by many that the Tyrant would put him to death and asked what he relied on so much, he answered, "My old age." However, *Laertius* claims (which seems more likely) that as soon as he had laid down his arms, <margin>* Lib. 17. cap. 21.</margin> he left the country. *Agelius* <note>Aulus Gellius</note> also notes that during the reign of *Servius Tullius*, while *Pisistratus* was the tyrant of *Athens*, *Solon* went first into voluntary exile.

<header>SOLON.</header>

## CHAPTER 11.

### *How he traveled to Lydia and Cilicia.*

Solon, upon his departure from *Athens*, received invitations from many people. He was invited by *Thales* to come to *Miletus*; by *Bias* to *Priene*; by *Epimenides* to *Crete*; and by *Cleobulus* to *Lindus*, as is evident from their various letters to that effect. Even *Pisistratus* urged him to return home with this letter:

->*Pisistratus to Solon*<-

> "I am neither the only person among the Greeks to rule, nor am I without a right to the kingdom I possess, being a descendant of *Codrus*. I have recovered that which the *Athenians* took away, despite having sworn to give it to *Codrus* and his heirs. In no other way do I offend either God or man. I take care that the commonwealth is governed according to the laws you ordained for the *Athenians*, and even better than they were under a democracy <term>democratie</term>. I permit no one to do wrong, nor do I enjoy any privileges of a tyrant <term>tyrant</term> <note>In the ancient Greek sense, a "tyrant" was a ruler who seized power unconstitutionally, not necessarily a cruel one.</note> beyond honor and dignity—only such rewards as were conferred upon the ancient kings. Every man pays a tenth of his estate, not to me, but for the maintenance of public sacrifices, other expenses of the commonwealth, or for times of war. I do not blame you for exposing my intentions; you did it more out of love for the state than out of hatred for me. Besides, you did not know what kind of government I intended to establish; if you had, perhaps you would have tolerated my rule and not banished yourself. Therefore, return home, and believe me even without an oath: *Solon* shall never receive any displeasure from *Pisistratus*. You know that even my enemies have not suffered, and if you will deign to be among my friends, you shall be the first among them, for I never saw anything in you that was deceitful or false. Otherwise, if you wish to live among the *Athenians*, use your freedom; only do not deprive yourself of your country for my sake."

Solon returned this answer:

->*Solon to Pisistratus*<-

> "I believe I shall not suffer any harm from you; for before you were tyrant, I was your friend, and at present I am no more your enemy than any other *Athenian* who dislikes tyranny. Whether it is better for them to be governed by a single person or by a democracy, let both parties determine. I..."

<meta>catchword: pro-</meta>

<summary>This page details Solon's invitations from various Greek sages and a conciliatory letter from the tyrant Pisistratus, who defends his rule as legitimate and lawful. Solon's brief reply maintains his personal friendship while reiterating his principled opposition to tyranny.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Solon's correspondence during his exile, concluding his response to Pisistratus and beginning his famous encounter with King Croesus of Lydia.</meta>

<page-num>59</page-num>
<header>SOLON.</header>

“...I declare you to be the best of tyrants; but I do not think it fitting to return to *Athens*, lest I incur blame. I am the one who settled an equality in the Athenian commonwealth and would not accept the tyranny <term>tyranny</term> <note>In the Greek context, "tyranny" referred to any rule by a single person who seized power, regardless of whether they were cruel or kind.</note> myself; by returning, I would appear to comply with your actions.”

Croesus also sent to invite him, to whom he answered in this way:

->Solon to Croesus.<-

“ **I** appreciate your humanity towards us; and by *Pallas* <note>The goddess Athena.</note>, if I did not prefer above all things to live under a democracy, I would much sooner choose to live in your kingdom than at *Athens* while *Pisistratus* rules there by force. However, it is most pleasing to me to live where all things are just and equal; yet I will come over to you, as I am desirous to become your guest.”

*Upon this invitation, Solon went to Sardis, where Croesus (says Herodotus) received him kindly. After the third or fourth day, at Croesus’s appointment, the officers led him into the treasuries to show him all their greatness and riches. When he had beheld everything, Croesus spoke to him in this way:* “Athenian guest, because we have heard much fame of your wisdom and experience—having traveled into many countries out of a love for philosophy—I have a desire to ask you if you have ever seen any man whom you could call truly happy.” *He asked this, hoping that he himself would be esteemed as such. Solon, not flattering him at all, answered according to the truth, saying,* “O King, *Tellus* the Athenian.” *Croesus, wondering at this speech, asked:* “Why do you judge *Tellus* to be the most happy?” “Because,” *replied Solon,* “in a well-ordered state, he had children who were honest and good, and he saw every one of them have children who all survived. Thus, having passed his life as well as is possible for a man, he ended it gloriously: a battle occurred between the Athenians and their neighbors in *Eleusis*, and he came to their rescue. After putting the enemy to flight, he died nobly; the Athenians buried him with much honor in the place where he fell.”

*While Solon recounted the happiness of Tellus, Croesus was moved and asked to whom he assigned the next place, having no doubt that he himself would be named second.* “*Cleobis,” said he, “and Bito. They were Argives by birth,* and they had sufficient means to maintain themselves. Moreover, they had such great strength of body that both were victors in the public games. It is reported of them that while the Argives were celebrating the festival of *Juno* <note>The Roman name for the Greek goddess Hera.</note>, it was necessary for their mother to be drawn to the temple by a pair of oxen. As there were no oxen ready in the field, these young men, pressed for time, took the burden upon themselves...”

<meta>The text ends mid-sentence; the catchword for the next page is "the".</meta>

<summary>Solon refuses to return to Athens under Pisistratus's rule and instead visits King Croesus of Lydia. He famously challenges Croesus's pride by identifying Tellus the Athenian and the brothers Cleobis and Bito as happier than the wealthy king.</summary>

--- Page 84 ---
<meta>This page continues Solon's account to King Croesus regarding why he considers certain private citizens happier than a wealthy king, following his mention of Tellus the Athenian on the previous page.</meta>

# SOLON.

“...the yoke and drew their mother's chariot forty-five stadia <note>A stadium was a Greek unit of distance, roughly 600 feet; 45 stadia is about 5 miles.</note> until they reached the Temple. When they had done this in the sight of all the people, they reached the happiest end of their days, by which the God <note>Specifically, the goddess Hera, as this story takes place at her temple in Argos.</note> declared it better for a man to die than to live. The Argives gathered around them; the men applauded the piety of the sons, and the women praised the happiness of the mother. The mother herself, infinitely overjoyed by the deed and its glory, stood before the statue and prayed the goddess to give her sons, *Cleobis* and *Biton*, the best thing that could happen to a man. After this prayer, having sacrificed and feasted, they lay down to sleep in the Temple and never woke again, but so ended their days. Their statues (as those of most excellent men) were made by the Argives and set up at *Delphi*. These, *Solon* ranked in the second degree.”

At this, *Croesus* grew angry and said, “Stranger, does our happiness seem so despicable that you will not rank us as equal even to private citizens?”

He answered, “Do you, *Croesus*, ask me concerning human affairs—I, who know that divine providence is severe and full of change? In the course of time, we see many things we do not wish to see, and we suffer many things we do not wish to suffer. Let us set seventy years as the term of a man’s life. These years consist of twenty-five thousand and two hundred days, excluding the intercalary <term>intercalary</term> <note>Additional months or days inserted into a calendar to align it with the solar year.</note> month. If we make every other year longer by that month to make the time align, the additional months belonging to those seventy years will be thirty-five, and the days of those months will be one thousand and fifty. Of all these days, not one is exactly like another. So it is, O *Croesus*, that every man is a product of chance! 

You appear to me to be very rich and are King over many people, but I cannot resolve the question you ask until I hear that you have ended your days happily. He who has much wealth is no happier than he who earns his living from day to day, unless fortune continues those good things for him and grants that he dies well. There are many men who are very rich, yet unfortunate, and many of moderate estates who are fortunate. Of these, the man who abounds in wealth but is not happy exceeds the fortunate man in only two things, while the fortunate man exceeds him in many. The rich man is better able to satisfy his desires and to withstand great misfortunes; yet the fortunate man excels him. He cannot indeed inflict harm on others or satisfy his own desires—his good fortune prevents the need for these—but he is free from ills, healthy, happy in his children, and handsome. If, in addition to this, a man dies well, he is the one you seek, who deserves to be called happy. Before death, he cannot be styled happy, but only fortunate. 

Yet for one man to obtain all all of this is impossible, just as one country cannot furnish itself with all things; it has some and lacks others, and the one that has the most is best. So it is with men: no one is perfect. What one man has,”

<summary>Solon explains to King Croesus the story of Cleobis and Biton as examples of happiness in death and argues that no man can be judged truly happy until his life has ended, as wealth does not guarantee a fortunate end.</summary>
<keywords>Solon, Croesus, Cleobis and Biton, Delphi, happiness, fortune, mortality, philosophy, Herodotean philosophy, intercalary months</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the conversation between Solon and King Croesus. Solon is explaining why he does not consider the wealthy King to be the happiest of men, emphasizing that a man's life can only be judged after its conclusion.</meta>

# SOLON.

“...the other lacks. He who consistently has the most, and at last quietly departs this life, deserves, in my opinion, O King, to bear that name. In everything, we must have regard for the end and where it leads; for God often gives a glimpse of good fortune to many, only to utterly ruin them in the end.” This story is related by Plutarch and is also mentioned by *Laertius*, who adds that “*Croesus*, being magnificently adorned and seated on his throne, asked him whether he had ever seen anything more glorious? Solon answered, ‘*Cocks, pheasants, and peacocks*,’ who are much more beautiful in their 
<margin>* Herodotus</margin>
*natural splendor* <note>original: "flower"</note>.” After this discourse with *Croesus*, Solon—not flattering him or showing him much respect—was dismissed and accounted unwise for neglecting present good in favor of the future. * *Aesop*, the writer of fables, was at that time at 
<margin>* Plutarch</margin>
*Sardis*, having been sent for by *Croesus*, with whom he was in much favor. He was grieved to see *Solon* so ungratefully dismissed and said to him, “*Solon*, we must either tell Kings nothing at all, or only what may please them.” “No,” says *Solon*, “either nothing at all, or what is best for them.” Thus was *Solon* much despised by *Croesus*.

<margin>* Herodotus</margin>
“Afterwards, *Croesus*—having been taken prisoner by *Cyrus*—was, at his command, fettered and set upon a great pile of wood to be burned. As he was in this posture, it came into his mind what *Solon* had divinely said to him: that no living man is happy. As soon as he remembered those words, he fell into a great depression of spirit and, sighing deeply, named *Solon* thrice. *Cyrus* heard this and commanded the interpreters to ask whom he called upon. They went to him and asked, but he remained silent. At last, pressing him further, he answered, ‘*I call upon him whom I wish, above all wealth, might have spoken with all tyrants.*’ Not understanding him after much pressure and importunity, he finally told them, ‘*Solon, an Athenian, came long ago to me; beholding all my wealth, he valued it as nothing. Moreover, all that he told me has come to pass; nor did it apply more to me than to all mankind, especially to those who think themselves happy.*’ 

While *Croesus* said this, the fire began to kindle, and the outer parts of the pile were seized by the flame. *Cyrus*, being informed by an interpreter of all that *Croesus* said, began to relent. He recognized himself to be but a man who was delivering another man—one no inferior to him in wealth—to be burned alive. Fearing punishment for that act and considering that nothing was certain in human affairs, he commanded the fire to be instantly quenched and *Croesus*, and those that were with him, to be brought off.
<margin>* Plutarch</margin>
From then on, for as long as he lived, Cyrus held him in high esteem. Thus *Solon* gained praise, for his speech preserved one King and instructed the other.

*Plutarch* relates that this occurred during *Solon’s* ten years of travel following the completion of his laws. Because of this, Plutarch offers a defense <note>original: "Apology"</note> for the chronological inconsistency of the story with the rules of *chronology*.

# SOLON.

...chronology, which would have been less necessary if, along with *Laertius*, he had placed it after *Pisistratus's* usurpation of the tyranny. *Laertius* says that he went from there to *Cilicia* and built a city there called *Soleis* after him, where he also brought a few Athenians. Because their language grew corrupt by the language of that country, they were said to "solecize" <note>To speak or write incorrectly; the term "solecism" derives from the name of this city</note>. This is undoubtedly how the Etymologist is to be understood when he derives the word "Soloikoi" from "Solon’s colony" <note>original: Σολοικοὶ ἀπὸ σόλον πλοικίαν</note> (for so we read it, not "from Solon’s clan" <note>original: ἀπὸ Σόλονος κλικὸς</note>). This is also attested by *Suidas* as a distinct account from that of *Cyprus*; in confirmation of this, *Laertius* adds that the Cilicians were called *Solenses*, while the Cyprians were called *Solii*.

## CHAP. XII.
### *His death.*

<margin>
* Plutarch.
* On Longevity.

* Plutarch.
</margin>
* Heraclides affirms that *Solon* lived long after *Pisistratus* began to reign. * *Lucian* claims that his life extended to a hundred years; those who say (as *Suidas* relates) that he lived in the fifty-sixth Olympiad agree best with him. * *But according to Phanias, Pisistratus took the tyranny upon himself when Comias was Archon, and Solon died while Hegestratus was Archon, who succeeded Comias, which was in the first year of the fifty-fifth Olympiad.* If this latter opinion had not generally taken precedence over the others, the disagreement between the time of *Solon's* death and the reign of *Croesus* would not have been urged by many as an argument against the story of their meeting.

After his death, the Athenians erected his statue in brass before the Painted Porch <note>original: "checker'd cloister'd"; referring to the Stoa Poikile</note> in the forum. Another was set up at Salamis, depicting him hiding (as Demosthenes and Aeschines describe it) his hand within his garment, in the same posture he used when making speeches to the Athenians. This statue perhaps carried this inscription:

> ->*Famed Salamis cast down the Persian pride,*<-
> ->*And gave birth to Solon, the renown of laws.*<-

Laertius bestows this epigram upon him:

> ->*A foreign Cyprian fire burned Solon, yet*<-
> ->*Salamis keeps his bones, their ashes turning to wheat;*<-
> ->*His soul to heaven mounts with his laws—so light*<-
> ->*A burden, they do not clog, but help his flight.*<-

## CHAP. XIII.

### His writings.

<margin>* In *Brutus*.
* *On the Orator*, Book 1.
* Oration 21.
* Plato's Oration 2.</margin>
His excellence in both rhetoric and poetry is attested by many. Cicero states, "Before Solon's time, no man is recorded for eloquence." And again, "Lycurgus and Solon we place among the number of the eloquent." Dion Chrysostom, Aristides, Lycurgus, Solon, and Epaminondas—and if there be any others of the same kind—ought to be esteemed as philosophers of the commonwealth, or as orators according to ingenious, true rhetoric. Aristides says, "Solon is said to have sung those things which concern the Megarians <note>original: *Megarenses*</note>. However, neither his laws nor his orations—which he sometimes made for the wealthy to the commoners, and sometimes for the commoners to the wealthy—did he sing or compose in verse. Instead, he used a rhetorical form, excellently demonstrating in all of these that he deserved to be esteemed as an orator and a wise man, having attained both those titles and faculties."

<margin>* In *Timaeus* and *Critias*.</margin>
As to poetry, Plutarch avers, "he addicted himself to it from the beginning, not for serious matters, but for lighthearted ones, used (as it seems) for his exercise and pastime. Afterwards, he included many philosophical sentences in verse, as well as many affairs of state—not as a formal history, but to vindicate his own actions, and sometimes also to correct and reprove the Athenians." Plato says that at the Apaturian festival <note>A three-day festival of the phratries in Athens</note>, the boys used to recite his poems. He adds that if Solon had applied himself to nothing but poetry as others did, and had finished the history he brought out of Egypt, and had not been con-

Regarding his *prose* writings, we must, following Laertius, first name his:

Some (says Plutarch) claim he began to translate his own laws into verse.

The last work he undertook concerned the Atlantic story or fable; having begun it late in life, he was deterred by the scale of the work (as Plutarch says) and cut short by death.

In addition to the letters already cited, these are also attributed to him by Laertius.

### Solon to Periander.

> "You informed me that there are many who plot against you. If you were to put them all to death, it would benefit you nothing. There may be someone among those you do not suspect who plots against you—either fearing for himself, or despising you, or desiring to win favor with the city, even if you have done him no injury. It is best, if you wish to be free from suspicion, to remove yourself from the cause <note>i.e., to give up the tyranny</note>; but if you intend to continue in your tyranny, take care to provide a larger force of foreign mercenaries than you have citizens in your own city. In this way, you shall have no need to fear any man, nor to put anyone to death."

<meta>This page continues the collection of Solon's writings, specifically transitioning from the list of his works to the text of his letters and poetic fragments, focusing on his political warnings regarding the rise of the tyrant Pisistratus.</meta>

<page-num>65</page-num>
<header>SOLON.</header>

### Solon to Epimenides.

> "Neither are my laws likely to benefit the Athenians for long, nor have you truly helped the city with your purification <note>original: "lustration"; a religious rite of cleansing</note>; for religious rites and law-givers cannot benefit cities on their own. It matters greatly what kind of mind those who lead the common people possess; religious rites and laws are profitable if they direct the leaders well, but if they direct them poorly, they accomplish nothing. Nor are the laws I gave in any better condition. Those who were in charge of the commonwealth, by failing to prevent Pisistratus’s usurpation of the tyranny, lost the city. When I warned them of this, I was not believed; the Athenians preferred to trust his flatteries rather than my truth. Therefore, laying down my arms before the armory <note>original: "Magazin"</note>, I said that I was wiser than those who did not see that Pisistratus aimed at tyranny, and braver <note>original: "stouter"</note> than those who did not dare resist him. They considered Solon a madman. Finally, I made this declaration: 'O country! Behold Solon ready to defend <note>original: "vindicate"</note> you in word and deed!' They again thought me mad. Thus, being the only person who opposed Pisistratus, I left them; let them guard him with their weapons if they wish. For know, dear friend, the man obtained the kingdom very cunningly. At first, he complied with the democracy; afterwards, having wounded himself, he entered the Heliaia <note>original: "Eliæa"; the supreme court of ancient Athens</note>, crying out that he had received those wounds from his enemies. He requested a guard of four hundred young men, which they granted, ignoring my advice. These men carried clubs <note>original: "halberds"</note>. After this, he dissolved the popular government. Truly, I labored in vain to free the poor from mercenary slavery, when they all now serve a single man, Pisistratus."

Such fragments of his poems as have been preserved until now are collected here.

#### *From his Elegies.*

*Born of Mnemosyne and Jove’s great line,* <note>Mnemosyne: Goddess of memory and mother of the Muses</note>
*Pierian Muses, to my prayer incline,* <note>Pierian Muses: The Muses, named after their birthplace in Pieria</note>
*Grant that my life and actions may bring down*
*Blessings from heaven, and raise on earth renown:*
*Sweet to my friends, and bitter to my foes,*
*To these my sight brings terror, joy to those.*
*Riches I wish, but not wealth that is placed*
*In unjust means, for vengeance comes at last.*
*Riches dispensed by heaven’s more generous hand*
*Provide a base on which we may stand unshaken.*
*But that wealth which men by injuries obtain,*

*That which by arts and deeds unjust they gain*
*Comes slowly, swiftly by revenge pursued,*
*And misery like a hidden spark includes,*
*Which soon to a devouring flame dilates,*
*Wrong is a weak foundation for estates:*
*Jove <note>Jupiter/Zeus</note> does the end of every thing survey:*
*As sudden vernal <term>vernal: relating to spring</term> blasts chase clouds away.*
*Ransack the bottom of the roaring main <note>the sea</note>,*
*Then swiftly overrun the fertile plain,*
*Rustling the wealthy ears; at last they rise*
*To Jove's high seat, a calm then smooths the skies.*
*The Sun's rich luster mildly gilds the green*
*Enamel of the meadows, no clouds are seen,*
*Such is Jove's heavy anger, differing far*
*From men, whose every trifle leads to war:*
*They are not hidden forever, who offend*
*In secret; judgment finds them in the end.*
*Some in the act are punished, others late,*
*Even he who thinks he has deluded fate;*
*At last resents it in just miseries,*
*Which descendants <note>original: "Nephewes"</note> for their ancestors' sins pay.*
*We think it fares alike with good and bad;*
*Glory and self-content our fancies glad*
*Till suffering comes, then their grieved spirits bleed,*
*Who did before their souls with vain hope feed.*
*He whom incurable diseases seize,*
*Soothes his deluded thoughts with hopes of ease.*
*The coward's valiant in his own esteem,*
*And to themselves, fair the deformed seem.*
*They who want means, by poverty oppressed,*
*Believe themselves of full estates possessed.*
*All is attempted; some new seas explore*
*To bring home riches from a foreign shore:*
*Seas, on whose boisterous back secured they ride,*
*And in the mercy of the winds confide:*
*Others to crooked plows their oxen yoke,*
*And Autumn with their plants and sets provoke.*
*Some Vulcan's <note>the god of fire and metalworking</note> and Minerva's <note>the goddess of wisdom and crafts</note> arts admire,*
*And by their hands, their livelihoods acquire.*
*Others the fair Olympian Muses trace,*
*And lovely learning studiously embrace.*
*One by Apollo <note>the god of prophecy, music, and healing</note> is prophetic made,*
*And tells what mischiefs others shall invade:*
*With him the Gods converse, but all the skill*
*In birds or victims <note>methods of divination</note> cannot hinder ill.*
*Some to medical <note>original: "Peonian"; referring to Paeon, the physician of the gods</note> knowledge are inclined,*
*Nor is the power of medicinal herbs <term>simples: medicines made from a single herb or plant</term> unconfined.*

<summary>This page contains a poetic discourse by Solon on the nature of divine justice and the various pursuits of humankind, emphasizing that while punishment for injustice may be delayed, it is inevitable and often falls upon future generations.</summary>
<keywords>Solon, Jove, divine justice, fate, prophecy, medicine, Muses, karma, vocational paths</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the poetic fragments of Solon, focusing on the unpredictability of human effort and a stern warning to the citizens of Athens regarding greed and civil unrest.</meta>

<header>SOLON.</header>

*The smallest hurts sometimes increase and rage,*
*More than all the art of medicine <term>physick: the art of healing or medicine</term> can assuage;*
*Sometimes the fury of the worst disease*
*The hand, by gentle stroking, will appease.*
*Thus good or bad events occur as the Fates design,*
*Man cannot decline what the Gods dispense.*
*All actions are uncertain; no man knows*
*When he begins a work, how it shall close.*
*Some, who weigh their business with prudent care,*
*Are often cut off from the intended outcome;*
*While others who have rashly designed anything,*
*Find a successful end to their labors.*
*There is no bound for those who acquire wealth,*
*For they who possess the most, desire*
*As much again; and who can satisfy everyone?*
*Even those full blessings which the Gods have lent,*
*Man applies variously to his own harm,*
*Whom Jove <note>Jupiter/Zeus</note> chastises by means just as various.*

## Again.

Our City can never be subverted
By Jove, or any other Deities;
For Pallas’s <note>Athena, the patron goddess of Athens</note> eye surveys with pious care
The walls, which are protected by her hand.
Yet the inhabitants of this great Town,
Foolishly <term>fondly: foolishly or naively</term> inclined toward wealth, will tear it down;
And those unjust, great persons who are bent
On wronging others, only bring themselves to discontent;
For their insatiable fancies do not have the power
To enjoy the sweetness of the present hour;
But by all wicked means, intent on gain,
They refrain neither from sacred nor from public things.
To possess riches through theft and deception <term>cozenage: cheating or fraud</term>,
They transgress the sacred bounds of justice.
Justice, who silently sees the present and knows the past,
Will take revenge for these injuries at last:
Causing a cureless rupture in the state,
And all our liberties shall be enslaved <term>captivate: to take captive or enslave</term>.
Rouse war from his long slumber, who the flower
Of all our youths shall bloodily devour.
For Cities which injuriously oppose
Their friends, are soon invaded by their foes.
These are the common evils; of the poor
Many are transported to a foreign shore,
To be sold there into bondage and fetters.

<meta>catchword: Each</meta>

<summary>Solon reflects on the unpredictability of fate and human endeavor before delivering a scathing warning to Athens about how the greed of its citizens and leaders will lead to civil ruin and slavery.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the fragments of Solon’s poetry, specifically the conclusion of his "Exhortation to the Athenians" (Eunomia), where he describes the consequences of civil injustice and the restorative power of law and order.</meta>

<header>SOLON.</header>

Each private house thus shares the public fate,
Nor can exclude it with a barred gate;
For scaling furiously the higher walls,
It falls even on those whom beds or corners hide.
My soul, Athenians, prompts me to relate
What miseries wait upon injustice:
But justice designs all things orderly,
And confines the unjust in strict fetters.
What is sour, she sweetens, and allays what cloys <term>cloys: surfeits or becomes sickeningly excessive</term>.
She repels wrong, and destroys evil in its growth,
Softens the stubborn, reforms the unjust,
And calms all seditious storms within the state:
Bitter dissension is suppressed by her reign,
Who wisely governs all things for the best.

### Another.

No man is blessed; everyone is unfortunate <note>The original "bad" here refers to being wretched or miserable in the face of fate, rather than moral wickedness.</note>
Who feels the warmth of the all-seeing sun.

### Another.

Let me not die unpitied; may every friend
Attend my final hour with sighs and tears.

<summary>Solon concludes his poem on "Good Order" by explaining how public injustice inevitably harms private citizens, whereas justice brings peace to the state. He also reflects on the universal nature of human suffering and his desire to be mourned by friends.</summary>

# CHILON

## CHAPTER I.

### The Life of Chilon.

<margin>
* Laertius.
* Sermon 28.
</margin>
CHILON was a Lacedæmonian <term>Lacedæmonian: a Spartan</term>, the son of Damagetus (who is mistakenly named in *Stobæus*, Page 6). He was famous among the Greeks for two prophecies.

<margin>
* Herodotus, book 1.
* Laertius.
</margin>
The first was to Hippocrates, a private citizen to whom a great prodigy happened at the Olympic games. Having prepared an offering and filled a cauldron with meat and water, it boiled over without any fire. Chilon, who happened to be present, saw this omen and advised Hippocrates that he should not marry a wife who might bear him children; or if he already had one, he should divorce her; and if he had a son, he should disown him. Hippocrates did not follow this advice and raised his son, Pisistratus. Later, during the civil strife between the coastal people and the inlanders at Athens—the former led by Megacles and the latter by Lycurgus—Pisistratus stirred up a third faction and seized absolute power.

<margin>
* Laertius.
* Book 7. 235.
</margin>
He was also highly renowned for his prophecy concerning Cythera, a Lacedæmonian island. Upon examining its location, he said, "Would to God it had never existed; or since it does, that it might be swallowed up by the sea." He wisely foresaw its danger. Later, Damaratus, a Lacedæmonian exile, advised Xerxes to seize that island; if Xerxes had followed this advice, it would have ruined all of Greece. His words (according to Herodotus) were these:

> You may achieve your desires if you send three hundred ships to the Lacedæmonian coast. There lies an island named Cythera, of which Chilon, the wisest man among us, said it would be better for the Lacedæmonians if it were underwater rather than above. It seems he expected some such threat as I am now describing—not that he foresaw your specific Navy, but he feared any such threat in general. Let your men launch attacks from this island against the Lacedæmonians to strike them with terror.

<margin>
* Laertius.
</margin>
Afterward, during the Peloponnesian War, Nicias captured the island and stationed Athenians there, who greatly harassed the Lacedæmonians.

Laertius says that Chilon was an old man <note>The OCR "sold" is likely a transcription error for the Latin *senex* (old) or perhaps a misreading of "flourished."</note> in the fifty-second Olympiad, at the time Aesop flourished. He also states that Chilon was an Ephor <term>Ephor: one of the five senior magistrates in Sparta who oversaw the kings</term> in the fifty-sixth Olympiad (though Casaubon reads it as the fifty-fifth). However, Pamphila (as cited by Laertius) says it was in the sixth. He was the first Ephor when Euthydemus was Archon <term>Archon: a chief magistrate of Athens, used here as a date marker</term>, as Sosicrates also confirms. He was the first to appoint the Ephors to be joined with the Kings, which Satyrus claims was an institution of Lycurgus. Consequently, it is uncertain whether

It also discusses his historical role as one of the first Spartan Ephors and the chronological discrepancies regarding his life.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the biography of Chilon of Sparta, focusing on his term as an Ephor (a high-ranking magistrate) and his reputation for absolute integrity in legal matters.</meta>

# CHILON

Whether *Chilon* was an Ephor in the sixth Olympiad or in the fifty-sixth is debated; the latter is more probable, since he held that office when *Euthydemus* was Archon at *Athens*. This appears from the *Arundel Marbles* <note>The *Marmor Arundelianum* or Arundel Marbles are a collection of ancient Greek inscriptions.</note>, where instead of "of Euthydemus" <note>original: *Ευθυδήμου*</note>, it is corruptly read as "among the people" <note>original: *εν δημω*</note>, translated as "with the people as Archon" <note>original: *Archonte populo*</note>. But it is likewise true that the Ephori were first created around the sixth Olympiad, when *Polydorus* and *Theopompus* were Kings of *Sparta*—one hundred and thirty years after *Lycurgus*, as *Plutarch* (in his *Life of Lycurgus*) affirms. From that time on, there were five annual Ephori <term>Ephori: powerful Spartan magistrates who oversaw the kings</term> chosen in *Sparta*, the first of whom is called "eponymous" <note>original: *ἐπώνυμος*</note> because the year was named after him. <margin>* Plutarch, *Life of Lycurgus*.</margin> The first man of the very first election was *Elatus*. *Chilon*, in the fifty-sixth Olympiad, was the first of the five elected for his year; this perhaps gave cause for the mistake made by those who believe him to have been the first of that institution, among whom is *Scaliger*.

<margin>* In Eusebius, p. 67.
* Diogenes Laertius.</margin>
How he behaved himself in this office may be gathered from his speech to his brother, who was displeased that he himself was not made an Ephor at the same time: "I can bear injuries," he said, "but you cannot."

<margin>* Diogenes Laertius.</margin>
He was so just in all his actions that in his old age he professed he had never done anything contrary to the conscience of an upright man, except for one thing about which he was doubtful: having given sentence against his friend according to the law, he advised his friend to appeal the decision (to him as his Judge) so as to preserve both his friend and the law. <margin>* Book 1, Chapter 3.</margin> *Aulus Gellius relates it thus:*

> “When his life drew toward an end, ready to be seized by death, he
> “spoke thus to his friends about him: ‘My words and actions
> “in this long term of years have been (almost all) such as I
> “need not repent of, which perhaps you also know. Truly, even
> “at this time I am certain I never committed anything, the
> “remembrance of which causes any trouble in me, unless this
> “one thing only—and whether it was done wrongly or not, I
> “am uncertain. I sat with two others as a Judge over the life of
> “my friend; the law was such that the person must of necessity
> “be condemned; so that either my friend must lose his life, or
> “some deceit must be used toward the Law. Revolving many things
> “in my mind for relief of a condition so desperate, I conceived
> “that which I put into practice to be the easiest of all
> “to be borne: Silently I condemned him myself, but persuaded those
> “others who judged with me to acquit him. Thus I preserved (in so
> “great a matter) the duty both of a judge and a friend. But
> “from that act I receive this trouble: I fear it is not free
> “from betrayal and guilt to persuade others, contrary
> “to what was in my own judgment best, in the same business, at
> “the same time, and in a public affair.’

<meta>Catchword: CHAPTER II.</meta>

<meta>This page continues the biography of Chilon of Sparta, transitioning from his life and political office to a collection of his most famous philosophical sayings, moral precepts, and verses.</meta>

# CHILON

## CHAPTER II.

->His moral sentences, precepts, and verses.<-

Of his aphorisms, these are remembered by *Diogenes Laertius*: he said that foresight regarding future events, gathered through reason, is a man’s true virtue. When asked how the learned differ from the unlearned, he answered, "In having good hope." When asked what is difficult, he replied, "To keep secrets, to use leisure time well, and to be able to endure an injury."

<margin>* Plutarch, *The Banquet of the Seven Sages*</margin>
When invited to a feast by *Periander* (along with the other wise men), he would not promise to attend until he knew who the other guests would be. He said, "A man is forced to tolerate a bad companion on a ship at sea or in a tent in a military camp, but to mix indiscriminately with all sorts of people at a feast is a lack of judgment." Upon the same occasion, *Plutarch* records these sentences of his: "A prince must not focus on fleeting, mortal things, but only on those that are eternal and immortal." "That commonwealth is best where the people respect the law more than the lawyers." "A family should resemble, as much as possible, a city governed by a king." <margin>* Plutarch, *On the Soul*</margin> Hearing a man say he had no enemies, Chilon asked him if he had any friends, believing that love and hate must necessarily follow one another.

<margin>* Stobaeus, *Sermon 28*</margin>
His moral precepts are delivered as follows by *Demetrius Phalereus*: "Know yourself. Do not speak much while drinking, for you will commit errors." (Or as *Laertius* records, "Rule your tongue, especially at a feast.") "Do not threaten free people, for it is not just." (*Laertius* records, "Threaten no one, for that is a womanish trait.") "Do not speak ill of your neighbor; if you do, you will hear things that will trouble you. Go slowly to the feasts of your friends, but hurry to their misfortunes." (*Laertius* records, "Go more readily to a friend in adversity than in prosperity.") "Celebrate marriage with moderation. Speak well of the deceased. Revere your elders." (*Laertius* records, "Honor old age.") "Hate those who pry into the business of others. Prefer loss over dishonest gain (for loss, *Laertius* adds, brings grief only once, but dishonest gain brings it forever). Do not mock the unfortunate. If you are strong, behave mildly so that you may be respected rather than feared" (*Laertius* adds, "by your neighbors"). "Learn to manage your household well. Do not let your tongue outrun your mind. Restrain your anger. Do not desire the impossible. Do not rush while traveling. Do not wave your hands while speaking" (*Laertius* adds, "during discourse"), "for it makes you look like a madman. Obey the laws. Be reconciled to those who have wronged you, but take vengeance against insults <term>contumelies: insulting or offensive treatment</term>." To which *Laertius* adds these: "Preserve yourself. Do not despise divination; make use of tranquility."

<margin>* Pliny, *Natural History*, Book 7, Chapter 32</margin>
*Pliny*, speaking of authority, says that men ranked *Chilon* among the oracles, consecrating three of his precepts at *Delphi* in golden letters: "Every man should know himself; desire nothing in excess; and misery follows when one involves oneself in another's money and legal disputes <note>original: "the companion of anothers mony and strife is misery." This likely refers to *sponsio*, the act of being a legal guarantor for someone else's debt, which Chilon famously warned against.</note>."

<margin>* Aulus Gellius</margin>
He alone kept the two most intense passions of the soul—love and hate—within proper bounds, saying, "Love with such limits that you might one day hate; and hate only so much that you might one day love."

<meta>catchword: Aufo- (likely referencing Ausonius)</meta>

# CHILON

*Ausonius* ascribes to him the effect of these verses:
> *May the common people not fear me, nor the great despise me,*
> *Keep death and health alike before your eyes.*
> *Never remember the benefits you give,*
> *But always be mindful of those you receive.*
> *Learn from yourself and your friend how to overcome bad fortune;*
> *Old age that resembles youth is a light condition,*
> *But youth that resembles old age is a greater burden.*

His particular maxim was, *To a guarantee, loss is near.* <note>This refers to the danger of acting as a legal or financial surety for another.</note>
Of his *Apophthegms* <note>Concise sayings or maxims.</note>, *Laertius* mentions this as the most eminent:
> *We find the value of gold by the touchstone,*
> *But gold is the touchstone of the mind.*

<margin>* Laertius</margin>
* He asked *Aesop* what *Jupiter* <note>The Roman name for Zeus.</note> was doing, who answered, "Pulling down the high, and raising up the low."

### CHAP. III.
->His death and writings.<-

<margin>* Laertius</margin>
He died (according to *Hermippus*) at *Pisa*, while embracing his son, who was a victor in the <term>caestus</term> <note>A boxing match where participants wore leather hand-wraps.</note> at the Olympic games. The weakness of his old age was overcome with an excess of joy. All who were present at that great assembly attended his funeral, <margin>* Lib. 7. cap. 32</margin> as is affirmed by *Pliny* and *Laertius*, who wrote this epigram about him:
> *To you, illustrious Pollux, I pay my thanks,*
> *That Chilon’s son carried away the Olive branch.*
> *The father died overjoyed to see his child*
> *So crowned: a happy death! May such befall me.*

Upon his statue was this inscription:
> *The birth of Chilon graced warlike Sparta,*
> *Who was placed in the first rank of the Seven Sages.*

<margin>* Laertius; * Ludus Septem Sapientum <note>Latin: "The Play of the Seven Sages."</note></margin>
* He was brief in speech, which is why *Aristagoras* calls that manner of speaking *Chilonian*; *Ausonius* also alludes to this in the speech he makes under Chilon's name.

<margin>* Laertius</margin>
* He wrote elegies extending to almost two hundred verses; there is likewise an existing letter of his to this effect:

->Chilon to Periander.<-

> "You send me word of an expedition you are preparing
> against foreigners, intending to go in person with your
> army. I think a monarch has little safety, even at home.
> I consider that tyrant happy who dies at home a natural
> death."
