# PITTACUS

## CHAPTER I.

### *The Life of Pittacus.*

<margin>
* Laertius and Suidas.
* So the Vatican manuscript of Suidas, which is better than the printed editions that read "and."
* Suidas.
</margin>
PITTACUS was from Mitylene (the chief city of *Lesbos*), the son of Caicus, or rather Hyrrhadius, a Thracian; his mother was a Lesbian. He was born in the thirty-second Olympiad <note>A four-year period used by the Greeks for dating; the 32nd Olympiad began around 652 BC.</note>. 

Laertius says he flourished in the forty-second Olympiad; at that time, he gave testimony of his great courage and love for his country by killing Melanchrus, the tyrant of Lesbos and Mitylene, with the assistance of the brothers of the poet Alcaeus.

<margin>
* Laertius.
* Strabo.
* Herodotus, Book 5, Section 94.
* Strabo, Book 13.
* Herodotus continues.
* Strabo; Laertius.
* Strabo; Laertius.
* Saturnalia.
* In Retiario.
* Strabo; Laertius.
</margin>
Pittacus, having become eminent by this action, was appointed General by the Mityleneans and sent with a fleet against the Athenians, with whom they had a long contest concerning the Achillean fields. The reason for their dispute was this: Pisistratus took Sigeum by force from the Mityleneans and established his illegitimate son, Hegesistratus (born of an Argive woman), there as king. Hegesistratus held the territory, though not without much conflict; for there was a long war between the Mityleneans and the Athenians, with the former sallying out of the Achillean town and the latter out of Sigeum. The Mityleneans claimed the town, asserting it was built by Archaeanax of Mitylene using the stones of old Troy (for the Lesbians claimed the greater part of Troas as their hereditary right, where they had built many houses—some of which Strabo says are still standing today, while others are demolished). The Athenians opposed this claim, alleging that the Aeolians <note>The Greek dialect group to which the Lesbians belonged.</note> had no more right to this Ilian country than they or any other Greeks who had assisted Menelaus in the recovery of Helen.

The Athenians sent Phryno there as General, a tall and robust man who had been a victor in all the Olympic exercises; he is perhaps the same person whom Eusebius mentions in the thirty-sixth Olympiad. Pittacus, having been defeated in battle several times, finally challenged Phryno to single combat. He met him armed with the equipment of a fisherman, hiding a net under his shield. Suddenly catching Phryno in the net, he killed him with a trident and dagger, and by his death, Pittacus recovered the field. From this stratagem of Pittacus was derived a similar style of fighting among the Roman gladiators called <term>Retiarii</term> <note>Gladiators who fought with a net and trident, attempting to entangle their opponents.</note> (as described by Lipsius), which is explicitly noted by Polyaenus and Festus.

But this war did not end there; until at last both parties referred—

<margin>
* Herodotus, Book 5, Section 95.
* Strabo.
</margin>
They refer]red themselves to *Periander*, *choosing him as Umpire. He awarded that each side should keep what they were in possession of, whereby *Sigeum* fell to the *Athenians*.* *Demetrius* accuses *Timaeus* of falsehood for affirming that *Periander* built *Achilleum* (a small town where the tomb of *Achilles* was located) in opposition to the *Athenians*, using *Ilian* stones <note>Stones from Troy</note>, and thereby aided *Pittacus*. But according to *Strabo*, it was neither built of such stones, nor was *Periander* the founder. How could he be chosen as an arbitrator if his actions had declared him an enemy?

<margin>
* Laertius.
* Aristotle, Politics, Book 3, Chapter 14.
* Valerius Maximus, Book 6, Chapter 5.
* Plutarch, *Amatorius*.
* Aristotle, Politics, Book 3, Chapter 14.
* Plutarch, *On the Malice of Herodotus*.
* Laertius; Suidas.
</margin>
* At this point, *Pittacus* was highly honored by the *Mytileneans*. Because they were being harassed by banished persons under the leadership of *Antimenides* and *Alcaeus* the poet, the citizens—either out of obligation to his merit or confidence in his fairness—voted freely (though *Alcaeus* denies it) to appoint him to the Tyranny <note>In this context, 'Tyranny' refers to an elective office of absolute power granted during a crisis (an Aesymnetes), rather than necessarily a cruel or illegal rule.</note> with many acclamations of praise and a great gathering of people (as is manifest from *Alcaeus*, who for that reason reprimands them). *They also, with general consent, offered him great gifts and told him to take as much as he wanted from the field he had recovered for the citizens. He, throwing his spear, demanded only as much land as the spear had passed over. He dedicated this to *Apollo*, and it was called (even up to the time of *Plutarch* and *Laertius*) the *Pittacean field*.* <margin>* Laertius.</margin> * *Sosicrates* asserts that he took part of it for himself, saying that "the half was <margin>* Valerius Maximus, Book 6, Chapter 5.</margin> more than the whole." *In this way, he turned his mind away from the gift, believing it was not fitting to diminish the glory of his virtue by accepting a massive reward.*

<margin>* Valerius Maximus, Book 4, Chapter 1.</margin>
* Once he possessed this power, he showed the moderation that filled his heart toward *Alcaeus* the poet. *Alcaeus* had behaved stubbornly against him with bitter hatred and scurrilous wit (of which *Laertius* records many instances). *Pittacus only informed him how easily he could have oppressed him.* <note>Showing Alcaeus that he had the power to punish him but chose mercy instead.</note>

<margin>
* On the Laws, Book 2.
* Politics, Book 2, Chapter 12.
</margin>
During his government, he made many laws. One is mentioned by *Cicero*, which forbade *any man from attending the funeral of anyone who was not a relative.* Another law mentioned by *Aristotle* was that *whoever struck another person while drunk should pay twice as much as if he had been sober.* Or, as *Laertius* records it, *whoever committed an offense while drunk should pay a double penalty.* He did this to restrain the *Mytileneans* from drunkenness because their island was abundant in wine. Even while he was king (as *Clearchus* affirms), his usual exercise was to grind corn. He considered it a healthy exercise and highly praised the mill because it provided exercise for many people in such a small space. There was a song <margin>* Pollux.</margin> called *epimylion* <note>original: "επιμύλιον" — a grinding-song or mill-song.</note> for that <margin>* Plutarch, *The Banquet of the Seven Sages*.</margin> reason. *Thales* affirms that he heard a female slave in *Lesbos* singing the beginning of it as the base melody, which went like this:

> Grind, grind my Mill amain,
> For *Pittacus* the *Lesbian* King
> To grind does not disdain.

<summary>Pittacus is elected as the ruler of Mytilene to defend against exiles, demonstrating his legendary moderation by refusing excessive land rewards and pardoning his rival, the poet Alcaeus. He also established notable laws, including double penalties for crimes committed while intoxicated.</summary>

<page-num>75</page-num>

<center># PITTACUS</center>

<margin>Hence, correct *Suidas* <note>The *Suda*, a 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia</note>, who says *Simon*.</margin>
When he was advanced in years, he was forced to take command of an army, whereupon he said, "It is hard to be good"; which *Simonides* mentions, saying:

> *Hard to be truly honest, this*
> *The Pittacean sentence is.*

*Plato* also mentions this in his *Protagoras*, where *Simonides* rebukes *Pittacus* for saying it is hard to *continue* being good. Simonides affirms that remaining good is easy, but *becoming* good is hard; in this, he differs from the way Diogenes Laertius expresses it.

<margin>Diogenes Laertius, Book 1, Chapter 75 <note>The original margin note says 6.5, but this likely refers to the section in the Life of Pittacus.</note></margin>
He continued in the government of the kingdom for ten years. *Valerius Maximus* says he ruled only as long as the war with the *Athenians* concerning *Sigeum* lasted. However, as soon as peace was obtained by victory—having settled the affairs of the commonwealth during this time—he resigned his position. He did this despite the *Mytileneans* protesting to the contrary, so that he would not remain lord over the citizens longer than the necessities of the kingdom required. He lived for ten more years as a private citizen.

<margin>Herodotus, Book 1, Section 27.</margin>
He traveled to *Sardis* at a time when (as some say, though others attribute this to *Bias*) *Croesus*, having made the *Greeks* in *Asia* pay tribute, had ordered the construction of a navy to invade the islanders. As soon as Pittacus arrived, *Croesus* asked him, "What news from *Greece*?" By his answer, he diverted the King from proceeding with his shipbuilding. "The islanders," he said, "have bought a vast number of horses, intending an expedition against *Sardis*." *Croesus*, believing he spoke the truth, answered, "*I wish the gods would put it in their minds to come against the* Lydians *on horseback*." Pittacus replied, "*It is with good reason, great King, that you wish and hope to catch the islanders on horseback on the mainland; but what do you think the islanders desire more—hearing of your preparation to set out a navy against them—than that they may catch the* Lydians *at sea? They wish to revenge the cause of those inland* Greeks *whom you have reduced to slavery.*" *Croesus* was much delighted with this speech, and because it seemed to him that Pittacus spoke very ingeniously, he was dissuaded from building a navy. He abandoned the project and established a friendship with the *Ionian* islands.

## CHAPTER II.

### His moral sentences, precepts, and verses.

Of his apothegms <term>Apothegm: A concise, instructive saying or maxim</term>, *Laertius* records these: *The gods themselves cannot resist necessity. Power reveals the man.* Being once asked what is best, he answered, *To do the present work well.* To *Croesus*, who asked which was the best form of government, he replied, *That of the "various"* (meaning "wood," referring to the Law carved on wooden tablets). To a *Phocian* who said, "I must find an honest man," he replied, "Though you search long, you shall not find

# PITTACUS

“...to see the future; what is past is certain; what is to come is obscure. The earth is faithful; the sea is faithless; gain is insatiable. Acquire honesty; seek obligingness <note>Archaic: "obsequiousness," meaning the quality of being helpful or compliant</note>; love discipline, temperance, prudence, truth, faith, experience, skill, community, diligence, thrift, and piety.”

->*Ausonius* cites these as his:<-

> *None know how to speak who know not how to refrain;*
> *One good man’s praise is worth more than many bad men's gain.*
> *He is mad who envies pride in the happy,*
> *Or mocks the grief of the unhappy.*
> *He who makes a law must not that law transgress;*
> *Gain all the friends you can in times of happiness,*
> *And trust the fewest in your distress.*

<margin>* Laertius</margin>
* Of his *Poems*, these were the most famous:

> *He who has a quiver and a bow*
> *Against a wicked man should go,*
> *Whose deceitful tongue never expressed*
> *The faithless meaning of his heart.*

His particular maxim was, *Know opportunity*.

## CHAP. III.
### His Death; Brother, Wife, Son, Writings.

<margin>
* Laertius
* On Longevity
* Laertius
</margin>
He lived to a ripe old age, over seventy years, or (following the account of *Suidas* regarding his birth) eighty. * *Lucian* goes further, reckoning him among those who lived 100 years; for he died when *Aristomenes* was Archon, in the third year of the 52nd Olympiad. Upon his monument was this epitaph:

> *Weep, citizens, as sacred Lesbos weeps*
> *For Pittacus; this tomb his ashes keeps.*

He had a brother who died without children, and so his estate passed to *Pittacus*; because of this, when he refused the wealth *Croesus* offered him, he said he already had more by half than he desired.

<margin>
* Plutarch, *On Tranquility of Mind*
</margin>
He married a wife of higher birth than himself, the sister of *Draco*, son of *Penthilus*. She behaved imperiously toward him, of which * *Plutarch* gives this example: Having invited some friends over, she came in and overturned the table. Seeing his guests were troubled, he said,
“*Each of you has some misfortune; he is happiest who has no more than this.*” *Laertius* says the advice he gave concerning equal marriage (Chapter 2) was born out of resentment of his own troubles.

<margin>* Laertius</margin>
* He had a son named *Tyrrheus*, who was accidentally killed by a metalworker <term>brasier: a worker in brass or a blacksmith</term> with the blow of a hatchet while sitting in a barber shop in *Cuma* (as was common among the ancients for those who liked to discuss the news). The *Cumeans* captured the offender and sent him to *Pittacus*. Upon being informed of the accident, he set the man free, saying, "*Pardon is better than repentance.*"

->*Pittacus* to *Croesus*<-

> "You command me to come to *Lydia* to see your wealth. Even without seeing it, I can easily believe the son of *Alyattes* to be the richest of kings; therefore, I do not need to go to *Sardis* for that reason. I do not lack gold, but have enough even for my friends. Nevertheless, I will come to you to enjoy your conversation as a guest."

# BIAS.

## CHAPTER I.

### The life of Bias.

<margin>* Laertius</margin>
Bias was from *Priene*, the son of *Tutamis*. Some affirm he was rich; others, that he had no estate but lived as a lodger <term>inmate: a person who resides in a house or institution, often as a boarder or guest rather than an owner</term>. *Satyrus* ranks him first among the Seven Sages. The occasion by which that title was conferred on him was this: he redeemed some captive *Messenian* virgins, brought them up as his own daughters, gave them dowries <term>portions: money or property brought by a bride to her husband at marriage</term>, and then sent them back to their parents. A tripod being afterwards found at *Athens* (as was related in the life of *Thales*, though the location is different) with this inscription in gold, *To the wise*, these virgins (as *Satyrus* affirms) or their father (as *Phanodicus* says) came into the assembly and pronounced *Bias* wise, declaring what he had done for them. Thereupon, the tripod was sent to him; but *Bias*, beholding it, declared *Apollo* to be the most wise and would not accept it. Some affirm he dedicated it to *Hercules* at *Thebes*, as he was descended from the *Theban* colony sent to *Priene*.

There are many instances showing that he lived up to this reputation:
<margin>* Laertius</margin>
* When *Alyattes* <note>King of Lydia and father of Croesus</note> was besieging *Priene*, *Bias* drove two exceedingly fat mules out of the town. When they arrived at the camp, *Croesus* wondered to see <margin>* Laertius. <note>original: "μέχρι τῶν ἀλόγων τὴν εὐθένειαν" — prosperity extended even to the irrational beasts</note></margin> their prosperity extended even to the animals; and being desirous of reconciliation, he sent a messenger to the town. *Bias* caused many heaps of sand to be made and covered over with wheat, and showed them to the messenger. When *Alyattes* was informed of this, he was more eager for peace than before, and immediately sent to request that *Bias* come to him. But *Bias* answered, "I wish *Alyattes* may feed on onions," meaning that he wished him to weep.

<margin>* Herodotus 1.27</margin>
* Some ascribe to *Bias* the diversion of *Croesus's* expedition against the Greek Islands, though others attribute this to *Pittacus*, as related in his life.

<margin>* Herodotus 1.170</margin>
* After *Cyrus* had captured *Croesus*, he sent an army against the Greeks. The *Ionians*, being much troubled, assembled at the *Panionium* <term>Panionium: the central sanctuary and meeting place of the Ionian League</term>, where *Bias* gave them wise advice which, had they followed (says *Herodotus*), they might have been the happiest of all the Greeks. He counseled them to join together in one fleet, sail to *Sardinia*, and there build one city common to all the Ioni-

<summary>This page introduces Bias of Priene, recounting his act of ransom for Messenian captives that earned him the title of Sage, his clever deception of King Alyattes during a siege, and his political advice to the Ionians facing Persian conquest.</summary>
<keywords>Bias of Priene, Seven Sages, Alyattes, Croesus, Cyrus, Messenian virgins, the Tripod, Priene, Panionium, Sardinia, Herodotus</keywords>

--- Page 110 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Bias of Priene, specifically focusing on his political counsel and his reputation for wisdom in dealings with foreign kings.</meta>

Ionians; by which they might preserve themselves from bondage, happy in possessing an island far greater than all the rest and commanding them. But if they continued in *Ionia*, there was no apparent hope of liberty. <note>Bias famously advised the Ionians to migrate to Sardinia to escape Persian rule.</note> This advice was justified when the Ionians were later subdued.

<margin>Valerius Maximus, Book 7, Chapter 2</margin>
*   *Bias* (when his country of *Priene* was invaded by enemies, and all those whom the cruelty of war allowed to escape were fleeing, laden with their most precious wealth) was asked why he carried none of his goods with him. "I," he said, "**carry all my goods with me.**" He bore them (*adds Valerius Maximus*) in his heart, not to be seen by the eye but prized by the soul; enclosed in the narrow dwelling of the mind, not to be demolished by mortal hands, present with those who are settled, and not forsaking those who flee.

<margin>Plutarch, *The Banquet of the Seven Sages*</margin>
*   He did not refuse the friendship of kings (as *Thales* did), particularly that of *Amasis*, King of *Egypt*, who sent him a sacrificial animal, commanding him to take from it the best and worst parts. *Bias* sent him the tongue; for this ingenuity, he was much admired. <note>The tongue was considered the best part of a man when used for wisdom and the worst when used for malice.</note>

<margin>Plutarch, *The Banquet of the Seven Sages*</margin>
*   He resolved another question from *Amasis* while he was at *Corinth* (having been invited there with the rest of the wise men by *Periander*), where *Niloxenus* brought him this letter:

> *Amasis, King of Egypt, says this to Bias, wisest of the Greeks: The King of Ethiopia contends with me for preeminence in wisdom. Having been mastered in other things, he finally requires an absurd and strange thing: that I drink up the sea. If I solve this proposition, I shall gain many of his towns and cities; if I do not solve it, I must lose all those which are around Elephantine. Ponder it, and send Niloxenus back with all speed; whatever we can do for your friends and country shall not be wanting.*

Having read the letter, *Bias*, after a short pause to recollect himself and having whispered to *Cleobulus* <note>OCR reads "Cleobubus," a misspelling of the Sage Cleobulus.</note> who sat next to him, spoke:

“What,” said he, “*Niloxenus*, does *Amasis*, who commands so many 
“men and possesses so excellent a country, wish to drink up the sea 
“for the sake of a few obscure, contemptible villages?” 

“*Bias*,” said *Niloxenus* smiling, “consider, as if he actually meant to do it, 
“how it might be performed.” 

“Tell the *Ethiopian*,” replied *Bias*, “to stop the rivers from running 
“into the sea until he has drunk off that which is now the sea; for the 
“challenge concerns only that which exists at present, not what shall 
“be added hereafter.” 

*Niloxenus* embraced him with joy, and the rest applauded his solution.

## CHAP. II.

### *His moral sentences, precepts, and verses.*

His apophthegms <term>Apophthegms: concise, pithy sayings or maxims.</term> are presented by *Laertius* <note>Diogenes Laertius, author of "Lives and Opinions of Eminent Philosophers."</note> and others as follows: *He is unfortunate who cannot endure misfortune. It is a sickness of the mind to desire things that cannot be obtained, and to be unmindful of the miseries of others.* To one who asked what is difficult, he answered: *To bear a change for the worse with courage.* While at sea in the company of wicked men who, when a storm arose, began to call upon the Gods, he said: *Hold your peace, lest they realize you are here.* To a wicked man inquiring what piety was, he remained silent; when the other asked the reason for his silence, he said: *I do not answer because you are inquiring about something that does not concern you.* When asked what is sweet to mankind, he answered: *Hope.* *It is better to settle a dispute between our enemies than our friends; for one of the friends will certainly become an enemy, while one of the enemies will become a friend.* When asked what a man does with delight, he answered: *Profit from labor.* *We should live as though our lives would be both long and short. Love as if you might one day hate, conducting friendship with caution and remembering that it may possibly turn to enmity.*

<margin>* Aulus Gellius, *Attic Nights*, Book 5, Chapter 11.</margin>
\* To one asking whether he should take a wife, he said: *She must be either beautiful or ugly; if beautiful, she will be shared <note>original: "common," implying unfaithful or promiscuous</note>; if ugly, she will be a penance <note>a source of suffering or a burden</note>.*

<margin>* Plutarch.</margin>
\* *That tyrant shall gain the most glory who is the first to obey the laws of his own country. That commonwealth is best ordered where every man fears the Law more than a tyrant. That family is best ordered where the master behaves voluntarily at home as he does in public by the constraint of the Law.*

<margin>* Stobaeus, *Sermon* 28.</margin>
\* *Those who busy themselves with useless knowledge resemble an owl, which sees only in the night but is blind in the light; so is their mind sharp-sighted regarding vanity, but dark at the approach of true light.*

*Ausonius* <note>Decimus Magnus Ausonius, a Roman poet.</note> has these verses under his name:

> *What is our greatest good? A conscience free,*
> *Our greatest ill? Man is man’s worst enemy.*
> *Who is poor? The greedy. Who is rich? He who desires nothing.*
> *A wife’s best dowry? The reputation a chaste life acquires.*
> *Who is chaste? She of whom rumor dares speak no ill.*
> *Who is wise? He who has the power to hurt, but lacks the will.*
> *Who is a fool? He who lacks the power, and yet desires to kill.*

<margin>* Stobaeus, *Sermon* 28.
* Diogenes Laertius.</margin>
His moral precepts, according to *Demetrius of Phalerum*, are these:
"Most men are evil. <note>This was considered his signature maxim.</note> Before you
"do anything, look at your face in a mirror; if it appears handsome,
"do handsome <note>virtuous</note> things; if it appears deformed, make up for the defects of nature."

<header>BIAS</header>

<margin>* Laertius</margin>
“Practice honesty. Deliberate before undertaking a task, but once begun, see it through to the end. Abhor speaking hastily. [<note>original: "Laert."</note> *Laertius says:* It is madness] lest you sin, for repentance follows. Be neither naive nor cunning. Do not allow for ignorance. Love prudence. Everywhere profess that the Gods exist. Weigh what is to be done. Listen much. Speak at the proper time. If you are poor, do not criticize the rich unless some great benefit may come of it. Do not praise an unworthy person because of his wealth. Acquire things through persuasion, not by violence. When you do good, do not credit yourself, but the Gods. In your youth, gain wealth; in your old age, wisdom. [Or as *Laertius* says: From your youth to your old age, gain wisdom, for it will be more secure to you than all other possessions.] Maintain remembrance in your actions; caution in opportunity; sincerity in your manners; patience in labor; wariness in fear; love in wealth; persuasion in discourse; propriety in silence; justice in judgment; fortitude in boldness; power in action; eminence in glory; and generosity in your nature.

* Of his <term>Apophthegms</term> <note>Apophthegms: concise, pithy sayings or maxims.</note> these were most esteemed:

->*To the whole city where you live, be kind,*<-
->*Those who show the most favor, most favor find:*<-
->*But pride is often with destruction joined.*<-

->And<-

->*Strength is a gift which nature's hand bestows,*<-
->*Rhetoric and policy the wise soul knows,*<-
->*Riches are a gift that from fortune flows.*<-

### CHAP. III.

->*His death and writings.*<-

<margin>
* Laertius
* Laertius: <note>original: "Δημόδικος ὁ Ἀλη-ειος: perhaps it should be Δημόδοκος ὁ Λέιος"</note> "Demodicus the Alerian; perhaps it should be Demodocus the Lerian."
</margin>
He was very devoted to legal pleading and very earnest in it, but he was always employed in just causes. This is what *Demodicus the Alerian implies when he says, *If you happen to be a judge, give your verdict in favor of the Prienean side;* and *Hipponax* <note>An ancient Greek iambic poet.</note> says, *To plead a cause better than Bias the Prienean.* 

He died in this manner when he was very old: while he was pleading a case, having ended his speech, he rested his head in the lap of his sister's son. After his adversary had finished his closing arguments, the judges gave their verdict in *Bias's* favor. When the court was dismissed, he was found dead in his nephew's arms. The city granted him a magnificent monument with this inscription:

# CLEOBULUS.

## CHAPTER I.

->The life, death, and writings of Cleobulus.<-

<margin>Diogenes Laertius</margin>
<image-desc>Decorative initial letter C with floral and architectural motifs.</image-desc>
CLEOBULUS was from Lindus (a city in Rhodes) or, according to Duris, from Caria. He was the son of Evagoras and a lineal descendant of Hercules. He was excellent in wisdom, outward beauty, and <margin>Suidas</margin> strength beyond everyone else of his time, and was learned in the philosophy of the Egyptians. That he was the tyrant of Lindus is clear from Plutarch. <margin>Plutarch</margin> <margin>On the E at Delphi <note>original: "De ei delph."</note></margin> He rebuilt the Temple of Minerva, which had been founded by Danaus.

<margin>Plutarch, The Banquet of the Seven Sages <note>original: "Plut. conviv. sept. sap."</note></margin>
He had a daughter whom he named Eumetis, but she was commonly called Cleobulina after her father. She composed verses and riddles in hexameters and was famous for her wisdom and sharp wit in those riddles. Some of her questions spread as far as Egypt. She used these riddles playfully, like dice upon occasion, only competing with those who provoked her. She was also endowed with an admirable nobility of mind and a wit that was both political and full of humanity, which led her father to govern his people with more mildness. Cratinus also mentions her in a comedy named *Cleobula* after her, which is often cited by Athenaeus.

<margin>Diogenes Laertius</margin>
He died full of years, reaching the age of seventy. His tomb bore this inscription:

> The Lindian shore, where he was born, deplores
> The death of wise Cleobulus evermore.

<margin>Diogenes Laertius</margin>
He composed verses and riddles to the number of three thousand, among which was this riddle concerning the year (attributed by Suidas to his daughter Cleobulina):

> One father has twelve sons, and each has a race
> Of thirty daughters with a double face:
> Their looks are black and white in succession;
> They are all immortal, yet they all must die.
<note>The riddle refers to the year (father), months (sons), and days and nights (daughters with black and white faces).</note>

<margin>Diogenes Laertius</margin>
Some assert he was the author of this epigram about Midas (not Homer, who, as they reckon, lived long before Midas, though Herodotus says otherwise).

<meta>catchword: A</meta>

-> **Cleobulus to Solon** <-

> "You have many friends and a home everywhere; but I dare affirm that *Lindus* would be most pleasing to Solon, being governed by a democracy. It is an island where there is no fear of *Pisistratus* <note>The tyrant of Athens who had seized power, causing Solon to go into exile.</note>; your friends will come to you there from all parts."

## CHAPTER II.

### His moral sentences, precepts, and verses.

<margin>Diogenes Laertius</margin>
Of his moral sayings are these: *Employ yourself in something excellent. Be not vain and ungrateful. Marry off your daughters while they are virgins in age, but matrons in wisdom:* implying that the virgins also should be instructed—a practice the Greeks did not use, though the Romans raised them in the liberal arts. *Do good to your friend so that he may be more your friend, and to your enemy so that he may become your friend; for we should beware of the slander of friends and the treachery of enemies. When any man goes out, let him consider what he is to do; when he returns, let him examine what he has done.*

<margin>Plutarch, The Banquet of the Seven Sages <note>original: "Plut. sympos. sept. sap."</note></margin>
*A prince may be happy if he trusts none of those who surround him. That commonwealth is best ordered wherein the citizens fear disgrace more than the law. That family is best wherein the members love the master more than they fear him.*

<margin>Stobaeus, Sermon 28; Diogenes Laertius</margin>
His precepts were thus collected by Demetrius Phalereus: *Moderation is best* (his specific maxim). *To reverence your father is a duty. Take care of your body and soul. Listen willingly, but do not trust rashly.* (Or as Laertius puts it, "It is better to love listening than to love speaking.") *It is better to know many things* (Laertius says "to love knowledge") *than to be ignorant of everything. Teach your tongue to speak well. It is characteristic of virtue, and contrary to vice, to hate injustice.* (Laertius says, "Be a friend to virtue, a stranger to vice.") *Preserve your piety. Advise your countrymen on what is best. Govern your tongue.* (Laertius says, "Govern your pleasure.") *Do nothing by violence. Instruct your children. Pray to fortune. Give up enmity. Consider the enemy of your country to be your own enemy. Do not quarrel with your wife, nor be overly affectionate toward her, in the presence of others; the first shows folly, the other shows madness. Do not discipline your servants when they are drunk; it makes it look as if you were drunk yourself.*

himself. *Marry your equal; for by matching into a higher family, you gain masters, not kinsmen. Do not laugh in agreement with one who mocks others, for you will be hated by those he mocks. When rich, do not be arrogant; when poor, do not be dejected. (Laertius adds: learn to bear the changes of fortune.)*

*Ausonius ascribes these to him:*

> *The more power you have, the less you should desire;*

> *All men would spare the good and cast down the bad;*

> *But we often inherit their shameful deeds.*

# PERIANDER.

## CHAPTER I.

### *The Country, Ancestors, and Parents of Periander.*

<margin>*</margin>
<margin>Diogenes Laertius</margin>
<margin>Herodotus, Book 1, Chapter 7</margin>
<margin>Book 5, Chapter 92</margin>
<image-desc>A large decorative initial 'P' featuring intricate woodcut-style scrolling floral vines and foliage.</image-desc>Periander was the son of Cypselus, the Tyrant of Corinth. His mother was Cratea, and his ancestors were the Heraclidae (* descended from Hercules and Iardana <note>Iardana is another name for Omphale, the Queen of Lydia</note>), who reigned as Kings of Lydia for five hundred and five years; the son continually succeeding the father for twenty-two generations. Receive this account of the origin of Cypselus and the manner in which he obtained the kingdom from Herodotus.

When Corinth was governed by an oligarchy inhabited by the Bacchiadae—who would never marry outside of their own family—one of them (named Amphion) had a lame daughter named Labda. When none of the Bacchiadae would take her as a wife, Eetion married her. He was the son of Echestrates of the Betrean tribe, but was distantly descended from the Lapithae and Caenis. Having no children, he consulted the Delphian Oracle about it; as soon as he entered, the priestess spoke to him as follows:

> Eetion, no one honors you, though you deserve great respect;
> Expect a stone from Labda's fruitful womb,
> Which shall crush the people and punish Corinth.

This oracle given to Eetion agreed with another delivered to the Bacchiadae (though they did not understand it at the time) to this effect:

> An eagle shall bring forth a lion
> Upon a rock—fierce and terrifying.
> Corinthians, consider well what I say,
> You who dwell in high Corinth and by Pirene <note>A famous fountain in Corinth</note>.

The Bacchiadae, who could not comprehend the meaning of this oracle until they heard the one given to Eetion, then understood their own through its similarity to the other. Thereupon, they secretly plotted among themselves to kill Eetion’s child. After his wife gave birth, they sent ten of their own men to the tribe where Eetion lived to murder the infant. When they arrived at Petra at Eetion's house, they demanded the child. Labda, not knowing their intent but thinking they came

<sig>A a</sig>
<meta>catchword: to</meta>

<summary>This page introduces Periander, the Tyrant of Corinth, detailing his royal Lydian ancestry and the prophetic warnings from the Delphian Oracle that led the ruling Bacchiadae to plot his father Cypselus's murder in infancy.</summary>
<keywords>Periander, Cypselus, Corinth, Heraclidae, Bacchiadae, Delphian Oracle, Herodotus, Labda, Eetion, Greek mythology, tyrants</keywords>

--- Page 124 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative from Herodotus concerning the birth of Cypselus and the attempts by the Bacchiadae to eliminate him to prevent the fulfillment of the Delphic prophecy.</meta>

<header>PERIANDER.</header>

To congratulate the father, she brought her son and gave him into the hands of one of them. They had agreed, while on their way, that whoever received the child should dash its brains against the ground. However, by divine providence, the child smiled at the man to whom *Labda* had given it. He was moved with such compassion that he could not find it in his heart to kill the child, but instead delivered it to another. That man passed it to a third, until at last it passed through the hands of all ten. Because none of them had the heart to kill it, they restored it to the mother. 

Then, going outside and standing before the door, they began to find fault with one another, especially with the one who first took the child for failing to perform the agreement. After some debate, they agreed to all go back in and be equal partners in the murder. But it was decreed that *Eetion's* child should be the oppressor of *Corinth*. *Labda*, standing at the door, heard their entire conversation. Fearing they might change their minds and murder him, she carried the child away and hid him in a corn-measure (called a *Cypsela*), a place she believed they would never think to search if they returned. 

And so it happened: they came back and searched everywhere. When they could not find him, they agreed among themselves to tell those who had sent them that they had done what was required; upon returning home, they did exactly that. *Eetion's* son grew up and was named *Cypselus* after the danger he had escaped in the corn-measures. When he reached adulthood, he consulted the *Delphian Oracle* and received an ambiguous answer. Confident in this, he attacked *Corinth* and captured it. The Oracle was as follows:

> *Happy is Cypselus, who to my temple <note>original: "fane"</note>*
> *This visit makes; he Corinth's crown shall gain;*
> *He and his sons (but not their grandsons) shall reign.*

Once he possessed the kingdom, he persecuted the *Corinthians*, depriving many of their property and far more of their lives. After reigning for thirty years, he died and was succeeded by his son *Periander*, whose reign completed this period of tyranny. According to *Aristotle*, this era lasted seventy-three years and six months. Thus, *Cypselus* began to reign in the second year of the thirtieth *Olympiad*.

<header>PERIANDER.</header>

### CHAP. II.

->The time of his birth, his reign, and the change of his disposition.<-

<margin>Aristotle, *Politics*, Book 5.</margin>
<margin>Excerpts of Nicolaus of Damascus; Suidas.</margin>
<margin>Plutarch, *The Banquet of the Seven Sages*.</margin>
Periander (calculating from his death, which, according to *Laertius*, was in the eightieth year of his age, during the last year of the forty-eighth Olympiad <note>An Olympiad is a four-year period used in ancient Greek chronology; the 48th Olympiad ended around 585 BCE.</note>) was born in the last year of the twenty-ninth Olympiad. His reign (which, according to *Aristotle’s* account, lasted forty-four years) began in the fourth year of the thirty-seventh Olympiad. *Suidas* says he succeeded to the kingdom as his father’s eldest son—which *Plutarch* calls <unclear>blank</unclear>, a disease hereditary to him <note>likely referring to tyranny as a "hereditary disease"</note>—and flourished in the thirty-eighth Olympiad.

<margin>Diogenes Laertius; Suidas.</margin>
<margin>Parthenius, *Love Romances*, chapter 17.</margin>
He was at first of a mild and gentle disposition, but afterwards he grew very rigid because of this occasion: while he was very young, his mother kept him much in her company, and as he grew older, she fell in love with him. Over time, her passion increased to such an extreme that she could no longer suppress it. Gaining confidence, she told her son that a beautiful lady had fallen in love with him and advised him not to disregard her affection. He answered that he would not transgress against law and virtue by touching a married woman. His mother pressed him with entreaties, and at last he consented. She appointed a night, advising him not to have any light in his chamber and not to force the lady to speak, but to excuse her silence for the sake of modesty. *Periander* committed himself to doing everything she directed. 

She, dressed as richly as she could, went to his chamber and departed again before daybreak. On the next morning, he asked if he had been pleased and if the lady should return to him. *Periander* said it was his greatest desire and that he loved her excessively. From that time on, she visited her son often. At last, he was moved by a great curiosity to know who she was and begged his mother very earnestly that he might have some conversation and acquaintance with her, since he was so taken with love for her. He argued it was unreasonable that he should be denied the sight of someone with whom he had so often been intimate. His mother claimed it could not be done out of respect for the lady’s modesty. 

Because of this, he ordered one of his servants to hide a light in his chamber. She came as she usually did, and when she was asleep, *Periander* rose and took the light. Seeing it was his mother, he was about to kill her, but he was held back by some spirit <term>genius: a guardian spirit or supernatural influence</term> or apparition and stopped. From that time forward, he was troubled in mind, grew cruel, and killed many of his subjects. His mother, blaming her own unhappy fate, killed herself. *Laertius* says they were both aware of this, and

<meta>This page continues the biography of Periander, moving from the secret shame of his incestuous relationship with his mother to his subsequent transformation into a cruel tyrant. ...and once that was discovered, he became cruel to everyone.

<margin>
Herodotus;
Laertius.
</margin>
In the beginning of his reign, he was much more merciful than his father. However, by keeping a correspondence through messengers with *Thrasybulus*, the Tyrant of *Miletus* (to whom he had been a guest in former times, before he came to power), he eventually became much more bloodthirsty than his father. He sent an express messenger to *Thrasybulus* to ask what course he should take to establish himself and how to govern the city in the best way. *Thrasybulus* led the messenger out of town, and as they walked together in a cornfield, he questioned him about his journey from *Corinth*. Meanwhile, he lopped off all the heads of corn that grew higher than the rest and threw them away. In this manner, he went over the whole field without speaking a single word to the messenger, and then sent him home. When the messenger returned, *Periander* greedily inquired what instructions he had brought. He answered that *Thrasybulus* had given him none, and that he wondered why Periander would send him to a madman who destroyed his own property. *Periander* asked exactly what *Thrasybulus* had done and immediately understood that he was advising him to put the most eminent men in the city to death. *Laertius* records a letter to that effect, which, if it is not a forgery, must have been sent at another time after this messenger departed.

### *Thrasybulus to Periander.*

> I gave your messenger no answer, but taking him into a field of corn, I lopped off with my stick such ears as grew higher than the rest while he followed me. If you inquire, he will relate to you all that he has heard or seen. Do the same yourself: if you mean to establish yourself in the government, take off the heads of the most prominent citizens, whether they are your professed enemies or others. A tyrant must suspect every friend.

<margin>Plutarch, *The Banquet of the Seven Sages*.</margin>
Though *Plutarch denies that he followed this advice, *Herodotus* asserts that from that time forward, *Periander* exercised every kind of cruelty upon his subjects, dispatching those who had escaped the severity and persecutions of his father. <margin>Laertius; Suidas.</margin> *He was the first to appoint a guard of halberdiers <note>Soldiers armed with halberds—a combined spear and battleaxe</note> to secure his person,* which consisted of three hundred men, and he converted the government into a tyranny <margin>Excerpts from Nicolaus of Damascus, and Suidas following him.</margin> (through his cruelty and violence). He forbade the citizens from keeping any servants or being idle, always finding some employment for them. If any man sat in the Forum <note>The marketplace or public square</note>, he was fined, for Periander feared they might plot against him. <margin>Laertius.</margin> *Though the citizens desired to live <unclear>at ease</unclear>, he would not allow them.* <margin>Suidas.</margin> *He was always at war, being of a military disposition. He built ships with three banks of oars <note>triremes</note>, which he used in both seas <note>the Ionian and Aegean seas</note>. <margin>Laertius.</margin> *He attempted to dig through the Isthmus <note>The Isthmus of Corinth</note> to separate it from the continent.*

<margin>Herodotus, Book 1, chapter 20.</margin>
*Herodotus* provides another instance of his friendship and correspondence with *Thrasybulus*, affirming that he sent word to inform him of

of

It also details his oppressive laws against idleness and his military and engineering ambitions, such as building triremes and attempting to cut a canal through the Isthmus of Corinth.</summary>

### CHAP. III.

->*On his being included among the Seven Sages.*<-
->*His maxims and writings.*<-

<margin>*The Banquet of the Seven Sages* <note>original: "Sept. sap. conv."</note></margin>
<margin>Diogenes Laertius</margin>
Periander (says * *Plutarch*), having become a tyrant through a hereditary disease passed down from his father, tried to cleanse himself of it as much as possible by associating with virtuous people and inviting wise men to visit him. To this end, he sent this letter to the wise men of *Greece* while they were meeting at *Delphi*.

->**Periander to the wise men.**<-

> *I give many thanks to Pythian Apollo that, since you are meeting together, my letters will also be brought to Corinth. I will entertain you very kindly, as you well know. I hear that last year you met at the Lydian King’s court in Sardis; do not delay coming to me now, the Tyrant of Corinth, for the Corinthians will look upon you with favor if you come to the house of Periander.*

Upon this invitation they went to him—not just seven, but twice as many. Among them was *Diocles*, *Periander’s* friend, in whose name *Plutarch* provides a long description of their entertainment. This was held not in the city, but at the port of *Lechaeum*, in a great hall intended for solemn feasts, next to the Temple of *Venus* <note>The Roman name for the Greek goddess Aphrodite</note>. He had not sacrificed to her since his mother's tragic death until that time. Because the account is so long, we refer the reader to *Plutarch* for the details of the feast.

<margin>*On the 'E' at Delphi* <note>original: "De ei Delph."</note></margin>
He himself was also included among these wise men. *Plutarch* says there were originally only five, but later *Cleobulus*, the tyrant of *Lindus*, and *Periander*, the tyrant of *Corinth*—who possessed neither virtue nor wisdom—used their great power, their many friends, and the favors they granted their supporters to force a reputation for themselves. They violently inserted themselves into the stolen title of "wise men." To achieve this, they spread maxims and famous sayings throughout all *Greece*...

<summary>This page describes Periander's efforts to associate with the Seven Sages of Greece to improve his reputation, despite Plutarch’s criticism that he and Cleobulus unworthily bought their way into the group. It also relates an anecdote about his vow to build a golden statue and his invitation to the Sages to feast at Lechaeum.</summary>
<keywords>Periander, Seven Sages, Plutarch, Corinth, Alyattes, Minerva, Lechaeum, Cleobulus, Lindus, Ancient Greece</keywords>

--- Page 128 ---
<meta>Continuing the account of Periander's inclusion among the Seven Sages and the dispute over his legitimacy as one of their number.</meta>

<header>PERIANDER:</header>

...which others had already said before. At this, the other original sages were much displeased, yet they would not expose or challenge their vanity, nor have any public controversy regarding that title with persons of such great wealth and power. Instead, meeting together at *Delphi* after some private debate, they consecrated the letter E (the fifth in the alphabet and in numbering) to testify to the God of that Temple that they were no more than five, and that they rejected and excluded the sixth and the seventh from their company as having no right to the title.

<margin>Suidas; Protagoras; Diogenes Laertius</margin>
Of those who excluded him from the number of the seven, some (such as *Plato*) substitute *Myson* in his place. Others say there were two men of this name who were cousins: one the Tyrant, the other from *Ambracia*. However, *Aristotle* and others assert that Periander of *Corinth* was the wise one. This title seems to have been conferred upon him not in respect to his actions, but for his moral sayings and writings, which were these:

*Do nothing for gain, for that is characteristic of tradespeople. Those who wish to rule safely must be guarded by love, not by arms. When asked why he remained King, he replied, "Because it is dangerous to step down voluntarily, or to be deposed involuntarily."*

<margin>Plutarch, *The Banquet of the Seven Sages* <note>original: "Sept. sap. conv."</note></margin>
When the other six had given their opinions concerning Tyranny (at the feast to which he invited them), he was asked to add his own. He answered with a troubled countenance, *“Enough has been said to deter any man of sound judgment from ruling.”* When they had in like manner declared their opinions concerning a commonwealth, he added that, as a result of everything that had been said, he most commended that democracy which came nearest to an aristocracy.

<margin>Stobaeus, 28</margin>
When asked what was the greatest thing in the smallest space, he answered, *"A good mind in a human body."*

<margin>Stobaeus, ibid.; Diogenes Laertius</margin>
His precepts (according to *Demetrius Phalereus*) were these: *Deliberation is everything* (which was his particular motto). *Quiet is good; recklessness is dangerous. Sordid gain is a reproach to nature. A democracy is better than a tyranny. Pleasures are mortal; virtues are immortal. In good fortune be moderate; in bad fortune be prudent. It is better to die than to be in want. Study to be worthy of your parents. Be praised while living and blessed after death. Be the same to your friends in both prosperity and adversity. What you have wrongly promised, do not perform. [Diogenes Laertius: Keep your word.] Do not betray secrets. Reproach others in such a way as if you might soon become their friend again. Use a new diet, but old laws. Punish those who have sinned; restrain those who are about to sin. Conceal your misfortune so that it does not gladden your enemies.*

*Ausonius* ascribes these verses to him:

> *Pleasure and profit never disagree.*
> *As you are more diligent, so shall you more happy be.*
> *It is bad to wish for death, but worse to fear it;*
> *With what necessity dictates, comply and bear it.*
> *If you are feared by many, you must fear many.*

<meta>catchword: Be</meta>

<header>PERIANDER</header>

->Be not exalted when your fortunes are clear,<-
->Nor be dejected if a storm appear.<-

<margin>* *Diogenes Laertius*</margin>
* He wrote two thousand verses of moral instructions.

## CHAP. III.

### The Story of Arion.

<margin>* Book 1. See also Aulus Gellius, who translates this account from Plutarch, Lucian, and others.</margin>
**D**uring the feast we mentioned (by *Plutarch's* account) or rather, according to *Eusebius*, in the 40th *Olympiad* <note>The 40th Olympiad occurred around 620–617 BC.</note>, there happened a strange accident which we shall relate in his own words, as *Herodotus* calls it a miracle shown to *Periander*.

*Arion*, the most famous lute player <term>Lutenist</term> of that time, having lived a long time with *Periander*, took a voyage to *Italy* and *Sicily*. Having gathered great wealth there, he intended to return to *Corinth*. At *Tarentum*, he hired a *Corinthian* vessel, trusting the *Corinthians* above all others. When they were at sea, the sailors plotted to throw *Arion* overboard so they might become masters of his wealth. Understanding this, *Arion* offered to give them everything if they would only spare his life. They refused and ordered him to either take his own life—if he wished to be buried in his own country—or to leap immediately into the sea. 

Reduced to this extremity, *Arion* entreated them to give him leave to put on his richest robes and stand upon the stern <term>poop</term> of the ship to play a tune, promising that as soon as he had finished, he would surrender himself into their hands. Moved by a great desire to hear the most excellent lute player in the world, the men retired from the stern to the middle of the ship. He put on his best ornaments and, standing upon the stern, began the tune they call the "morning hymn." As soon as he had ended it, he threw himself into the sea with his robes and lute, and the ship sailed on to *Corinth*. 

It is reported that a dolphin took him upon its back and carried him to *Taenarum* <note>Modern Cape Matapan</note>, where he landed and took ship again for *Corinth*. He arrived there in the same clothing and recounted all that had passed. *Periander*, not believing him, held him in close custody and did not permit him to go anywhere; in the meantime, he sent for the sailors. When they arrived, he asked them for news of *Arion*. They answered that he was doing very well in *Italy* and that they had left him safe at *Tarentum*. Immediately, *Arion* appeared, dressed as he was when he leaped out of the ship, at which they were so confounded they could not say anything in their own defense. This story is attested to by both the *Corinthians* and the *Lesbians*. At *Taenarum*, there is a small statue—a votive offering—of a man sitting upon a dolphin's back. That *Periander* caused such a statue to be made is evident from this epigram by *Bianor*.

<summary>This page recounts the legendary story of the musician Arion, who was saved by a dolphin after being forced overboard by mutinous sailors, and how Periander later exposed the sailors' lies.</summary>

## CHAP. V.

### Of his Wife.

<margin>
* Diogenes Laertius.
* Athenaeus, *Deipnosophistae*.
* Diogenes Laertius.
</margin>
His wife was named *Lysis*, whom he called *Melissa*. She was the daughter of *Procleus*, the Tyrant of *Epidaurus*, and *Eristhenea*, who was the daughter of *Aristocrates* and the sister of *Aristomedes*; these individuals ruled over the greatest part of *Arcadia*. * He fell in love with her after seeing her in Peloponnesian dress—wearing only her petticoat without a gown—as she was giving drink to her father’s workmen. * Long after, in a fit of fury, he killed her while she was pregnant by hitting her with a stool or kicking her. He was driven to this by the false accusations of his concubines, whom he afterwards burned alive.

<margin>
* Herodotus, Book 5.
* Suidas.
</margin>
* One day, he sent messengers to *Thesprotia* on the River *Acheron* to consult an oracle of the dead <term>Necromancy</term> concerning a hidden treasure <term>depositum</term>. *Melissa’s* ghost appeared but said she would not reveal where it was hidden because she was cold and naked. She claimed the clothes she was buried in were of no use to her because they had not been burned. She proved she was telling the truth by mentioning that *Periander* had "put his bread into a cold oven." When this answer was brought back to *Periander*, it confirmed the suspicion that (out of an excess of lust) <note>original: "νεκρῷ εἶχε Μελίσσῃ μίγνυσθαι" — he had committed necrophilia with the dead Melissa.</note>. 

Thereupon, he issued a proclamation that all the Corinthian women should come to the Temple of *Juno* to celebrate a festival, dressed in their richest ornaments. When they arrived, he placed a guard of soldiers in ambush and stripped them all of their clothes without distinction, whether they were free women or servants. He carried the clothes to *Melissa’s* grave and, after offering prayers, burned them in her honor. Once this was done, he sent messengers to inquire a second time; *Melissa’s* ghost appeared again and told them exactly where the treasure was laid.

## CHAP. VI.

### Of his Children.

<margin>* Herodotus, Book 3. 49.</margin>
By *Melissa*, he had two sons, *Cypsalus* and *Lycophron*. The younger was clever, while the elder was a fool. He also had a daughter. At the time of their mother’s death, the elder son was eighteen years old and the younger was seventeen. Their maternal grandfather, *Procleus* (the Tyrant of *Epidaurus*), sent for them to stay with him. He loved them dearly, as was natural for his own daughter’s children. When it was time for him to send them...

<meta>Continuing to the next page with the word: them</meta>

<summary>This page describes Periander’s brutal domestic life, including the murder of his wife Melissa, his act of necrophilia, the ritual stripping of Corinthian women to appease her ghost, and the introduction of his two sons.</summary>
<keywords>Periander, Melissa, Procleus, necrophilia, Acheron, Juno's Temple, Corinth, Cypsalus, Lycophron, Epidaurus</keywords>

--- Page 131 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Periander, moving from the death of his wife Melissa to the subsequent conflict with his sons and his father-in-law, Procleus.</meta>

<header>PERIANDER.</header>

As he sent them back, Procleus said to them, "Children, do you know who killed your mother?" The elder son paid no attention to this speech, but *Lycophron*, the younger, was so troubled by it that when he returned to *Corinth*, he would neither speak to his father nor give him any answer. He viewed his father as the murderer of his mother. Because of this, *Periander* eventually became so enraged that he threw him out of the house. 

Once he was gone, *Periander* questioned the elder son about what their grandfather had said to them. He recounted how kindly Procleus had treated them but mentioned nothing of what he had said at their departure, for he had not taken any notice of it. *Periander* insisted it was impossible that he had not said more and pressed him more strictly. At last, the elder son remembered and told him this as well. *Periander*, taking great offense and being unwilling to treat his son with any leniency, sent word to the people with whom his son lived during his exile, forbidding them to receive him into any of their houses. 

Turned out of the house where he had been staying, *Lycophron* sought to go into another, but was denied. *Periander* had threatened those who might host him and commanded everyone to drive him away. Expelled from there, he went to another of his acquaintances, who, knowing he was the son of *Periander*, took him in, though with great fear. Eventually, *Periander* proclaimed that whoever received him into their house or even spoke to him should pay a fine to *Apollo* <note>The fine was a religious penalty to be paid to the temple of Apollo</note> that he would impose. From that time on, no one dared to risk hosting him or speaking to him. Nor would *Lycophron* himself attempt to try something he knew was hopeless; instead, he spent his time in the public walkways. Four days later, *Periander*, seeing him impoverished and wasted away <term>extenuated</term> from fasting, took compassion on him. Laying aside his anger, he approached him and said:

> "Son, which is better: to undergo what you now suffer, or, by obeying your father, to enjoy my wealth and kingdom? You, being my son and the next heir to the kingdom of prosperous *Corinth*, have chosen the life of a vagabond by angrily opposing the person you ought not to oppose. If any misfortune occurred in those matters you suspect me of, it happened to me, and I bear the greater share of the burden, as I was the instrument of it. Learn how much better it is to be envied than to be pitied, and what it is to be angry with your parents or those above you."

In these words, *Periander* rebuked his son, who gave him no other answer than:

> "That he [Periander] ought to pay a fine to the god for speaking to him."

*Periander*, perceiving that his son’s resentment was incurable, removed him from his sight. He sent him by ship to *Corcyra* <note>Modern Corfu</note>, over which he was also the Tyrant. Having disposed of him in this way, he went to war with his father-in-law, *Procleus*, viewing him as the chief cause of all that had happened. *Laertius* mentions a letter which he sent to him to this effect.

<summary>This page describes the falling out between Periander and his son Lycophron, who refuses to reconcile after learning of his mother's murder. Periander eventually exiles his son to Corcyra and declares war on his father-in-law, Procleus.</summary>

<header>PERIANDER.</header>

## Periander to Procleus.

We committed that crime against your daughter unwillingly; but if you are willfully turning my son's mind against me, you are acting unjustly. Therefore, either soften his heart toward me, or I shall take revenge for this injury. I have already satisfied your daughter’s honor by burning the garments of all the women of *Corinth* in her memory. <note>Herodotus records that Periander stripped the women of Corinth and burned their clothes to appease the ghost of his dead wife, Melissa.</note>

<margin>* Herodotus, ibid.</margin>
* In the end, he captured *Epidaurus* and took *Proclus* prisoner there, though he kept him alive.

<margin>* Herodotus, ibid.</margin>
* As time passed and *Periander* grew old, knowing himself to be no longer fit for the burdens of the commonwealth, he sent a message to *Corcyra* to invite *Lycophron* to take over the government of the kingdom. He believed his eldest son was incapable of that office because of his lack of intelligence <term>stupidity</term>. *Lycophron* would not even deign to speak to the messenger. *Periander* (still feeling affection for him) sent a second time, choosing his son’s sister—his own daughter—hoping he would be more easily persuaded by her. When she arrived, she said to him:

> "Brother, would you rather the kingdom fall into the hands of strangers and our father's house be destroyed than return home and rule it yourself? Return to your own house and stop harming yourself. Obstinacy is a miserable inheritance. Do not try to cure one evil with another. Many people prefer compliance over strict justice; many, in pursuit of their mother's rights, have lost their father's kingdom. A kingdom is a precarious <term>slippery</term> thing, desired by many, and our father is now old and feeble. Do not let your own inheritance go to others."

Thus she pleaded with him, just as her father had instructed her. He replied that he would never come to *Corinth* as long as his father lived there. As soon as *Periander* understood this, he sent a messenger a third time to let him know that he himself would move to *Corcyra*, and commanded his son to come to *Corinth* to take the government upon himself. To this proposal, the son agreed. *Periander* prepared to leave for *Corcyra*, and his son prepared for *Corinth*. However, when the people of *Corcyra* were informed of this, they killed the son so that *Periander* might not come into their country. In revenge for this deed, *Periander* sent three hundred boys from the leading families of the *Corcyreans* to *Sardis*, to be delivered to *Alyates*, King of *Lydia*, to be castrated <term>guelt</term>. The *Corinthians* who had charge of them were driven by the winds to *Samos*. When the *Samians* understood why the boys were being sent to *Sardis*, they advised them to take sanctuary in the Temple of *Diana* and would not allow them to be dragged away, as they were now legal suppliants. When the *Corinthians* refused to let any food be given to them, the *Samians* established a festival, which is still observed today (says *Herodotus*). When night came, groups of young men and women danced while the children prayed; during their dance, they carried cakes made of meal and honey and threw them among the children so they could be kept alive. They continued this until the *Samians* who had charge of the children...

...children, were forced <term>fain</term> to go away and leave them; then the *Samians* conveyed the children home to *Corcyra*. * *Antenor* and *Dionysius* affirm that the *Gnidians* came to *Samos* with a fleet, drove away *Periander's* guard from the Temple, and carried the children to *Corcyra*. For this reason, the *Corcyraeans* granted the *Gnidians* many honors and privileges <term>immunities</term>, which they did not give even to the *Samians*.

## CHAP. 7.

### His Death.

<margin>* Diogenes Laertius <note>original: "Laert."</note></margin>
Excessive melancholy (in the midst of these misfortunes) caused his death in the last year of the 48th Olympiad, during the eightieth year of his age. Because he desired that no one should know where he was buried, he contrived it this way: He commanded two men to go to a certain place at night, kill the first person they met, and bury him. After them, he sent four more to kill and bury those two; after the four, he sent even more. They obeyed his order, and the first pair killed him. <note>By placing himself in the path of the first two men, Periander ensured his own death and the subsequent elimination of witnesses to keep his burial site secret.</note> The *Corinthians* erected for him an empty monument <term>cenotaph</term> with this inscription:

> *Periander lies within Corinthian ground,*
> *Renowned above all for power and wisdom.*

*Laertius* wrote this *epigram* upon him:

> *Do not be sad at whatever shall happen;*
> *Be equally glad for all that God provides.*
> *Wise Periander died from grief,*
> *Because events did not align with his desire.*

## The precepts of the seven Sophists:

<note>In this context, "Sophists" refers to the Seven Sages of Greece; the term originally meant "wise men" before it became associated with professional rhetoricians.</note>

*Follow God. Obey the law. Worship the Gods. Revere your parents. Suffer for the sake of justice. Understand what you learn. Know what you hear. Know yourself. When about to marry, choose the right opportunity. Consider mortal things. When you are a guest, acknowledge it. Respect hospitality. Control yourself. Support your friends. Govern your anger. Exercise prudence. Honor providence. Do not swear. Love friendship. Apply yourself to discipline. Pursue glory. Emulate wisdom. Speak well of that which is good. Disparage no one. Praise virtue. Do what is just. Be kind to your friends. Avenge yourself on your enemies. Practice generosity. Abstain from evil. Be public-spirited. <note>original: "Be generall." In this context, it means being communal or approachable to all people.</note> Keep what is yours. Refrain from what belongs to others. Speak words of good omen. Hear all things. Gratify your friend. Nothing in excess. Manage your time wisely. <note>original: "Husband time."</note> Regard the future. Hate injury. Have respect for your servants. Instruct your children. If you have anything, give to others. Fear deceit. Speak well of everyone. Be a lover of wisdom. Judge according to equity. What you know, do. Abstain from bloodshed. Wish for things that are possible. Converse with your wife. Examine characters. <note>original: "wits."</note> What you have received, restore. Distrust no one. Make use of skill. Do not delay what you intend to give. Value benefits. Envy no one. Guard yourself. Approve of hope. Hate slander. Possess things justly. Revere the good. Acknowledge your judge. Be moderate in marriage. Respect fortune. Avoid making financial pledges for others. <note>original: "Fly engagements for any." This refers to the ancient Greek warning against acting as a legal guarantor or surety for someone else's debt.</note> Converse with everyone. Associate with those like yourself. Regulate luxury. Enjoy what you possess. Exercise modesty. Return benefits. Pray for prosperity. Love fortune. In hearing, see. Aim for things that can be achieved. Hate dissension. Abhor reproach. Curb your tongue. Repel injury. Judge impartially. Make use of your wealth. Examine without corruption. Criticize the present. <note>original: "Blame the present." This likely refers to a philosophical stance of not being complacent with current conditions.</note> Speak with knowledge. Use no violence. Live pleasantly. Converse mildly. Carry out your undertakings without fear. Be kind to everyone. Do not confide in your children. Govern your tongue. Do well for yourself. Be affable. Answer at the right time. Labor with fairness. Do that which you will not regret. When you have sinned, be penitent. Control your eyes. Give profitable counsel. Complete things quickly. Preserve friendship. Be grateful. Observe harmony. Reveal no secrets. Fear what is more powerful. Pursue what is profitable. Wait for opportunity. Dissolve enmities. Anticipate old age. Do not boast of your strength. Accustom yourself to speak well. Shun hatred.*

These precepts offer a concise guide to ancient Greek ethics, covering religious duty, social conduct, and personal discipline.</summary>

Possess wealth justly. Do not forsake glory. Hate malice. Do not grow weary of learning. Take risks prudently. Do not abandon what you take joy in. Admire oracles. Love those you support. Do not blame the absent. Revere your elders. Teach those who are younger. Do not trust in wealth. Stand in awe of yourself. Initiate no injury. Honor your ancestors. Die for your country. Endure as a mortal. Do not trust in fortune. In childhood be modest, in youth temperate, in manhood just, and in old age prudent.

--- Page 137 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the list of philosophical precepts by Sosiades to the beginning of Decimus Magnus Ausonius's "Ludus Septem Sapientum" (The Play of the Seven Sages), a fourth-century theatrical work that brings the Greek sages onto a Roman stage.</meta>

(103)

# THE PLAY OF AUSONIUS
<note>original: "Ausonii Ludus"</note>
## of the Seven Sages.

### *The Prologue.*

The Seven Sages (a name applied to them in times past, which posterity has never denied) present themselves before you this day. Why do you blush, toga-wearing Roman? Are you discontented that such grave men should be brought upon the stage? Is it a shame to us? It was not thought so in *Athens*, whose council chamber was their theater. 

True, here in Rome, several places are assigned for business: the Circus for meetings, courts for legal enrollments, and forums in which to make pleas. But in old *Athens* and throughout all *Greece*, no other place for business was known but this one <note>namely, the theater</note>. It was only later luxury that raised such structures in *Rome*. In the past, the Aedile <term>Aedile: a Roman magistrate responsible for public buildings, markets, and the organization of festivals</term> used to build a temporary scaffold-stage for plays, not a structure of carved stone; so it was with *Gallius* and *Murena*, as is well known. 

But later, when great men spared no cost—thinking it the highest glory they could boast to build a more eminent setting for plays—the theater grew to this vast extent, which *Pompey*, *Balbus*, and *Caesar* enlarged, vying with one another to see who could exceed in grandeur and expense. 

But to what end is all this? We did not come here to tell you who first built the theater, the forum, or the city gates, but to usher in grave sages who have been approved by the gods. Such men as these, in pleasing and instructive verse, rehearse their own judicious maxims—known to the learned, and perhaps to you as well. But if your memories cannot easily recall things spoken so long ago, the *comedian* shall tell you all, for he knows them better than I do.

<meta>catchword: Enter</meta>

<summary>This page serves as the prologue to Ausonius's play, defending the presentation of the Seven Sages on a theatrical stage by comparing Greek and Roman civic customs and architectural history.</summary>
<keywords>Ausonius, Seven Sages, theater history, Rome vs Athens, Aedile, Pompey, Balbus, Caesar, Greek philosophy, prologue</keywords>

--- Page 138 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to Ausonius's play, moving from the general prologue to a Comedian introducing the Seven Sages and their famous maxims, followed by the entrance of Solon of Athens.</meta>

( 104 )
<meta>catchword: Croesus</meta>

### Enter Comedian.

Athenian *Solon*, as Fame sings, wrote at *Delphi*:
"Know thyself" <note>original: "Γνῶθι σεαυτὸν"</note>; for that is what it means.
But most people assign this to *Spartan Chilon*.
Some question whether this one is yours, *Chilon*:
"Behold the end of a long life" <note>original: "Τελὸς ὁρᾷν μακρῦ βίου"</note>;
Regard the close of a long life. But most suppose
That *Solon* spoke this to *Lydian Croesus*.
Take this motto from *Lesbian Pittacus*:
"Know the time" <note>original: "Γίγνωσκε καιρὸν"</note>; that is, "Know the time."
But by "time," he means "opportunity."
"Most men are bad" <note>original: "Οἱ πλεῖστοι κακοὶ"</note>, *Bias*, came
From you; that is, "Most men are evil." Take heed
That you do not mistake him; for by "evil men" here,
He means the ignorant. The next you hear
Is *Periander's* "Practice is everything" <note>original: "Μελέτη τὸ πᾶν"</note>;
That is, "Care is all in all"—a thoughtful man!
But *Lindian Cleobulus* protests:
"Moderation is best" <note>original: "Ἄριστον Μέτρον"</note>; the middle path in all is best.
*Thales* cries, "Give a pledge, and ruin is near" <note>original: "Ἐγγύα, πάρα δ' ἄτη"</note>.
"Upon a guarantee, immediate damage lies."
But to say this might perhaps displease
Those who profit from it. Now *Solon* comes; farewell. *Exit*.

### Enter Solon.

Look! *Solon* in his *Greek* dress treads the stage,
To whom (as the greatest of the seven Sages)
Fame gave the prize of wisdom above the rest;
But fame is not the strict test of judgment.
I do not consider myself either first or last,
For there is no hierarchy in equality.
The *Delphic Prophet* joked well with the man
Who asked which of the *Wise Men* might seem to be first,
Saying that if he wrote their names upon a globe,
He would find none first or lowest upon it.
I come from the middle of that learned circle so that
What I once spoke to *Croesus*, everyone here now
Might take as spoken to themselves; it is this:
"Behold the end of a long life" <note>original: "Ὁρᾷν Τελὸς μακρὸῦ βίου"</note>, which means:
"Mark the end of a long life." Until then, refrain
From saying these people are wretched or those are happy;
For everyone is in a doubtful state until then.
We will relate the proof of this in a few words.

<summary>A Comedian recites the signature maxims of the Seven Sages of Greece before Solon enters to explain his famous philosophy: that no man can be judged happy until his life has ended.</summary>
<keywords>Solon, Chilon, Pittacus, Bias, Periander, Cleobulus, Thales, Seven Sages, Croesus, Delphi, Greek maxims, Gnothi seauton, philosophy, drama</keywords>

--- Page 139 ---
<meta>This page continues Solon's speech from the previous page, where he was introduced as one of the Seven Sages. He now recounts his famous interaction with King Croesus of Lydia.</meta>

( 105 )

King *Croesus*, or tyrant (choose you which)
Of *Lydia*, thought to be happy and incredibly rich,
Who built gold-walled temples to his gods,
Invites me over; I yield to his summons.
His royal summons called me to *Lydia*,
Desiring that through me, his subjects might find
Their King improved and bettered in his mind.
He asked me whom I thought the happiest man?
I said *Tellus* the Athenian,
Who nobly gave his life for his country;
He scoffs at that and demands another.
I then told him of *Aglaus*, who never in his whole life
Passed beyond the borders of his own grounds.
Smiling, he says, "What do you think of me then,
Who am esteemed the happiest in the whole world?" I
Replied that only his end could make that known.
He took this poorly; I, wishing to be gone,
Kissed his hand and so left him. In the meantime,
For certain goals, he intended a war against *Persia*;
And with all things ready, he went in person.
How did he fare? He was defeated, a prisoner to his foe,
And was ready now to draw his last breath
(For he was doomed to death by the victor).
Upon the funeral pyre, surrounded by flames
And smoke, he cried out with a loud voice:
"**O *Solon! Solon!*** now I plainly see
That you are a true prophet!" Calling my name three times like this
Moved *Cyrus* (the conqueror), who
Commanded the fire be quenched; fortunately, it was extinguished
By a shower of rain falling then.
From there, he was conveyed to the King by a select guard
And questioned as to who that *Solon* was, and why
He called out his name so. In reply,
He explained everything in order. At this, pity
Moved the heart of *Cyrus*; and *Croesus* was
Received into favor, and lived afterward
In a princely manner, honored in the *Persian* court.
Both kings approved and praised me; but what I
Said then to one, let every man here apply
To himself—it was for that purpose I came.
Farewell: let your hands proclaim your approval <note>meaning: applause</note>. [*Exit.*

<sig>Ee</sig>
<meta>catchword: Enter</meta>

<summary>Solon recounts his visit to King Croesus, explaining how he cautioned the wealthy monarch that no man is truly happy until his death. This wisdom is proven true when Croesus is defeated by Cyrus and narrowly escapes execution, ultimately earning a place of honor in the Persian court.</summary>
<keywords>Solon, Croesus, Cyrus, Lydia, Persia, happiness, wisdom, Tellus, Aglaus, Seven Sages, funeral pyre, irony of fate</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the presentation of the Seven Sages of Greece. Following Solon’s departure, Chilon, Cleobulus, and Thales enter to offer their own philosophical maxims and brief histories.</meta>

( 106 )

<warning>The page has significant foxing and ink spots throughout, occasionally obscuring small portions of the text.</warning>

->Enter Chilon.<-

**M**y hips ache from sitting, and my eyes from watching,
Waiting for *Solon* to reach an end.
How little you Athenians say, yet how long you chatter!
Scarcely one word of weight in three hundred lines,
Or a serious thought—how did he look at me
As he left? Now let Spartan *Chilon* speak,
Who with Spartan brevity commends
To you the knowledge of yourselves, kind friends!
<gloss>Know thyself <note>original: "Γνῶθι σεαυτὸν"</note></gloss>, carved in a temple at *Delphi*.
It is a hard task, but rewarded with gain.
Test your own strengths; examine what you
Have done already, and what you ought to do.
All duties of our life, such as modesty,
Honor, and constancy are included
In this, as well as glory, which we still despise.
Farewell; I neither respect nor prize your applause. [Exit.

->Enter Cleobulus.<-

**I**, *Cleobulus*, though my birthplace
Is a small island, am the author of a great
And glorious sentence: <gloss>Moderation is best <note>original: "Μέτρον ἄριστον"</note></gloss>.
A middle path is best. You sirs, who sit upon
The fourteen middle benches next to
The orchestra <note>In Roman theaters, the first fourteen rows were reserved for the equestrian order; Cleobulus uses this as a metaphor for the "middle way."</note>, may best judge if this is true.
Your nodding shows your agreement; we thank you. But
We shall proceed in order: was it not
One *Afer* <note>Referring to the Roman playwright Terence (Publius Terentius Afer).</note> (who is a man of your own region)
That once said in this place, *Nothing in excess* <note>original Latin: "Ut ne quid Nimis"</note>?
And our <gloss>Moderation <note>original: "Μέτρον"</note></gloss> aims for the same.
The Greek and the Latin mean the same thing.
In speaking or being silent, or in sleep,
In good deeds or in bad, always keep a middle path;
In study, or whatever you intend.
I have spoken; and as I mean to keep to that, here I end. [Exit.

->Enter Thales.<-

**I** am *Thales*, who argues (as *Pindar* sings)
That water is the best and first of all things.
To whom, by *Apollo's* <note>original: "Phœbus"</note> mandate, fishermen brought
A golden tripod which they caught while fishing;
It was intended by him as a gift for the wisest man,
Which I refused, and sent to others—

<meta>catchword: In</meta>
