<meta>This page continues the presentation of the Seven Sages of Greece. Following the speeches of Chilon and Cleobulus on the previous page, Thales of Miletus concludes his address before Bias of Priene and Pittacus of Mytilene take the stage to present their own famous maxims.</meta>

( 107 )

<warning>Significant ink bleed-through from the reverse side is visible on the right half of the page.</warning>

I thought they were my superiors in knowledge. The golden tripod was brought from one Sage to another, and then returned by them back to me; I now consecrate it to *Apollo*. Since he commanded us to seek the wisest man, I judge that he meant no man, but God himself by that design. Now I come onto the stage (like those before me) to assert the truth of my own axiom. Perhaps some may find it offensive, but not those who have been taught by sad experience. We say, <gloss>Guarantee a loan, and ruin is near <note>original: "Ἐγγύα πάρεστι δ' ἄτη"</note></gloss>. Act as a guarantor for someone else's debt, and you are sure to be a loser. I could produce a thousand instances to prove that the only thing to be gained from it is regret, but we will refrain from citing examples by name here. Apply this to yourselves, and imagine the damage men receive by this single act. Do not take our good intentions the wrong way: those of you who like it, clap; those who dislike it, hiss.

->Enter *Bias*.<-

I am *Bias of Priene*, who once taught that <gloss>most men are bad <note>original: "Οἱ πλείους κακοὶ"</note></gloss>. I wish it had remained unsaid, for truth breeds hatred. But by "bad men," I mean the uneducated, and those who barbarously disregard all laws, religion, and justice. Within this circle, I see none but those who are good. You may consider all those whom I proclaim as bad to be among your enemies. Yet no one is so biased toward vice that they would not side with the good—whether they truly are such, or merely want to gain the reputation of a good man. Everyone shuns the hated name of a "bad man." Therefore (most excellent men), give your praise, and I am finished.

->Enter *Pittacus*.<-

I am *Pittacus*, who once penned this maxim: <gloss>know your opportunity <note>original: "Γίγνωσκε καιρὸν"</note></gloss>; that is, seize the right moment. But by "Time," we meant the right season, just as <gloss>I arrived at the right time <note>original: "In tempore veni"</note></gloss> is your Roman phrase. And your own comic poet *Terence* makes opportunity the most important of all things, in the scene where *Dromo* goes to *Antiphila* <note>A reference to Terence's play "The Self-Tormentor" (Heauton Timorumenos), where timing and opportunity are central themes.</note>.

<summary>Thales concludes the story of the golden tripod and warns against financial guarantees, followed by Bias and Pittacus, who present their famous maxims on the nature of humanity and the importance of timing.</summary>
<keywords>Thales, Bias of Priene, Pittacus, Seven Sages, golden tripod, Apollo, financial surety, Terence, opportunity, Greek philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 142 ---
<meta>This page continues the presentation of the Seven Sages of Greece. Following the departure of a previous speaker (likely Bias or Thales), Periander takes the stage to present his own philosophical maxim.</meta>

( 108 )

Just in the nick of time; consider what I say,
And notice how great a disadvantage
Most suffer through this lack of foresight.
But now it is more than time we should be gone;
Farewell, and give your approval. [Exit.

->Enter *Periander*.<-

Now see *Periander* move onto the stage!
He who once said—and what he said, he will prove—
*Practice is everything* <note>original: "Μελέτη τὸ πᾶν"</note>. <gloss>Thought is all in all.</gloss>
Since we may call him a perfect actor
Who first considers what he undertakes;
For we should always forecast, as *Terence* <note>Publius Terentius Afer, a famous Roman playwright.</note> shows,
The outcome of business, whether good or bad,
Before we undertake it: where the best location
For planting may be had, where to build,
When to wage war, and when to set up a battlefield.
Nor should we thoughtlessly take in hand
Either great or small things, for that causes a standstill
In the free progress of all new designs;
In these matters, there is nothing that political wisdom requires
Like consultation; hence we see it clearly,
Those who do not use it steer by chance, not by counsel.
But I will retire, while you, with better fortune,
Employ your thoughts on how to uphold your state:

# ANACHARSIS.

## CHAP. I.

### *The life and writings of Anacharsis.*

<margin>
* Herodotus, Book 4. 46.
* Strabo, Book 7.
</margin>
Those nations (says Herodotus) which border upon the **Euxine Sea** <note>The Black Sea</note> are the most illiterate of all, the **Scythians** being the only exception. We can allege nothing relating to the learning of any people within the region of that sea, nor do we know of any learned person there except the Scythian **Anacharsis**. Among these people, despite the roughness of their education (for they fed upon mare's milk and lived in wagons), there were some who far exceeded all others in justice.

<margin>
* Diogenes Laertius.
* Plutarch, The Banquet of the Seven Sages.
</margin>
Such a man was **Anacharsis**, the son of **Gnurus** and brother of **Kadouidas**, King of **Scythia**. His mother was a **Grecian**, by which means he had the advantage of knowing two languages. However, he owned no house other than what the custom of that country allowed: a chariot. For this reason, he compared his dwelling to that of the sun, which is carried in such a manner around the heavens.

<margin>
* Aelian, Various History, Book 5.
* Herodotus, 4. 46.
* Diogenes Laertius.
* Lucian, The Scythian.
</margin>
The **Scythians** never travel beyond their own borders, but **Anacharsis**, as a person endowed with more than ordinary wisdom, extended his journey further. Being sent by the King of **Scythia** to **Greece**, he came to **Athens** in the first year of the 47th Olympiad <note>Approximately 592 BC</note>, while **Eucrates** was Archon. He first met with **Toxaris**, one of his own countrymen, who, as the most direct way to survey **Athens** and **Greece**, introduced him to **Solon**. How **Solon** received and entertained him has already been related in **Solon's** life. **Solon** instructed him in the best disciplines, recommended him to the favor of the noblest persons, and sought every means of showing him respect and honor. **Anacharsis** admired **Solon's** wisdom and followed him continually; in a short time, he learned all things from him and was kindly received by everyone for **Solon's** sake. As **Theoxenus** attests, he was the only stranger whom the Athenians ever made a citizen of their city.

<margin>
* Strabo, Book 9.
* Clement of Alexandria, Stromata 1.
* Clement of Alexandria, Stromata 5.
* Aelian, Book 2.
</margin>
Thus, he was greatly honored by the Greeks for his perfection, wisdom, and temperance, in which he excelled many of their own philosophers. Consequently, they conferred the title of "Wise" upon him, and some accounted him one of the Seven Sages. **Periander** invited him, along with the rest, to **Corinth**; the feast is described at length by **Plutarch**. There, **Anacharsis**, carrying with him chaplets of flowers, ivy, and laurel, drank—as is the Scythian custom—to great

<margin>Herodotus, Book 4, 76.</margin>
<margin>Clement of Alexandria, Exhortation to the Greeks.</margin>
<margin>Clement of Alexandria, ibid.</margin>
<margin>Herodotus continues.</margin>
<margin>For "by the brother" (ὑπὸ τῶ ἀδελφῶ) read "from the brother" (ἀδελφοῦ).</margin>
<margin>Spargapithes; Lycus; Gnurus; Anacharsis; Saulius, or Caduida; Indathyrsus.</margin>
*Having seen much of the world and improved his knowledge, he returned to Scythia (as Lucian suggests, not until Solon was dead). As he sailed along the Hellespont, he stopped at Cyzicum and found the people celebrating a festival to the Mother of the Gods with great splendor and generosity. He made a vow that if he returned home safely, he would sacrifice in the same manner he had seen at Cyzicum and establish a vigil. When he arrived in Scythia, he withdrew privately to Hylaea, near the Achillean Course—a place full of trees—and performed the rites of the goddess with a tambourine <note>original: timbrell</note> and a cymbal around his neck. A Scythian saw him and told King Saulius, who went there immediately. Being an eyewitness to the ritual, Saulius shot him through with an arrow (* to punish his perceived "softness" and prevent the infection of foreign customs in others). *And now, if anyone inquires about Anacharsis, the Scythians deny they ever knew him, because he traveled into Greece and adopted the customs of that country. As I am informed by Timnes, the tutor of Spargapithes, he was the uncle of Indathyrsus, King of Scythia, and the son of Gnurus, who was the son of Lycus, who was the son of Spargapithes. Now, since Anacharsis belonged to this family, it is clear he was killed by his brother (the fact that his brother was the King of Scythia and killed him is confirmed by Laertius, though he uses a different name). For Indathyrsus was the son of Saulius, and Saulius was the one who killed Anacharsis; consequently, he is the same person whom Laertius calls Cadovides. Some report that he shot him while hunting, because Anacharsis was devoted to Greek customs and was attempting to alter the laws of Scythia. As he was dying, he said he had returned safely out of Greece, protected by his own wisdom, but was killed at home by the envy of others. Concerning him, Laertius wrote this epigram:*

> *From travel Anacharsis came at last,*
> *And Scythia in a Grecian mold he would cast;*
> *While teaching how, he met his sudden demise,*
> *By a winged arrow carried to the skies.*

---

<margin>Diogenes Laertius; Plutarch, Banquet of the Seven Sages.</margin>
<margin>Diogenes Laertius.</margin>
*Many statues were erected in his honor by the Greeks, upon which was written: "Master the tongue, the belly, and the genitals" <note>original Greek: γλώσσης, γαστρὸς, ἀιδοίων κρατεῖν</note>. *He was temperate and skilled in many things. He spoke freely and extensively about the types of diet and medicine the Scythians used in curing the sick. *From the plain honesty he used in speech, a proverb arose: *“The Scythian phrase.”* He wrote eight hundred verses of regulations for the Scythians and Greeks concerning frugal living and military affairs. There are also two of his letters <note>original: Epistles</note> still in existence.*

<summary>This page recounts the death of Anacharsis, who was killed by his brother King Saulius for introducing foreign Greek religious rites to Scythia, and notes his legacy as a philosopher of temperance and "Scythian" plain-speaking.</summary>

### Anacharsis to Croesus.

King of the Lydians, I came to Greece to be informed of their customs and studies. I do not need money; it is enough if I can return to Scythia a better man. However, I will come to Sardis because I highly value your favor.

<margin>* Cicero, Tusculan Disputations, Book 5.</margin>
<margin>* Clement of Alexandria cites this fragment, doubtless out of the same epistle: <note>original: "Ἐμοὶ πείβλημα, χλαῖνα δΐκτυος, γάλα, τυρός."</note> "My covering is a rug, my food is milk and cheese." Stromata, Book 1.</margin>
### * Anacharsis to Hanno, greetings. <note>original: "health"</note>

My apparel is a Scythian rug, my shoes are the hardness of my feet, my bed is the earth, and my sauce is hunger. I feed on milk, cheese, and meat. You may come to me as to one who is content; but as for those gifts which you value so highly, bestow them either on your citizens or the immortal gods.

<margin>
* Strabo, Book 7.
* Diogenes Laertius; Suidas.
* Suidas.
</margin>
* He is said to have invented tinder, the anchor, and the potter’s wheel; but this latter claim is clearly proven false by * Strabo, because it is mentioned by Homer, who lived long before him—Anacharsis living in the time of Croesus.

# CHAPTER II.

## His Apophthegms. <term>Apophthegms are concise, pointed sayings or maxims.</term>

<margin>* Diogenes Laertius.</margin>
His apophthegms are these: * He said a vine bears three types of grapes: the first of pleasure, the second of drunkenness, and the third of repentance. He wondered that among the Greeks, experts competed against each other while those who were not experts judged the results. When asked by what means a man might be convinced not to love wine, he said, "By setting before his eyes the unseemly actions of drunkards." He wondered that the Greeks, who punished physical injuries by law, rewarded the athletes at public exercises for beating one another. Upon being told that a ship's hull is four inches thick, he remarked, "Those who sail are that close to death." He said oil was a recipe for madness because the athletes, the more they were anointed with it, the more fiercely they fought one another. "How is it," he asked, "that those who forbid lying will themselves lie openly when they sell their goods?" He wondered that the Greeks, at the beginning of a feast, drank from small cups, but when they were full, they drank from large ones.

<margin>* Plutarch, Banquet of the Seven Sages; Strabo, Book 15.</margin>
* When asked (by Ardalus) whether there were any flutes in Scythia, he answered, "Not even any vines" (which 
<margin>* Aristotle, Posterior Analytics, Book 1, Chapter 13.</margin>
* Aristotle calls a demonstration of the fact <term>original: ὅτι (hoti)</term>, based on a remote cause). 
<margin>* Plutarch, Banquet of the Seven Sages.</margin>
* Ardalus added, "Are there no gods among the Scythians?" "Yes," he replied, "gods who understand all languages." 
<margin>* Diogenes Laertius.</margin>
* When asked which ship was the safest, he answered, "The one that is in the harbor." He claimed the most remarkable thing he had seen among the Greeks was that they left the smoke upon the mountains and carried the wood into their cities. When asked whether the number of the dead or of the living was greater, he asked in return, "In which group do you count those who are at sea?" To an Athenian who reproached him for being a Scythian, he said, "My country...

"[My country]," he said, "is a disgrace to me, but you are a disgrace to your country." When asked what in man is both good and bad, he answered, "The tongue." He affirmed that it is better to have one friend of great worth than many who are worth nothing. He said the Marketplace was the proper place for cheating and dishonest profit. To a young man who insulted him at a feast, he said, "Young man, if at your age you cannot handle wine, when you grow old, you will not be able to handle water."

<margin>* Herodotus, Book 4, Chapter 46.</margin>
* When he returned to Scythia, he told the King who had sent him that the Greeks were busy with all kinds of wisdom, except for the Lacedemonians <note>Spartans</note>, who only knew how to give and receive prudently.

</margin>
* He said the Greeks made no other use of money except for keeping accounts with it.
* At a public assembly in Athens, he said he wondered why, in the Greek meetings, wise men proposed business and fools decided it.
* That Prince is happy who is wise. That city is best where (all other things being equal) virtue is in a better condition and vice in a worse.

<margin>* Plutarch, *The Banquet of the Seven Sages*.</margin>
* To one who, as they were drinking, looked at his wife and said, "Anacharsis, you have married a woman who is not at all beautiful," he answered, "I am of that opinion as well; but pour less water in my wine, so that I may find her beautiful." <note>Ancient Greeks typically diluted their wine with water; Anacharsis suggests that becoming more intoxicated would make her appear more attractive.</note>

<margin>* Stobaeus, Sermon 16.</margin>
Describing the qualities of the vine to the King of Scythia and showing him some of its clippings, he added that by this time it would have reached into Scythia if the Greeks did not prune its branches every year.

<margin>Athenaeus, *The Deipnosophists*, Book 10.</margin>
* At a feast, when entertainers were brought in to provide amusement, he alone did not smile. Later, when an ape was brought in, he laughed, saying, "That beast is ridiculous by nature; man is ridiculous by art and effort."

<margin>
* Athenaeus, *The Deipnosophists*, Book 10.
* Athenaeus, *The Deipnosophists*, Book 14.
* Clement of Alexandria, *Stromata*, Book 5.
* Clement, *Stromata*, Book 1.
</margin>
While he slept, he used <note>original: "κατέχειν τῇ μὲν λαιᾷ τὰ αἰδοῖα τῇ δεξιᾷ δὲ τὸ στόμα"</note> to hold his private parts with his left hand and his mouth with his right, implying that a man ought to take great care to govern both, but that it is harder to restrain our pleasure than our tongue. He said that to him, all the Greeks were Scythians. <note>Anacharsis here reverses the Greek stereotype of Scythians as barbarians.</note>

## MYSON.

---

<summary>This page concludes the apophthegms of Anacharsis, focusing on his witty critiques of Greek social life, government, and personal discipline, before introducing Myson.</summary>
<keywords>Anacharsis, Scythia, Athens, Lacedemonians, Greek philosophy, moderation, Myson, Plutarch, Herodotus, morality</keywords>

--- Page 147 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the life of Anacharsis to a biography of Myson of Chenae, who is sometimes included among the Seven Sages of Greece.</meta>

# M Y S O N.

<margin>* Diogenes Laertius</margin>
MYSON was (according to *Hermippus*) the son of *Stremon*, born in *Chenae*, a village in either *Oetea* or *Lacedaemonia* <note>Sparta</note>; his father was a tyrant <note>term: tyrant; in this period, a term for a local ruler or autocrat, not necessarily cruel</note>. When *Anacharsis* asked the Oracle if anyone were wiser than he, the answer was (as was mentioned in the life of *Thales* regarding *Chilon*):

> *I declare that Oetean Myson*
> *Is wiser than those who are wisest.*

His curiosity increasing because of this answer, he went to the village and found Myson fitting a plowshare to a plow. He said, "*Myson*, it is not yet time to plow." But Myson answered, "It is, however, time to prepare." Others affirm the Oracle called him *Etean*, a point about which there is much disagreement. *Parmenides* says that *Etea* is a Lacedaemonian village where *Myson* lived. *Sosicrates* says he was *Oetean* on his father’s side and *Chenean* on his mother's. *Euthyphron* claims he was a Cretan, and that *Etea* was a city in *Crete*. *Anaxilaus* says he was an Arcadian. *Hipponax* mentions him with these words: "*And Myson, whom Apollo declared the wisest of all men*."

*Aristomenus* affirms he was of the same temperament as *Timon* and *Apemantus*—a misanthrope <note>term: man-hater</note>. He retired from *Lacedaemon* into the wilderness and was once surprised there all alone, smiling. When asked why he was smiling when no one else was present, he answered, "For that very reason." *Aristoxenus* says he was of little reputation because he did not come from a city but from an obscure village; for this reason, some attribute his sayings to *Pisistratus*. However, others reckon him as one of the Seven Sages; *Plato* places him in the group in the stead of *Periander*. He said, "*We must not seek things from words, but words from things; things were not made for words, but words for things*." He died at ninety-seven years old.

<summary>This page provides a biographical sketch of Myson of Chenae, highlighting his recognition by the Oracle as the wisest of men and his inclusion by Plato among the Seven Sages.</summary>

# EPIMENIDES.

<margin>
* Valerius Maximus 8. 13.
* One of these names is perhaps corrupt.
</margin>
<image-desc>Decorative historiated initial 'E' featuring a figure reclining in a landscape, likely representing the sleeping Epimenides.</image-desc>
Epimenides is universally acknowledged as a *Cretan* (though, contrary to the custom of that place, he wore his hair long), but writers do not agree on the town where he was born. *Laertius*, following the majority of writers, says it was *Cnosus*; *Strabo* says it was *Phaestus*. There is no less disagreement regarding the names of his parents. Some call his father *Phaestius* or *Phaestus*, others *Dosiades*. His mother is called *Blasta* by some, and *Agasiarchus* by others. *Apollonius*, *Bolus*, *Laertius*, and *Suidas* cite *Plutarch* as calling her *Balta*, a supposed nymph.

<margin>
* Apollonius, *History of Wonders* <note>original: "hist. com."</note> chapter 1; Pliny 7. 52; Laertius.
* *On the Latin Language* <note>original: "De ling. lat."</note>, book 6.
* *Whether an old man should engage in public affairs* <note>original: "An seni sit gerenda resp."</note>.
* *On the Soul* <note>original: "de anima"</note>, 46.
* Attic; the number in Suidas seems false, as also in the Vatican appendix of adages; one has 6, the other 7.
* Pliny 7. 52.
* Laertius; Suidas.
</margin>
It is reported that when he was a youth, his father and brothers sent him to their field to bring a sheep back to the city. Exhausted by the heat and the effort of the search, he withdrew at noon (or as *Apollonius* says, at night) from the common road into a private cave. There he slept—for fifty-seven years according to *Theopompus*; for fifty according to *Varro*, *Plutarch*, and *Tertullian*; and for forty according to *Pausanias*. During this interval of time, most of his relatives died. At the end of his sleep, he awoke and resumed his search for the sheep, thinking it was the same day or the day after he had lain down, and that he had slept only a little while. When he could not find it, he returned to the field, where he saw everything changed and a stranger in possession of the land. He went to the city in great amazement; entering his own house, he was questioned about who he was. At last, he met his younger brother, who had now grown old, by whom he was informed of all that had passed and the length of his absence. *Plutarch* says he woke up as an old man; *Pliny* and *Laertius* claim that he grew old in as many days as he had slept years. Some affirm he did not sleep at all but went away for a while to occupy himself with gathering roots.

<margin>
* Apollonius.
* Laertius.
* Laertius.
* Suidas.
</margin>
Many other wonders are reported of him. Some say he received food from the nymphs, which he kept in an ox’s hoof and took a little of every day, requiring no other nourishment; he was never seen to eat. He often faked returning from death to life, his soul leaving his body whenever he pleased and returning again.

<margin>
* Laertius.
* Strabo.
</margin>
He is reported to be the first who <term>purified</term> <note>original: "lustrated"</note> houses and fields, which he performed through verse. To this end, the Athenians, who were visited by a plague during the forty-sixth Olympiad and commanded by the Oracle to purify the city, sent for *Nicias*, the son of *Nice-*

# EPIMENIDES.

...vatus with a ship to Crete, to request that Epimenides come to them. He did so and there established an acquaintance with Solon, whom he privately instructed, setting him on the path of making laws. He reduced the expenses of religious rites, moderated the mourning of the citizens, and added certain sacrifices to funeral ceremonies, removing the barbaric customs that women used on those occasions. Most importantly, through rituals of atonement, official ceremonies, and offerings, he purified and cleansed the city, making the people more obedient to justice and unity. 

<margin>Laertius</margin>
He stayed the plague in this manner: He took sheep, both black and white, and brought them to the Areopagus. There he let them loose to wander wherever they wished, giving orders to those following them that wherever any of them lay down, they should sacrifice in that place to the appropriate deity, by which means the plague ceased. Consequently, even today (as Laertius says), nameless altars—monuments to that purification—can be seen in many Athenian villages. Some affirm he attributed the plague to the Cylonian impiety <note>A sacrilegious murder involving the followers of Cylon, previously discussed in the life of Solon</note> and alleviated it by putting two young men, Cratinus and Ctesibius, to death.

<margin>Laertius</margin>
He is supposed to have been the first to build temples. He erected one in Athens to the Eumenides <note>The Furies</note>; he intended to dedicate another to the nymphs, but a voice from heaven was heard saying, "Epimenides, not to the nymphs, but to Jupiter."

<margin>Plutarch, *Life of Solon*</margin>
Beholding the harbor of Munychia, he said to those who stood by, "How blind man is to the future! The Athenians would tear this harbor to pieces with their teeth if they foresaw how much it will plague the city." He predicted this many years before it came to pass, which occurred in the second year of the 114th Olympiad, when Antipater placed a garrison of Macedonians in Munychia.

As the Athenians were afraid of the Persian Navy, he told them it would not invade them for many years, and when it did, the Persians would not achieve the least of their hopes but would return home having suffered greater losses than they had inflicted on their adversaries. This was fulfilled in the battles of Marathon and Salamis.

He predicted to the Lacedaemonians <note>Spartans</note> (and the Cretans as well) the defeat they would suffer from the Arcadians, which happened when Euricrates and Archidamus reigned in Lacedaemon.

<margin>Laertius</margin>
<margin>Plutarch, *Solon*</margin>
<margin>Laertius</margin>
<margin>Titus 1</margin>
Because of these predictions (for which the Greeks esteemed him <note>divine</note> beloved of the gods and placed him among the number of their <term>Sophists</term> <note>wise men</note>, and the Cretans—whose prophet he is called by Saint Paul—sacrificed to him as a god), they were either not known or not credited by Aristotle. Aristotle claims he did not use to prophesy about future things, but only about things that were past and obscure.

<meta>This page concludes the biography of Epimenides, detailing his return to Crete, his legendary longevity, his literary works—including the verse famously cited by Saint Paul—and a letter of encouragement to Solon.</meta>

# EPIMENIDES.

<margin>Diogenes Laertius, citing Phlegon; Pliny, book 52; Apollonius, *History*, chapter 1, citing Phlegon; Suidas.</margin>
For his purification of the city and other deeds, he was greatly honored by the Athenians. They offered him many gifts and intended to reward him with a talent <term>a unit of currency weighing approximately 75 pounds of silver</term>, and they commissioned a ship to transport him back to Crete. However, he refused their gifts and money, accepting nothing but a small branch of the sacred olive from the citadel <note>original: "Tower"; likely referring to the Acropolis</note>. Having negotiated a treaty between the Cnossians and the Athenians, he returned home. He died soon after at 157 years of age, or according to others, 150. The Cretans claim he was only one year short of 300. Xenophanes affirms that he heard Epimenides speak when the latter was 154 years old. The Lacedemonians kept his body by direction of the Oracle. When it was taken up many years later, it was found to be marked all over with written characters <note>possibly tattoos or mystical inscriptions</note>, which gave rise to a proverb concerning obscure or hidden things: *the skin of Epimenides*. He called himself Aeacus, while others called him Cures.

<margin>Suidas.</margin>
He was a great poet and wrote many things in verse. The subjects of his writings included:
*Initiations, Purifications, and other obscure matters in verse.*
*The generation and genealogy of the Gods of the Curetes and Corybantes, 5,000 verses.*
*The building of the ship Argo, and the expedition of Jason to Colchis, 6,500 verses.*
*On sacrifices, in prose.*
*On the Cretan Commonwealth.*
*On Minos and Rhadamanthus.*
<margin>Saint Jerome in the *Epistle to Titus*; see the passage at Titus 1:12.</margin>
*On Oracles and responses, from which Saint Paul cites this verse:*
> <note>original: Κρῆτες ἀεὶ ψεῦσται, κακὰ θηρία, γαστέρες ἀργαί.</note>
> *The Cretans are always liars, evil beasts, and lazy gluttons.*

According to Laertius, there is an epistle extant under his name addressed to Solon concerning the orders of government given by Minos to the Cretans. Demetrius believes this is of a later date, as it is written in the Athenian rather than the Cretan dialect; however, I have encountered another letter to this effect:

->Epimenides to Solon.<-

Be comforted, my friend; for if Pisistratus were ruling over Athenians who were accustomed to slavery and lacked discipline, his rule might perhaps last forever. But now he subdues not a base people, but those who are mindful of Solon’s instructions; they are ashamed of their bondage and will not tolerate his tyranny. Even though he should establish himself in a government that seems unmovable, I hope it will not pass down to his children, for it is difficult for free people, raised under excellent laws, to endure slavery. As for you, do not wander, but come to me in Crete, where you will find no oppressive monarch. If in traveling about you should encounter some of his friends, I fear you may suffer some harm.

<margin>Laertius</margin>
There were two others of this same name: one a genealogist, and the other who wrote in the Doric dialect concerning Rhodes.

# PHERECYDES.

Pherecydes was from Syros (one of the Cyclades islands near Delos), the son of Badys or, as others say, Babis. According to *Suidas*, he was born in the 46th Olympiad <note>approximately 596–593 BCE</note>. He lived during the time of Alyattes, King of Lydia, and was a contemporary of the seven Sophists <note>the Seven Sages of Greece</note>, and was considered by some to be one of them. Diogenes Laertius states he lived during the 59th Olympiad; <margin>* Cicero, *Tusculan Disputations*, Book 1.</margin> Cicero places him in the time of Servius Tullius.

Some affirm that he was a student of Pittacus; others say he had no master, but instead acquired and studied the abstruse books of the Phoenicians on his own.

<margin>* Laertius.
* Apollonius, *History*, chapter 5.
* Laertius.</margin>
Many extraordinary things are told about him. Once in Syros, feeling thirsty, he asked one of his students for water. After drinking the water (which had been drawn from a well), he declared that there would be an earthquake on that island within three days. When it happened exactly as he foretold, he gained great fame—though Cicero attributed this not to divine insight but to a natural cause <note>likely observing changes in the well water's clarity or level before seismic activity</note>.

<margin>* Apollonius, ibid.
* Laertius.</margin>
On another occasion, while going to the Temple of Juno in Samos, he saw a ship entering the harbor with full sails. He told those present that it would never reach the port. While he was still speaking, a storm arose and the ship sank before their eyes.

<margin>* Laertius.</margin>
While traveling through Messana to Olympia, he advised Perilaus, the man whose house he was staying in, to move away with his whole family. Perilaus did not obey; Messana was captured soon after.

<margin>* Laertius.</margin>
He commanded the Lacedaemonians <note>Spartans</note> not to value gold or silver, claiming that Hercules had ordered him to say so in a dream. Hercules also appeared to the Kings and commanded them to obey Pherecydes. Some attribute this story to Pythagoras.

<margin>* Achilles Tatius, *Introduction to Aratus*.
* Cicero, *Tusculan Disputations*, Book 1.
* *Chiliades*.</margin>
He held opinions contrary to those of Thales, yet he agreed with him that water is the primary principle of all things. He said the Gods called a table <term>θυωρὸς</term> <note>original: "thyoros"; likely meaning 'watcher of offerings'</note>. According to some, he was the first to assert the immortality of the soul. Tzetzes claims he was the teacher of Thales, though that does not fit their chronology. However, it is generally acknowledged that he instructed Pythagoras.

<margin>* Laertius.</margin>
The manner of his death is described in various ways. Hermippus says that during a war between the Ephesians and the Magnesians, Pherecydes desired that the Ephesians should be victorious. He asked a bystander where he was from, and the man replied that he was from Ephesus. Pherecydes then said, "Drag me by the legs into the Magnesian territory, and tell your countrymen to bury me there after they have won the battle." I am

<page-num>130</page-num>
# PHERECYDES.

<margin>* Aelian, *Historical Miscellany*, Book 4, Chapter 28; see also Book 5, Chapter 2.</margin>
Pherecydes delivered this message; they defeated the Magnesians and, finding Pherecydes dead, they buried him with honors. Some claim he went to Delphi and threw himself from the Corycean Mountains; but the more general opinion is that he died a most wretched death, his entire body consumed by lice (*Pliny* says it was snakes that broke out of his skin). When his face became disfigured, he avoided and refused to see his friends. When anyone came to visit him (as *Pythagoras* did) and asked how he was, he would put his finger out through the keyhole; since it was eaten away by the disease, he showed them the state of his whole body, saying "the skin shows" <note>original: χρω̂μα; this implies the surface condition reveals the inner decay</note>. Philosophers interpret these words in a negative sense. The people of Delos claim the god of that place sent this disease to him in anger because he boasted excessively about his own wisdom to his students, saying that even if he never sacrificed to any god, he would lead a life no less pleasant than those who offered hecatombs <term>sacrifices of a hundred oxen</term>. *Pythagoras* buried him; his tomb carried this inscription:

> Of wisdom I have reached the furthest bound;
> He who would be further satisfied, must sound
> *Pythagoras*, the most renowned of *Greeks*.

Some claim he was the first who wrote in prose, though others attribute this to *Cadmus*. He wrote:
* *Heptamuchos* <term>Seven Recesses</term> or *Theocrasia* <term>Mixing of the Gods</term>; this is perhaps the same as his *Theology*, consisting of ten books containing the origin and succession of the gods (unless it is mistaken for the *Theogony* of the younger *Pherecydes*). It is a dark and obscure work; *Isidore* (cited by *Clement of Alexandria*) believes its allegories were taken from the prophecy of *Ham* <note>original: Cham; referring to the biblical son of Noah</note>.

Regarding this book, there is an extant letter under the name of *Pherecydes*, but it may well be suspected to be a forgery.

### Pherecydes to Thales.

> *May you fare well when your final hour arrives. As soon as I received your letter, I fell ill, was overrun with lice, and developed a fever. Therefore, I have instructed my servants that, as soon as I am buried, they should bring this book to you. If you and the other wise men approve of it, publish it; if you do not approve, do not publish it, for it does not please me; there is no certainty in it. Whatever the theologian says must be understood differently, for I write in fables. Forced by my illness, I have not admitted any physician or friend. When they came to the door and asked how I was, I put my finger out through the keyhole and showed them how desperately ill I was, and requested them to come the next day to the funeral of Pherecydes.*

There was another man of this name from the same island who was an astrologer; more are mentioned by *Suidas*.

->FINIS.<-

# ANAXIMANDER

## CHAP. I.

### Of his life.

<margin>* Diogenes Laertius, Preface.</margin>
* Philosophy had two beginnings: one from Anaximander, another from Pythagoras. Anaximander was a disciple of Thales; for this reason, that philosophy was called *Ionic*; Thales being an *Ionian*, for he was from *Miletus*. Pythagoras was a disciple of Pherecydes; that kind of philosophy was called *Italic*, named after the place where he taught. Thales was succeeded by Anaximander, Anaximander by Anaximenes, Anaximenes by Anaxagoras, and Anaxagoras by Archelaus, in whom (as Plutarch, Laertius, and others affirm) it ended, as Socrates (the scholar of Archelaus) introduced morality <note>Ethics</note>.

<margin>* Cicero, *Academic Questions*, 4.</margin>
<margin>* Suidas.</margin>
<margin>* Strabo, Book 1.</margin>
<margin>* Clement of Alexandria, *Stromata*, 1.</margin>
<margin>* Laertius.</margin>
Anaximander, a Milesian, was a countryman, companion, and kinsman of Thales; he was his disciple as well, and his successor in the propagation of his doctrine. He was the son of Praxiades (corruptly called by some Praxidamus), born in the third year of the 42nd Olympiad. He flourished most in the time of Polycrates, the tyrant of Samos.

<margin>* Suidas.</margin>
<margin>* Strabo, Book 1.</margin>
* He demonstrated a summary of geometry, being—after Homer—the first master of that science. He first set forth a geographic table <note>a map</note>, which is what Laertius refers to when he affirms that Anaximander designed the circumference of the sea and land.

<margin>* Pliny, 2. 8.</margin>
<margin>* Heinsius.</margin>
<margin>* Laertius.</margin>
<margin>* Suidas.</margin>
<margin>* *Plinianae Exercitationes*.</margin>
* In the 50th Olympiad, he discovered the obliquity <note>tilt</note> of the Zodiac; that is (says Pliny), he opened the gates of things. He invented the <term>gnomon: the part of a sundial that casts a shadow</term> and set up the first one in an open place at Lacedaemon. He discovered the equinoctial solstices and horologies <note>time-measuring devices</note>. He framed horoscopes <term>horoscopes: instruments used to observe the hours or mark the positions of the heavens</term> to denote the tropics and equinoxes. From this, Salmasius conceives that the use of his dial was only to delineate the tropic and equinoctial points, and that they did not serve to distinguish the hours or the twelve parts of the day. He proves this because the very name <note>original: "ωρα"</note> "hour," in that sense or as the division of the day into twelve equal parts, was not known for a long time afterward.

<margin>* Cicero, *On Divination*.</margin>
<margin>* Pliny, 2. 79.</margin>
* He advised the Lacedaemonians to leave their city and houses and to lie armed in the open field, foretelling an earthquake which threw down the whole city and tore away a piece of Mount Taygetus.

I i
<meta>catchword: * As</meta>

<summary>This page introduces the life and scientific achievements of Anaximander, the successor of Thales in the Ionic school of philosophy. It highlights his inventions, including the gnomon and geographical maps, and his successful prediction of an earthquake.</summary>
<keywords>Anaximander, Thales, Ionic philosophy, geometry, geography, gnomon, zodiac, equinox, Lacedaemon, Mount Taygetus</keywords>

# ANAXIMANDER.

<margin>* Diogenes Laertius.</margin>
\* As he sang, the boys used to mock him, whereupon he said, "We must learn to sing better for the boys."

Among his students are remembered *Anaximenes* and *Parmenides*.

Of his writings, these are known:

<margin>* Suidas.</margin>

## CHAP. 2.

### Of his Opinions.

### Sect. 1. That Infinity is the principle of all things.

<margin>
Cicero, *Academic Questions*, 4.
* Plutarch, *On the Opinions of the Philosophers*, 1. 3.
* Justin Martyr, *Exhortation to the Greeks*.
* Diogenes Laertius.
</margin>
Thales (says \* *Cicero*), who held that all things consist of *water*, could not persuade his countryman and companion *Anaximander* of this; for Anaximander asserted that infinity is that from which all things were made. Or (according to \* *Plutarch*, *Laertius*, and \* *Justin Martyr*) he held that it is the principle and element of all things. These two terms he treated as one, as was observed of his master *Thales*. \* However, he did not declare what this infinity is—whether it is *air*, *water*, *earth*, or any other body—for which he was criticized by *Plutarch*.

<margin>
* Simplicius, *Commentary on Aristotle's Physics*, 1. 2. and 1. 5.
* Plutarch, *On the Opinions of the Philosophers*, 1. 3.
* Diogenes Laertius.
* Chapter 6.
* Cicero, *Academic Questions*, 4.
* Plutarch, *On the Opinions of the Philosophers*, 1. 3.
* Justin Martyr, *Exhortation*.
</margin>
\* He held that this principle is one, and infinite in magnitude (though not in number). Because of this, \* *Aristotle* criticizes him for imagining that contrary qualities can proceed from the same principle. \* He argued that it is infinite for the reason that it may never be exhausted.
\* He taught that its parts are changed, but the whole is immutable (\* *Simplicius* says it is movable). \* He claimed that all things proceed out of it and resolve back into it.
\* He believed that there are infinite worlds generated, which decay back into that from which they were generated.

### Sect. 2. Of the Heavens.

<margin>
* Cicero, *On the Nature of the Gods*, 1.
* Plutarch, *On the Opinions of the Philosophers*, 1. 7.
* Stobaeus.
</margin>
His opinion (according to \* *Cicero*) was that the *gods* are born <note>native</note>, rising and setting at long intervals, and that there are innumerable worlds. This, \* *Plutarch* and *Stobaeus* apply to the heavens and stars. But how (adds *Cicero*) can we understand a God who is not eternal? \* He also held that heaven consists of a mixture of cold and heat.

<header>ANAXIMANDER:</header>

<margin>* Stobaeus</margin>
He held that the stars are spherical bodies consisting of air filled with fire, breathing out flames from a certain part. * They are moved by the circles and spheres in which they are fixed; an assertion that Aristotle borrowed from him.

<margin>* Stobaeus</margin>
He believed that the Sun is positioned highest, the moon next, and then the fixed stars and planets.

<margin>* Plutarch, *On the Opinions of the Philosophers*, 2. 20.
* Plutarch, *On the Opinions of the Philosophers*, 2. 21.
* Diogenes Laertius.</margin>
That the circle of the Sun is * 28 times (Theodoret says 27) larger than the earth, having a hollow ring around it like a chariot wheel, full of fire. In one part of it there is an opening through which the fire is seen, as if through the hole of a flute; this is the Sun, * which is equal in size to the Earth.

<margin>* Plutarch, *On the Opinions of the Philosophers*, 2. 24. Stobaeus.</margin>
* He taught that the cause of a solar eclipse is the blocking of that opening in the center through which the fire emerges.

<margin>* Plutarch, *On the Opinions of the Philosophers*, 2. 25.
* Plutarch, *On the Opinions of the Philosophers*, 2. 29.</margin>
* That the circle of the Moon is 29 times larger than the Earth, shaped like a chariot wheel, having a hollow orb in the center full of fire (like the Sun) and placed at an angle, breathing fire out of one part as if through a funnel.

<margin>* Stobaeus.
* Plutarch, *On the Opinions of the Philosophers*, 2. 28.</margin>
* That the eclipse of the Moon happens according to its rotations, when the opening through which the fire emerges is blocked.

<margin>* Diogenes Laertius.
* As a learned person concludes from the words of Diogenes Laertius.

* That the Moon has a light of her own, but it is very faint; * and that she shines by the light she borrows from the Sun. These two assertions are so far * from being contradictory that it is a common opinion * that both are true.

## Sect. 3. Of Meteors.

<margin>* Plutarch, *On the Opinions of the Philosophers*, 3. 7.</margin>
He held that wind is a flow of air, occurring when its finest and most fluid parts are either stirred or dissolved by the Sun.

<margin>* Plutarch, *On the Opinions of the Philosophers*, 3. 3.
* Stobaeus.
* Seneca, *Natural Questions*, 2. 18.</margin>
* That thunder, lightning, <term>presters</term> <note>fiery whirlwinds or hurricanes</note>, and whirlwinds are caused by wind enclosed in a thick cloud, which breaks out violently because of its lightness. The rupture of the cloud creates a crackling sound, and the tearing apart of the cloud, due to its darkness, causes a flashing light. * Seneca states more specifically: He ascribed everything to wind. Thunder (he says) is the sound of a breaking cloud. Why is it uneven? Because the breaks are uneven. Why does it thunder on a clear day? Because even then, the wind breaks through the thick and dry air. Why does it sometimes thunder and not lighten? Because the thinner and weaker <term>spirit</term> <note>original: "spiritus," meaning pneuma or pressurized air</note> is able to produce a flame but not a sound. What is lightning? The agitation of the air separating itself and rushing down, revealing a faint fire. What is Thunder? The motion of a piercing, dense spirit.

<margin>* Seneca, *Natural Questions*, 2. 19.</margin>
* All things are so ordered that some influence descends from the <term>aether</term> <note>the upper atmosphere or heavenly fire</note> upon lower things; thus fire makes a sound when forced upon cold clouds. When it shines, the smaller flames produce lightning, and the larger ones produce thunder; a great part of the rest was altered from its natural kind by his excessive heat.

<margin>* Plutarch, *On the Opinions of the Philosophers*, 5. 19.</margin>
* That the first living creatures were bred in moisture and enclosed within sharp, thorny skins; but as they grew older, they became drier, and at last, when the skin broke around them, they lived for only a short time afterward.

# ANAXIMENES

## CHAPTER I.

### His life.

<margin>
* Diogenes Laertius
* Simplicius
* Cicero, *Academic Questions*; Pliny, *Natural History* 2.76
* Suidas
* Diogenes Laertius
* Book 2
</margin>
Anaximenes was a Milesian, the son of Eurystratus, and a friend, disciple, and successor to Anaximander. According to Eusebius, he flourished in the second year of the 56th Olympiad <note>Approximately 555 BCE</note>. Suidas says he lived during the 55th Olympiad at the time Sardis was captured, when Cyrus the Great overthrew Croesus. Thus, the account of Apollodorus (who claims he was born in the 63rd Olympiad) is incorrect. Some also claim he studied under Parmenides. He used the Ionic dialect, writing in a plain and unadorned style. Pliny calls him the inventor of gnomonics <term>the art of constructing and using sun-dials</term>, but this is perhaps a mistake for Anaximander. Among his students were Anaxagoras and Diogenes of Apollonia.

These two letters of his are preserved by Diogenes Laertius.

->Anaximenes to Pythagoras.<-

> *Thales, having lived happily even into old age, ended his days unfortunately. One night, while going out of his house (as was his habit) with his maidservant to contemplate the stars, he was gazing upward and not paying attention to where he was walking; he came upon a steep edge and fell. This was the fate of the Milesian astronomer. But let those of us who were his students preserve the memory of the man, and let our sons and students do so after us. Let us always retain his sayings, and begin all our discourses with a tribute to Thales.*

->Anaximenes to Pythagoras.<-

> *You are wiser than we are; having left Samos for Croton, you live there in peace. The descendants of Aeacus <note>original: "Æacides"; the ruling family of Samos</note> prove harmful to others, and the Milesians do not lack tyrants of their own choosing. The King of Media <note>The Persian ruler</note> is also a terror to us, but would not be so if we paid him tribute. However, the Ionians are determined to go to war with the Medes for the sake of general liberty,*

...if they fight, we have no hope of safety. How then can Anaximenes, perplexed with fear of death and slavery, apply his mind to celestial speculations? But you are sought after by the Crotonians and all Italians; students come to you from as far as Sicily.

# CHAP. II.
## His opinions.
### Sect. 1. That the Air is the principle of all things.

<margin>
Plutarch, *On the Opinions of the Philosophers* <note>original: "Plut de. plac."</note>
Justin Martyr, *Exhortation to the Greeks* <note>original: "Justin Martyr. paren."</note>
Book 3, *Philosophers* <note>original: "l. 3. phil."</note>
* Cicero, *On the Nature of the Gods*, Book 1 <note>original: "Cic. de nat. deor. 1."</note>
* Cicero, *Academic Questions*, Book 4 <note>original: "Cic. acad. quest. 4."</note>
</margin>
He held that *Air* is the principle of the *Universe*, from which all things are *produced*, and into which they *resolve*. Our souls, by which we live, are air; so *spirit* and *air* sustain the whole world in being: for *spirit* and *air* are two names signifying one thing.

He believed that the *air* is God—*born, immense, infinite, and ever in motion*—but that those things which arise out of it are *finite*. First, the earth, water, and fire are born; then, all things are born from these. Cicero understands the statement that the air is God as referring to the faculties penetrating through the elements or bodies.

### Sect. 2. Of the Heavens.

<margin>
* Plutarch, *Opinions of the Philosophers*, 2.11.
* Plutarch, *Opinions of the Philosophers*, 2.23.
* Diogenes Laertius <note>original: "Laert."</note>
* Plutarch, *Opinions of the Philosophers*, 2.19.
* Plutarch, *Opinions of the Philosophers*, 2.19.
* Plutarch, *Opinions of the Philosophers*, 2.24.
Plutarch.
Plutarch, *Opinions of the Philosophers*, 2.29.
</margin>
That *the outer surface of Heaven is earthy.*

That *the stars are of a fiery substance, with invisible earthy bodies mixed among them; and that they are fixed like nails in crystal.*

That *they are forced back by the thick resisting air, and move not above (or under) but around the earth.*

That *the sun is flat like a plate and made of a fiery substance.*

That *the signs of summer and winter are caused not by the moon, but by the sun alone.*

That *the sun is eclipsed when the mouth from which its heat issues is closed.*

That the Moon is likewise of a fiery nature.

That the Moon is eclipsed when the mouth from which her heat issues is closed.

### Sect. 3. Of Meteors.

<margin>
Plutarch, *Opinions of the Philosophers*, 3.4.
Stobaeus <note>original: "Stob."</note>
Plutarch, *Opinions of the Philosophers*, 3.5.
</margin>
That the clouds are made by the condensation of air, rain by the condensation of the clouds (out of which it is squeezed), snow from rain frozen while falling, and hail from the same contracted by a cold wind.

Concerning thunder, lightning, etc., to the assertion of Anaximander he added the comparison of the sea, which shines when broken with oars.

That the rainbow is made by reflection of the sun's beams upon a thick

<meta>This page continues the physical theories of Anaximenes, specifically focusing on his explanations for meteorological and geological phenomena like earthquakes, and the relationship between temperature and the density of matter, before transitioning to the life of Anaxagoras.</meta>

...thick clouds; because they are unable to pierce the cloud, they are refracted by it.

<margin>
Plutarch, *On the Opinions of the Philosophers*, 3.15. <note>original: "Plutarch. de plac. 3. 15."</note>
* Aristotle, *Meteorology*, 2.7. <note>original: "* Meteor. 2. 7."</note>
Seneca, *Natural Questions*, 6.10. <note>original: "Senec. nat. quaest. 6. 10."</note>
</margin>
**Earthquakes** result from the <term>thinness</term> <note>original: "rarity"</note> and dryness of the Earth—one being caused by excessive heat, the other by excessive cold. This was further explained by * Aristotle in this way: He held that the Earth breaks apart when it is excessively moist as well as when it is dry, and it is shaken by those large pieces which fall into the cavities within it. Consequently, earthquakes occur either during droughts or heavy rains: in droughts, the earth is cracked, and by heavy showers, the parts that are excessively moistened likewise break asunder.

<margin>Plutarch, *On the Principle of Cold* <note>original: "Plutarch. de primo. frig."</note></margin>
He called the contraction and condensation of matter "cold," and the loosening and <term>thinning</term> <note>original: "rarity"</note> of it "heat." This is why a person breathes both hot and cold air from his mouth: breath compressed by the lips and condensed is cold, but breath exhaled with an open mouth is hot due to its thinness.

# ANAXAGORAS.

## CHAPTER I.

### *Anaxagoras's Country, time, and study of Philosophy.*

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'A' featuring floral motifs and foliage patterns.</image-desc>

<margin>
Laertius
</margin>
**A**naxagoras was from *Clazomene*, the son of *Hegesibulus* (or *Eubulus*). He was born in the 70th Olympiad, according to *Apollodorus*, in its first year. He was famous for his noble birth and great wealth, but even more so for his noble contempt of them. 
<margin>
* Plutarch, *Against Usury*; Lysander.
</margin>
* He left his lands and inheritance (says 
<margin>
* Cicero, *Tusculan Disputations*, Book 5.
</margin>
* *Cicero*) to learn and obtain the divine delight of philosophy, and 
<margin>
* Cicero, *On the Orator*, Book 3.
</margin>
* he turned himself from civil affairs to the pursuit of knowledge. *Suidas* claims he left his fields to be grazed upon by sheep and camels; because of this, *Apollonius of Tyana* said he taught philosophy to beasts rather than to men. 
<margin>
* Plato, *Hippias Major*.
</margin>
* *Plato* mocks him for quitting his estate; *Laertius* reports that he assigned it to his friends. When they accused him of recklessness because of this, he answered, *"Why do you not take care of it?"* To one who reproved him for taking no care of his country, he said, *"Do not wrong me; my greatest care is my country,"* while pointing toward the heavens. To another asking for what purpose he was born, 
<margin>
* Laertius
</margin>
* he answered, *"To contemplate the sun, moon, and heavens."* In short, he withdrew himself into the contemplation of natural philosophy, disregarding civil affairs. In this study, *Anaximenes* was his 
<margin>
* Cicero, *On the Nature of the Gods*, Book 1.
</margin>
master, 
<margin>
* Laertius
</margin>
* from whom he received his learning.

* In the twentieth year of his age—the first year of the 75th Olympiad, when *Colliades* was Archon (whom *Laertius* incorrectly calls *Callias*)—at the time of *Xerxes'* expedition into *Greece*, he went to *Athens* to study philosophy. He remained there for thirty years and was honored with the title of *Nous* <term>the Mind</term>, as he was the first who added that principle to matter. As Ammonius says:

<margin>
* Laertius, perhaps <note>original: "ὁ νῶς ὧδε αὐτὰς" (The Mind thus [ordered] these things)</note>
</margin>
->*Where dwells famed Anaxagoras, the Mind,*<-

->*Which arranged confused things into an order.*<-

# ANAXAGORAS.

## CHAPTER II.
### Of his opinions.
### Section 1. Of the first principles and the beginning of things.

<margin>
* Plutarch, *Opinions of the Philosophers*, 1. 3.
* Aristotle, *Physics*, 3. 4.
* Aristotle, *Physics*, 3. 7.
* Plutarch.
Laertius.
* Book 1.
</margin>

He held that the material principle of all things is both "one and many": <term>homoeomery</term> <note>original: ὁμοιομερῆ; meaning parts that are similar to the whole</note>, consisting of infinite, similar, and contrary parts that are continuous to the touch. These parts sustain themselves and are not contained by any other. 

His grounds for this were as follows: First, because according to the common rule of natural philosophers, "nothing proceeds from nothing," it is not possible for anything to be made of that which does not exist, or for that which has being to be resolved into that which has none. Secondly, because contraries are made mutually from each other, they must have existed within each other previously. For if it is necessary that whatever is made must be made either of that which is or that which is not—and it is impossible for it to be made of that which is not (a point on which all who have ever discussed nature agree)—it follows necessarily that things are made of things that already exist. These exist within the things themselves, though they are not discernible to us because of their smallness. 

This is why they say that "everything is mixed with everything," because they see that anything can be made from anything. However, things seem different and are called different names in relation to one another because of the abundance of infinite parts that predominate within the mixture. For the whole is neither entirely white nor black, nor entirely flesh nor bone; rather, everything seems to be of the nature of that which it contains in the greatest quantity. 

From simple nourishment, such as bread, water, and the like, are produced the hair, veins, arteries, nerves, bones, and other parts of the body. Therefore, all these things—nerves, bones, and so on—are present in this food, discernible by reason though not by the senses. The whole world consists of these atoms, just as gold consists of grains. These <term>homogeneous</term> <note>composed of parts of the same kind</note> parts are the matter of all things. His opinion is expressed by *Lucretius* <note>Titus Lucretius Carus, Roman poet and philosopher</note> as follows:

> Next, we must pursue *Anaxagoras*,
> And his *Homoeomery* review;
> A term that is mentioned nowhere but among
> The *Greeks*; it is too rich for our narrow tongue:
> Yet the sense may be expressed in more words;
> The principle of all things: intestines made
> Of smallest intestines, bone of smallest bone,
> Blood of small drops of blood reduced to one;
> Gold of small grains, earth of small sands compacted,
> Small drops to water, sparks to fire contracted;
> He supposed the like in everything, yet he...

<summary>This page explains Anaxagoras's theory of "Homoeomery," arguing that all matter consists of infinite, microscopic "seeds" of every substance, where an object's identity is determined by whichever component is most abundant.</summary>
<keywords>Anaxagoras, Homoeomery, material principle, Lucretius, atoms, natural philosophy, Aristotle, Plutarch, homogeneous parts</keywords>

--- Page 165 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Anaxagoras's philosophical principles, moving from the nature of the first material principles to his theological views on the Divine Mind and his specific theories regarding astronomy and the heavens.</meta>

# ANAXAGORAS.

---

->*Nature was declared free of all vacuum;*<-
->*And he held that every physical being might*<-
->*Be subdivided infinitely.*<-

<margin>* Lactantius, *On False Religion*, 1. 5. * Cicero, *On the Nature of the Gods*, 1. * Augustine, *The City of God*, 8. 2.</margin>
He held that God is an infinite, self-moving mind; that this divine, infinite mind, not enclosed in any body, is the efficient cause of all things. Out of the infinite matter consisting of similar parts <note>homoeomery</note>, every thing was made according to its species by the divine mind, who, when all things were at first confusedly mingled together, came and reduced them to order.

### Section 2. Of the Heavens.

<margin>Aristotle, *Metaphysics*, 1. 3.</margin>
He held that the higher parts of the world are full of fire; the power that is there is called *ether*. This is named properly, says Aristotle, for the body which is continually in quick motion is conceived to be divine by nature, and for that reason is called ether <note>original: αἰθήρ, which Aristotle associated with αἰεὶ θεῖν, "to run always"</note>, none of the things here below being of that kind.

<margin>* Plutarch, *Opinions of the Philosophers*, 2. 13. * Plutarch, *Opinions of the Philosophers*, 2. 16.</margin>
He believed that "the surrounding ether, being of a fiery nature by the swiftness of its motion, snatches up stones from the earth, which, being set on fire, become stars; these are all carried from East to West."

<margin>Plutarch, *Opinions of the Philosophers*, 2. 23.</margin>
He taught that "the stars are driven by the condensation of the air about the Poles, which the Sun makes stronger by compression."

<margin>Achilles Tatius, *Introduction to Aratus*.</margin>
He held that "the stars are earthly, and that after the first separation of the Elements, the fire, separating itself, drew some parts of the earth into its own nature and made them like fire." Whereupon he further affirmed:

<margin>* Plutarch, *Opinions of the Philosophers*, 2. 20. Laertius. Achilles Tatius, *Introduction to Aratus*. * Plutarch, *Opinions of the Philosophers*, 2. 21.</margin>
"The Sun is a red-hot plate or stone, many times larger than the *Peloponnese* <note>A large peninsula in southern Greece</note>, whose turning <note>referring to the solstices</note> is caused by the resistance of the Northern air, which he makes stronger by compressing it."

<margin>* Plutarch, *Opinions of the Philosophers*, 2. 25. * Plutarch, *Opinions of the Philosophers*, 2. 30. Laertius. * In *Cratylus*.</margin>
"The Moon is a dark body, enlightened by the Sun, and is habitable, having plains, hills, and waters. The unevenness in her face proceeds from a mixture of cold and earthly matter, for there is darkness mixed with her fiery nature, which is why she is called a star of false light." Plato says that the Moon was a cause of dishonor to Anaxagoras because he claimed the origin of this opinion—that she borrows her light—for himself, whereas it was actually much older.

<margin>* Plutarch, *Opinions of the Philosophers*, 3. 1. *Meteorology*, 1. 8.</margin>
He held that "the Milky Way is the shadow of the earth upon that part of heaven when the Sun, being underneath, does not enlighten everything." Or, as Aristotle records, that "the Milky Way is the light of certain stars; for when the Sun is under the earth, he does not look upon some stars, and the light of those he does look upon is not seen, being swallowed up in his own; the natural light of those which..."

<meta>catchword: which</meta>

That "earthquakes are caused by the air or ether which,
" being of its own nature apt to ascend, gets into the
" veins and caverns of the earth. Finding difficulty in

" of the earth contract themselves due to rain, while Na-

" spongy. The parts are like those in a ball: the upper part
" and the lower part. The upper is that which is inhabited by us; the
" lower is the other side. This wind, getting into the lower parts, breaks the
" condensed air with the same force as we see clouds broken
" when, upon their collision and the motion of the agitated
" air, fire breaks forth. This air falls upon that which is next to it,

" heaven or forces one." *Laertius* obscurely expresses this as
" the repulsion of the air upon the earth."

<margin>Cicero, *Academic Questions*, 4.</margin>
That "snow is not white, but black; nor did it seem white
" to him, because he knew the water from which it is frozen
" to be black."

# ANAXAGORAS.

### Section 4. Of the Earth.

<margin>Diogenes Laertius</margin>
That "at the beginning of motion proceeding from the Mind, the heavy bodies occupied the lowest place, such as the earth; the light bodies the highest, such as fire; and those between the two, the middle, such as air and water. Thus, the sea remains upon the surface of the earth, which is flat, with the moisture being rarefied by the Sun."

<margin>Plutarch, *Opinions of the Philosophers*, 3. 16.</margin>
That "the original moisture being spread out like a pool, was burned by the motion of the Sun around it; as the oily part was exhaled, the remainder became salt."

<margin>Plutarch, *Opinions of the Philosophers*, 2. 8.</margin>
That "as soon as the world was made and living creatures were produced out of the world, the world inclined of itself towards the south, according to divine providence, so that some parts of it might be habitable and others uninhabitable, due to the extremes of heat and cold."

<margin>Plutarch, *Opinions of the Philosophers*, 1. 17.</margin>
That "the mixture of the Elements is by apposition <term>placing side-by-side</term>."

<margin>Plutarch, *Opinions of the Philosophers*, 4. 1.</margin>
That "the flooding of the Nile is caused by the snow of Ethiopia, which melts in summer and freezes in winter."

### Section 5. Of living Creatures.

<margin>Diogenes Laertius</margin>
That "creatures were first generated from moisture, heat, and earthy matter; afterwards, they were produced from one another, males on the right side and females on the left."

<margin>* Plutarch, *Opinions of the Philosophers*, 4. 1.</margin>
That "the soul is that which moves; that it is aerial, and has a body of the nature of air."

<margin>* Plutarch, *Opinions of the Philosophers*, 5. 24.</margin>
* That there is a death of the soul likewise, which is its separation from the body.

That all animals have active reason.

* That sleep is an action of the body, not of the soul.

<margin>* Plutarch, *Opinions of the Philosophers*, 5. 25.</margin>
* That man's skill consists entirely in his hands.

<margin>* Plutarch, *On Brotherly Love* <note>original: "de amore fratrum"</note></margin>
* That "the voice is made by the wind hitting against firm, resisting air, which returns the counter-blow to our ears; this is the same manner in which the reflection of air is formed, called an Echo."

<margin>* Aristotle</margin>
That "the gall <note>bile</note> is the cause of acute diseases; when it overflows, it is dispersed into the lungs, veins, and ribs <term>original: costs</term>."

## CHAP. III.

### *His predictions.*

Suidas says that he foretold many things, but only two instances of these have been preserved until now. The first is related by Pliny as follows: *The Grecians celebrate Anaxagoras of Clazomenae for foretelling—by his learning and science in the second year of the 78th Olympiad—on what day a stone would fall from the Sun. This happened during the day in a part of Thrace at the river Aegos <note>Aegospotami</note>; that stone is shown even today, being about the size of a wagon-beam and of a scorched color, with a comet also burning during those nights.*

<margin>* Life of Lysander.</margin>
* Plutarch adds that in his time, it was not only shown but also revered by the Peloponnesians. Eusebius calculates the fall of this stone to the fourth year of the 78th Olympiad, which is two years after Pliny’s account of the prediction. Silenus, as cited by Laertius, says it fell when Dimylus was Archon; if that name is to be read as *Dyphilus* (since the other name is not found near these times), it would be the first year of the 84th Olympiad. But the marble at Arundel House <note>The Arundel Marbles, a collection of ancient Greek inscriptions</note> (engraved about the 129th Olympiad and to be preferred above any other chronological account) expressly names the fall as occurring in the 4th year of the 77th Olympiad, when Theagenides was Archon—two years earlier. Pliny says the event was foretold. It was believed to have portended (as Plutarch testifies) the great defeat given to the Athenians by Lysander at the river Aegos sixty-two years later, namely in the fourth year of the 93rd Olympiad.

<margin>* Meteorology 1. 7.</margin>
<margin>* Life of Lysander.</margin>
Aristotle gives a very slight account of the wonder, affirming:
> "it was a stone snatched up by the wind, and it fell in the
> "daytime while a comet happened to appear in those nights,"
which is disproved by * Plutarch, who provides this large discourse upon it:
> "It is
> "said that Anaxagoras did prognosticate that if one of the bodies
> "contained in the heavens should be loosened by shaking, it would fall to
> "the ground. The stars are not in the place where they were first
> "created; they are heavy bodies of the nature of stone, shining by
> "the reflection of the ether, being drawn up by force and kept there
> "by the violence of that circular motion—just as at the beginning, in
> "the first separation of things, cold and heavy elements were restrained.
> "There is another more probable opinion which says that
> "those which we call falling stars are not outpourings of the
> "ether extinguished in the air almost as soon as they are lit, nor
> "inflammations or combustions of any part of the air which
> "spreads upwards, but they are celestial bodies failing
> "to remain in their ordinary heavenly course and being thrown
> "down—not upon the habitable earth, but into the sea, which
> "is the reason we do not see them. Yet the assertion of Anax-

<summary>This page discusses Anaxagoras's famous prediction of a meteorite fall at Aegospotami and the varying historical accounts of its timing, as well as the philosophical explanations for falling stars offered by Aristotle and Plutarch.</summary>

--- Page 169 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Anaxagoras, focusing on accounts of the meteorite at Aegospotami and his influence on his most famous student, Pericles.</meta>

-># ANAXAGORAS.<-

[Anax]agoras’s account is confirmed by Damachus, who writes in his book on Religion that for 75 days before this stone fell, a great body of fire like an inflamed cloud was seen in the air. It did not stay in one place but moved back and forth unpredictably. From its movement, flashes of fire issued and fell in many places like falling stars. When this great body of fire fell in that part of the earth, the inhabitants were emboldened to come to the place to see what it was. They found no sign of fire, but only a great stone on the ground—which was very small in comparison to that body of fire. In this matter, Damachus needed a sympathetic audience; but if what he says is true, he refutes the arguments of those who maintain the stone was a piece of a rock torn from the top of a mountain by the force of a violent wind. They argue it was carried in the air as long as the whirlwind continued, but as soon as the wind stopped, the stone fell immediately—unless this lightning-like body which appeared for so many days was indeed fire, which, upon dissolving and being extinguished, triggered a violent storm strong enough to tear off the stone and cast it down.

<margin>* Seneca, *Natural Questions*, Book 7, Section 5.</margin>
This is likely what Charimander meant, who says in his book on Comets that Anaxagoras observed a great, unusual light in the heavens, the size of a huge pillar, and that it shone for many days.

<margin>* Suidas.</margin>
The other memorable prediction by Anaxagoras was of a storm. He signaled this by going to the Olympic games in a shaggy gown <note>A heavy cloak or *gausapa* used for warmth or rain</note> even though the weather was fair. When the rain came pouring down, all the Greeks (says Aelian) saw this and marveled that he possessed a divine knowledge beyond human nature.

***

# CHAP. IV.
## *His Students and Listeners.*

<margin>* Cicero.</margin>
<margin>* Plutarch, *Life of Pericles*.</margin>
The following are remembered as his students and listeners:
* **Pericles**, the son of Xanthippus, was instructed by Anaxagoras and could easily direct his mental energy from obscure, hidden matters to public and popular causes. Pericles greatly esteemed him and was taught natural philosophy by him. Besides other virtues, he was freed from superstitious fears arising from ignorance of physical causes. An instance of this occurred when the head of a ram with only one horn was brought to Pericles. The soothsayers interpreted this as an omen <note>Latin: *prodigium*</note>. Anaxagoras opened the skull and showed that the brain did not fill its natural place, but instead contracted by degrees into an oval shape toward the part where the horn grew. Afterward, Anaxagoras was neglected and became weak with age. In a state of melancholy distress over this, he lay down and covered his face, resolving to starve himself to death. When Pericles heard of this...

<meta>catchword: hearing,</meta>

<summary>This page discusses historical accounts of the Aegospotami meteorite and relates how Anaxagoras used natural philosophy to debunk superstitious omens for his student, Pericles.</summary>
<keywords>Anaxagoras, Damachus, meteorite, Aegospotami, Charimander, Seneca, Pericles, natural philosophy, superstition, Greek history</keywords>

--- Page 170 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Anaxagoras, detailing his relationship with his students, including Pericles, Euripides, and Socrates, and begins the account of his trial and eventual exile.</meta>

-># ANAXAGORAS.<-

[...hearing this,] Pericles came immediately to him, mourning not for Anaxagoras but for himself, as he was about to lose such an excellent counselor. Anaxagoras, uncovering his face, said, "Pericles, those who wish to use a lamp must supply it with oil." <note>Anaxagoras was starving himself to death because he felt neglected by Pericles; this was his rebuke that a patron must support his teacher.</note>

Archelaus, son of Apollodorus, was a disciple of Anaxagoras and, as Diogenes Laertius affirms, was called "the natural philosopher" for being the first to bring that kind of learning to Athens. However, Isaac Casaubon rightly questions how this aligns with the account of Anaxagoras who, as is acknowledged, studied natural philosophy in Athens for thirty years.

<margin>* These are his words (as they were never published before): <note>original Greek: "Euripides, seeing Anaxagoras of Clazomenae suffering for his consistent doctrines, turned to tragedy."</note></margin>
Euripides—as the writer of his biography affirms—was the son of Mnesarchus and was born during the first expedition of Xerxes into Greece, on the same day the Greeks overthrew the Persians. He was first a painter and then a student of Anaxagoras; but seeing his teacher persecuted for his opinions, he eventually turned his efforts to tragic poetry.

Socrates, son of Sophroniscus, was a student of Anaxagoras according to Aristoxenus until the philosopher left the city, at which point he applied himself to Archelaus. Porphyry estimates this occurred when Socrates was over seventeen, or more likely nineteen years old.

Some also claim that Democritus, being forty years younger than Anaxagoras, applied himself to him as a student. However, Laertius affirms that Anaxagoras could not endure Democritus and shunned his company. Favorinus likewise testifies that because Anaxagoras would not admit him, Democritus declared himself his enemy and denied the originality of Anaxagoras's opinions on the Sun and Moon. He claimed they were ancient theories that Anaxagoras had stolen, along with his description of the world and his assertions concerning the mind.

Metrodorus of Lampsacus is likewise mentioned by Laertius as a friend of Anaxagoras.

---

## CHAPTER V.

### Regarding his trial, death, sentences, and writings.

Regarding his trial, Laertius says there are several different reports. Sotion, in his treatise on the succession of philosophers, says he was accused of impiety by Cleon for asserting that the Sun is a burning plate of metal. However, being defended by his student Pericles, he was fined five talents and banished.

<margin>Laertius.</margin>
<margin>Cicero, *Tusculan Disputations*, Book 3; Plutarch, *On the Control of Anger*; *Varro, History*, Book 3.</margin>
Satyrus records that he was summoned to court by Thucydides, who was of the political faction opposing Pericles. He was accused not only of impiety but also of holding secret communications with the Persians and was condemned to death in his absence. When news reached him at the same time of both the death of his sons (who, according to Aelian, were two in number—all he had) and of his own condemnation, he said regarding the latter: "Nature long ago condemned both them and me to..."

# ANAXAGORAS.

[...condemned] to death. Regarding his sons (to which he responded with a calm look): "You tell me nothing new or unexpected; I knew that I begot them mortal." Some ascribe this saying to *Solon*, and others to *Xenophon*. *Demetrius Phalerus* says that he buried them with his own hands.

<margin>Plutarch, *Consolation to Apollonius*; Simplicius, *Commentary on Epictetus*; Valerius Maximus 5.10.</margin>
*Hermippus* states that he was imprisoned to be executed, but *Pericles* appeared before the judges and asked if they knew anything in his life for which they could accuse him. When they answered "nothing," he said, "I am his disciple; therefore, do not be carried away by slanders to kill this man, but believe me and set him at liberty." Thus he was dismissed, but unable to endure the disgrace, he killed himself.

<margin>Laertius.</margin>
*Hieronymus* says that *Pericles* brought him into the court in poor clothing, weakened by sickness—an object better suited for compassion than for justice. This is what *Laertius* records regarding his trial.

<margin>Laertius.</margin>
*Suidas* writes that he was cast into prison by the Athenians for introducing a new opinion concerning God and was banished from the city, even though *Pericles* undertook to plead his cause; he adds that upon going to *Lampsacum*, he starved himself to death there.

*Josephus* states that because the Athenians believed the Sun to be a god, while Anaxagoras affirmed it was without sense or knowledge, he was condemned to death by the votes of a few of them.

<margin>Plutarch, *Life of Pericles* and *On Superstition*.</margin>
But if we believe *Plutarch*, he was neither condemned nor accused. Instead, he was sent out of the city by *Pericles*, who feared the ordinance of *Diopithes*, which summoned those who held profane or "sublime" <note>In this context, theories concerning celestial or meteorological phenomena that challenged traditional religious beliefs.</note> opinions. Yet, elsewhere Plutarch admits he was accused.

<margin>Cicero, *Tusculan Disputations*, Book 1; Plutarch, *Political Precepts*.</margin>
His departure from *Athens*, occurring 30 years after his arrival there, happened in the third year of the 82nd Olympiad, in the 63rd year of his age. From there he went to *Lampsacum*, where he lived for the remainder of his life, which extended another 22 years. He was so little concerned with *Athens* or his own country that when someone told him he was deprived of the Athenians, he answered, "No, they are deprived of me." To his friends, who asked him when he fell sick if he wished to be carried back to *Clazomenae*, his native country, he replied, "No, there is no need; the way to the grave is the same from everywhere." Before he died, the city magistrates asked if there was anything they could do for him. He answered that his only request was that the boys might be given leave to play annually on the day of the month when he died; *Laertius* notes that this custom continues to this day. The people of *Lampsacum* buried him magnificently with this epitaph:

->*Here lies Anaxagoras, who traveled through the truest paths*<-
->*Of the celestial world.*<-
