<margin>Aelian</margin>
* It is observed that he was never seen to either laugh or smile.

<margin>Laertius</margin>
* When asked if the mountains of Lampsacum <note>A Greek city in Asia Minor</note> would in time become the sea, he answered, "Yes, if time does not run out first."

<margin>Laertius</margin>
* Upon seeing the tomb of Mausolus, he said that a sumptuous monument was a sign that one's wealth had been turned into stone.

<margin>Laertius</margin>
* He was the first to affirm that the poetry of Homer consists of virtue and justice, to which Metrodorus added that the poet was skilled in natural philosophy.

<margin>Stobaeus</margin>
* He believed that there are two lessons regarding death: the time before our birth, and sleep.

Laertius and Clement of Alexandria assert that he was the first of the philosophers to publish a book. He wrote:

<margin>Aristotle, *Physics*, book 5; Plutarch, *Against Colotes*</margin>
***On Natural Philosophy***, from which Aristotle cites these fragments: "*All things were together*," which was the beginning of the book; and, "*To be such is to be changed.*"

<margin>Plato, *Greater Hippias*, Book 2</margin>
* Plato cites this: "*The Mind is the arranger and cause of all things.*" * Athenaeus cites this: "*What is commonly called the 'milk of the hen' is the white of the egg.*" * Plato criticizes the book for not using the Mind <note>Greek: *Nous*</note> at all, nor assigning any cause for the order of things except for aerial, ethereal, and aquatic natures, and similar incredible things as causes.

<margin>Plutarch, *On Exile*</margin>
***The Quadrature of the Circle***: a treatise which Plutarch says he composed during his imprisonment.

<margin>Laertius</margin>
* There were three others of the same name: the first an orator and follower of Isocrates; the second a sculptor mentioned by Antigonus; the last a grammarian and student of Zenodotus.

<meta>This page transitions from the life of Anaxagoras to his student Archelaus, providing a biographical sketch and a summary of his physical and ethical doctrines.</meta>

<page-num>19</page-num>

# ARCHELAUS.

<margin>Laertius</margin> Archelaus was either an Athenian or a Milesian; his father was Apollodorus, or according to some, Mylon. He was a student of Anaxagoras and the master of Socrates. He first transferred natural philosophy from Ionia to Athens (though Casaubon <note>Isaac Casaubon, a renowned classical scholar</note> rightly questions how that can be, since his master Anaxagoras taught there for thirty years). Therefore, he was called *the Natural Philosopher*. With him, natural philosophy ended, as his student Socrates introduced morality. However, he also seems to have touched upon moral philosophy, for he treated laws and things honest and just. Since Socrates received his learning from him and expanded upon it, Socrates is therefore thought to have invented it. Yet, as Gassendi <note>Pierre Gassendi, a French philosopher and scientist</note> observes, moral philosophy was far more ancient; it was the primary basis for the title of "wise" conferred upon the Seven Sages, whose learning lay chiefly in that direction. But Socrates is called its author because he was the first to reduce it to a science. Archelaus asserted:

<margin>Stobaeus</margin> *That the principles of all things are twofold: one incorporeal—the mind (though not the maker of the world)—and the other corporeal, infinite in number and dissimilar,* <margin>Plutarch, *Opinions of the Philosophers*, Book 3</margin> *which is the air and its rarefaction and condensation, of which one is fire and the other water.*
*That the Universe is infinite.*
*That the causes of generation are two: heat and cold.*
*That the stars are burning iron plates.*
*That the Sun is the greatest of stars.*
*That the Sea is created by the filtration of water through the hollow parts of the Earth.*
*That living creatures are generated from slime or warm Earth, which emits a milky kind of slime like chyle <note>A milky fluid produced during digestion</note>; that this humid matter being dissolved by fire, the part of it which settles into a fiery substance becomes earth, while that which evaporates becomes air.*
<margin>Seneca, *Natural Questions*, 6.12</margin> *That when winds get into the hollow places of the Earth and fill all the spaces, and the air is condensed as much as possible, the wind that comes next presses upon the first, forcing and disturbing it with frequent impulses. As this wind seeks room through narrow places and endeavors to break out of its prison, it happens that while the wind struggles for passage, the earth is moved <note>i.e., an earthquake occurs</note>.*
Of the definition of the Voice, which Plutarch attributed to Anaxagoras, Laertius makes Archelaus the author, describing it as a *percussion of the air.*
*That what is just or dishonest is defined by Law, not by Nature.*
<margin>Plutarch, Laertius</margin> These five—Anaximander, Anaximenes, Thales, Anaxagoras, and Archelaus—succeeding one another in a continuous descent, complete the Ionic sect.

# SOCRATES.

## CHAPTER I.

### Socrates: His Country, Parents, and Time of Birth.

<margin>* Laertius.</margin>
<image-desc>A large decorative drop cap 'S' at the beginning of the text, featuring ornate foliage and scrollwork within a square frame.</image-desc>
Socrates was by country an Athenian, born at Alopece, a town—according to Suidas and Phavorinus—belonging to the Antiochian tribe. This was one of those small villages scattered through Attica before Theseus consolidated the people within the walls of a city; despite his decree, these villages were not deserted but continued to be preserved by their inhabitants.

<margin>
* Laertius; Plato's *Theaetetus*; *Alcibiades*; Libanius.
* Laertius, Book 2; Valerius Maximus, "stone-cutter" <note>original: "marmorarius"</note>.
* *Theaetetus*.
* *Deipnosophistae*, Book 5.
* Libanius, *Apology*.
</margin>
His parents were of very humble rank. His father, Sophroniscus (an Athenian), was a statuary, or a carver of stone images; his mother, Phanareta, was a midwife—a woman of a bold, generous, and quick spirit, as implied by the character Plato gives her (though this was distorted by Athenaeus). Regarding his parents' professions, it is observed that he was so far from being ashamed of them that he often took the opportunity to mention them.

<margin>
* *Apology*; Laertius.
* Book 11.
* Archonship 2. 7.
* *Dialogue on the Writings of Socrates*.
</margin>
Apollodorus, Laertius, and Suidas affirm he was born in the fourth year of the 77th Olympiad. This may likewise be gathered from the marble at Arundel House <note>The Arundel Marbles, a collection of ancient Greek sculptures and inscriptions</note>, which says he died when Laches was <term>Archon</term> <note>A chief magistrate of Athens</note> and reckons seventy years of his life. This lifespan was complete, because Plato says he lived "more than seventy" <note>original: "πλείω ἑβδομήκοντα"</note>. This is also supported by Demetrius Phalereus (who was himself Archon in the fourth year of the 117th Olympiad), who says Socrates died in the first year of the 95th Olympiad after he had lived seventy years. Counting seventy years inclusively backward, we arrive at the fourth year of the 77th Olympiad, when Apsephion (or, as some call him, Aphepsion) was Archon. In the records of Diodorus Siculus, no more of this Archon’s name remains than "Phaedrus" <note>original: "φαίδρῳ"</note>, which should be "Phaedo" <note>original: "φαίδωνι"</note> but has been carelessly altered to "Phaedrus." If Meursius had observed this, he would not have corrected Laertius without cause, nor would he and Allatius have followed the mistake of Scaliger (whom they call "Anonymous") in placing Apsephion in the fourth year of the 74th Olympiad.

<margin>
* Laertius.
* *Various History*, Book 2.
</margin>
The day of Socrates' birth, according to Apollodorus, was the sixth of the month Thargelion <note>roughly equivalent to May/June</note>. This day was memorable (says Laertius) for the birth of Diana, according to the traditions of the Delians; it was also the day upon which the Athenians performed a yearly <term>lustration</term> <note>a ceremonial purification</note> of their city. Many other instances of good fortune happening to the Athenians upon this day are recorded by Aelian. The day following, namely the seventh...

<summary>This page introduces the life of Socrates, detailing his humble parentage as the son of a stone-carver and a midwife, and provides a scholarly chronological argument for his birth in the 77th Olympiad.</summary>
<keywords>Socrates, Alopece, Sophroniscus, Phanareta, Archon, Olympiad, Thargelion, lustration, genealogy, chronology</keywords>

--- Page 178 ---
<meta>This page continues the chronological discussion of Socrates' birth, specifically reconciling the Athenian calendar with the Julian and Gregorian systems, noting the proximity of his birthday to Plato's.</meta>

<header>SOCRATES.</header>

<margin>
* Porphyry, *Life of Pythagoras*.
Plutarch, *Symposiacs* 8.1.
</margin>
The [seventh] of this month was the birthday of *Plato*. Both dates were observed with great solemnity by the Greek philosophers (even until the time of *Plotinus*), as is affirmed by *Plutarch. Plutarch observes that it was a result of Providence rather than chance that their birthdays were so close, and that the Master's birthday preceded the student's.

Reconciling this date with our modern calendar is neither easy nor certain. However, because it may provide some satisfaction as a conjecture, we shall base it upon these hypotheses, following the order of months provided by *Petavius*:

1. That after the start of the Olympiads, the beginning of the Greek year was always on the first of *Hecatombaion*, and the Olympic games were held on the 15th.

2. That the *Neomenia* <term>the new moon marking the first of the month</term> of *Hecatombaion* did (at least during the period we are investigating) never precede the solstice. Since the solstice was then around the Calends <term>the first day of the month</term>, or the day before the Calends of July—and they assumed them to be in the eighth degree of the signs—it did not precede the ninth of *July*. This postulate, though twice questioned by *Petavius*, is not contradicted by any of his arguments concerning the period after *Meton's* time.

3. That based on that supposition, if *Scaliger* has correctly ordered the *Neomenia* in his Olympic period (against which *Petavius* brings no single sufficient argument), and consequently the rest of the dates, then the Olympic period definitely reveals the *Neomenia* of *Hecatombaion*. It is true that *Petavius* disputes the period of 76 years, as it was never used until *Calippus's* time, but we use it here only proleptically <term>assigning a date to an event that happened before the calendar system was established</term>, just like the Julian year to which we wish to reconcile it.

4. That since this was after *Solon's* time, the civil year in *Athens* was lunar (consisting of months that alternated between 29 and 30 days), although various parts of Greece—especially those further away from Athens—did not abandon their thirty-day months for a long time afterward.

Assuming these points, the sixth of *Thargelion* (according to the Julian calendar used proleptically) falls on Tuesday, the twentieth of *May*. According to the Gregorian calendar, it falls on Tuesday, the thirtieth of *May*, in the year 4247 of the Julian period, 467 years before the Incarnation, in the fourth year of the 77th Olympiad—the time when *Socrates* was born.

<meta>The text concludes the technical dating of Socrates' birth and moves toward the next chapter of his life.</meta>

<summary>This page provides a detailed chronological analysis to determine the exact date of Socrates' birth, reconciling the ancient Athenian lunar calendar with the Julian and Gregorian systems. It also notes that Socrates and Plato shared birthday celebrations among later philosophers.</summary>
<keywords>Socrates, Plato, chronology, Athenian calendar, Hecatombaion, Thargelion, Petavius, Scaliger, Julian period, Olympiad</keywords>

<header>SOCRATES.</header>

## CHAPTER II.

### *His Early Education.*

<margin>* Plutarch, *On the Daemon of Socrates*.</margin>
Plutarch says that as soon as he was born, his father *Sophroniscus*, consulting the Oracle, was advised by it to allow his son to do whatever he pleased—never compelling him to do what he disliked, nor diverting him from that to which he was inclined. He was told to give thanks for the boy by sacrifice to *Jupiter Agoraeus* <note>The aspect of Jupiter/Zeus associated with the marketplace and public assembly.</note> and the *Muses*, and to be no further anxious for him, for he had a guide for his life within him <note>A reference to Socrates' "daemon" or inner divine voice.</note> who was better than five hundred masters.

<margin>* Aristoxenus, cited in Theodoret, *Cure of Greek Affections*, Book 12.</margin>
But his father, not following the Oracle's direction, set him to his own trade of carving statues, contrary to the boy's inclination. Because of this, some have accused him of disobedience, reporting that many times when his father commanded him to work, he refused and went away to follow his own will.

<margin>* Plato, *Apology of Socrates*; Diogenes Laertius, Book 9; Aristophanes, *The Clouds*, line 36.5.</margin>
When his father died, he left him (according to *Libanius*) eighty *minae* <term>an ancient Greek unit of currency</term>. These were entrusted to a friend to be invested, but the money was lost. Despite this loss (though it was his entire inheritance and he was thereby reduced to incredible poverty), *Socrates* passed over it in silence; however, he was then forced to continue his trade for his basic survival. *Suidas* suggests this when he says Socrates was first a stone carver. *Duris*, *Pausanias*, and the *commentator on *Aristophanes* affirm that the three statues of the *Graces*—which were clothed (for they were made that way in ancient times, not naked)—set up before the entrance to the Acropolis <note>referred to as "the Tower" in the text</note> at *Athens*, were his work. *Pausanias* says the same of a statue of *Mercury* in that same place. *Pliny* seems not to have understood this, as he says they were made by a certain person named *Socrates*, but not the philosopher. Regarding this, *Timon* wrote:

-> From these beginnings the eloquent sculptor came, <-
-> Honored throughout Greece, who did against the title <-
-> Of "Orator" abusively declaim. <-

But being naturally averse to this profession, he only followed it when necessity forced him. *Aristoxenus* says he worked for money and saved what he earned until it became a small fund; when that was spent, he returned to the same work.

<margin>* Diogenes Laertius; Diogenes Laertius, *Life of Crito*.</margin>
These breaks from his trade were spent on Philosophy, to which he was naturally devoted. When this was observed by *Crito*, a wealthy philosopher of *Athens*, *he took Socrates from his shop—being much in love with his frankness and natural talent—and instructed him, or rather provided the means for him to be instructed by others. Crito took *so much care of him that he never

<sig>Pp</sig>
<meta>catchword: suffer'd</meta>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Socrates' financial status and introduces his various teachers in philosophy, music, rhetoric, and other sciences.</meta>

<header>SOCRATES.</header>

<margin>
* Aelian, *Various History*, 2; Plutarch, *On the Utility of Enemies*.
* Plutarch, *Comparison of the Lives of Aristides and Cato*.
* Plato, *Apology*.
</margin>
...allowed him to lack the necessities of life. Although his poverty was at first so great that it was turned into a proverb by some, he eventually became—as Demetrius affirms—the owner of a house and eighty minae <term>an ancient Greek unit of currency</term>, which Crito invested for him. But his mind (says Libanius) *was raised far above his circumstances, and more to the advantage of his country. Not aiming for wealth or the acquisition of it through dishonest arts, he considered that of all things which man can call his own, the soul is the most important; that he alone is truly happy who purifies the soul from vice; and that the only means of achieving this is wisdom. In pursuit of wisdom, he neglected all other paths to profit and pleasure.*

# CHAPTER I.

## His Teachers.

<margin>
* Diogenes Laertius.
</margin>
The first master of Socrates was Anaxagoras. From this and other circumstances, it is demonstrable that the account of Laertius is incorrect, as Anaxagoras did not die in the 78th but in the 88th Olympiad <term>a four-year period used for dating in ancient Greece</term>.

<margin>
* Cicero, *Tusculan Disputations*, 5.
* Diogenes Laertius.
</margin>
Aristoneus says that as soon as Anaxagoras left the city, Socrates applied himself to Archelaus, which according to Porphyry was in the 17th year of his age. He was much beloved by Archelaus and traveled with him to Samos, to Pytho, and to the Isthmus.

<margin>
* Plato.
</margin>
He was also a student of Damon, whom Plato calls a most delightful teacher of music and all other things that he would teach to young men. Damon was a student of Agathocles and a teacher to Pericles, Clinias, and others; he was also an intimate friend of Prodicus. He was banished by the unjust ostracism <term>a procedure where a citizen could be expelled from Athens for ten years</term> of the Athenians because of his excellence in music.

<margin>
* Plato, *Meno*.
</margin>
He also heard (as he himself acknowledges) Prodicus the Sophist, a native of Ceos, whom Eusebius ranks in the 86th Olympiad, contemporary with Gorgias, Hippias, and Hippocrates the physician.

To these add Diotyma and Aspasia, women who were excellently learned; the first was supposed to have been inspired with a prophetic spirit. By her, he affirms that he was instructed concerning love: how to use physical beauty to discover the beauty of the soul and the angelic minds of God. See Plato's *Phaedrus* and that long discourse in his *Symposium* upon this subject, which Socrates confesses he owes to her.

<margin>
* Plato, *Menexenus*.
</margin>
Aspasia was a famous Milesian woman, not only excellent herself in rhetoric, but she also brought many students to great perfection in it, among whom were Pericles the Athenian and (as he himself acknowledges) Socrates.

<margin>
* Maximus of Tyre.
* Plato, *Theaetetus*.
* Scholion on Aristophanes' *The Clouds*.
* Epistles, 9, 22.
</margin>
From Euenus he learned poetry; from Ischomachus, husbandry; and from Theodorus, geometry. Aristagoras, a Melian, is also named as his master. Last in this catalog is Connus, a <note>original: "nobilissimus fidicen"</note> most renowned lyre-player, as Cicero calls him.

<meta>catchword: ro</meta>

<summary>This page lists the many diverse teachers of Socrates, ranging from natural philosophers like Anaxagoras to rhetoricians like Aspasia and musicians like Connus. It also addresses the conflicting historical accounts regarding his personal wealth and his philosophical commitment to prioritizing the soul over material gain.</summary>

# CHAPTER IV.
### Of His School and Manner of Teaching.

<margin>* Aristophanes</margin>
<margin>* The Clouds</margin>
That *Socrates* had a formal school may be inferred from *Aristophanes*, who mocks certain details of it and calls it his *Phrontisterion* <term>Greek for "thinking-shop" or place of study</term>.

<margin>* Phaedrus</margin>
<margin>* Socratic Epistles</margin>
*Plato* and *Phaedrus* mention several places frequented by him and his listeners: the *Academy*, the *Lyceum*, and a pleasant meadow outside the city by the side of the River *Ilissus*, where a very beautiful plane tree grew. According to the myth, *Boreas* <term>the north wind</term> kidnapped *Orithyia* from there; three furlongs from that spot, there was a temple dedicated to her, and another to *Diana* <term>Artemis</term>.

<margin>* Xenophon, Memorabilia, 1.</margin>
*Xenophon* asserts that he was constantly out in public. In the morning, he visited the places for public walking and exercise when they were full, as well as the *Forum* <term>the Agora or marketplace</term>. For the rest of the day, he sought out the most populous gatherings, where he debated openly for anyone who wished to hear.

<margin>* Plutarch, *Whether an Old Man Should Engage in Public Affairs*</margin>
He did not only teach, says *Plutarch*, when the benches were prepared and he was in the professor's chair, or during set hours of reading and lecture, or at scheduled walks with his friends. Instead, he taught even when he played, when he ate or drank, when he was in the army camp or the market, and finally even when he was in prison. In this way, he made every place a school of virtue.

<margin>* Quintilian, Book 9, Chapter 2.</margin>
<margin>* Cicero, *On the Orator*, 2.</margin>
<margin>* Cicero, *Academic Questions*, 4.</margin>
His manner of teaching aligned with his opinion that the soul existed before the body. He believed that in its first separate state, the soul was endowed with perfect knowledge, but upon being immersed in matter, it became stunned and essentially lost until awakened by dialogue concerning physical objects. Through this, the soul gradually recovers its original knowledge. For this reason, he taught only through Irony and Induction. *Quintilian* defines the first (Irony) as a complete concealment of one's will, where the intent is more apparent than openly stated. In this method, the words differ from the literal meaning, and the sense differs from the speech; the entire presentation of the case—even one's whole life—seems to carry an irony. Such was the life of *Socrates*, who for that reason was called an *eiron* <note>original: εἴρων</note>; that is, one who pretends to be unlearned and acts as an admirer of others' wisdom. *In this irony (says *Cicero*) and pretense, he far exceeded all men in wit and urbanity <term>social refinement and wit</term>. It is an elegant, sweet, and witty style of speech, sharp yet serious, refined with rhetorical words and pleasant talk. *In disputes, he downplayed his own abilities and attributed more to those he intended to refute. Thus, even when he said or thought something else, he freely used that pretense which the Greeks call Irony (which *Annius* says was also a trait of *Africanus*).

<margin>* Cicero, *On Invention*, 1.</margin>
*Induction* is defined by *Cicero* as a style of discourse which gains the assent of the person with whom it is held using undisputed facts. By these [assents]...

<summary>This page describes the public nature of Socrates' teaching in Athens and introduces his primary philosophical methods: Irony (pretending ignorance to expose others' lack of knowledge) and Induction (leading an opponent to a conclusion through agreed-upon facts).</summary>
<keywords>Socrates, Connus, Aristophanes, Phrontisterion, Academy, Lyceum, Ilissus, Xenophon, Plutarch, Irony, eiron, Cicero, Induction, Socratic Method</keywords>

--- Page 182 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the Socratic method, specifically focusing on his use of induction and his "midwife" approach to teaching, before transitioning into a defense of his broad learning against critics like Porphyry.</meta>

<margin>
* Plato, *Laches*.
* Libanius, *Apology*.
</margin>
...agrees that it causes him to yield to a doubtful point because of its similarity to the things he has already agreed to. Socrates used this kind of speech most often because he did not want to use persuasive arguments himself. Instead, he chose to draw something out from what was granted by the person with whom he disputed; because of what the opponent had already conceded, they were forced to approve of the conclusion. Socrates provides a significant example of this in Plato’s *Meno*. Thus, whoever debated with him on any subject (his only goal being to promote virtue) was eventually led to give an account of their past and present life. Once they entered that discussion, he never gave up on them until he had sufficiently examined those matters, and—like Proteus <note>A Greek sea-god who could change his shape to avoid being caught; here it refers to how Socrates' opponents would try to shift their arguments to escape his logic.</note>—he never let them go until they finally faced themselves.

<margin>
* Plato, *Euthyphro*.
* Plato, *Theaetetus*; Plutarch, *Platonic Questions*, 1.
* Scholiast on Aristophanes' *The Clouds*, p. 129.
* Plato, *Apology*.
</margin>
For this reason, he used to say that his skill had some similarity to his mother's. He was like a midwife who, though barren himself (as he modestly claimed), used a special gift to help others bring forth what they already had within themselves. This was one reason why he refused to take money, asserting that he knew nothing himself and that he was never a master to anyone.

<margin>
* Diogenes Laertius, *Life of Xenophon*.
* Diogenes Laertius, *Life of Plato*.
* *Epistle to Aeschines*.
* 14. 3.
</margin>
These disputes of *Socrates* were recorded in writing by his students. *Xenophon* set the example for the others by being the first to do so, and also the most precise. *Plato*, however, took the most liberty, mixing in so much of his own thought that it is not easy to distinguish the master from the student. When *Socrates* heard him recite his *Lysis*, he remarked, "How many things does this young man invent about me?" And while *Xenophon* denied that *Socrates* ever debated about the heavens, natural causes, or the other disciplines which the Greeks call *mathemata* <term>Greek for subjects of study or mathematical sciences</term>, he said that those who attribute such discussions to him lie falsely. In this, as Aulus Gellius observes, he refers to *Plato*, in whose books *Socrates* discusses natural philosophy, music, and geometry.

## CHAPTER V.
### Of his Philosophy.

<margin>
* Nicephorus, *Ecclesiastical History*, 10. 36.
* Theodoret.
</margin>
Porphyry (who was so abusive, as *Nicephorus* observes, that he slandered *Socrates* with as much bitterness as if he were trying to outdo his accusers, *Anytus* and *Meletus*) claimed that he was talented in nothing, unlearned in everything, and barely able to write—and that when he did write, it was a joke. He even claimed Socrates could read no better than a stammering schoolboy. To this, we shall oppose these authorities: *Xenophon*, who testifies that he was excellent in all kinds of learning, specifically citing arithmetic, geometry, and astrology; *Plato*, in natural philosophy; *Idomeneus*, in rhetoric; and *Diogenes Laertius*, in medicine. In short, *Cicero* declares that, by the testimony of learned men and the judgment of all Greece,

<summary>This page describes Socrates' "midwife" method of teaching and the historical debate over his literacy and range of knowledge, contrasting Xenophon's and Plato's accounts.</summary>
<keywords>Socratic method, induction, mid-wifery, Plato, Xenophon, Porphyry, mathemata, education, philosophical sources</keywords>

--- Page 183 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the description of Socrates' personal methods of dialogue (the "midwife" approach) to a systematic overview of how he redefined the scope of philosophy, moving it away from abstract natural sciences toward ethics and practical human life.</meta>

# SOCRATES.

In Greece, whether in wisdom, sharpness, refinement, and subtlety, or in eloquence, variety, and abundance—no matter what subject he applied himself to—he was, without exception, the prince of all.

<margin>* Xenophon, *Memorabilia*, Book 1, page 710.</margin>
Having investigated all kinds of science, he observed these inconveniences and imperfections: First, that it was improper to abandon those affairs which concern mankind to inquire into things outside ourselves. Secondly, that these things are beyond the reach of man, which causes all disputes and disagreements—some acknowledging no God, others worshipping idols <note>original: "stocks and stones"</note>; some asserting one simple being, others infinite; some that all things are in motion, others that all things are immovable. And thirdly, that these things, even if understood, could not be put into practice; for he who contemplates divine mysteries and inquires by what necessity things were made cannot make anything himself, or produce winds, waters, seasons, or the like when needed.

Thus, valuing theoretical knowledge only as far as it leads to practice, he cut out from all sciences whatever he considered of least use: <margin>* Xenophon, *Memorabilia*, Book 4.</margin> In *Arithmetic*, he approved only as much as was necessary <gloss>(* Plato gives examples in trade and military tactics)</gloss>, but he rejected proceeding to useless operations. <margin>* From *The Laws*.</margin> In *Geometry*, he allowed that part which teaches measurement as being no less easy than useful; however, he rejected proceeding to infinite propositions and demonstrations as being completely unprofitable. In *Astrology* <note>In this context, "astrology" refers to astronomy.</note>, he approved of the knowledge of the stars and the observation of the night, months, and seasons, as these are easily learned and very beneficial in navigation and for those who hunt by night; but he discouraged examining the difference between celestial spheres, the distance of the stars from the earth, and their orbits as useless.

<margin>* Laertius.</margin>
Finally, noting how little advantage theoretical speculation brought to the life and conduct of mankind, he turned philosophy toward action. <margin>* Cicero, *Academic Questions*, Book 1.</margin> He was the first, says *Cicero*, who called Philosophy away from things hidden by nature in secrecy—where, until his time, all philosophers had been employed—and brought her to common life to inquire about virtues and vices, good and evil.

**Man**, who was the sole subject of his philosophy, having a twofold relation to divine speculation and human social conduct, his doctrines were *metaphysical* in the former respect and *moral* in the latter.

<sig>Qq</sig>
<meta>catchword: Sect.</meta>

<summary>This page describes how Socrates redirected philosophy from the study of natural phenomena and abstract science toward ethics and the practical conduct of human life. He limited the study of arithmetic, geometry, and astronomy to their practical applications, arguing that human affairs should be the primary focus of wisdom.</summary>

# SOCRATES.

### Sect. 1. Metaphysics.

His metaphysical opinions are collected and summarized from *Plato*, *Xenophon*, *Plutarch*, and others as follows:

<margin>Plato, *Phaedo*.</margin>
> "Philosophy is the way to true happiness, the functions of which are twofold: to contemplate God and to separate the soul from physical sensations.

<margin>Plutarch, *On the Sentiments of the Philosophers*, Book 1, Chapter 3.</margin>
> "There are three principles of all things: God, matter, and Ideas. God is the universal intellect; matter is the primary substance subject to creation and decay; Idea is an incorporeal substance, the intellect of God. God is the intellect of the world.

<margin>Plato, *Phaedo*.</margin>
> "God is one, <note>original: "τὸ ἓν αὐτῷ ἔχοντα ἕκαστον αὐτὰ τὰ πολλά, αὐτὸ τὸ ζωπύριον"</note> the one having in himself each of these many things, the very spark of life; he is perfect in himself, giving existence and well-being to every creature. What he is (Socrates says), I do not know; what he is not, I do know.

<margin>*Xenophon, *Memorabilia*, Book 1.</margin>
> "That God, not chance, made the world and all creatures is demonstrable from the logical arrangement of their parts, both for utility and defense, and from their instinct to preserve themselves and continue their species. He particularly regards man in his body, seen in his excellent upright form and the gift of speech; and by allowing him <note>original: "τὰς τροφὰς δι’ ἐπινοίας καὶ συνεχῶς παρέχειν"</note> to provide nourishment through foresight and continuity; and in his soul, from its excellence above others. In both, he provides for divination and the prediction of dangers. He cares for individuals based on his care for the entire species. He will reward those who please him and punish those who displease him, according to his power to do so, and from the belief he has imprinted in man that he will do it—a belief held by the wisest and most civilized cities and eras. He sees all things at once, just as the eye can scan many miles at once, and the mind can consider things happening in distant places. Finally, he is so great that he simultaneously sees all, hears all, is everywhere, and governs all. This is the summary of his discourse with *Aristodemus*, to which we may add what is cited under his name (if not mistaken) by *Stobaeus*:

He also provided reason, by which man reasons through the recollection of physical objects, and speech, by which he communicates all that he knows, establishes laws, and governs states. Despite being invisible, God exists, as evidenced by his ministers, who are also invisible—such as thunder and wind—and from the human soul, which shares something with the divine nature by governing the body, even though it cannot be seen. This is the essence of his discourse with Euthydemus.

## Sect. 2. Ethics.

His morals consider a man either as an individual, as the father of a family, or as a member of a commonwealth. The first category comprises his Ethics, in which such sayings as have been preserved by Xenophon, Diogenes Laertius, Stobaeus, and others, are collected as follows:

### Of virtue and vice.

<margin>Xenophon, *Memorabilia*, Book 1, page 720.</margin>
HE exhorted his friends to: “Strive to be as wise and beneficial as possible, because that which lacks reason lacks respect—just like the bodies of dead friends, or hair, nails, and the like, which are cut off and thrown away.”

<summary>This page details Socratic metaphysical arguments for the soul's immortality and pre-existence, alongside the beginning of his ethical system which categorizes human duty toward the self, family, and state.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the ethical and philosophical sayings of Socrates, focusing on his views on industry versus idleness, the inseparable nature of justice and happiness, and the definition of virtue as a singular essence rather than a collection of roles.</meta>

# SOCRATES.

<margin>Xenophon, *Memorabilia*, Book 1, page 720.</margin>
“To be employed is good and beneficial; to be idle is harmful and evil. Those who do good are employed; those who spend their time in vain recreations are idle.

<margin>Xenophon, *Memorabilia*, Book 3, page 778.</margin>
“He who has the greatest natural advantages, as well as he who has the least, must learn and meditate on those things in which he wishes to excel.

<margin>Xenophon, *Memorabilia*, Book 3, page 779.</margin>
“Only he is truly idle who could be better employed.

<margin>Xenophon, *Memorabilia*, Book 3, page 78.</margin>
“To do good is the best course of life; in that, fortune has no share. <note>Socrates argues that practicing virtue is a choice within one's control, unlike luck or "fortune."</note>

<margin>Xenophon, *Memorabilia*, Book 3, page 780.</margin>
“They are the best, and most pleasing to God, who do anything with skill or a professional calling; he who follows none is useless to the public and hated by God.

<margin>Clement of Alexandria, *Miscellanies*, Book 2, page 417; Cicero, *On Duties*, Book 3; *On Laws*, Book 1.</margin>
He taught everywhere “that a just man and a happy man were one and the same,” and used “to curse him who first, by his own opinion, divided integrity and profit (which are naturally linked) as having committed an impious act. For they are truly wicked who separate what is profitable from what is just, which depends on law.” The Stoics followed him so far in this that they esteem whatever is honorable to be the same as what is profitable.

<margin>Plutarch, *On Having Many Friends*, page 93.</margin>
He asked Memnon, a Thessalian—who thought himself very learned and believed he had reached (as Empedocles says) the top of wisdom—“What is virtue?” He answered readily and boldly that “there is one virtue for a child, another for an old man, one for a man, another for a woman, one for a magistrate, another for a private person, one for a master, and another for a servant.” “Very good,” replied Socrates, “I ask for one virtue, and you give us a whole swarm.” He truly understood that a man does not know what virtue is if he can only name so many different types of it.

<margin>Plutarch, *On the Education of Children*.</margin>
When asked by Gorgias if he did not account the Great King of Persia to be happy, Socrates answered, “I do not know how well he is provided with learning and virtue.” He believed that true happiness consists of these two things, and not in the fragile gifts of fortune.

<margin>Diogenes Laertius, *Lives of the Philosophers*.</margin>
When Euripides said in his play *Auge* that it is best to carelessly part with virtue, Socrates rose and left, saying, “It was ridiculous to go looking for a lost servant while allowing virtue to be lost so easily.”

<margin>Diogenes Laertius, *Lives of the Philosophers*.</margin>
He said, “He wondered at those who carve images out of stone, taking such care to make the stones resemble men, while they neglect themselves and allow themselves to resemble stones.”

<margin>Diogenes Laertius, *Lives of the Philosophers*. <note>What "small" (*mikron*) means is explained by Aristotle in *Politics*, Book 5, Chapter 3, and *Physics*, Book 5, Chapter 4.</note></margin>
He advised “young men to look at themselves every day in a mirror; if they were beautiful, they should study to deserve that beauty; if they were deformed, they should seek to hide or compensate for it through learning.”

<margin>Diogenes Laertius, *Lives of the Philosophers*; Clement of Alexandria, *Miscellanies*, Book 5.</margin>
He said, “To begin well is no small thing, even though it depends on a small moment.”

He said, “Virtue was the beauty, and vice the deformity, of the soul.”

### Of affections, Love, Envy, Grief, Hope, etc.

<margin>Xenophon, *Memorabilia*, Book 2</margin> That “God intended two brothers to be more helpful to each other than two hands, feet, eyes, or whatever else nature has formed; doubtless because, if they love one another, they may help each other even from a great distance.” This is the purpose <term>scope: the aim or intended theme</term> of his discourse with *Chaerecrates*.

<margin>Xenophon, *Memorabilia*, Book 3</margin> That “all things are good and fair regarding those things with which they agree, but bad and deformed in respect to those things with which they do not agree,” is the conclusion of his second discourse with *Aristippus*.

<margin>Xenophon, *Memorabilia*, Book 3</margin> “Envy is a grief, not at the adversity of friends, nor the prosperity of enemies, but at the prosperity of friends; for many are so foolishly inclined as to resent <term>maligne: to feel or speak ill of</term> those in good fortune, whom they pitied in misfortune.”

<margin>Stobaeus, Book 1</margin> “A ship ought not to trust in one anchor, nor life in one hope.”

<margin>Stobaeus, Book 16</margin> “To ground hopes on a poor opinion is to trust a ship to a flimsy <term>slight: weak or frail</term> anchor.”

<margin>Stobaeus, Book 139</margin> “The beauty of fame is blasted by envy as if by a sickness.”

<margin>Stobaeus, Book 139</margin> “Many adorn the tombs of those whom they persecuted with envy while they were living.”

<margin>Stobaeus, Book 139</margin> “Envy is the saw of the soul.” <note>This metaphor suggests that envy grates or wears away at the spirit of the person who feels it.</note>

<margin>Stobaeus, Book 144</margin> “Nothing is pleasant or unpleasant by nature, but by habit <term>custom: established habit or social practice</term>.”

<margin>Stobaeus, Book 215</margin> “Inappropriate <term>unseasonable: ill-timed or misplaced</term> love is like hate.”

<margin>Stobaeus, Book 240</margin> Being asked, “What is grievous to the good?” he answered, “the prosperity of the wicked.”

<margin>Stobaeus, Book 240</margin> Being asked “how a man might live without trouble,” he answered; “it was not possible, for he who lives in a city or a family must sometimes be afflicted.”

### Of Piety and Obedience.

<margin>Xenophon, *Memorabilia*, Book 2</margin>
He argued that the greatest of vices is ingratitude for obligations, especially toward parents. He stated that the gods will not bless a disobedient son, nor will men love him, because they would doubt he would return their kindness, knowing he fails to do so where so much is owed. This is the summary of his discourse with *Apiles* <note>Likely a reference to Lamprocles, Socrates' son, whose conversation on this topic is recorded in Xenophon's *Memorabilia*</note>.

<margin>Xenophon, *Memorabilia*, Book 1, 722; Book 4, 804</margin>
"Our prayers should be for blessings in general, for God knows best what is good for us. Our offerings should be proportioned to our abilities, for He considers integrity, not munificence <term>extravagant generosity or the size of a gift</term>."

<margin>Xenophon, *Memorabilia*, Book 1, 722</margin>
He said (agreeing with the Pythian Oracle) that "the gods are to be worshipped according to the law of the city where a man lives." He thought those who do otherwise were superstitious and vain.

<margin>Xenophon, *Memorabilia*, Book 4, 803</margin>
"The best way of worshipping God is to do what He commands."

<margin>Stobaeus, 43</margin>
"Superstition is as obedient to pride as if it were its parent."

<margin>Stobaeus, 193</margin>
"A harsh father, like a severe law, must nonetheless be obeyed."

<margin>Stobaeus, 201</margin>
"The reproof of a father is a kind remedy; it brings more relief than pain."

### Of fortitude and weakness.

<margin>Xenophon, *Memorabilia*, Book 1</margin>
He taught that "a man ought to accustom himself to voluntary labor and endurance, so that what is imposed by necessity may appear to be free choice rather than compulsion." He argued that soft ways of living and indulgence in pleasures produce neither a healthy body nor a knowledgeable mind, and that endurance raises us to great achievements. This is the essence of his discourse with *Aristippus*.

<margin>Xenophon, *Memorabilia*, Book 3</margin>
**To one who was fearful of traveling as far as *Olympia* on foot, he demonstrated (to make the journey seem easy) that it was no more than his daily walk within his house; if extended in a straight line, his daily movement would easily reach that distance.**

<margin>Xenophon, *Memorabilia*, Book 3</margin>
**When a man complained that he was weary from a journey, Socrates reproved him for being more tired than his servant, who followed him while carrying a heavy load.**

<margin>Plutarch, *Consolation to Apollonius*</margin>
**He said, "Death resembles either a deep sleep, a long journey out of our native country, or the absolute annihilation of soul and body." Examining all of these, he affirmed that death was in no respect an evil. As to the first, says *Plutarch*, "It is not bad for those who sleep, and we consider that sleep sweetest which is deepest." If we look on it as a journey, it is rather a blessing, for by it we are freed from the slavery and passions of the flesh which possess and infatuate the mind. In the last respect, it makes us as insensible to evil and pain as we are to good and pleasure.**

<margin>Stobaeus, *Florilegium*, Chapter 296</margin>
**"A statue stands firm on its base; a virtuous man stands firm on his resolutions."**

<margin>Stobaeus, 5</margin>

<margin>Stobaeus, 48</margin>

<margin>Stobaeus, 49</margin>

<margin>Stobaeus, 269</margin>

<margin>Aelian, *Various History*</margin>

### Of Temperance, Self-Control, and Contentment.

<margin>Xenophon, *Memorabilia*, Book 1</margin>
**He advised people to avoid all occasions for lack of self-control, affirming that those who associated much with beautiful women could not easily remain self-controlled.**

<margin>Xenophon, *Memorabilia*, Book 1</margin>
**The sum of his discourse to *Xenophon* and *Critobulus* is that "the sight and kisses of the beautiful infuse a poison more dangerous than that of scorpions and spiders."**

<margin>Xenophon, *Memorabilia*, Book 1</margin>
**The conclusion of his discourse *on self-control* <note>original: "de continentia"</note> is that "a free man ought not to employ a servant addicted to pleasures; he who is a slave to pleasures should pray to the gods for better masters."**

<margin>Xenophon, *Memorabilia*, Book 1</margin>
**The conclusion of his discourse with *Antipho* is that "happiness consists not in luxury and pride; to want nothing is divine, and to want as little as possible is next to the divine."**

<summary>This page records several of Socrates' famous observations on the nature of death, the necessity of physical and mental discipline, and his philosophical arguments for temperance and self-sufficiency. He emphasizes that true happiness and divinity are found in needing as little as possible rather than in pursuing luxury.</summary>

<margin>Xenophon, *Memorabilia*, Book 3</margin>
> He advised those who could not easily practice self-restraint at feasts to beware of things that persuade those who are not hungry to eat, and those who are not thirsty to drink; for he said such things destroy the appetite, the head, and the soul. He used to say jokingly that *Circe* turned men into swine by feasting them with such rich foods, but that *Ulysses*—partly through *Minerva’s* advice and partly through his own temperance—remained unchanged by refraining from them.

<margin>Xenophon, *Memorabilia*, Book 3</margin>
> He taught that health of the body ought to be diligently preserved, because all knowledge of the soul depends upon it. This is the main point of his discourse with *Epigenes*.

<margin>Xenophon, *Memorabilia*, Book 3</margin>
> He advised a man who complained that he took no delight in his food to refrain from eating for a while; by doing so, his diet would become more pleasant, inexpensive, and wholesome.

<margin>Xenophon, *Memorabilia*, Book 3</margin>
> Regarding the word <term>euocheisthai</term> <note>original: "εὐωχεῖσθαι"</note> (to feast), he noted that the particle <term>eu</term> <note>original: "εὐ"</note> implies that we should eat only such things as will not hurt the mind or the body and are easy to obtain.

<margin>Xenophon, *Memorabilia*, Book 4</margin>
> Only temperate persons can discern and choose the best things while refraining from the worst. Through temperance, men become the most excellent, the most happy, and the most fit for intellectual discourse; this is the summary of his conversation with *Euthydemus*.

<margin>Plutarch, *On Tranquility of Mind*</margin>
> Hearing one of his friends say, "This town is exceedingly expensive; Chian wine costs a mina, purple cloth costs three, and a pint of honey costs five denarii," Socrates led him to the flour merchants. "Look," he said, "a pint is sold here for an obolus; it is cheap living in this town." Then he took him to those who sold olives: "A choenix <note>A Greek dry measure, roughly one quart.</note> costs only two farthings." Finally, he went to the secondhand clothes dealers: "A suit costs ten drachmas; things are cheap in this town."

<margin>Diogenes Laertius</margin>
> He said that the hungry required no sauce, and the thirsty required no special choice of wines.

<margin>Diogenes Laertius</margin>
> He commended quiet and leisure above all other things.

<margin>Diogenes Laertius</margin>
> He said that those who buy early-season fruits at high prices must believe that the fruits will not arrive in their proper season.

<margin>Diogenes Laertius</margin>
> Being asked what a young man's virtue was, he answered, "To do nothing in excess."

<margin>Diogenes Laertius</margin>
> Seeing someone eat broth very greedily, he asked, "Which of you here uses bread as a side for broth, and broth as a side for bread?" Regarding this, see more at length in Xenophon’s *Memorabilia*.

<margin>Aelian, *Various History*, Book 9</margin>
When someone said that it was a great accomplishment to abstain from what a man desires, he answered, "But it is a much greater thing not to desire it at all."

<margin>Stobaeus, 28</margin>
> A clear fire is an ornament to the chimney, and serenity is an ornament to the mind.

<margin>Stobaeus, 37</margin>
> He said, "We ought not to seek pleasures in others, but in ourselves, provided the body is disciplined as it ought to be."

<margin>Stobaeus, 37</margin>
> He said, "It is the nature of God to need nothing; to need the least is to be nearest to God."

### *On Generosity, Wastefulness, and Greed.*

<margin>Xenophon, *Memorabilia*, 1</margin>
He believed that "those who accepted money from anyone acknowledged them as their masters in the lowliest form of servitude."

<margin>Xenophon, *Memorabilia*, 1</margin>
He taught that "wisdom is prostituted as much as beauty when one accepts money for it; he who meets a talented person should share all the good he can for free, thereby gaining a friend and performing the duty of a good citizen." This is the summary of his second conversation with *Antiphon*.

<margin>Saint Basil, Homily 24, *To Young Men, on How They Might Profit from Pagan Literature*</margin>
He said that if a rich man is proud of his wealth, "he could not praise him until he knew how he intended to employ it."

<margin>Stobaeus, 28</margin>
"No one can safely manage a horse without a bit, nor riches without reason."

<margin>Stobaeus, 54</margin>
He compared "greedy people to birds: one devours everything it finds until it chokes itself, while the rest, falling upon what the first left behind, are choked one after another as well."

<margin>Stobaeus, 55</margin>
"The wealth of greedy people is like the sun after it has set: it brings joy to no one."

<margin>Stobaeus, 77</margin>
"He who gives to a rich man throws water into the sea."

<margin>Stobaeus, 78</margin>
"The life of a greedy person is like the feasts made for the dead: he has everything, but enjoys nothing." <note>Ancient "feasts for the dead" (silicernium) involved food left at gravesites which the deceased could not actually consume.</note>

<margin>Stobaeus, 230</margin>
He compared "the wealth of wasteful people to fig trees growing on a cliff; no one benefits from these except kites and crows, just as only harlots and flatterers benefit from the wasteful."

<margin>Stobaeus, 230</margin>
When asked who was greedy, he answered, "Those who pursue shameful profit and neglect their true friends."

<margin>Stobaeus, 231</margin>
"Wine changes with the vessel; riches follow the character of the owner."

## *Of Magnanimity and Pride.*

<margin>Xenophon, *Memorabilia*, 1</margin>
To one who was angry because a man he greeted did not return the salute, Socrates said: “It is ridiculous if you are not angry with everyone you meet who is in worse physical shape than yourself, yet you are angry with someone for having less manners.”

## *Of Patience.*

<margin>Plutarch, *On those whom God is slow to punish*</margin>

<margin>Xenophon, *Memorabilia*, 4</margin>

### On Friendship.

<margin>Xenophon, *Memorabilia*, 2</margin>
The scope of his discourse *on friendship* <note>original: "de amicitia"</note> is that "a wise and virtuous friend is the most fruitful of all possessions and ought to be valued above all else."
<margin>Xenophon, *Memorabilia*, 2</margin>
The effect of his discourse with Antisthenes is that "every man should examine what value he is to his friend and strive to be of the greatest worth he can to him."
<margin>Xenophon, *Memorabilia*, 2</margin>
The scope of his discourse with Critobulus is that "wicked men cannot be friends, either among themselves or with the good; the way to gain friends is, first, to strive to be good in those areas where one wishes to appear good; and all virtues may be increased by study and learning."
<margin>Xenophon, *Memorabilia*, 2</margin>
The effect of his discourse with Aristarchus is that "we ought to use our utmost abilities to relieve the needs of our friends."
<margin>Plutarch, *On Brotherly Love*</margin>
He said "he would rather have Darius as his friend than his <term>darics</term> <note>Gold coins of the Persian Empire named after King Darius.</note>."
<margin>Diogenes Laertius</margin>
He wondered why "every man kept an inventory of his goods, but none of his friends."
<margin>Stobaeus, 213</margin>
"Those who violate friendship, though they may escape the punishment of their friends, shall not escape the vengeance of God."
<margin>Stobaeus, 213</margin>
"Those who forsake their own brothers to seek out other friends..."

### Section 3. <term>Economics</term> <note>In this context, the management of a household and private estate, from the Greek *Oikonomikos*.</note>.

The second area of his study is Economics, which he learned from Ischomachus. These principles were explicitly presented by Xenophon in a treatise on that subject, to which these few sentences may be added:

<margin>Xenophon, *Memorabilia*, 3</margin>
> "Design your house so that the parts facing south are highest, so the winter sun is not blocked. The parts facing north should be lowest so they are less exposed to the wind. In short, arrange it so that one can live in every part of the house with the most comfort and safety. Paintings and decorative colors often take away more pleasure than they provide."

<margin>Xenophon, *Memorabilia*, 3; Diogenes Laertius</margin>
To one who beat his servant for being gluttonous, covetous, and idle, he said, *"Did you ever consider whether you deserve to be beaten more than he does?"*

<margin>Stobaeus, 183</margin>
To one who asked his advice about taking a wife, he said, *"Whether you do or do not marry, you will regret it."*

<margin>Stobaeus, 183</margin>
To others who asked his opinion concerning marriage, he said, *"Just as fish in a net want to get out, while those outside want to get in, take care, young men, that it does not happen to you."*

<margin>Stobaeus, 193</margin>
Men must obey the laws of their country; wives must obey their husbands.

### Section 4. Politics.

The third area of his study is Politics, which Hesychius Illustrius claims are the same as those Plato presented under that title. You may find them there, though they are colored by Plato's own language and additions. To these may be added those sentences of his on the subject from Xenophon, Stobaeus, and others:

<margin>Xenophon, *Memorabilia*, 1</margin>
> "Those who cannot be useful to an army, a city, or a commonwealth when needed, yet are full of self-confidence, should be kept under restraint—no matter how rich they are."

<margin>Xenophon, *Memorabilia*, 1</margin>
Antipho asked how he could make others skilled in politics when he himself did not get involved in them, even though he knew how to manage them. Socrates replied, "Antipho, in which way do..."

### CHAP. VI.

->Of his <term>Dæmon</term> <note>A guiding or attendant spirit; not to be confused with the modern sense of "demon" as an evil entity.</note>.<-

<margin>* See chapter 2 above.</margin>
That Socrates had an attendant spirit (understood by Plutarch to be the meaning behind the Oracle's answer to his father) which warned him of dangers, is contested by Athenaeus. This is not without much prejudice, which the bitterness of his discourse betrays. "Souls that are not sincere and think ill of the best," says Origen, "never refrain from calumny, seeing that they mock even the <term>Genius</term> <note>In this context, a tutelary spirit or guardian angel.</note> of Socrates as a fabricated thing." On the <margin>* Found in Athenaeus.</margin> contrary, we have the testimony of Plato, Xenophon, and Antisthenes, who were contemporary with him; this is confirmed by Laertius, Plutarch, Maximus Tyrius, Dio Chrysostom, Cicero, Apuleius, and by the Church Fathers: Tertullian, Origen, Clement of Alexandria, and others. A <margin>Cicero, *On Divination*, book 1.</margin> great many instances of this (as Cicero says) were collected by Antipater, though only these few are preserved by other authors.

<meta>This page continues the exploration of Socrates’ "Dæmon" or divine sign, providing specific anecdotes from historical sources about how this inner voice provided warnings and guided both Socrates and his associates.</meta>

<page-num>20</page-num>
<header>SOCRATES. 20</header>

<margin>* Plutarch, *On the Sign of Socrates*</margin>
Theocritus, while going to consult Euthyphron the soothsayer, found him walking in the streets with a large group of people. Among them were Simmias and Socrates, who was very busy asking Euthyphron many questions. In the middle of his discourse, Socrates suddenly stopped. After a pause, he turned back and went down another street, calling out to the rest of the company to return and follow him, as he had been warned by the *Dæmon* <term>Dæmon: Socrates' divine inner voice or guiding spirit</term>. Most of the group did so, but the others continued forward on purpose to disprove the *Dæmon*. They brought with them a flute-player named Charillus. However, on the path—which was so narrow that they could not pass by—they were met and knocked into the mud by a large herd of pigs. Because of the repetition of such accidents, Charillus often defended the *Dæmon* afterward.

The advice of this spiritual attendant did not only concern the well-being of Socrates himself, but also extended to the friends who spoke with him, of which he gives these examples:

<margin>* Plato, *Theages*; Aelian, *Various History*, 8. 1.</margin>
Charmides, the son of Glaucon, was going to compete in the Nemean race. While he was talking with Socrates, Socrates—upon noticing the voice—dissuaded him from going. Charmides replied that perhaps the voice only meant he would not win. He said, "However, I will still benefit from the exercise," and went to the Games. There he met with an accident which, although not specifically described, is acknowledged to have justified the advice given to him by the *Dæmon*.

<margin>* Plato, *Theages*</margin>
Timarchus and Philemon, the son of Philemonides, had plotted together to murder Nicias, the son of Heroscomander <note>Original: Hircoscomander</note>. While they were drinking with Socrates, Timarchus, intending to carry out their plan, prepared to leave the table. He said to Socrates, "Well, Socrates, keep drinking; I will just step out for a moment and return immediately." "Do not leave," said Socrates (having heard the *Dæmon* as soon as Timarchus spoke), "for the *Dæmon* has given me the usual sign." Consequently, Timarchus sat back down. Shortly after, he tried to leave again, but Socrates, hearing the voice, held him back. Finally, while Socrates was distracted by something and not paying attention, Timarchus slipped away and committed the murder. For this, he was led to execution. His last words to his brother Clitomachus <note>Original: Glitomachus</note> were that he had come to this untimely end because he did not obey Socrates.

<margin>Cicero, *On Divination*, 1. 54.</margin>
Another time, seeing that his friend Crito had his eye bandaged, Socrates asked him why. Crito answered that as he was walking in a field, someone pulled a branch which snapped back and hit him in the eye. "Then you did not take my advice," replied Socrates, "for I called you back, making use, as is my custom, of divine foreknowledge."

<margin>* Plato, *Theages*</margin>
Socrates also claims that this voice had a great influence on the souls of those who associated and lived with him. He cites Aristides, the son of Lysimachus, and Thucydides, the son of Melesius, as examples. When Aristides left Socrates to go to war, he and his company lost the habit of learning which he admitted he had gained—not through any verbal instructions of

<page-num>22</page-num>
-># SOCRATES.<-

...which he received none from him, except by being near him, seeing him, and sitting in the same room with him. The second man attained the same habit just as easily through the same means.

<margin>* Plato, *Theages*</margin>
These predictions extended not only to specific people but also to general affairs. He foretold to some friends the defeat of the *Athenian Army* in *Sicily*, as is attested by *Plutarch* and mentioned by Socrates himself in *Plato*. There, he gives another clear example, or rather a trial of the truth of the *Dæmon’s* predictions, when speaking of an undertaking whose outcome was then uncertain: "*You will hear,*" he said, "*from many in Sicily who know of it, what I foretold concerning the destruction of the army, and we may now have a test of whether the Dæmon speaks the truth. Samionus, the son of Calus, has gone on an expedition; the sign came to me. He goes with Thrasylus to war against Ephesus and Ionia. My opinion is that he will either be killed or, at least, be in great danger; I greatly fear for the whole mission.*" These are his words in *Plato*, delivered (as mentioned before) before the event took place, which turned out exactly as he predicted. For *Thrasylus* was repulsed and defeated by the *Ephesians*,

<margin>* Xenophon, *History of Greece*, Book 1</margin>
the *Athenians* were put to flight with the loss of four hundred men. To celebrate this victory, the *Ephesians* erected two trophies. This occurred in the twenty-first year of the *Peloponnesian War*.

We have cited the universal agreement of *authors* that *Socrates* had such a spiritual attendant; yet there is some disagreement regarding its name, and even more concerning its nature.

<margin>* Augustine, *The City of God*, 8. 4. <note>original: "De civitate dei"</note></margin>
It is commonly called his *Dæmon* <term>Dæmon: a guiding spirit or divine sign, not an evil entity in this context</term>, a title by which he himself identified it. *Plato* sometimes calls it his *Guardian*, and *Apuleius* calls it his *God*—because, says *Saint Augustine*, the name *Dæmon* eventually became hateful. However, we must observe that Socrates did not consider it a *God* itself, but rather something sent from *God*; in that sense, he affirmed the signs came from *God*—specifically, through the mediation of this spirit. We can argue this, among other places, from his first *Epistle*. When he speaks of the sign itself, he uses the word *Dæmon*; when he speaks of the advice, for which that sign was the instrument, he names *God*. This is how we are to understand these and all other similar passages in *Plato*, where *Socrates*, speaking of the *Dæmon*, says: "*If it please God, you shall learn much,*" and, "*the sign from God did not attempt to stop me.*"

<margin>* Plutarch, *On the Sign of Socrates* <note>original: "De genio Socratis"</note></margin>
As for the sign or the manner of the prediction, *some claim it was through sneezing, either by himself or others. If anyone happened to sneeze while standing in front of him, behind him, or on his right side, he immediately proceeded with what he intended. If the sneeze was on his left side, he stopped or desisted. If he sneezed himself before a project, it was a sign of approval; if he sneezed during the action, it was a warning to stop.* There is no need for much argument to disprove this theory. If this sneezing <term>sternutation: the act of sneezing</term> resulted from either chance or his natural physical makeup, it could not have had such a prophetic, supernatural effect. If it proceeded from some more excellent...

<meta>The text cuts off mid-sentence; the catchword is "excellent".</meta>

<summary>This page recounts Socrates' accurate prediction of the Athenian defeat in Sicily and explores the debate over the nature of his "Dæmon," distinguishing it as a divine messenger rather than a deity, while also dismissing the folk belief that his signs were triggered by sneezing.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the analysis of the nature of Socrates's "Daemon" or "Genius," transitioning from his specific predictions to a theological and philosophical debate about whether this attendant was a psychological phenomenon, a guardian angel, or an evil spirit.</meta>

# SOCRATES.

->U u<-

...from some excellent outward cause, we recur to the Genius.

Others limit this foreknowledge within the soul of *Socrates* himself; when he said *his Genius advised him*, they interpret it as we usually say, *his mind told him*, or *he was so inclined*. In this sense, the term *Daemon* is indeed often used; but this is inconsistent with the description *Socrates* gives of a voice and signs coming <note>original: "ab exteriore"</note> from the outside. Furthermore, this knowledge is above human nature.

*Plutarch*, having rejected the opinion of *Terpsion* regarding sneezing, first conjectured that it might be some apparition; but he finally concludes that it was his observation of some inarticulate, unaccustomed sound (or voice) conveyed to him by some extraordinary way, as we see in dreams. This does not avoid the problems of the previous theory; if *Socrates* first interpreted this sound himself, it is the same as the last opinion—that his soul had a prophetic inspiration. If he had any help, it leads back eventually to the Genius.

Some believe it to be one of those spirits which have the particular care of men, which *Maximus Tyrius* and *Apuleius* describe in such a manner that they lack only the name of a good angel.

<margin>
* *On the Origin of Error*, 2.15.
* On Psalm 91.
* *On Perennial Philosophy*, 25.
* *Argument on the Apology of Socrates*.
</margin>
But there are those who do give it that name: *Lactantius, having proved that God sends angels to guard mankind, adds, *and Socrates affirmed that there was a* Daemon *constantly near him, which kept him company from childhood, and by whose signal and instruction he guided his life.* *Eusebius*, commenting on these words of the Psalmist, *He has given His angels charge over you, to keep you in all your ways*, says, "we learn from Scripture that every man has a guardian appointed to him from above." And *Plato* does not hesitate to write in this manner: *All souls, having chosen a condition of living, proceed in order toward it, being moved by the* Daemon *which is assigned to each person and is sent along with them to preserve them in this life, and to fulfill those choices they have made.* And immediately after, he says: *You may believe that Socrates meant this when he often affirmed that he was governed by a* Daemon. *Eugubinus* speaks even more plainly: *The* Daemon *of Socrates,* he says, mentioned so often by *Plato* (*seeing that* Socrates *was a good man and exhorted all men to virtue, and was always encouraged toward what was good by the* Daemon), *may perhaps not unjustly be thought of as his angel—like the one that appeared to* Balaam *the Prophet and diverted him from his wickedness.* But *Ficinus* says explicitly, speaking of this spirit: *If you are not pleased to call the familiar guide of a man his spirit, call it, if you please, his good angel.*

<margin>
* *On the Soul*, page 544, section 14.
</margin>
The primary argument of *Collius*, who opposes this opinion and attempts to prove it was an evil spirit, is that the *Daemon* never discouraged or diverted him from vice, but only from outward danger; whereas the contrary is evident enough.

<summary>This page explores various interpretations of Socrates's "Daemon," debating whether it was a psychological impulse, a sensory phenomenon, or a literal guardian angel, as argued by early Christian and Neoplatonist scholars.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of Socrates’s "Demon" (his internal or divine guide), specifically how it functioned to restrain rather than urge him, before transitioning into Chapter 7, which details his military service during the Peloponnesian War.</meta>

# SOCRATES.

...from the foregoing story of *Timarchus* and *Philemon*.
<margin>* Cicero, *On Divination*, Book 1.</margin>
It is true that the advice of the *Demon* was always dissuasive, never—as *Cicero* says—*impelling*, and often *restraining*. *Apuleius* flatters *Socrates* with this reasoning: *Socrates*, he says, being a man absolute and perfect in himself, ready in all duties that concerned him, never needed anyone to urge him on, but sometimes needed someone to restrain him if it happened that there was any danger in what he was doing. Thus, being forewarned, he might take heed and decline the undertaking for that time, which he might later resume or attempt in some other way.

## CHAP. VII.
### His Military Actions.

<margin>Laertius.</margin>
It is observed by many that *Socrates* had little interest in travel; his life was spent entirely at home, except when he went out in military service. 
In the second year of the eighty-sixth *Olympiad* <note>Approximately 431 BCE</note>, the greatest war that ever occurred among the *Greeks* broke out between the *Lacedemonians* <term>Spartans</term> and the *Athenians*. The causes and excuses for it arose from the controversies of the *Athenians* with the *Corinthians* concerning *Corcyra* and *Potidaea*. Both of these cities had revolted from the *Athenians*, to whom they had been tributary, and sought aid from the *Lacedemonians*, who sent forces to the relief of *Potidaea*. 

In this war, *Socrates* was personally engaged three times: first, at the siege of *Potidaea* in the [blank] year of the *Olympiad*, against which the *Athenians* sent one thousand six hundred choice men-at-arms under the command of *Phormis*. He besieged it from the sea with his galleys and on the land side by a wall; among these soldiers were *Socrates* and *Alcibiades*. *Laertius* says they were on the sea side and that there was no way to advance further on the land <margin>*Symposium*</margin> side. *Plato* writes that they both served in the infantry, which does not disagree <margin>*Plutarch, Symposium*</margin> with the other account, for there was no set battle during the entire time of the siege, only sorties and skirmishes. 

Here, as his comrade *Alcibiades* testifies, *Socrates* surpassed all soldiers in hardiness. If at any time (as often happens in war) provisions failed, no one could bear the want of food and drink like him; yet, on the other hand, in times of feasting, he alone seemed to truly enjoy them. Though he would not drink of his own accord, if invited, he far out-drank all others—and what is strangest of all, no one ever saw him drunk. He also wonderfully endured the extreme winter cold, which is extraordinary in that country. When the frost was so sharp that very few dared to leave their tents, and those who did wrapped their legs and thighs in skins and furs, he went along with them wearing no more clothes than he usually wore. He walked barefoot upon the ice with less sensitivity than others felt in shoes, to the wonder of the soldiers.

...soldiers, who felt shamed by his endurance. His contemplative rapture at the same time was no less worthy of admiration; he fell into a deep contemplation one morning and continued standing in the same posture all the while. At noon, it was noticed by the soldiers, who told one another that Socrates had been standing still in the same place all morning. In the evening, some Ionian soldiers wrapped themselves up warmly and came to lie down by him in the open field to watch if he would continue in that same posture all night. He did so until the morning, and as soon as the sun rose, he saluted it and retired. Aulus Gellius says that he had many of these kinds of raptures. 

We must not omit how he behaved himself there in battle; * seeing his friend Alcibiades in the heat of combat and heavily wounded, he stepped before him, defended both him and his weapons from the enemy, and brought him safely away. Nor was his modesty inferior to his love or courage; for although after the battle the generals were to bestow an honorable reward upon the person who had fought best, and the judges assigned it to Socrates, he declined it. Through his earnest intercession, he ensured that it was awarded to Alcibiades instead.

* In Plato.

* Plato.

The second military action of Socrates was in the first year of the eighty-ninth Olympiad <note>424 BCE</note> at Delium, a town in Boeotia which the Athenians had captured. The Boeotians (says Thucydides), led by Pagondas, followed them and offered battle. The left wing of the Boeotians, up to the very middle of the army, was overthrown by the Athenians and fled toward the other parts of the line where the fight was still ongoing. However, the Boeotian right wing had the advantage over the Athenians; they gradually forced them to give ground and pursued them from the very start. 

While the left wing of his army was in distress, Pagondas secretly sent two companies of horsemen around the hill. When they suddenly appeared, the victorious Athenian wing—fearing they were a fresh army—was thrown into a panic. The entire Athenian army was now doubly terrified by this event and by the Thebans, who continually gained ground and broke their ranks. They took to flight; some fled toward Delium and the sea, others toward Mount Parnes, and others in different directions, wherever there appeared hope of safety. The Boeotians, especially their cavalry and the Locrians who arrived after the enemy was defeated, followed and killed them. 

Socrates behaved in this engagement with his accustomed valor (so well, in fact, that * Laches confesses that if the rest had fought like him, they would not have lost the day) and care for his friends. * Seeing Xenophon unhorsed in the flight and thrown down on the ground (Socrates himself, having also had his horse slain under him, was fighting on foot), he took him upon his shoulders and carried him for many stadia <note>A stadium was approximately 185 meters or 600 feet.</note>, defending him until the enemy ended the pursuit. Being thus caught in the loss of the day and dispersed in flight with others (among whom were Laches the Archon <term>a high magistrate or general</term> and Alcibiades), * Socrates, in the constant slowness of his retreat, expressed a courage far above that of Laches. He frequently looked back and all around, as if eager to be revenged upon the enemy,

* As they came to a fork in the road, Socrates stopped and advised those with him not to take the path they were currently on along Mount Parnes, but to take the other way by Retiste. "For," he said, "I heard the Daemon's voice." <note>The "Daemon" or "Genius" of Socrates was an inner divine sign or voice that warned him against specific actions; original: *Dæmon*.</note> The majority of the group were angry, as if he were joking at such a serious time. A few were persuaded to go with him, including Laches and Alcibiades, and they reached home safely. The rest were met by some horsemen who, returning from pursuit, fell upon them. They resisted at first, but were eventually enclosed by the enemy, who outnumbered them. They gave way and were ultimately overwhelmed and all killed, except for one man who escaped with the help of his shield and brought the news to Athens. Another, Pyrilampes son of Antiphon, was wounded by a javelin and taken prisoner. When Pyrilampes heard from the Athenian envoys sent to Thebes to treat for peace that Socrates and the others with him had reached home safely, he openly declared to the Thebans that Socrates had often called him and others of his company back; those who did not follow the advice of his divine sign were slain.

The last military engagement was in the same year at Amphipolis, * which was then taken by Brasidas, the Spartan <term>Lacedaemonian</term> General.

### CHAP. IX.

->How he conducted himself during the Democracy and the Oligarchy.<-

<margin>* Aelian, *Varia Historia*, Book 3; * Plato, *Apology*.</margin>
Socrates refused to accept any office in the commonwealth (except, in his later years, that of Senator). This was either—as Aelian says—because he saw the Athenian government, though under the form of a democracy, was actually closer to a tyranny or monarchy; or, as he himself claimed, because he was dissuaded by his divine sign from meddling in public affairs. This advice was his preservation, for he was too honest to comply with the injustices of the commonwealth, and to oppose them was extremely dangerous, as he discovered by experience in that short time.

<margin>* Plato, *Apology* and *Gorgias*; * Chapter 1; * Xenophon, *Memorabilia*, Book 1, page 711; * Plato, *Apology*.</margin>
* He was chosen for the Senate for the Antiochian tribe, to which (* as we have said) Alopece—the town where he was born—belonged. * In order to serve, he took the oath which Solon appointed to be given to every Senator: to give judgment according to the laws, unbiased by favor, hatred, or any other pretext. In the third year of the 93rd Olympiad, * the presidency coming by rotation to the Antiochian tribe, and Socrates thereupon becoming President of the people, he had this...
