...this occasion of manifesting his constancy. * There occurred a sea-fight between the Athenians and the Lacedaemonians <term>Spartans</term> at *Arginusae*. The Athenian commanders were ten; the Lacedaemonian commander-in-chief was *Callicratidas*. The Lacedaemonians were overthrown, and their Admiral was sunk. The Athenians returned to *Arginusae* having lost twenty-five ships and all the men in them, except for a few who escaped to land. The ten commanders ordered *Theramenes* and *Thrasybulus* (captains of the galleys) to search for the shipwrecked vessels. However, as they were about to do so, a sudden tempest arose and hindered them. Six of these commanders returned to *Athens*, where, as soon as they arrived and gave their account of the fight, the Senate committed them to prison. *Theramenes* was their accuser, urging that they should be questioned for failing to rescue those who were lost in the shipwreck. The commanders justly answered that they had given orders for their rescue, and that *Theramenes* and *Thrasybulus*, on whom that duty had been placed, were the ones to be condemned (if anyone). Yet, they said they would not turn the blame back onto their accusers, for the storm sufficiently excused them. This satisfied the Senate at the time. However, at the next festival, the *Apaturia*, some friends of *Theramenes*—at his instigation—shaved their hair and put on mourning clothes, pretending to be kinsmen of those who had drowned. They came in this garb to the Senate and caused the charges against the ten commanders to be renewed. This so incensed the people that, by threats contrary to all law, they forced the Senate to condemn them.

*Socrates*, being ordered to write the decree against them <note>Socrates was serving as the presiding officer or *proedros* of the Assembly at the time.</note>, avoided it by pretending he could not write and did not know the proper form. This caused laughter in the Senate (and perhaps led to the slander by *Porphyry* that he was scarcely able to write, and that when he did, it was to his own ridicule). But the true reason is acknowledged by *Athenaeus* to be his constant fortitude; he would not violate the laws of the Commonwealth in contradiction to the oath he had taken. * He paid more heed to his oath than to the violence with which he was threatened. For when the Senate proceeded to their condemnation, he * alone opposed it with his vote. Consequently, many orators prepared to accuse him, and the people cried out with loud clamors that he should be brought to answer for it. But he chose rather to hazard himself for the sake of Law and Justice than to consent to injustice through fear of imprisonment and death—the death of these men being recognized as an injustice later, even by the Athenians themselves. This injustice was soon after punished in *Theramenes* by a similar fate, during which *Socrates* gave the same testimony of his courage.

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Socrates’s political life in Athens, moving from the trial of the commanders after the Battle of Arginusae to the rise of the Thirty Tyrants following the Athenian defeat in the Peloponnesian War.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>28</page-num>

# SOCRATES.

->Theramenes<-

<margin>* Diodorus Siculus, Book 14.</margin>
[Ge]neral in the first year of the 94th Olympiad, there arose some debate concerning the alteration of the government from a democracy to an oligarchy. *Theramenes* stood for the continuance of the democracy, but being overswayed by the power and threats of *Lysander*, he yielded to the constitution of thirty persons—by title "Governors," but in effect "Tyrants." Among this number was *Theramenes* (whom they chose, in regard to his known moderation and equity, to restrain the greed and avarice of the others), *Critias* (initially a friend, but now a great enemy to *Socrates* for reproving his love for *Euridamus*), *Charicles*, and others. Their names are recorded by *Xenophon*, as are also their murders, unjust seizures of lands, and confiscations of goods. They began with the punishment of the worst persons, proceeded to the richest, and ended with the best. Never (*says Seneca*) was any city more miserable. They put 1,300 (*Aeschines says 1,500*) of the best persons to death without any legal trial; nor was their fury calmed by this, but rather more inflamed. That city where the Areopagus <term>the highest judicial council of Athens</term> was held—the most sacred court of justice, where the Senate and people used to assemble—was daily turned into a sad gathering of executioners. It was an unhappy court, too narrow for the *Tyrants*, who never rested from oppression, leaving the people without hope of liberty or remedy. Everyone fled the city except *Socrates*, who during all this time never set foot outside the gates. He was continually among the people, comforting the grieving fathers and encouraging those who despaired for the state. He reproached the rich, who had lived in fear of losing their wealth, for their late repentance of their dangerous greed; and to those who wished to imitate him, he provided a great example as he walked freely among the thirty oppressors.

*Theramenes*, opposing this cruelty and injustice, was accused by *Critias* of betraying the trust of the commonwealth, a charge of which he acquitted himself to the satisfaction of the Senate. But *Critias* and his faction, fearing he might overthrow the oligarchy, seized him with a troop of soldiers. *Theramenes* ran to the Altar, but being dragged from there by the officers, he behaved (says *Diodorus*) like a disciple of *Socrates*. The people pitied him, but none of them dared to offer help because he was surrounded by the soldiers, except for *Socrates* and two of his companions, who ran to him and endeavored to rescue him out of the hands of the officers. *Theramenes* desired them to stop, telling them that he much loved and commended their kindness and virtue, but that it would be the greatest misfortune he could have if their love for him should cause their deaths. Whereupon *Socrates* and his companions, seeing that no one else came to join them in his aid and that the opposing party was too strong for them, gave up.

# SOCRATES.

Theramenes was carried to prison, and there (being sentenced to drink hemlock) died.

<margin>Aelian, *Various History*, Book 2</margin>
These outrages of the Thirty Tyrants *Socrates* did not hesitate to criticize. Seeing many prominent persons put to death, and the rich deceived and betrayed to extreme punishments, he said to Antisthenes: "Do you regret that we have done nothing in our whole lives great and remarkable, like those monarchs who are described in tragedies—the Atreuses, Thyesteses, Agamemnons, and Aegisthuses? In those plays, they are beheaded, fed their own flesh, and generally destroyed; but no poet was ever so bold and shameless as to bring a slaughtered pig onto the stage." <note>Socrates suggests that the powerful are the primary targets of tragic ruin, while the common or "lowly" are often ignored by fate and tyrants alike.</note>

<margin>* Diogenes Laertius</margin>
To another who grumbled because he was not respected since they began to rule, "Are you sorry for that?" he said. <margin>* Xenophon, *Memorabilia*, Book 1</margin> He said likewise "that it would be strange if a <term>cowherd</term> (neatherd) who diminished and impoverished his herd did not admit himself to be a bad cowherd; but it is even stranger that one who, being set over a city, made the citizens worse and their number smaller, should not admit himself to be a bad governor." This came to their knowledge, whereupon *Critias* and *Charicles* sent for him and strictly forbade him to teach or discourse with any of the young men. 

*Socrates* asked them if, in acts of prohibition, he might be permitted to question what he did not understand. When they granted this, he continued: "I am ready to obey the laws; but lest I transgress them through ignorance, I desire to be informed whether, when you forbid me the act of speaking, this act is to be understood as speaking things correctly or incorrectly. If the first, I must refrain from speaking what is right; if the second, I must take care to speak nothing but what is right." 

At this, *Charicles* became displeased and said, "Since you do not understand that, *Socrates*, we command you something that is easier to understand: from now on, do not speak at all with any of the young men." To remove all ambiguity, *Socrates* replied, "So that I do not exceed my limitations, let me know expressly at what age you call a man 'young'?" 

"As long," said *Charicles*, "as he is incapable of being a Senator and has not attained the height of his judgment; you are not to speak with anyone under thirty." 

"May I not buy anything," *Socrates* answered, "from anyone under that age, nor ask them the price of any item?" 

"You may do that," said *Charicles*, "but your custom is to ask questions about things which you know very well; stop doing those." 

"And shall I not then," *Socrates* replied, "make an answer if anyone asks me where *Charicles* lives, or where *Critias* is?" 

"To such questions," said *Charicles*, "you may." 

"You must," *Critias* continued, "refrain from the craftsmen, whose ears you have sufficiently irritated with your irrelevant talk." 

"I must then refrain," said *Socrates*, "from discussing justice, piety, and the like?" 

"Even from the cowherds themselves," *Charicles* replied, "which unless you do, take care that you do not lose your head." <note>original: "take heed your head come not short home"</note>

This ill will and jealousy which they had conceived against *Socrates* was increased by the secret departure of some of his friends from the city, which was reported to have been done by his contrivance to give intelligence to the *Thebans*. Nor was that

<meta>This page continues the account of Socrates’s resistance to the Thirty Tyrants, specifically his refusal to participate in the arrest of Leon of Salamis, and begins to describe his long-standing conflict with the Sophists and his accuser, Anytus.</meta>

<margin>Plato, *Apology*</margin>
...suspicion without reason, as is shown by his last Epistle. Upon this, they summoned him into the Court, where some complaints were brought against him. Having acquitted himself of these, they (to find a better cause for a quarrel against him) ordered him and four others to go to the Piraeus <note>The port of Athens.</note> and arrest Leon, whom they intended to put to death so that they might seize his estate. But Socrates refused, adding that he would never willingly assist in an unjust act. Whereupon Charicles said, "Do you think, Socrates, that you can talk so boldly and not suffer for it?" "A thousand ills," answered Socrates, "but none so grievous as to act unjustly." Charicles made no reply, nor did any of the rest. The other four went for Leon, while Socrates went directly to his house. But from then on, the jealousy they had of him increased so much that, if their power had not been soon overthrown, they would have come close to taking away his life.

## CHAP. XI.

### His falling out with the Sophists, and with Anytus.

<margin>Cicero, *Brutus*; Cicero, *The Orator*; Libanius, *Apology*</margin>
The Sophists, who were the masters of language in those times, according to Cicero (including Gorgias of Leontini, Thrasymachus of Chalcedon, Protagoras of Abdera, Prodicus of Ceos, Hippias of Elis, and many others), professed in arrogant words to teach how an inferior cause—for such was their phrase—might be made superior through speaking. They used a sweet, fluent kind of rhetoric, clever in its phrasing and lofty in its words, which was more suited for show than for legal pleading, and better for the schools and academies than for the <term>Forum</term> <note>The public marketplace and center of judicial and political business.</note>. They were so highly esteemed that, wherever they went, they could persuade young men to forsake all other company for theirs. 

Socrates opposed these men. Often, through his subtle way of disputing and refuting their principles with his accustomed questioning, he demonstrated that they were actually much less than the high opinion people had of them, and that they themselves understood nothing of what they undertook to teach others. He withdrew the young men from their empty conversation. These Sophists, who until then had been looked upon as angels for their wit and eloquence, he proved to be vain pretenders of words, ignorant of the things they professed. He showed they had more need to pay money to be taught than to take money for teaching, as they usually did. The Athenians, taken with these reproofs which Socrates gave them, mocked the Sophists and encouraged their children to the study of solid virtue.

Another quarrel Socrates had was of long duration, for it was the ultimate cause of his death, though it began many years earlier. This was with Anytus, an orator by profession, who was privately supported and enriched by leather-sellers. He had sent two of his sons to Socrates to be taught; however, he was not pleased that, while they were in that path of study, they had not learned enough to be able

->S O C R A T E S.<-

...in order to earn their living. He took them away from Socrates and put them into the trade that he himself was ashamed to admit owning. Socrates was greatly displeased by this regarding the two youths, whose ruin he predicted (and rightly so, for they later fell into debaucheries that caused it). He did not hesitate to reproach Anytus while speaking to his students, <margin>Socratic Epistle 14.</margin> telling them:

> "The trade of leather-dressing is not a fit subject to be discussed among young men. Those who benefit from any art cherish and openly profess it—as Acumenus <note>A physician and friend of Socrates.</note> does with medicine, and Damon and Connus with music. Even Anytus, while his sons were his students, was not ashamed of what they learned, even though it was not enough to support them through legal pleading. Yet he prided himself on walking invisible, as if wearing Pluto’s Helmet <note>The Cap of Hades, which rendered the wearer invisible.</note> or Gyges' Ring <note>A mythical ring mentioned by Plato that granted invisibility to the wearer.</note>, hiding from the people the true source of his income: dressing leather. It is not right to be ashamed of the trade but not of the profit; he ought to either own the business or disclaim the money."

<margin>Various History</margin>
<margin>Scholiast on Aristophanes</margin>
<margin>Libanius</margin>
<margin>Aelian</margin>
Anytus (according to Aelian) studied every opportunity and method for revenge to answer this reproach. However, he feared the Athenians, doubting how they would react if he accused Socrates, since Socrates was held in high esteem for many reasons—chiefly for opposing the Sophists, who neither taught nor possessed any solid learning. He consulted with Meletus, a young orator unknown to Socrates, described by Plato as having long straight hair, a hooked nose, and a thin beard. He was a man who could be bought for a single <term>drachma</term> <note>A silver coin of ancient Greece; here implying he was cheaply bribed.</note> to do anything. By his advice:

> "He began by testing smaller matters to see how the Athenians would react to a capital charge against Socrates's life. He felt it was unsafe to accuse him directly at first, both for the reasons already mentioned and for fear that Socrates's friends and followers might turn the judges' anger against him for falsely accusing a person who was so far from harming the state that he was its greatest ornament. To this end, he bribed Aristophanes, a comic poet whose only business was to create laughter, to bring Socrates onto the stage. He was to accuse him of crimes that most men knew he was innocent of: irrelevant discourse, making a bad cause seem good through argument, and introducing new and strange deities while he himself believed in and respected none. This was intended to spread a bad opinion of him, even among those who associated with him most often. Aristophanes took this theme and wove it together with much abusive humor. The best of the Greeks was his subject—not Cleon, the Spartans, the Thebans, or Pericles himself, but a person dear to all the gods, especially Apollo. At first, because of the novelty of the thing and the unusual impersonation of Socrates..."

<meta>This page continues the account of how Anytus and Meletus conspired to ruin Socrates' reputation by hiring Aristophanes to write a satirical play about him.</meta>

# SOCRATES.

“...upon the stage, the *Athenians*, who expected nothing less, were struck with wonder. Then (being naturally envious and prone to disparage the best people—not only those who held office in the commonwealth, but anyone eminent for learning or virtue), they began to be captivated by *The Clouds* (for so the play was named). They praised the actor who played *Socrates* with more applause than any before him, awarding him the victory with many shouts and sending word to the judges that they should record no name other than that of *Aristophanes*.

“*Socrates* seldom went to the theater, except when *Euripides* <note>A famous Athenian playwright and friend of Socrates.</note> competed with some new tragedian there or in the *Piraeus*; he went then because he loved the wisdom, goodness, and sweetness of his verse. Sometimes *Alcibiades* and *Critias* <note>Two of Socrates' most famous and controversial students.</note> would invite him to a comedy and, in a manner of speaking, compel him to go. He was far from respecting comic poets; instead, he despised them as dishonest, abusive, and useless, which greatly offended them.

“These circumstances (along with other things suggested by *Anytus* and *Meletus*) were the basis for *Aristophanes’* comedy. It is likely he received a large sum of money for it, as they were eager to carry out their plan, and he, driven by poverty and malice, was ready to take their direction. Ultimately, the play gained extraordinary acclaim, confirming the words of *Cratinus*:

->The theater was then
Filled with malicious men.<-

<margin>
* Aelian, *Various History*, book 5.
* Plutarch, *On the Education of Children*.
* Scholiast on Aristophanes.
</margin>

“As it was then the festival of *Bacchus*, a great number of *Greeks* went to see the play. Since *Socrates* was being portrayed on stage and frequently named (it was not surprising for the actors to represent him, for potters frequently depicted him on their stone jugs), the foreigners who were present (not knowing whom the comedy was mocking) began to hum and whisper, everyone asking who this *Socrates* was.

“Observing this—for he had not come there by chance, but because he knew he would be mocked in the play and had chosen the most prominent seat in the theater—he stood up to satisfy the foreigners' curiosity. Throughout the entire play, he remained standing in that position, laughing. One person who was present asked him if it did not irritate him to see himself brought onto the stage. ‘Not at all,’ he answered. ‘I feel as if I am at a banquet where everyone is enjoying me.’

“This comedy was first performed when *Isarchus* was Archon <note>The chief magistrate of Athens.</note> and *Cratinus* was the winner, in the first year of the eighty-ninth Olympiad <note>Approximately 423 BCE.</note>. Because some criticized *Aristophanes* for it, he had it performed again the following year to vindicate himself, when *Aminias* was Archon, but it was received with less enthusiasm than the first time.”

## CHAPTER IX.

### His Trial.

<margin>Plato; Apuleius; Libanius, *Apology*.</margin>
Many years passed since the first dispute between *Socrates* and *Anytus*. During this time, one continued to openly rebuke the other while the other secretly undermined him, until at length *Anytus* saw a time that suited his plan. He persuaded *Meletus* to bring an indictment against *Socrates* to the Senate in these terms:

> *Meletus, son of Meletus, of the Pitthean district, accuses Socrates, son of Sophroniscus, of the Alopece district. Socrates violates the law by not believing in the deities in which this city believes, but introducing other new gods. He also violates the law by corrupting the youth; the punishment is death.*

<margin>* See *Suidas* <note>A massive 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia.</note> under that word.</margin>
<margin>* Libanius, *Apology*, page 644.</margin>
This indictment being brought under oath (which Plato calls an <gloss>affidavit</gloss> <note>original: "ἀντωμοσία" (antomosia), a preliminary sworn statement by both parties in an Athenian lawsuit.</note>), *Crito* posted bail for his appearance on the day of the trial. Soon after, *Anytus* sent word to him privately, desiring him to stop mentioning his trade <note>Anytus was a wealthy tanner; Socrates often used examples from common trades to make philosophical points, which Anytus found insulting.</note> and assuring him that he would then withdraw the lawsuit. But *Socrates* returned this answer: *that he would never stop speaking the truth as long as he lived; that he would always use the same speeches concerning him; and that Anytus’s accusation was not powerful enough to make him refrain from saying those things which he previously felt obliged to say.*

<margin>* Xenophon, *Apology* and *Memorabilia*, book 4.</margin>
The interval of time between his accusation and trial he employed in his usual philosophical exercises, taking no care to prepare his defense. Being observed and questioned about this by *Hermogenes*, son of *Hipponicus*, *Socrates* said, "I provide defense enough by considering and pursuing the constant course of my life." When *Hermogenes* asked how that could be, he replied, "Because I never did any unjust act, which I consider the best defense." 

"But we often see judges," said *Hermogenes*, "swayed by rhetoric to condemn the innocent and acquit the guilty." 

"The truth is," *Socrates* replied, "that when I went about making my defense, I was twice withheld by my *Daemon*." <note>Socrates' *daimonion*, an internal "divine sign" or voice that warned him against certain actions.</note> 

When *Hermogenes* wondered at this, *Socrates* continued: "Is it strange that God should think it fit for me to die at this time? Up to this point, no man has lived more uprightly, which is now my greatest comfort, just as it was once my greatest delight."

<summary>This page describes the formal legal indictment of Socrates by Meletus and Anytus on charges of impiety and corrupting the youth, as well as Socrates' refusal to prepare a formal defense, trusting instead in the integrity of his life.</summary>
<keywords>Socrates, Anytus, Meletus, trial, indictment, impiety, Crito, Hermogenes, Daemon, Athenian law</keywords>

--- Page 210 ---
<meta>This page continues Socrates' conversation with Hermogenes, where he explains his willingness to face death rather than endure the infirmities of old age. It then details his refusal of a prepared legal defense and the beginning of his trial.</meta>

...and friends. If I live longer, I know I must undergo what is common to old age: defects in hearing and sight, slowness to understand, and a tendency to forget. How can I then be pleased to live longer and grow worse? It is likely that God, in his love for me, has ordained that I should die at the most convenient age and by the gentlest means. For if I die by sentence, I am allowed the benefit of the easiest kind of death. I shall give my friends the least trouble, I shall do nothing unseemly before those who are present, and I shall depart sound in body and soul; is this not very desirable?

God, with much reason, forbids me to make any defense. If I could achieve it, I should only stay longer to be taken away by the torment of diseases and the imperfections of age—which truly, *Hermogenes*, I do not desire. If, when I give an account of my actions toward God and men, the judges think fit to condemn me, I will rather choose to die than to beg from them a life worse than death.

Other friends used the same arguments with him, assuring him of victory.

<margin>
* Cicero, *On the Orator*, Book 1.
Laertius.
</margin>
* *Lysias*, an excellent orator, offered him an oration which he had written in his defense, desiring him to make use of it at his trial if he thought it good. *Socrates* read it through and told him that it was a good speech, but not fit for him. When *Lysias* asked how that could be, Socrates replied, "Why may a garment or shoes not be expensive, and yet not fit me? If you should bring me Sicyonian shoes <note>Luxury footwear from Sicyon, often associated with decadence in ancient Greece.</note>, I would not wear them even if they fit my feet, because they are effeminate." He felt the oration was
<margin>
* Plutarch.
* Laertius.
</margin>
ingenious and eloquent, but not bold and manly; * for though it was very bitter against the judges, * it was more rhetorical than became a philosopher.

<margin>* Plato, *Apology*.</margin>
The day of the trial having arrived, * *Anytus*, *Lycon*, and *Meletus* prepared to accuse him—one on behalf of the people, the second for the orators, and the last for the poets. *Meletus* first went up into the chair set apart for that purpose and there delivered a speech which was in itself mediocre enough; furthermore, it was delivered so poorly and in such a schoolboy-like manner that he sometimes lost his place out of fear and had to turn around to be prompted like an actor. This was enough to cause laughter, even among those most concerned with such a serious case. Part of the effect of this trial seems to be the reputation which is recorded by *Xenophon* in various places, some details of which are confirmed by *Libanius*:

> That *Socrates* persuaded his listeners to despise the established laws, saying it was only fit for fools to be governed by a bean <note>Referring to the Athenian practice of using beans for voting or drawing lots for public office.</note> (meaning the votes of the Senate gathered in that way).

> That he was intimately acquainted with *Critias* and *Alcibiades*—the former being the most greedy and violent man in the Oligarchy <note>Critias was a leader of the Thirty Tyrants who briefly overthrew Athenian democracy.</note>, and the other being ambitious for tyranny.

"That he taught disrespect and disobedience to parents, telling
his scholars he would make them wiser than their fathers;
and that it was lawful for anyone to bind his father if he
were mad, and for those who were more wise to do as
much to those who were less wise.
"That he also taught disrespect for all kinsmen, saying
they were not useful to the sick, or to the accused—the first being
in more need of a physician, the latter of an orator; that
the goodwill of unable friends was worth nothing: that only
the most knowledgeable persons were most worthy of honor,
by which means he would arrogate all respect to
himself.
"That he selected out of the poets some bad passages, and perverted
others that were not so, to excite his friends to impious
actions; such as that of *Hesiod*:

> There is no work that brings shame;
> It is idleness that merits blame.

"He interpreted this as if the poet meant all acts might be committed
for gain.
"That he often repeated and misinterpreted these words of
*Homer*, as if the poet allowed the poor to be beaten:

> When he a Prince or some great person meets,
> He greets such with soft language, kindly thus:
> "Happy above the reach of fear are you,
> Sit down, and bid your followers do so too."
> But when he speaks to any of the lower sort,
> His anger breaks forth in these words with blows:
> "Be quiet; wretch, submit to your betters;
> For you are unfit for both action and advice."

<margin>* Libanius, *Apology*</margin>
*Melitus* (his oration ended) came down; * next to him came
*Anytus* with a long, malicious speech, and last of all *Lyco*, with
all the artifice of rhetoric, concluded the accusation.

<margin>* Cicero, *Tusculan Disputations*, Book 1; * Cicero, *On Divination*, Book 1; * *Socratic Epistles*, 14; * Plato, *Apology*; * Cicero, *Tusculan Disputations*, Book 1; * Xenophon.</margin>

* not as a suppliant or guilty person, but as if he were master of the
judges themselves, with a free contumacy <term>contumacy: stubborn resistance to or contempt of authority</term> proceeding not
from pride, but from the greatness of his mind.
" * But I wonder first (*Athenians*) how *Melitus* came by...

--- Page 212 ---
<meta>This page continues the account of Socrates' trial, transitioning from the accusations made by Melitus and Anytus to the beginning of Socrates' own defense speech. He addresses the charges of impiety and the introduction of new gods by explaining his "Divine Sign" and his way of life.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>35</page-num>

<center># SOCRATES.</center>

"...with this knowledge, that (as he says) I do not worship those gods the city worships? Others have seen me (and so might *Melitus*, if he had pleased) sacrifice at common festivals on the public altars. How do I introduce new deities when I profess to be directed in all my actions by the voice of God? Those who observe the calls of birds or the answers of men are guided by voices; no one doubts that thunder, whether loud or oracular, is a voice. Does not the priestess on the tripod <note>The Pythia, the priestess at the Oracle of Delphi</note> convey to us by voice what the God delivers to her? And that He foreknows events, communicating them to whom He pleases, all men (as well as I) believe and profess. Others call those who foretell events 'augurs,' 'soothsayers,' and 'diviners'; I call it the Divine Sign <term>Dæmon</term>, and I believe I do so more religiously than those who ascribe divine power to birds. Many can attest that I am no impostor; they have asked my advice and never found it to fail." 

(Here there arose a murmur in the Senate, some not believing, others envying what he said—that he should surpass them in such a particular favor from the deity.) 

"Let those who are incredulous hear this also, to confirm their opinion that I am not favored by the gods: when *Chaerephon*, in the presence of many witnesses, questioned the *Delphian* Oracle concerning me, *Apollo* answered that no man was more free, more just, or more wise." 

(Here another murmur arose among the judges. He proceeded:) 

"Yet the same God said more of *Lycurgus*, the *Lacedaemonian* <note>Spartan</note> lawgiver: that he knew not whether to call him a god or a man. Me he did not compare with the gods, though he gave me priority among men. But do not trust the God in this; examine me closely yourselves. Whom do you know who is less a servant to corporeal pleasures? Who is more free? I accept neither rewards nor gifts. Who is more just than he who so conforms himself to the present time that he needs no help from any other? Who will say he does not deserve the title of 'wise' who, since he was able, never ceased to learn all possible good through inquiry? And that I did not take this pain in vain is evident in that many citizens and strangers who study virtue prefer my conversation above all others. 

What is the reason that, though all men know I have no wealth to repay them, so many desire to oblige me with gifts? Why do I require no return from anyone, yet engage the loyalty of so many? Why was I no more moved when the city was besieged <note>Likely referring to the Siege of Athens during the Peloponnesian War</note> and everyone lamented their condition than when it was most flourishing? While others spend money on outward things to please themselves, I furnish myself from within with things that please me better. If no one can disprove what I have said, do I not deserve the commendation of both gods and men? And yet you, *Melitus*, pretend that..."

<summary>Socrates begins his defense by arguing that his "Divine Sign" is consistent with traditional divination and citing the Delphic Oracle's proclamation of his superior wisdom and virtue. He challenges the jury to find any evidence of his supposed corruption or greed, pointing instead to his self-sufficiency and dedication to learning.</summary>
<keywords>Socrates, Melitus, Chaerephon, Apollo, Lycurgus, Lacedaemonian, Dæmon, Delphic Oracle, trial of Socrates, impiety defense</keywords>

--- Page 213 ---
<meta>This page continues the account of Socrates' trial. Socrates argues against the charge of corrupting youth by comparing education to other specialized skills like medicine and military leadership. After being found guilty, he addresses the nature of death and refuses to offer a significant fine as a counter-penalty, leading to his final death sentence.</meta>

<center># SOCRATES.</center>

...with these instructions, do I corrupt the youth? Everyone knows what it means to corrupt youth. Can you name even one person whom I have turned from being religious to being impious, from modest to impudent, from frugal to prodigal, from sober to debauched, from hardy to effeminate, or the like? But I know their answer: "Melitus, you have persuaded them to be more obedient to you than to their own parents." Socrates replied, "I confess that as far as instruction is concerned, they know this to be my proper care. For their health, men obey physicians rather than their parents; in lawsuits, they obey counselors rather than their relatives. Do you not in war prefer the most experienced soldiers for command rather than your own allies?" 

"Yes," answers Melitus, "it is right that we should." 

"And do you think it reasonable then," replies Socrates, "if others are preferred for those things in which they excel, that I—who in the opinion of some have an advantage over others in educating youth, which is the greatest benefit among men—ought therefore to die?" 

<margin>* Plutarch, *On Tranquility of Mind*</margin>
"Anytus and Melitus," he said, addressing himself to the judges, "may procure my death, but they cannot hurt me. To fear death is to seem wise without actually being so, for it is to pretend to understand what we do not understand. No man knows what death is, or whether it may not be the greatest happiness that can happen to a man; and yet everyone fears and shuns it as if they were certain it was the greatest misfortune."

<margin>* Plutarch, *Consolation to Apollonius*</margin>
This and more, says Xenophon, was said both by Socrates himself and his friends. However, the judges were so little pleased with his unusual manner of pleading that, 
<margin>* Diogenes Laertius</margin>
as Plato went up to the speaker's platform and began a speech with these words, "*Although I, Athenians, am the youngest of those who come up to this place*," they all cried out, "*...and the first of those who go down!*" Plato was consequently forced to step down. When they proceeded to vote, Socrates was convicted by 281 votes. 

It was the custom of Athens, as Cicero observes, that when anyone was convicted of a non-capital offense, a monetary fine <term>mulct</term> was imposed. When the judges had voted in that manner, the guilty person was asked to name the highest rate at which he estimated his own offense. The judges, willing to favor Socrates, put that demand to him. He answered twenty-five (or as Eubulides says, one hundred) drachmas <note>A drachma was a standard silver coin; 100 drachmas equaled one mina</note>. 

<margin>* Xenophon; * Plato's *Apology*</margin>
Nor would he allow his friends—Plato, Crito, Critobulus, and Apollodorus (who desired him to estimate it at fifty minae <note>About 5,000 drachmas, a very substantial sum</note>, promising to pay the sum themselves)—to pay anything for him. He said that to pay a penalty was to admit to an offense. He told the judges that, for the actions he stood accused of, he actually deserved the highest honors and rewards, including daily meals at the public expense in the Prytanæum <term>Prytanæum: the town hall of Athens where distinguished citizens and Olympic victors were maintained at the state's expense</term>, which was the greatest honor among the Greeks. With this answer, the judges were so infuriated that they condemned him to death by eighty more votes than the original conviction.

<meta>catchword: The</meta>

<summary>Socrates defends his role as an educator and challenges the fear of death before being convicted by the Athenian jury. His defiant refusal to propose a significant fine, instead claiming he deserved state honors, leads the judges to sentence him to death.</summary>
<keywords>Socrates, Melitus, Anytus, trial of Socrates, Plato, Xenophon, Prytanæum, Athenian law, death penalty, philosophy of death</keywords>

--- Page 214 ---
<meta>This page continues the account of Socrates' trial and the beginning of his imprisonment. Following his death sentence, Socrates addresses his friends regarding the injustice of his accusers and the peace of his own conscience.</meta>

<center># SOCRATES.</center>

<margin>* Xenophon, *Apology*</margin>
After the sentence was passed, he could not help but smile, and turning to his friends, he spoke as follows: "Those who have bribed false witnesses against me, and those who have given such testimony, are undoubtedly conscious of their own great impiety and injustice. But as for me, why should I be more dejected now than I was before I was condemned, since I am not any more guilty? They could not prove that I named any new Gods instead of Jupiter, Juno, and the rest, or that I swore by such. How did I corrupt young men by habituating them to endurance and frugality? My very adversaries acquit me of capital crimes, such as sacrilege, theft, and treason; this makes me wonder how I came to be condemned to die. Yet the fact that I die unjustly will not trouble me; it is not a reproach to me, but to those who condemned me. I am greatly satisfied by the example of Palamedes <note>A Greek hero framed for treason by Ulysses during the Trojan War; he was executed but later regarded as a martyr for truth.</note>, who suffered death in a similar way; he is much more commended than Ulysses, the procurer of his death. I know that both future and past times will witness that I never hurt or injured anyone, but on the contrary, I have benefited all who conversed with me to the utmost of my ability, sharing whatever good I could, free of charge." Having said this, he went away, his demeanor matching his words; his eyes, gestures, and gait expressed great cheerfulness.

### CHAP. XI.

#### *His imprisonment.*

<margin>
* Seneca, *Consolation to Helvia*
* Maximus of Tyre
* Xenophon, *Memorabilia*, Book 4
* Plato, *Phaedo*
</margin>
Socrates (says Seneca) *with the same resolute look with which he alone opposed the thirty Tyrants, entered the prison, and took away all shame from the place—for it could not be a prison while he was there.* Here (being fettered by the eleven officers <note>The "Eleven" were the Athenian magistrates responsible for prisoners and executions.</note>) he remained for thirty days after he was condemned, because of the following occasion: The ship which carried Theseus and fourteen others to Crete had been the subject of a vow—if they returned home safely (as they fortunately did), it would be dedicated to Apollo and sent every year with a gift to Delos. This custom the Athenians religiously observed. Before the ceremony, they used to purify their city, and all condemned persons were granted a stay of execution until the ship returned from Delos, which sometimes took a long time if the wind was not favorable. The Priest of Apollo began the ceremony by crowning the stern of the ship; as this happened the day before Socrates was condemned, it caused him to remain in prison for so long afterward.

During this interval, he was visited by his friends, with whom he spent the time in discussion after his usual manner. He was often urged by them to escape, and some of them offered to carry him away by force, which he not only refused but ridiculed...

<meta>catchword: ridiculed</meta>

<summary>Following his death sentence, Socrates maintains his composure and asserts his innocence before being imprisoned for thirty days. His execution is delayed due to a religious tradition involving a sacred ship sent to Delos, during which time he continues to philosophize with his friends.</summary>
<keywords>Socrates, Palamedes, Seneca, death sentence, imprisonment, Eleven Officers, Ship of Theseus, Delos, Apollo, Athenian law</keywords>

--- Page 215 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of Socrates' imprisonment following his trial and sentencing. Having accepted his fate, he remains in prison awaiting the return of the sacred ship from Delos, which signals the time for his execution.</meta>

<center># SOCRATES.</center>

...asking if they knew any place outside of *Attica* where death could not come? <margin>* Plato, *Crito*</margin> *Crito*, two days before Socrates' death, came to him very early in the morning for the same purpose. He had gained some influence with the jailer through his frequent visits and gifts; but finding Socrates asleep, he sat quietly by him, admiring the peacefulness of his sleep and the happy equanimity of his mind. As soon as Socrates woke, Crito told him that he brought sad news—if not sad to Socrates, then certainly to all his friends—that the ship would definitely arrive home tomorrow at the latest (as some who came from *Sunium* <note>A promontory at the southernmost tip of the Attic peninsula.</note> affirmed they had left it there). Therefore, in all likelihood, it would arrive that day, and he would have to die the next. 

"May it be at a good time," answered *Socrates*, "but I do not believe it will arrive today. For I must die on the following day, according to those who have the power in their hands. But I guess that I shall not die tomorrow, but the day after, based on a dream I had tonight: a very beautiful woman in a white garment greeted me by my name, saying,

> Before three days are told,
> You shall behold rich *Phthia*. <note>A reference to the *Iliad* (9.363), where Achilles speaks of returning home to Phthia; here, it symbolizes Socrates' "return home" through death.</note>

(According to *Laertius*, he made the same report to *Aeschines*.) *Crito* took this opportunity to persuade him to save himself, which he urged with many arguments: "That his friends would be accused of greed, as being more desirous to save their wealth than to redeem him; that it could be accomplished with little trouble and expense to those who were prepared for it; that he himself was rich enough to do it, or if not, *Simmias*, *Cebes*, and others would join him; that he ought not voluntarily to thrust himself into destruction when he might avoid it; that he would leave his children in an uncertain and meager state; and that his refusal would not be interpreted as consistency, but as a lack of courage. Consider these reasons well," said he, "or rather (for there is no time left for considering), be persuaded; what is to be done must be done tonight, or it will be too late." 

*Socrates* answered that Crito's cheerful readiness to help him was much to be valued if it were consistent with justice; otherwise, the less just it was, the more blamable. He argued that public opinion and censure should not be regarded, but only truth and equity; that a wrong must not be repaid with another wrong; that faith should be kept more strictly with a City <note>The State or the body politic.</note> than with private individuals; and that he had voluntarily subjected himself to the laws of his country by living under their government, and to violate them at the last would be a great injustice. He added that by breaking out of prison, he would not only bring his friends into many inconveniences but also himself into many dangers, only to live and die in exile; and that in such a...

> "...condition, he would be no better able to raise his children well; but by dying honorably, his friends would take better care of them. He argued that whatever inconvenience might follow, nothing was to be preferred over justice; and that if he should escape through treachery, the rest of his life would be no happier, nor would he be better received in the next world after death. 'These things,' he said, 'I hear like the music of the *Corybantian* pipes <note>The Corybantes were the armed and crested dancers who worshipped the goddess Cybele with loud, rhythmic music; Socrates suggests the voice of the laws rings so loudly in his ears that he can hear nothing else.</note>; the sound of these words makes me deaf to everything else. Therefore, whatever you say to the contrary will be in vain. But if you have any other business, speak.' *Crito* answered that he had nothing else to say. 'As for this, then,' Socrates concluded, 'speak of it no more; let us follow the path that God points out to us.'"

### CHAPTER XII.

->The time and manner of his death.<-

<margin>
* Diogenes Laertius.
* Book 14.
</margin>
The time of *Socrates'* death was mentioned earlier; the *Arundel Marbles* <note>The Arundel Marbles (Marmor Parium) are a collection of ancient Greek inscriptions; at the time of this writing, they were housed at Arundel House in London.</note> state that he died when *Laches* was Archon, at the age of seventy, which (according to *Plato*) was complete, for he says "more than seventy" <note>original: πλείω ἑβδομήκοντα</note>. *Demetrius Phalerius* says he died in the first year of the ninety-fifth *Olympiad*, having lived seventy years. *Diodorus Siculus* asserts that it happened in that year while *Laches* was Archon.

<margin>
* Xenophon, *Memorabilia*, Book 1.
</margin>
Although nothing in Greek history is settled by better authority than the years of *Socrates'* life, *Leo Allatius*—with much confidence but little reason—disputes the accepted chronology of his life and death. The reason is this: the fourteenth of the *Socratic Epistles* published by him mentions an oration by *Polycrates* as if it were spoken at the trial of *Socrates*. However, since that oration mentions the walls of *Athens* being repaired by *Conon* (which happened six years after *Socrates'* death), the letter is rendered suspicious. The truth seems to be this: after the death of *Socrates*, it became a common theme in the schools of rhetoric (which were much studied at *Athens* at that time) to speak for and against *Socrates*. *Polycrates*, a Sophist, wrote an invective to exercise his wit. *Lysias*, a famous orator who died around the 100th *Olympiad*, had written (as we have already said) a defense, which is cited by the commentator on *Aristides* in response to *Polycrates*. Defenses were likewise written by *Plato*, *Xenophon*, and (much later) by *Libanius*. Although *Isocrates* warned *Polycrates* about certain errors in his speech against *Socrates*, the anachronism remained, for chronology was not yet studied in *Athens*. This is why *Plato* himself is so often criticized in that respect by *Athenaeus*, *Aristides*, *Macrobius*, and others: the 

<summary>Socrates concludes his refusal to escape, choosing to follow God's will over Crito's pleas. The text then establishes the historical date of Socrates' death while dismissing anachronistic claims made by Leo Allatius regarding a speech by Polycrates.</summary>
<keywords>Socrates, Crito, justice, Corybantian pipes, Arundel Marbles, Laches, Polycrates, Conon, chronology, rhetoric, Lysias, Plato</keywords>

--- Page 217 ---
<meta>This page continues the scholarly debate regarding the chronology of Socrates' life and death, transitioning from a discussion of conflicting historical sources to a narrative account of his final hours as told in Plato's Phaedo.</meta>

### SOCRATES.

The writer of the *Socratic Epistle* accepts *Polycrates* as the accuser at the trial and treats the oration as having been spoken then and there; *Hermippus*, whom *Laertius* cites, says the same. However, *Favorinus*, a critic of later times when chronology was more exact, detects the error by calculating the dates. *Allatius* will by no means allow the criticism of *Favorinus* and labors to introduce an uncertainty regarding the time, so that he may persuade others that *Socrates* lived beyond the rebuilding of the walls of *Athens*. The great engine with which he labors to demolish all that has been asserted by the ancients is the testimony of *Suidas*, who (I know not upon what authority) says he lived eighty years. His smaller artillery includes the baseless corrections of *Meursius* and the mistake of *Scaliger* noted earlier; the absurd chronological error <term>metachronism: an error in dating an event</term> of the *Alexandrian Chronicle*, which claims *Socrates* died in the 104th Olympiad in the 90th year of his age; the historical ignorance <term>anistoresy: a lack of historical knowledge or research</term> of the unknown writer of *Aristotle's* life, who supposes that at seventeen years old he heard *Socrates* for three years; and most ridiculous of all, the notorious anachronisms of *Plato* are used as indisputable arguments to challenge the truth. With these proofs, in the sophisticated disguise of a dialogue, he endeavors to puzzle the unwary reader.

The account of his death is recorded by *Plato* in the character of *Phaedo*, an eyewitness:

> "Every day," he says, "I went with his other friends to visit him. We met in the court where he was tried, as it was near the prison; there we occupied ourselves with conversation until the prison was opened, then we went in to him and often spent the whole day with him. But that day we met earlier than usual, for the evening before, as we came out of the prison, we heard the ship had returned from *Delos* <note>The return of the sacred ship from Delos marked the end of a religious period during which no executions could take place in Athens.</note>. Therefore, we appointed to meet early the next morning at the usual place. When we arrived, the porter came out to us and told us that we must wait a while before we could be admitted, for the Eleven Officers <term>The Eleven: Athenian magistrates in charge of prisons and executions.</term> were there taking off his chains, having brought him word that he must die today. Not long after, he came out again and told us we might go in. When we entered, we found *Socrates’* chains newly taken off and *Xantippe* sitting by him with a child in her arms. As soon as she saw us, she burst into tears and cried out, 'Ah, *Socrates*, this is the last time your friends shall ever speak to you, or you to them!' *Socrates* (addressing himself to *Crito*) said, '*Crito*, let somebody carry her home.' Whereupon some of *Crito's* servants led her away as she wailed and beat her breast. *Socrates*, who was sitting upon the bed, drew up his leg and rubbed it, saying meanwhile, 'How strange a thing, friends, is that which men call—'

### SOCRATES.

“How strangely related pleasure is to pain, to which it seems so contrary! They do not indeed arrive together, but he who takes one is immediately overtaken by the other, as if they were tied together. If *Aesop* had observed this, he certainly would have made a fable about it, as if God, wishing to settle their dispute, had joined them at the ends since He was unable to make them absolutely one; so that whoever has one must soon have the other also. As it happens to me at this time, the pain my fetters gave me just now has now turned into a kind of pleasure and tickles me.” 

“*Cebes* said, ‘You have opportunely reminded me to ask why, since your imprisonment (which you never did before), you have written **poems**—a hymn to *Apollo* and *Aesop’s* Fables rendered into verse. Many have questioned me about it, particularly *Evenus*; if he asks again, what answer shall I give him?’” 

“*Socrates* answered, ‘Tell him that I truly did it not to compete with him and his poetry, but to comply with a dream (which I have had more than once) commanding me to practice music. In obedience to this, I first composed verses in honor of the God whose festival this was. Then, believing it essential for a poet to write fictions—which I do not do by nature—I made use of some of *Aesop’s* fables that I remembered, as they first came into my mind. Tell *Evenus* this, and bid him farewell from me; and if he is wise, tell him to follow me, for it seems I must depart today, as the Athenians have ordered it.’”

“Simmias said, ‘What is it you are asking *Evenus* to do? I have often conversed with him, but as far as I understand him, he will not be at all ready to follow your advice.’ ‘What,’ said he, ‘is he not a philosopher?’ ‘He seems so,’ answered *Simmias*. ‘Then he will,’ replied *Socrates*, ‘and so will all who deserve that name; but perhaps he will not lay violent hands upon himself, for that is not lawful.’” 

And as he was speaking thus, he set his leg back down on the ground, and sitting so, continued all the rest of the discussion. Then *Cebes* asked why it should be prohibited to take one's own life, yet a philosopher ought to desire to follow a dying person? 

He answered, “Men are the possessions of God. Would you not be angry if your slave should kill himself against your will, and punish him if it were in your power? We must wait for a summons from God, an inevitable necessity (such as I have at this time) to take us from here.” 

“This is true,” replied *Cebes*, “but what you asserted just now is inconsistent with it. Since God takes care of us as His possessions, can a wise man desire to be out of His protection? He cannot think to improve his condition by freeing himself from such an excellent government.” 

*Socrates* seemed much pleased with the subtlety of *Cebes*, and turning to us said, “*Cebes* is always inquisitive and will not easily accept anything; to

<summary>Socrates reflects on the duality of pleasure and pain and explains that he composed poetry in prison to fulfill a recurring dream. He then argues that while a philosopher welcomes death, suicide is unlawful because humans are the property of the gods.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Socrates’ defense of his decision to face death calmly, following his discussion with Simmias and Cebes about whether a philosopher should follow him into the afterlife.</meta>

### SOCRATES.

“—deserve. ‘To me,’ said *Simmias*, ‘what he has said seems reasonable. How can wise men endure—much less strive—to part from those who are so much better than themselves? But *Cebes* is referring to you, who are so ready to leave us and the gods, whom you acknowledge as good governors.’ ‘You are right,’ answered *Socrates*. ‘I suppose you would have me make my defense as if in a court of law.’ ‘By all means,’ said *Simmias*. ‘Well then,’ he replied, ‘I will strive to defend myself better before you than I did before the judges. Truly, if I did not believe I was going to just gods, and to men better than any now living, I would be inexcusable for despising death. But I am certain I will go to the gods—who are very good masters—and I hope to meet with good men. I am of good courage, hoping that something of man remains after death, and that it will then be much better for the good than for the bad.’

“At this point, *Crito* interrupted him and said that the man who was to administer the poison advised him to speak little and not get overheated with debating, for that would interfere with the poison’s effect. Some who had ignored this advice had been forced to take the poison two or three times. ‘Never mind him,’ said *Socrates*. ‘Let him prepare enough to serve for two or three times, if necessary.’ Then he proceeded with a lengthy discourse, declaring that the chief duty of a philosopher is to meditate on death; therefore, he ought not to fear its approach. He argued that as death is the release of the soul from the body, it is the philosopher’s duty to free the soul from bodily passions. He reasoned that if we understand things better as the soul is more disengaged from the senses, we shall understand most perfectly when the soul is completely freed from the body by death—and this perfection of knowledge is the sole aim of philosophy.”

When this part of the discourse ended, *Cebes* prompted its renewal by asking him to prove the immortality of the soul. Socrates did this first by arguing the necessary cycle of generation and decay as opposites (the basis for the *Pythagorean* doctrine of reincarnation <term>transmigration</term>). Next, he argued from the soul's manner of reasoning, which, being based only on recollection <term>reminiscence</term>, suggests it existed before the body (when it had perfect knowledge of those Ideas which it recovers through sensory objects) and consequently shall continue to exist after the body. Much more is spoken by *Plato* in *Socrates’* name, though almost all of it is clearly *Plato’s* own work, and it is impossible to separate his words from the rest. The conclusion of his discourse (as summarized by *Cicero*) was: <margin>Tusculan Disputations, page 127.</margin> “That there are two paths and a twofold journey for souls when they leave the body. Those who have defiled themselves with human vices and abandoned themselves to pleasures that blinded them—whether they were polluted by personal sins or used unforgivable <term>inexpiable</term> deceptions to wrong the public—take a side-path

<summary>Socrates argues that death is the ultimate goal of philosophy as it frees the soul for perfect knowledge, and he begins to prove the soul's immortality through the theories of opposites and recollection.</summary>

-># SOCRATES.<-

“...pathway secluded from the Council of the Gods. But those who have preserved themselves whole and chaste from the least contagion of their bodies, having always withdrawn themselves from them, and in human flesh imitated the lives of Gods, find a ready way open for them, leading them to those from whom they came. And as swans are (not without reason) sacred to *Apollo*, because they seem to have learned divination <term>the art of foretelling the future</term> from him, whereby foreseeing the good that is in 
<margin>* Plato</margin> 
death they die with songs and delight, so ought all good and knowing persons to do. Let everyone, therefore, prepare for this journey against the time that fate shall call him away. You, *Simmias*, *Cebes*, and the rest here present, shall go at your appointed hour; fate now summons me (as the Tragedian saith), and perhaps it is time that I go to the bath, for I think it is best to wash before I take the poison, so that I may save the women the labor of washing me after I am dead.”

When he had finished speaking, *Crito* asked him what directions he would leave concerning his sons and other affairs, and if they could do anything that might be acceptable to him. “I desire no more,” said he, “than what I have often told you: if you take care of yourselves, whatever you do will be acceptable to me and mine, even if you promise nothing. If you neglect yourselves and virtue, you can do nothing acceptable to us even if you promise ever so much.” 

“That,” answered *Crito*, “we shall observe; but how will you be buried?” 

“As you think good,” said he, “if you can catch me, and if I do not give you the slip.” Then with a smile, turning to us, “I cannot persuade *Crito*,” said he, “that I am anything more than the carcass you will soon behold, and therefore he takes this care for my burial. It seems that what I told him just now—that as soon as I have taken the poison I shall go to the joys of the blessed—has been to little purpose. He was my bail, bound to the judges for my appearance; you must now be my sureties <term>guarantors</term> to him that I have departed. Let him not say that Socrates is carried to the grave, or laid underground; for know, dear *Crito*, such a mistake would be a wrong to my soul. Do not be dejected; tell the world my body only is buried, and that in whatever manner you please.”

This said, he arose and retired into an inner room, taking *Crito* with him, leaving us discussing our own misery, soon to be deprived like orphans of so dear a father. After his bathing, his wife and the other women of his family came with his sons—two of them children, one a youth. When he had arranged his domestic affairs with them, he dismissed them and came out to us.

<summary>Socrates explains why the wise do not fear death, likening himself to a swan, and gives final instructions to Crito regarding his burial and family. He emphasizes that his true self will depart to the blessed, while only his body will remain to be buried.</summary>

-># SOCRATES.<-

“It was now sunset (for he had stayed long inside) when the officer entered, and after a little pause said, ‘*Socrates*, I have not observed that conduct in you which I have found in others; rather, I thought you the most generous, the mildest, and the best of all men that ever came into this place. So I now see you do not hate me for that for which others are the cause; you know the message I bring. Farewell; bear what you cannot remedy.’ With that he departed weeping. ‘And fare thee well,’ said *Socrates*, ‘I will. How polite is this man? I found him the same throughout the time of my imprisonment; he would often visit me and talk with me, always using me courteously, and now see how kindly he weeps for me! But come, *Crito*, let us do as he bids us; if the poison is ready, let it be brought in.’ 

‘The sun is yet scarcely set,’ answered *Crito*; ‘others take it late after a plentiful supper and full cups; do not be in such a hurry; there is time enough.’ 

He replied, ‘Those who do so think they gain time; but what shall I gain by drinking it late, only to deceive myself by being covetous of life and sparing of that which is no longer mine? Please, let it be as I say.’ 

Then *Crito* sent one of the attendants, who immediately returned, and with him the man who was to administer the poison, bringing a cup in his hand. *Socrates* said to him, ‘Please, honest friend (for you are well versed in these matters), what must I do?’ 

‘Nothing,’ said he, ‘except that as soon as you have drunk, walk until you find your legs begin to fail; then lie down.’ And in so saying, he gave him the cup. 

*Socrates* took it cheerfully, not changing either his expression or his color; and looking pleasantly upon him, he asked whether he might pour any of it out as a libation <term>a ritual pouring of a liquid as an offering to a god</term>. The man answered that he had prepared no more than would just serve. ‘Yet,’ said *Socrates*, ‘I may pray to God, and I will, that my passage from here may be happy, which I beseech him to grant.’ In that same instant, he drank it off easily without any disturbance. 

Many of us who until now had refrained from tears, when we saw him put the cup to his mouth and drink off the poison, were not able to contain ourselves any longer. *Socrates*, observing this, said, ‘*Friends, what do you mean? For this reason I sent away the women, lest they should be so restless; I have heard we should die with gratitude and praise <note>original: "gratulation and applause"</note>. Be quiet then and take it patiently.*’ 

These words made us suppress our tears with shame. When he had walked a while, perceiving his legs to fail, he lay down on his back as the executioner directed him. The man, looking at his feet, pinched them hard, asking him if he felt it; he answered, ‘No.’ He did the same to his legs, and showing us how every part successively grew cold and stiff, he told us that when that chill reached his heart, he would die. Not long after, he spoke his last words: ‘*O Crito, I owe a cock to Asclepius <note>The Greek god of medicine and healing. Socrates suggests that death is the "cure" for the sickness of earthly life.</note>; pay it, do not neglect—*’”

<summary>This page describes the final moments of Socrates as he calmly drinks the hemlock, rebukes his friends for their emotional outbursts, and begins to succumb to the poison's effects. He ends with a famous final request to Crito to settle a religious debt to the god of healing.</summary>

...neglect it not. "It shall be done," said *Crito*; "Will you have anything else?" He made no answer, lay still for a while, and then stretched himself out. With that, the executioner uncovered him; his eyes were set, and *Crito* closed them. This (says *Plato*) was the end of the best, the wisest, and the most just of men—a story that *Cicero* claims he never read without tears.

*Aristotle* says that a <term>Magus</term><note>A member of a hereditary priestly class of ancient Persia, often associated with wisdom or sorcery.</note> coming from *Syria* to *Athens* not only criticized *Socrates* for many things but also foretold that he would die a violent death. *Laertius* ends the account of his life with this epigram:

->Acknowledged as wisest by the gods and by wisdom herself.<-
->The *Athenians* gave you a poisonous draft,<-

### CHAPTER 14.

### *What happened after his death.*

<margin>* Plutarch, *On Moral Virtue*</margin>
<margin>* Plutarch, *Lives of the Ten Orators*</margin>
<margin>* Libanius</margin>
He was buried with tears and great ceremony (contrary to his own instructions) by his friends. Among them, the excessive grief of *Plato* is noted by *Plutarch*, as is the mourning attire of *Isocrates*. As soon as they had performed that last service, fearing the cruelty of the tyrants, they stole out of the city—the greater part going to *Euclid* at *Megara*, where they were kindly received—and the rest to other places.

<margin>* Socrates, *Epistles*</margin>
Soon after, a Lacedaemonian youth, who had no acquaintance with *Socrates* other than what his reputation provided, traveled to *Athens* intending to become his disciple. Having come as far as the city gates and being ready to enter—rejoicing to be so near the goal he aimed for—instead of *Socrates*, he met the news of his death. He was so troubled by this that he would not go inside the city gates, but inquiring where he was buried, went there and broke into a passionate discourse, accompanied by many tears, to the entombed body. When night came, he fell asleep upon the sepulcher. The next morning, affectionately kissing the dust that lay upon it and passionately taking leave of the place, he returned to *Megara*.

*Suidas* tells a similar story (for *Libanius* implies there were more examples of this kind) of a man from Chios named *Cyrsas*. Coming to *Athens* to hear *Socrates*, he went to his tomb and slept there; *Socrates* appeared to him in a dream and conversed with him. With only that satisfaction, he went directly home again.

<margin>* Socrates, *Epistles*</margin>
By these events, the *Athenians* were awakened to a sense...

-># SOCRATES.<-

...sense of their injustice, considering they were liable to the judgment of the Lacedaemonians <note>Spartans</note> for extraordinary crimes. These Lacedaemonians were so affectionate toward the philosophers whom the Athenians had murdered that they took such long journeys just to see Socrates—whom the Athenians would not keep while he was with them. At this, the people became so exasperated that they were ready to tear those wicked men, who had caused his death, piecemeal with their teeth. The whole city cried out, disowning the act and declaring that its authors ought to be put to death.

<margin>
* Diogenes Laertius, *Life of Anytus*
</margin>
Antisthenes furthered their rage in this way: some young men from Pontus, drawn to Athens by Socrates' fame, met Antisthenes. He took them to Anytus, telling them he was much wiser than Socrates. Those present were so indignant that they drove Anytus out of the city. From there he went to Heraclea, where some say the citizens also expelled him; <margin>* Themistius, *Oration 2* </margin> <margin>* Laertius </margin> <margin>* Diodorus Siculus, 14 </margin> <margin>* *Socratic Epistles* </margin> <margin>* Plutarch, *On Envy and Hatred*</margin> others say they stoned him to death. Melitus was condemned and put to death by the Athenians. Others affirm the same of all his accusers without trial. Plutarch writes that the people hated them so much that they would not allow them to light fires at their houses, would not answer any of their questions, and would not wash in the same water—instead throwing away the water the accusers had touched as if it were impure—until, unable to endure this hatred, the accusers hanged themselves.

In further testimony of their repentance, the Athenians called home Socrates' friends to their former freedom of meeting. They forbade public spectacles, games, and wrestling for a time. They caused his statue, made in bronze by Lysippus, to be set up in the Pompeum <note>A public building in Athens used for the preparation of religious processions.</note>. Because a plague ensued, which they attributed to the injustice of this act, they made an order that no man should mention Socrates publicly or in the theater, so that they might forget what they had done. Euripides, restrained by this order from doing it directly, reproached them covertly in a tragedy named *Palamedes* (in whom he alluded to Socrates), particularly in these verses:

<margin>
<note>original: "Τὰν δαὶ ἀλγύνοις φαῖσα."</note> The <unclear>nightingale</unclear> shown in pain. For <unclear>"only"</unclear>, it seems to have been some gloss to explain <unclear>"nightingale"</unclear>. So Callimachus in his *Epigrams*: <note>original: "Αἰ αἰ θανεῖν ἀηδόνα"</note> <unclear>Alas, alas, for the death of the nightingale.</unclear>
</margin>
> *The nightingale, who knew no mischief,*
> *Is slain (alas!) is slain by you.*

At these words, all the spectators, understanding they were meant for Socrates, fell to weeping.

<margin>
* Eunapius
* In *Aedesius*
</margin>
The death of this single person, says Eunapius, brought a general calamity upon the city. For it may easily be gathered by calculating the dates that from then on the Athenians did nothing significant; instead, the city gradually decayed, and with it all of Greece.

## CHAPTER XV.

### Of his person and virtues.

<margin>
* Plato, *Phaedrus*, *Theaetetus*
* Scholiast on Aristophanes
* Plato, *Phaedrus*, *Theaetetus*
* Plato, *Protagoras*
</margin>
Regarding his person, he was very unattractive and had a melancholy complexion. He was bald, had a flat nose, bulging eyes, and a stern, downcast look. He was hesitant in his speech and very concise; his language was rough and informal, yet it was more effective than all the eloquence of *Themistocles*, *Pericles*, or anyone else. He was so sharp that he could argue either side of any question, and for this reason, he was criticized by *Aristophanes* as having "two tongues," one of which was used to defend wrongdoing. He was passionate in dispute and often became so carried away that he would strike himself and tear his beard, much to the ridicule of the bystanders, which he bore calmly.

<margin>
* Plutarch, *Phaedrus*
</margin>
He was patient when refuted. Sometimes he covered his face while speaking so that he would not be distracted by any visual object.

<margin>
* Seneca, *Epistles*, book 1, 103
* Xenophon, *Memorabilia*, book 1
* Laertius
* Plato, *Symposium*
* *Socratic Epistles*; Libanius
* Plato, *Phaedrus*
* Cicero, *Tusculan Disputations*, 5 & *On Fate*
</margin>
His constitution was strong and hardy, which he maintained through diligent care of his health. He could well endure cold and hunger and, on special occasions, could drink an excess of wine without becoming intoxicated or losing control. His clothing was the same in winter as in summer, as he owned only one garment a year. He wore no shoes, and his diet was meager. In short, his appearance suggested so little potential that *Zopyrus*, a physiognomist <note>one who judges character from physical appearance</note> who claimed to discover the dispositions of men by their looks, said Socrates was stupid because there were blockages in the parts of his neck. He added that Socrates was prone to lust for women and many other vices. At this, *Alcibiades* and other friends of his who were present, knowing him to be free from those accusations, began to laugh; but *Socrates* defended the man's skill, answering that he was by nature inclined toward those vices, but he suppressed his inclinations through reason. 

<margin>
* Plato, *Symposium*
* Scholiast on Aristophanes, p. 136
</margin>
For this reason, *Alcibiades* used to say that he resembled the statues of *Silenus* <note>a tutor to Dionysus, often depicted as a bald, snub-nosed old man</note> (as he indeed did in his face, baldness, and flat nose) carved on the outside of small boxes, which showed him sitting and playing a pipe. Just as those boxes contained images of the gods within, so was Socrates adorned with chastity, integrity, and all internal beauty. He was overcome, as 

<margin>
* *Against Colotes*
</margin>
*Plutarch* says, with a divine zeal for virtue. *Xenophon*, *Laertius*, and others assert these examples of his character.

<margin>
* Xenophon, book 4
* Plutarch, *Against Colotes*; Libanius, *Apology*
</margin>
He was so wise that he never failed in judging between better and worse, nor did he need the help of others to do so. Yet he constantly claimed that the only thing he knew was that he knew nothing. For this reason, he was declared the wisest of all men by the Oracle of *Apollo* at *Delphi*. This message was given to *Chaerephon* while many witnesses were present:

<margin>
* Scholiast on Aristophanes
</margin>
> Wise Sophocles, wiser Euripides,
> But wisest of all men is Socrates.

<margin>* Cicero, *Academic Questions*, Book 1. * Diogenes Laertius.</margin>
* **Apollo** (says **Cicero**), believing that the only wisdom of mankind consists in people not thinking they know things of which they are actually ignorant. * This Oracle, although he was not at all arrogant because of it, caused him much envy.

<margin>* Xenophon, *Memorabilia*, Book 4, p. 818. * Xenophon, *Memorabilia*, Book 1, p. 710. * Plato, *Philebus*. * Suidas. * Scholiast on Aristophanes.</margin>
* He was so religious that he never did anything without first consulting the **Gods**; he was never known to attempt or speak any impiety. * He held a reverence for the **Gods** that was not merely human, but transcended the greatest fear. * Some say it was out of his great reverence for the **Divinity** that he used to swear by a Cock, a Dog, and a Plane-Tree (under which they used to sit), though this was interpreted by others as Atheism.

<margin>* Diogenes Laertius. * Aelian, Book 9. Cicero, *Tusculan Disputations*, Book 3; *On Duties*, Book 1. * Pliny, Book 7, Chapter 19.</margin>
* He was constant and a lover of the public good, as appears in his vote to acquit the ten Captains <note>The Athenian generals at the Battle of Arginusae, whom Socrates refused to condemn collectively in violation of legal procedure.</note>, in his refusal to obey the Thirty Tyrants <note>The pro-Spartan oligarchy that ruled Athens after the Peloponnesian War.</note> when they ordered him to fetch **Leon** in, in his refusal to escape from prison, and in his pitying those who grieved for his death. * **Xantippe** used to say that when the State was oppressed with a thousand miseries, he always went out and came home with the same facial expression (never more cheerful nor more troubled); for he maintained a mind that was calm and cheerful on all occasions, far removed from grief and above all fear. In his old age, falling sick, he was asked by a visitor how he was doing. "Very well," he said, "either way: if I live, I shall have more emulation <note>In this context, he likely means he will continue to be a model of virtue for others to strive toward.</note>; if I die, more praise."

<margin>* Xenophon, *Memorabilia*, Book 4, p. 818. * Plato, *Phaedo*. * Xenophon, *Memorabilia*, Book 1, p. 712. * Aelian, Book 13.</margin>
* He was so temperate that he never preferred what is pleasant over what is wholesome. He never ate more than his appetite (which was his "sauce" <note>Meaning hunger served as his seasoning.</note>) made delightful. All drink was pleasing to him because he never drank except when he was thirsty, and even then with such temperate caution that * he poured out the first draught of water upon the ground as a libation. If he were ever invited to a feast—which is very difficult for others—he very easily took care not to eat more than was consistent with his health. * He was very careful of his health because the exercises of the soul depend on it; in pursuit of that, he used to walk constantly before meals. Upon being asked by an observer what he was doing, he replied, "I am getting broth for my supper" <note>Meaning he was working up an appetite.</note>. It is attributed to this temperance that although **Athens** was often visited by the plague during his time, he alone escaped it.

<margin>* Xenophon, *Memorabilia*, Book 1, p. 711. * Libanius. * Diogenes Laertius.</margin>
* He was so frugal that no matter how little he had, it was always enough. * Lacking the means to live splendidly, he did not anxiously seek how to acquire more, but rather how to adapt his lifestyle to what he had. * He was so contented with this that he claimed he came closest to the **Gods** because he lacked the least. Seeing the great variety of things for sale, he would say to himself, "How many things there are that I do not need!" and he often had these verses in his mouth:

<margin>Aelian, Book 9.</margin>
Alcibiades, being ambitiously generous, sent him many great presents. Xantippe, admiring their value, desired him to accept them. "We," answered Socrates, "will compete in generosity with Alcibiades by not accepting what he has sent us, which is its own kind of grand gesture."

<margin>* Diogenes Laertius.</margin>
* To the same man, who offered him a large plot of ground to build a house upon, he said: "And if I needed shoes, would you give me leather to make them? Would I not deserve to be mocked if I accepted it?"

<margin>* Diogenes Laertius.</margin>
<margin>* Seneca, *On Benefits*, 5. 6.</margin>
