* He snubbed Archelaus, King of Macedonia, and Scopas, son of Cranonius, and Eurylochus, son of Larissaeus, by refusing their money and declining to visit them. * When Archelaus sent for him to request his company, Socrates said he would not go to someone from whom he would receive favors that he could not repay in kind. * To Perdicas, who asked why he would not come to him, he answered, "Lest I die the most dishonorable death; that is, lest I receive a favor I cannot repay."

<margin>Aelian, Book 9.</margin>
Coming home late one night from a feast, some wild young men who knew of his return lay in wait for him. They were dressed like Furies <note>In Greek mythology, the Furies (Erinyes) were deities of vengeance, often depicted with snakes for hair and carrying torches.</note>, wearing masks and carrying torches, which they used to frighten people they met. As soon as Socrates saw them, he was not troubled at all; he stopped and began to question them in his usual manner, as if he were in the Lyceum or the Academy.

<margin>* Diogenes Laertius.</margin>
<margin>* Stobaeus, 71.</margin>
<margin>* Diogenes Laertius.</margin>
<margin>* Plutarch, *On the Education of Children*.</margin>
* He despised those who mocked him. * When he was told that a certain person had reviled him behind his back, he said, "Let him beat me while I am not there." When told that another spoke ill of him, he said, "He has not yet learned to speak well."
* Being kicked by an insolent young man, and seeing those who were with him were much incensed and ready to pursue the fellow, he said, "If an ass kicks me, would you have me kick back or sue him?" But the fellow did not escape unpunished, for everyone reproached him for this insolence and called him "the reviler," so that at last, out of frustration, he hanged himself.

<margin>Seneca, *On Anger*, 3.</margin>
When another man struck him with a box on the ear, he said no more than that it was a shame a man did not know when to go outdoors wearing a helmet.

<margin>St. Basil.</margin>
Another man attacked him with great violence, which he endured without the least disturbance, allowing the man to vent his anger until he had made Socrates' face completely swollen and bruised.

Socrates immediately replied, "And would you not have done better if you had told me that in private?"*

<margin>Xenophon, *Memorabilia*, Book 2, page 752.</margin>
* Regarding this, his outward efforts were as much valued and desired by others as he valued and desired them.

## CHAPTER XVI.

->His Wives and Children.<-

He had two wives. The first was Xanthippe, the daughter of an Athenian citizen, as Theodoret claims. Theodoret adds that she was unfaithful before he married her—even with Socrates himself—among others. Athenaeus also says that after they were married, Socrates lent her to a friend and that Alcibiades slept with her. However, Aristoxenus and Porphyry, from whom these insults originate, have been noted for having too much malice to be considered reliable authorities.

<meta>This page continues the account of Socrates’ character, shifting focus from his public virtues to his private life and his famously difficult relationship with his wife, Xantippe.</meta>

# SOCRATES

<margin>Aulus Gellius, Book 1, Chapter 17.</margin>
She was (according to the characterization *Aulus Gellius* gives her) ill-tempered, stubborn, and always chiding and scolding both day <margin>Diogenes Laertius.</margin> and night. For that reason, he chose her, as he professed to *Antisthenes*, because he observed that those who want to be excellent in horsemanship choose the wildest horses. They know that if they are able to manage them, they may easily rule others. Socrates, being desirous to have much social interaction with people, took her as his wife, knowing that if he could endure her, he might easily converse with all men. To *Alcibiades*, who said her scolding was intolerable, Socrates professed it was nothing to him. He was used to it, he said, like those who live with the continual noise of a mill. "Besides," he said, "cannot you endure the cackling of hens?" "But they," answered *Alcibiades*, "bring me eggs and chickens." "And my *Xantippe*," replied *Socrates*, "brings me children."

<margin>Diogenes Laertius.</margin>
There are several instances of her impatience and his endurance. One day, in front of some of his friends, she fell into her usual extravagant passion. When he did not answer her at all, he went outside with his friends. But he was no sooner out the door when she ran up to her room and threw water down upon his head. Turning to his friends, he said, "Did I not tell you that after so much thunder we would have rain?"

<margin>Diogenes Laertius.</margin>
Another time, she pulled his cloak off his shoulders in the open Forum. Some friends who were present advised him to hit her. "Yes," he said, "so that while we two fight, you may all stand by and cry out, 'Well done, *Socrates*! Go to it, *Xantippe*!'"

<margin>Marcus Aurelius, Book 11, Section 18.</margin>
*Marcus Aurelius* <note>The original text refers to the Emperor Marcus Aurelius as "Antoninus."</note> alludes to another story of the same kind in these words: *How Socrates looked when he was forced to wrap himself in a skin after Xantippe had taken his clothes and carried them outside with her; and what he said to his friends, who, out of modest respect, turned back when they saw him dressed that way.*

<margin>Plutarch, *On the Control of Anger* <note>original: "de ira cohib."</note></margin>
Having brought *Euthydemus* from the gymnasium <term>palaestra</term> to dine with him, *Xantippe* ran to the table in a rage, scolding, and overturned it. *Euthydemus*, much troubled, rose up and wanted to leave, but *Socrates* would not let him. "The other day," he said, "a hen did the very same thing at your house, yet I was not angry about it."

<margin>Aelian, *Various History*, Book 11, Chapter 12.</margin>
When *Alcibiades* sent him an exquisite marzipan <term>march-pane</term>, *Xantippe* furiously (as was her manner) threw it out of the basket and trod upon it. At this, *Socrates* laughed and said, "Now you shall not have your share of it either."

<margin>Aelian, Book 7, Chapter 10; Diogenes Laertius; Stobaeus.</margin>
Another time, she offered to go to a public show dressed indecently. "Take heed," he said, "that you do not become the spectacle rather than the spectator."

<margin>Stobaeus, 183.</margin>
With good reason, therefore, he said, "I have had three evils: grammar, poetry, and a bad wife. Two I have shaken off, but my bad wife I cannot."

<meta>catchword: His</meta>

<summary>This page recounts several anecdotes illustrating the legendary patience of Socrates in the face of his wife Xantippe's famously volatile and abusive behavior. Socrates viewed his marriage as a form of discipline to better prepare him for dealing with all types of people in society.</summary>
<keywords>Socrates, Xantippe, Alcibiades, patience, marriage, Antisthenes, Aulus Gellius, Diogenes Laertius, Marcus Aurelius, Plutarch, Stoicism</keywords>

--- Page 229 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Socrates' domestic life, transitioning from his relationship with Xantippe to the accounts of his second wife, Myrto, and the legal context of bigamy in ancient Athens.</meta>

# SOCRATES

<margin>* Theodoret.</margin>
His other wife was named *Myrto*, the niece of *Lysimachus* and granddaughter of *Aristides*. She was not the daughter of *Aristides* "the Just," as *Laertius* and *Suidas* (following him) claim, but of another man of that name, the third in descent from him, as noted by *Athenaeus*. For the two daughters of *Aristides* the Just would have been of a very great age long before the 77th Olympiad, when *Socrates* was born. *Aristides* died an old man in exile long before that time; it is certain that *Themistocles* died in the second year of the 77th Olympiad, and as *Aemilius Probus* affirms, *Aristides* died four years before *Themistocles* was banished from Athens. Consequently, *Plutarch* more cautiously calls her the niece, rather than the daughter, of *Aristides*.

<margin>* Socratic Epistles.</margin>
Because *Xantippe* outlived him (as is clear from *Plato*), some believe he was first married to *Myrto*. However, *Demetrius Phalereus*, *Aristoxenus* (to whom *Athenaeus* says *Aristotle* gave the research material), *Callisthenes*, and *Porphyrius* all attest that he had both of these wives at the same time. This is why *Aristippus*, in his letter to his daughter *Myrto*, advises her to go to Athens and, above all, to honor *Xantippe* and *Myrto*, and to live with them just as he lived with *Socrates*.

<margin>* Laertius.</margin>
The occasion for which the *Athenians*—who since the time of *Cecrops* had strictly observed monogamy—allowed bigamy in the time of *Socrates* was this: In the second year of the 87th Olympiad and the third year of the 88th, *Athens* was severely afflicted by a plague. Accompanied by war and famine, this caused such a great shortage of men that they issued an edict making it lawful for any man who wished to take two wives. *Euripides* took advantage of this indulgence, and *Satyrus* the Peripatetic and *Hieronymus* of Rhodes (who recorded the decree) attest that *Socrates* did as well. *Athenaeus* attributes the silence of the comic poets on this matter to this decree, as they otherwise missed no opportunity for <term>reproach</term> <note>slander or mockery</note>. *Plutarch* implies that he took her out of charity, for she was a widow <margin>* Theodoret.</margin> living in extreme want, without any <term>portion</term> <note>dowry</note> or assets.

*Porphyrius* reports that when these two (*Xantippe* and *Myrto*) quarreled, they would eventually both turn on *Socrates* and beat him, because he stood by and never tried to separate them. Instead, he laughed as much when they fought with him as he did when they fought with one another.

By *Xantippe*, he had a son named *Lamprocles*, who could not tolerate her <term>impatience</term> <note>ill temper</note> as well as his father could. Having been provoked by her into disobedience, he was <term>reclaimed</term> <note>reformed or corrected</note> by *Socrates*. He died young, as may be gathered from *Plutarch*, who says that *Timarchus* of *Chaeronea*, when dying very young, earnestly requested of *Socrates* that he might be buried near his son *Lamprocles*, who had died only a few days before and was his dear friend of the same age. It appears from *Plato* that he had more sons by her; in his *Apology*, he mentions three: two grown men and one child.

<meta>catchword: which</meta>

<keywords>Socrates, Myrto, Xantippe, bigamy, Athens plague, Aristides, Lamprocles, Plutarch, domestic life, Athenian law</keywords>

...which seems to be the same child brought by *Xantippe* to him in prison on the day of his death, and as *Plato* describes it, held in her lap.

By *Myrto* he had two sons: the eldest, *Sophroniscus*; the youngest, *Menedemus* or *Menexenus*, though some say he had *Menedemus* by *Xantippe*.

### CHAPTER XVII.

### *His Scholars and Students.*

<margin>* De Oratore [On the Orator], book 3.</margin>
Whereas (says *Cicero*), many people emerged from *Socrates* because his various disputes were spread everywhere, one student seized on one idea while another seized on another. As a result, there were, so to speak, many several "families" or schools of thought that differed among themselves, often being very disconnected and in disagreement; yet all these philosophers wanted to be called, and considered themselves to be, *Socratics*. These included:

*Plato*, from whom came *Aristotle* and *Xenocrates*; the first taking the name of *Peripatetic*, the other of *Academic*.

*Antisthenes*, who chiefly favored the patience and endurance in *Socrates*' discourse; from him came first the *Cynics*, then the *Stoics*.

*Aristippus*, who was more delighted with his more pleasure-oriented <note>original: "voluptuous"</note> discussions; from him sprung the *Cyrenaic Philosophy*.

There were others who likewise called themselves *Socratics*, but their sects were broken and made quite extinct by the strength and arguments of the former groups; such were:

*Phaedo*, an *Elean*, who instituted a particular school, named the *Eliac* after him; it was later called *Eretrian*, after *Menedemus* who taught at *Eretria*. From him came *Pyrrho*, and from there the *Pyrrhonians*.

*Euclid* of *Megara*, founder of the *Megaric* school (named after him), which was called the *Dialectic* school by his disciple *Clinomachus*, and ended with *Zeno* the *Citiean*, who introduced the *Stoic* school.

The *Herillians* are also named as a *sect* that wished to be called *Socratic*. To these listed by *Cicero*, *Suidas* adds:

*Bryso* of *Heraclea*, who together with *Euclid* invented **disputative logic**.

*Theodorus*, surnamed the **Atheist**, who invented a peculiar sect called the *Theodorean*; the opinion which he taught was *Indifference* <note>original: "Αδιαφορία"</note>.

There were other disciples of *Socrates* who followed his philosophy without establishing any particular sect from it, and therefore most properly deserve the title of *Socratics*; such are *Crito*, *Chaerephon*, *Xenophon*, *Aeschines*, *Simmias*, *Cebes*, *Glauco*, and *Terpsion*.

The last kind of his students were those who made no profession of philosophy, of whom were

<meta>Catchword: Critias</meta>

<summary>This page details Socrates' sons and provides an overview of the various philosophical schools—such as the Academics, Cynics, and Stoics—that claimed descent from his teachings, as well as a list of his most faithful individual disciples.</summary>
<keywords>Sophroniscus, Menexenus, Socratics, Plato, Aristotle, Antisthenes, Cynics, Stoics, Aristippus, Cyrenaics, Phaedo, Euclid of Megara, Theodorus the Atheist, logic</keywords>

--- Page 231 ---
<meta>This page continues the catalog of Socrates' students, focusing on his influence over the prominent Athenian figures Critias and Alcibiades, as well as the sons of his friend Crito.</meta>

<header>SOCRATES.</header>

*Critias* and *Alcibiades* later proved to be the most ambitious spirits among the Athenians. However, while they associated with *Socrates*, this trait was not apparent in either of them—perhaps because they were too young to express such vice, or because they cunningly complied (as *Xenophon* suggests) with *Socrates* in hopes that his conversation would enable them to manage their own ambitious plans. As soon as they attempted those plans, they ended their friendship with *Socrates*. *Critias* abandoned him and turned his affection into hatred because *Socrates* reproved his love for *Euthydemus*. *Alcibiades*, who was naturally dissolute <term>immoral or licentious</term>, was reformed by *Socrates* and remained so as long as he associated with him. He was of such exquisite beauty that it gave some cause to slander the friendship between him and *Socrates*; regarding this, *Aristoxenus* is cited by *Diogenes Laertius* and *Athenaeus*, and some verses by *Aspasia* are cited by the latter. We refer to *Plato* and *Xenophon* for his vindication.

There are several instances of *Socrates'* instructions to *Alcibiades*:

<margin>* Cicero, *Tusculan Disputations*; Plato, *Symposium*; Plutarch.</margin>
* He told him that he was nothing of what a man ought to be, and that he had no advantage because of his high birth over an ordinary porter. At this, *Alcibiades* was greatly troubled and, with tears, begged him to instruct him in virtue and reform his vices.

<margin>* Aelian, book 3, chapter 28.</margin>
* Perceiving *Alcibiades* to be exceedingly proud of his riches and lands, *Socrates* showed him a map of the world and told him to find *Attica* <note>The region surrounding Athens</note> on it. When that was done, he asked him to show his own lands. *Alcibiades* answered that they were not there. "Do you boast," replied *Socrates*, "of that which you see is no significant part of the Earth?"

<margin>* Aelian, book 2, chapter 1.</margin>
* Because of his youth, *Alcibiades* was bashful and fearful of making a speech to the people. *Socrates* encouraged him by asking, "Do you not consider that shoemaker (naming him) an insignificant fellow?" *Alcibiades* agreed. "And likewise," continued *Socrates*, "that crier and that tent-maker?" When *Alcibiades* granted this, *Socrates* said, "Does the Athenian Commonwealth not consist of these men? If you despise them individually, do not fear them in an assembly." To these, add:

* The youngest son was *Ctesippus*.

# CHAPTER XVII.

## *His writings.*

Those who affirm that *Socrates* wrote nothing (such as *Cicero, Plutarch, Dio Chrysostom, Aristides, Origen,* and others) mean in respect to his philosophy; in that field, he never wrote anything himself, but what he discussed was committed to writing by *Xenophon, Plato*, and others of his scholars. Hence, the works of *Plato* (particularly *Phaedo*) were circulated under the name of *Socrates* and are so cited by *Aristotle*. However, that some things were written by *Socrates* himself is evident from those who affirm the following:

<margin>Laertius.</margin>
\* He wrote together with *Euripides* and aided him in composing tragedies, which led *Mnesilochus* <note>A comic poet of Athens.</note> to write:

> *The Phrygians is Euripides' new play,*
> *But Socrates gave it the best array.*

->**And again: Euripides is steered by Socrates and Callias.**<-

> *Now thou with pride and self-conceit overflowest,*
> *But all the cause to Socrates thou owest.*

<margin>Tusculan Disputations, Book 4.</margin>
To this we may refer the account of \* *Cicero*, who says that when *Euripides* wrote his play *Orestes*, *Socrates* revised the first three verses. He also wrote
*Some Fables of Aesop in verse, not very elegant, mentioned by Plato, Plutarch, and Laertius, beginning thus:*

# Socrates's Epistles.

## Epistle I.

<margin>* Allatius says otherwise.</margin>
<margin>1 <note>original: "τὸ δ' ὄντι τι, κ' μὴ"</note> In this sense, the words are acceptable (unlike the interpretation by Allatius: "and the power of hearing given to all even if they are unwilling"). But I believe the words of Socrates were <note>original: "τὸν τιμᾷν μὴ"</note>, meaning "not to honor him."</margin>
<margin>* Allatius says otherwise.</margin>
<margin>* Instead of <note>original: "αἰσχῦον"</note> (shameful), the reading is <note>original: "ἄμεινον"</note> (better).</margin>
You seem unacquainted with my resolutions; otherwise, you would not have sent a second time and increased your offers. You believe *Socrates*, as well as the Sophists <term>teachers who took money for their lessons</term>, to be mercenary with his advice, * and that what I wrote before was not sincere, but only intended to extract larger rewards from you. Therefore, you now promise wonders, confident that you can oblige me with your many presents to abandon my interests and connections with the Athenians and come over to you. I think it most unbecoming for a philosopher to sell his advice, and extremely contrary to my practices. For ever since I first entered into philosophy by God's command, I have never been known to take anything. Instead, I keep my exercises in public, * for everyone to hear who wishes to. I neither lock the door when I teach, as is reported of *Pythagoras*, nor do I go out to the crowds and demand money from my listeners, as some have done in the past and some still do in our times. I have enough from within myself; should I accept more from others, I would not know where to deposit it, nor whom to trust more than the givers themselves. If I suspect their loyalty, I shall be thought unwise to trust them; if they are honest, I can receive from them even if I store nothing with them. For those who would be faithful keepers of money will not be unfaithful preservers of their own gratitude; they will never attempt to defraud me of what they would have given. Rather, having received from me for free that for which others take money, they will * provide for me when I am in need. In a word, if they are friends, they will—*like you—share what they have with us; if they are not friends, they will seek to deprive us of what is ours.

<margin>* This interpretation seems to be confirmed by the particle <note>original: "καὶ"</note> before "to them."</margin>
<margin>* Allatius says otherwise.</margin>
<margin>* reading <note>original: "πρόδημα"</note>.</margin>
Besides, I do not have the leisure to hoard money, and I marvel at those who say they acquire riches * for their own sake and think highly of themselves because of their wealth. They neglect learning to devote themselves to profit, and thus they become admired for their riches but ridiculed for their ignorance—esteemed for everything except themselves. * But if we so much abhor having to turn to friends * or depending on others for our food, why is it that we are not ashamed to suffer the same dependence * on money? Do we not know that these men are respected only for their wealth, and if fortune turns, they live in total disrespect? They are not fully satisfied even when they are esteemed, because it is not for their own merit; but when they fall into disrepute, they are much

...more discontented, being themselves the cause of their own dishonor.

<margin>
* "But they are responsible for the one, but for being deprived of justice himself?" <note>original: "ἀλλὰ τῶν μὲν ἑτέρων ἔχουσι τὴν αἰτίαν, τοῦ δὲ δίκῃ αὐτὸς στερεῖσθαι"</note>; by which we may have some light to find perhaps the true reading, "but the fact of not being blamed by them" <note>original: "ἀλλὰ τὸ μὴ ὑπ’ αὐτῶν τὴν αἰτίαν"</note>.

* Perhaps "it is wonderful" <note>original: "ἔστι θαυμαστόν"</note>.

First, therefore, you were mistaken if you imagined *Socrates* would do for money what he would not do without it, not knowing that many circumstances—but chiefly the needs of my country—detain me. Do not wonder that I say I am performing my country’s business even though I am not employed in either the army or the court. Everyone ought to apply themselves to what they are capable of; * things above their reach they must leave to others, and perform those that are within their power. In cities such as this, not only counselors or commanders for sea or land are necessary, but also some who may * admonish others in their duties. For it is * nothing strange <term>unusual</term> that they fall, as it were, asleep under the weight of their responsibilities and need a goad <term>a spiked stick used for driving cattle; here, a metaphor for a stinging reminder</term> to waken them. God has placed me over these people, for which I have become—and not without cause—hated by them.

But he in whom I most confide <note>Socrates refers to his *Daimonion*, or inner divine voice</note> will not allow me to go. He knows better than I do what is good for me. When I resolved to come to you, he held me back; and when you sent for me a second time, he forbade me. I dare not disobey him. *Pindar* taught this wisdom, saying: *When God points out the beginning of any work, it is the direct way to obtain virtue, and the end is glorious.* The verses are very much to this purpose. Other poets have said as much regarding the gods: that what is undertaken with their advice succeeds well, but what is done without God is unprofitable to those who attempt it. The wisest cities of *Greece* consult the Oracle of *Delphi*, and as many as follow it have good success and do not commonly suffer harm.

Yet I do not wonder if you have no faith in what I say concerning the Daemon <term>the divine inner voice or spirit Socrates claimed guided him</term>, for I have met many people who were just as skeptical. Most of those who were in the Delian fight <note>The Battle of Delium (424 BCE), where the Athenians were defeated by the Boeotians</note> did not believe me. I was then under arms and went out from the city with the people to skirmish. Many of us were scattered in flight, and as we came to a certain path, the accustomed sign came upon me. I stopped and said, "In my opinion, friends, we should not go this way, for I heard the Daemon’s voice." The majority were angry, as if I were joking at such a serious time. A few were persuaded to go another way with me and arrived safely home. One person who came from the others brought word that they were all killed; some cavalrymen returning from the pursuit had fallen upon them. They resisted at first, but being eventually surrounded by those who were greater in number, they retreated and were finally overwhelmed and killed. He who brought this news was dangerously wounded and escaped only with the help of his shield. I have also, by instructions from God, foretold many events to particular persons.

You offer part of your kingdom and invite me to it, not

<summary>Socrates explains that his refusal to leave Athens is rooted in his civic duty to act as a moral provoker and his obedience to a divine inner voice. He defends the reliability of this spiritual guidance by recounting how it saved those who listened to him during the retreat from the Battle of Delium.</summary>

<keywords>Socrates, Daemon, Battle of Delium, Pindar, Oracle of Delphi, gadfly, divine sign, Leo Allatius</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the first epistle of Socrates, where he refuses an invitation to a royal court, arguing that ruling requires skills he does not possess and that he is guided by a divine voice to remain in Athens.</meta>

<margin>
<unclear>
Speculative
as well
outside
as within...
</unclear>
</margin>
...as to a changed government, but to rule both your subjects and yourself. But I confess, I have not learned how to command, and I would no more undertake to rule without knowing how than I would play at dice without having ever been taught. Undoubtedly, if other men were of the same mind, there would be fewer troubles in life; whereas now, the confidence of those who are ignorant—undertaking things they do not understand—causes these many disturbances. Because of this, they make Fortune appear greater than she is and, by their own folly, increase her power. 

Besides, I am not unaware that a King ought to be more honored and admired than a private person. However, just as I would not undertake to be a horseman if I had no skill in horsemanship, but would much rather be a footman even though the position is less honorable, I hold the same opinion regarding kings and private individuals. I will not, driven by ambition, desire more "glorious" afflictions. Those who invented the fable of *Bellerophon* <note>In Greek mythology, Bellerophon attempted to fly to Mount Olympus on the winged horse Pegasus; he was thrown down by Zeus and spent the rest of his life wandering in misery.</note> seemed to imply something to this purpose; for he was overwhelmed with misfortunes not because he sought to rise higher in rank, but because he aimed at things above his reach. Being thrown down from his hopes, he led the rest of his life poorly and shamefully, driven by mockery out of cities into the wilderness and avoiding common paths—not just what we commonly call paths, but that freedom with which everyone orders their own life. 

But let the poets interpret this as they please; you now hear my resolution again: I will not change this place for that one, *conceiving this to be the fittest for me*. Nor is God willing that I should, for He has been, until now, my counselor and guide.

* "seeming better" <note>original: "ἀμείνων δοκῶν"</note>
Others, "seeming invincible" <note>original: "ἀνείκτον δοκῶν"</note>.
Perhaps "seeming so to men" <note>original: "ἀνθρώποις δοκῶν"</note>.
Or "seeming to persist" <note>original: "ἐμμενεῖν δοκῶν"</note>.
</margin>

->Letter II<-

You are not ignorant of the great esteem we have for *Chærephon* <note>A devoted friend and follower of Socrates</note>, who, having been chosen as an Ambassador by the City to the Peloponnesians, will perhaps come to you. A philosopher is entertained with little trouble, but the journey is dangerous, especially because of the riots that are there at this time. If you protect him from these, you will preserve our friend and put us deeply in your debt.

->Letter III<-

Aneso of *Amphipolis* was recommended to me at *Potidæa*. He is now coming to *Athens*, having been thrown out of his house by the people; for at present, affairs there are much embroiled and clouded. However, I believe that within a little while they will clear up. By assisting him, you will oblige a deserving person and benefit both cities: *Amphipolis*, lest by rebelling it incurs irremediable danger, and ours, lest we be involved in their troubles.

<summary>Socrates explains his refusal to rule by citing his lack of training and the cautionary tale of Bellerophon, concluding with two short letters recommending his friends Chærephon and Aneso for protection and assistance.</summary>
<keywords>Socrates, Bellerophon, Chærephon, Amphipolis, Potidæa, Athens, Peloponnesians, government, philosophy, ambition, divine guidance</keywords>

<meta>This page continues from the previous letter where Socrates discussed the dangers of overreaching and the wisdom of staying within one's own abilities. The opening fragment concludes a previous thought, likely referring to the military hardships at Potidaea.</meta>

...as at this present time we are reduced almost to extremity regarding *Potidaea*. <note>Potidaea was a city-state whose siege (432–430 BCE) was a catalyst for the Peloponnesian War; Socrates served there as a hoplite.</note>

### Epistle IV

Meeting with *Critobulus*, I persuaded him to study philosophy, but I think he is of another mind and more devoted to affairs of State. In this, he intends to choose the best method and the best instructor among the many excellent visitors now in *Athens*, and we are intimate with many of them. This is the situation regarding him; as for us, *Xanthippe* and the children are well, and I continue as I did when you were with me.

### Epistle V

<margin>Read: *Concerning Proxenus and the voyage across the sea to Asia.* <note>original: "Περιξένου διὰ θάλασσαν τὴν εἰς τὴν Ἀσίαν"</note></margin>
We hear you are at *Thebes*, and that *Proxenus* has gone into *Asia* to take part with *Cyrus*. <note>Proxenus of Boeotia invited his friend Xenophon to join the expedition of Cyrus the Younger against Artaxerxes II.</note> Whether your designs will prosper, God knows; they are condemned by many here, for it is considered improper that the *Athenians* should assist *Cyrus*—the man through whose means they were deprived of their command by the *Lacedaemonians*—and fight for him who once fought against them. <note>Cyrus the Younger had provided the financial support that allowed the Spartans (Lacedaemonians) to defeat Athens in the Peloponnesian War.</note> It is not therefore strange if, the state of affairs being altered, some are ready to accuse you of being a <term>temporizer</term> <note>One who changes their political allegiance to suit the current circumstances.</note>; and the better your success is, the greater will be their slanders, for I am well acquainted with the dispositions of some of them. But since we have undertaken this, let us prove ourselves honest men and call to mind what we usually say of virtue, accounting this one of the best sentences of the Poet: "Our father's house must not be discredited." Know therefore, that for war, these two things are requisite: courage and generosity. For the latter, we are loved by our friends; for the former, we are feared by our enemies. In both, you have domestic precedents.

### Epistle VI

<margin>Perhaps *Lysias*.</margin>
I have taken such care of your foreign guests as you desired and have retained a friend of ours to plead their cause before the people. He professed himself the readier to undertake it out of his desire to serve you.

As for what you write in jest concerning wealth and those who are anxious for it, perhaps it is not unreasonable. First, because while others study how to be rich, I choose to live simply. Then, though I might receive many gifts and legacies from living and dead friends, I freely refuse them. For a man thus inclined to be judged "mad" by others is nothing strange. But we must examine not only this, but the rest of our...

<summary>This page contains three letters attributed to Socrates: one regarding Critobulus's political ambitions, one advising a friend (likely Xenophon) on the political optics of joining Cyrus's expedition, and a third regarding Socrates's rejection of wealth in favor of a simple life.</summary>
<keywords>Socrates, Critobulus, Potidaea, Xanthippe, Proxenus, Cyrus the Younger, Athenians, Lacedaemonians, Lysias, philosophy vs politics, voluntary poverty, ancestral honor</keywords>

--- Page 239 ---
<meta>This page continues Socrates' discourse on the nature of a virtuous life versus a life devoted to wealth and social standing. It follows the themes of self-discipline and the pursuit of wisdom established in the previous epistles.</meta>

->SOCRATES.<-

<page-num>63</page-num>

<margin>
* For *bodies* <note>original: "σωμάτων"</note> read *wealth* <note>original: "χρημάτων"</note>.
</margin>
...our lives. Since we disagree on how to use them, it is no wonder that we differ on how to acquire **wealth**. My diet is very sparse, and my clothing is the same in winter as in summer. I never wear shoes, and I am not captivated by popular applause, but rather by the study of wisdom and integrity. However, those who lack self-control—who are excessive in their eating and put on new clothes every day rather than every year—are carried away by unlawful pleasures. Just as those who lose their natural complexion resort to makeup, these people, having lost the true glory of virtue which everyone should possess, fly toward a reputation that depends on pleasing others. They court common applause with gifts and feasts. I suppose this is why they need so much wealth; they cannot live on a small amount themselves, and others will not admit them into their company unless they are paid to praise them.

<margin>
* Read: *that he who follows... is most blessed,* etc. <note>original: "μακαριώτατος εἶναι τὸν τῷ παρχρω, &c."</note>
</margin>
But my life is successful in both these respects. I will not deny that I may fail in some things, and I know that the wisest men prefer these virtues, while most men prefer wealth. Sometimes, when I reflect upon God, I find that He surpasses us because He needs nothing. It is the nature of a most excellent being to want for nothing and to possess within Himself everything He enjoys. Thus, the person who imitates the Wisest is wiser than others, and the person who resembles the **most happy** is happier. If riches could achieve this, then riches would be preferable; but since only virtue can obtain it, it would be folly to abandon real good to pursue what only appears to be good. Therefore, I cannot easily be persuaded that my condition is not better than theirs.

<margin>
* Perhaps *to those things which are now truly desirable goods, etc. until it is necessary now even for those who have surrounded themselves with truly human goods...* <note>original: "πρὸς οἷς ἤδη γε τῶν ὄντως ἀσπασμάτων ἀγαθῶν, κ.τ.λ. ἐς ὃ χρὴ ἤδη καὶ τῶν ὄντως ἀνθρωπείων ἀγαθῶν περιεστηκότας"</note> Certainly Allatius cannot prove that *to those which* <note>original: "τοῖς οἷς"</note> is used for *at whose house* or *because of which* <note>original: "πάρ’ ᾧ δι’ ὃ"</note>. * Others read *and, not words only*, etc. <note>original: "καὶ, ἐ λόγους μόνον"</note>, which I chose, reading immediately after *for someone to guard and teach* <note>original: "φυλάσσειν διδάσκειν τις"</note>.
</margin>
Regarding children, whom you say ought to be provided for: everyone can see the care I take for them. I know of only one foundation for happiness: wisdom. The fool who places his trust in gold does not truly possess what he has; moreover, he is so much more miserable than others because those who are oppressed by poverty may eventually become wise. But the fool, because of his false opinion of his own happiness, neglects true gain and is corrupted by plenty. * Besides the fact that he has never yet obtained man's essential good, he is deprived of any hope of it for the future. Nor is it possible for such a man to progress securely toward virtue when he is entangled in the flatteries of those who are masters of all manipulative arts, and caught in the charms of pleasures that glide into the soul through every sense, driving out all sound and wise judgment. How, then, can he do anything but give his children reasons for folly rather than instruction? * He expresses not only in words but in actions that he has placed his hope in these material things. If his children do not turn out well, their means of survival fail, and they die miserably for lack of food—justly punished for their idleness. Parents are required by law to raise their children until they are men. But you, perhaps some citizen may say to his

<meta>catchword: sons</meta>
->Ggg<-

<summary>Socrates argues that true happiness and wisdom come from self-discipline and imitating the divine nature of needing nothing, rather than from the pursuit of wealth and public flattery. He further asserts that teaching children to rely on riches leads to their moral and physical ruin, whereas wisdom is the only secure inheritance.</summary>
<keywords>Socrates, virtue, wisdom, wealth, temperance, education, Leo Allatius, divinity, happiness</keywords>

--- Page 240 ---
<meta>This page continues Socrates' discourse on the value of virtue over material wealth, specifically addressing how he intends to provide for his children after his death through the legacy of his friendships rather than through money.</meta>

-># SOCRATES.<-

<page-num>64</page-num>

Sons greedy to inherit, do not spare me while I am dying; and while you live, you rely on me for maintenance, yet even after I am dead, you are not ashamed to lead a life more lazy than death itself. You expect that my fortune should support others even after my decease, yet your own resources are not sufficient for yourselves while you are still alive. He will perhaps use such harsh speeches to his children, taking the liberty of both a father and a patriot. My wealth, in the estimation of other men, is meager; but in its effect, it is not at all inferior to that of the rich. I will not leave my children money, but a more honorable heritage: wise friends. As long as they keep these friends, they cannot lack necessities; and if they treat them poorly, they would undoubtedly use their money even worse.

<margin>
Perhaps *those coming back to life* <note>original: "ἀναβιωσκόντες"</note>. This is how Allatius <note>Leo Allatius (1586–1669), a scholar and critic who edited ancient texts</note> seems to read it.
* Reading *more* <note>original: "πλειών"</note> instead of *fuller* <note>original: "πλείω"</note>.
</margin>

But if I seem to give bad advice to you, who know how negligent friends can be, I answer that not all men feel the same toward their friends. Some take care of them even after they are dead, and it is likely that our friends are not of a neglectful nature, but are pleased with the past benefits they received from us, just as much as with a current, brief favor. A small favor brings a small return; lasting benefits produce a reward equal to their value. I foresee that what is mine will hereafter appear more graceful to my friends, and therefore I demand no rewards from them. I consider nothing of equal value to be exchanged for philosophy except friendship. Unlike the Sophists, I have no lack of confidence in the things that are mine; for as they grow old, they renew themselves, and in their decaying age they * flourish. This makes them more acceptable to the disciples, and their "father" more esteemed. * While living, he obtains honor; when dead, he is considered worthy of memory. And if he leaves a kinsman behind, they will respect him like his own nephews and brothers, showing him every kindness, as they are allied to him by more than just a natural kinship. Even if they wanted to, they cannot neglect him in times of misfortune, any more than we can ignore those who are near to us by blood. For a kinship of the soul forces them to help the son of the deceased as if he were their own brother when they remember his father, whose dishonor they would consider their own.

Now, judge if I manage my affairs poorly or take no care for my children, so that when I die they will be destitute of necessities. I leave them not wealth, but such guardians as will care for them. As for wealth, no history mentions any man who has been made better by riches. A proven friend is to be preferred over pure gold in this regard: gold is not beneficial to everyone who desires it, but a friend is beneficial to those he loves most. Nor does a friend only supply the necessities of life; he is also of service to the soul of the one who has him and is most helpful in achieving virtue, without which nothing is profitable. But we will consider these things more closely when we meet; let this serve as a brief answer to your request.

<meta>The catchword "Epist." indicates the beginning of the next section, likely a new Epistle.</meta>

<summary>Socrates argues that leaving his children a network of virtuous and loyal friends is a far greater inheritance than material wealth, which does not improve a person’s character. He emphasizes that kinship of the soul is more enduring and reliable than blood relations or money.</summary>

### Letter VII.

I am not surprised by what you write: that you suspect the Thirty <note>The "Thirty Tyrants," a pro-Spartan oligarchy installed in Athens after the Peloponnesian War</note> still feel the same way toward us as they did before you left. As soon as you were gone, they began to be jealous of me, and a rumor spread among them that these things were not done without Socrates' involvement. Within a few days, they summoned me to the Court, where several charges were brought against me. After I defended myself, they ordered me to go to the Piraeus <note>The port of Athens</note> to arrest Leon. Their intention was to execute him so they could seize his estate and make me an accomplice in their crimes. When I refused, saying I would never willingly agree to an unjust act, Charicles <note>One of the leaders of the Thirty Tyrants</note> was present and was deeply annoyed. "Socrates," he said, "do you think you can speak so boldly without suffering ten thousand hardships?" "Charicles," I replied, "none of those hardships are as heinous as committing an injustice." He did not say a word, nor did any of the others, but ever since then they have liked me even less.

Regarding you, some people who were present reported that your affairs are going as you hoped—that the Thebans welcomed you kindly during your exile and will do everything they can to help you return. Some were troubled by this news, especially because it diminished their hopes of receiving aid from Sparta <note>Lacedaemon</note>. For those who arrived with the ambassadors claimed that the Spartans were tied up in a major war, and the Ephors <term>Ephors: senior Spartan magistrates</term>, hearing of these troubles, were dissatisfied. They said that the Spartans had not entrusted the city to the Thirty only to see it destroyed;

<margin>
* Perhaps, they say they have fared blindly <note>original: "φασί γε χρησθάμεν, πεπονθέναι ὡς ἀβλεπτο[ς?]" - "they say they have fared (literally: experienced) blindly or unexpectedly"</note>.
</margin>

for if they had wanted to destroy it, it would have been very easy for those in command to do so, especially since they were urged on by the Corinthians and Thebans, and they argued that the city might be better governed under an oligarchy than a democracy. If all this is true and your affairs are going as reported, it is very likely that if you return with the Thebans—while the Spartans provide no help to the current rulers—everything here can be easily settled. Furthermore, many of the locals who are currently silent out of fear would quickly abandon this party if they saw the slightest sign that you were resolute. This is because, under this current government, nothing remains intact for them; everything has been thrown into chaos by frequent and constant atrocities. Most people have rebelled just as you have; the rest would endure the same fate as you if they had the slightest encouragement from abroad. Thus, even if there were no other proof, this example would show that the

<summary>Socrates describes his confrontation with the Thirty Tyrants, specifically his refusal to participate in the arrest of Leon, and discusses the political instability in Athens and the potential for a successful return of exiled citizens with Theban support.</summary>
<keywords>Socrates, Thirty Tyrants, Charicles, Leon, Athens, Piraeus, Thebes, Sparta, Lacedaemon, Ephors, Oligarchy, Democracy</keywords>

--- Page 242 ---
<meta>This page concludes Letter VII, where Socrates warns his exiled correspondent about the self-destructive nature of the Thirty Tyrants' rule and expresses a final hope for liberation from their oppressive regime.</meta>

<page-num>66</page-num>
<header>->SOCRATES.<-</header>

The greatest misfortune of cities is the wickedness of their rulers. They are so blinded by self-interest that they will not stop, even though they see everything falling into ruin. Instead, they think they can settle affairs using the same methods that first caused the trouble, continuing their practices of banishment, sequestration <term>sequestration: the confiscation or seizure of property by the state</term>, and unjust executions. They do not consider that a man is a poor doctor if he prescribes the very cause of a disease as its remedy.

But these men are incurable. You would do well to take care of yourself, for all those who remain here have only this hope left: that if you act wisely, we may be freed from this oppressive and painful tyranny.

# THE CLOUDS
## of Aristophanes.

->*Added (not as a comic diversion for the reader, who can expect little of that kind from a subject so ancient and specific, but) as a necessary supplement to the life of Socrates.*<-

---

### Act I. Scene I.

->Strepsiades, Phidippides, Servant.<-

*Strepsiades.* Oh, oh!
Great Jove <note>Jupiter</note>, how long this night is—how endless!
Will it never be day? I heard the rooster crow again,
Yet my servants are still snoring. It was not always like this;
<margin>The Athenians, during the war with the Spartans <note>Lacedaemonians</note>, passed a decree that no one should beat their servants, for fear they might defect to the enemy. —Scholar</margin>
Curse this war that restrains me,
And will not let me beat these rascals.
My "good" son sleeps too, wrapped up over his head and ears.
Well, let me try to join them in sleep.
Alas, I cannot; I am so anxious and tortured
By expenses, bills for horse fodder, and interest.
All for this "hopeful" son, who, with his curled locks,
Enters races, drives his carriage, and dreams of horses,
While I (unfortunate man!) see the unwelcome moon
Bring on the payment day <note>Quarter day: a day when debts and rents were traditionally due</note> and the threat of interest <note>use-money</note>.
Boy! Trim the lamp and bring my account book here,
So that I may total my debts and interest.
Let's see: twelve pounds to Pasias. Ha! Twelve pounds
<margin>Horses were named based on the marks they were branded with: if they had a 'K' (Koppa), they were called *Koppatia*; if they had an 'S' (Sigma), they were called *Samphoras*. —Scholar</margin>
To Pasias? For what? To buy a branded horse <note>original: 'Coppatia'</note>.
I wish I had lost an eye instead of paying that!
*Phidippides (talking in his sleep).* Hold, Philo!
You are off the track; start again.
*Strepsiades.* There it is!
This is the misery that ruins me;
Even his sleep is consumed by horses.
*Phidippides.* How many laps will the <term>manage</term> <note>manage: a training exercise or track for horses</note> hold?
*Strepsiades.* You lead your father through many a weary "lap"!
But how much more do I owe to Pasias besides this?

"Three pounds to Amynias for chariot wheels."
*Phidippides (in his sleep).* "Go, servant, take that horse and turn him out."
*Strepsiades.* Yes, you have turned me out of all my money!
Legal costs will consume me, and my creditors
Threaten to sue me for an execution <note>a court-ordered seizure of property to pay a debt</note>.
*Phidippides.* Why do you stay awake all night and toss so much, Father?
*Strepsiades.* I cannot sleep; the money lender <term>Scrivener: a clerk or notary who also acted as a money lender</term> bites me so.
*Phidippides.* Yet let me rest a little longer.
*Strepsiades.* Go ahead then.
All these debts will one day fall upon your head.
Cursed be the hour when I first saw your mother!
I lived most sweetly in the country before,
Well-stocked with sheep and bees, olives and grapes,
Until I took this niece of Megacles
From the house of the Alcmaeonids <note>The "Megaclean house" refers to the wealthy and powerful Alcmaeonid family</note> in the city—
A woman well-fashioned, high-born, and richly dressed.
We married, as I said, and lay together:
I smelling strongly of wine-dregs and greasy wool,
But she of perfumes, saffron-perfumed <note>Crocus</note> wanton kisses,
Of vain expense, dainties, and luxury.
I will not say she was idle,
And yet I often told her, while showing her this threadbare coat,
<margin>original: σπαθᾷν, λίαν τρυφᾷν καὶ σπαταλᾷν. <note>to waste money, to live excessively luxuriously and extravagantly —Scholia Manuscript</note></margin>
"Woman, you 'spin' a very expensive thread." <note>A pun on a Greek word that means both to weave and to waste money</note>
*Servant.* Sir, all the oil in the lamp is gone.
*Strepsiades.* What?
Why did you put in such a "drunken" wick?
If you were near me, I would beat you.
*Servant.* Why, Sir?
*Strepsiades.* Because the wick is thicker than the oil.
Well, at last my good wife and I had this son;
We disagreed about what to name him.
She wanted a name related to horses:
*Callippides, Xanthippus, or Charippus*;
I wanted to name him after his grandfather, *Phidonides*.
We argued for a long time, but at last agreed
He should be called *Phidippides*. She would take this son,
And stroking him kindly, she would instruct him:
> "When you are grown a man, go to the city,
> Follow the fashion, and keep a coach and horses,
> Like your Uncle Megacles." "No," said I,
<margin>A stony, craggy place in Attica where goats thrive best. —Manuscript</margin>
> "Go in a simple coat and drive your goats
> Into *Phelleus*, as your father does."
But my advice had little effect on him,
For now he wastes my means by keeping horses.
I have been thinking all night how
To remedy this, and now I have found the way;
If I could only persuade him to follow it, I would be happy.

Streps. Phidippides, Phidippides,
Phid. What is your will, Sir?
Streps. Kiss me, give me your hand.
Phid. Here, Sir.
Streps. Do you love me?
Phid. By Neptune, God of Horses.
Streps. Do not name
That God, for it is from him that all my sorrow springs:
But if you love me truly and heartily,
O son, be ruled.
Phid. In what should I be ruled?
Streps. Change your course of life without more delay,
And do as I would have you.
Phid. What is that?
Streps. But will you do it?
Phid. Yes, by Bacchus, I will.
Streps. Come here then; do you see that little door?
<margin>Wherein it is shown that he lies there contemplating the gods and great matters. <note>original: "Ἐν ᾧ δηλοῦται κείμενος φροντίζει περὶ θεῶν καὶ μεγάλων πραγμάτων."</note> Scholia Manuscript.</margin>
That is the *Phrontisterium* <term>The "Thinking-shop" or school of Socrates</term> of wise souls,
Of learned men who tell us Heaven is an oven,
And we are the coals enclosed in the wide arch;
If we give them but a little money,
They will teach us how to win all causes, right or wrong.
Phid. Who can these be?
Streps. I do not know their names: they are good
Men, and busy in continual study.
Phid. Oh, now I know the wretches that you mean:
The meager, pale, proud, barefoot, begging fellows,
Whose evil geniuses are Socrates
And Chaerephon.
Streps. Peace! Speak no more so foolishly;
If you will obey a father, let me see you
Give up your horses and become one of these.
Phid. Not I, by Bacchus! No, even though you should tempt me
<margin>An excellent horse-breeder. <note>original: "ἄριστος ἱπποτρόφος."</note> Scholia Manuscript.</margin>
With all of *Leogoras's* breed of racers.
Streps. Dear son, be ruled and learn.
Phid. What should I learn?
Streps. It is said they have two tongues <note>The "Just" and "Unjust" arguments, often personified in the play</note>, and one of them
Is able to prove any injustice to be reasonable;
If you could but learn that language, we would be made,
And might argue our stubborn creditors
Out of the debts I have incurred for you;
They would get nothing then but words.
Phid. I cannot do it; if I were so lean and pale,
I would not dare to look a jockey in the face.
Streps. By Ceres then, you shall stay with me no longer—
Neither you, nor your coach-horse, nor your Samphoras <term>A horse marked with the letter Sigma (Σ)</term>.

But leave my house at once, all of you. My Uncle *Megacles* will not let either me or my horses go in want; so long as that is true, I do not care. *Exit.*

### Scene 2.

->*Strepsiades, Scholar.*<-

*Streps.* Though I have failed, I will not give up yet. I will say my prayers and go to the school myself to learn this art. But how can I—at my age, dull and forgetful—master such subtle ideas? Yet I will press on; why should I hesitate? Hey there, friend!
*Schol.* A plague on you! Who is that knocking at the door?
*Streps.* *Strepsiades*, son of *Phædo* of *Cecinna*.
*Schol.* It was rudely done to knock so hard; you have made my laboring brain miscarry a thought.
*Streps.* Forgive me, I was raised far off in the country. But please, what was the thought that proved abortive?
*Schol.* It is only lawful to reveal that to fellow scholars.
*Streps.* Then you may tell me, for I have come here to become one of you.
*Schol.* I will; and you must value it as a mystery. The other day, *Socrates* asked *Chærephon* how many of its own feet a flea could leap; for one had by chance bitten *Chærephon’s* eyebrow and leapt from there onto the head of *Socrates*.
*Streps.* How could he measure this?
*Schol.* Most skillfully. He dipped both of the flea's feet in melting wax, which immediately hardened into shoes. He then plucked these off and used them to measure the distance exactly.
*Streps.* Great *Jupiter*, how clever these minds are!
*Schol.* If you should hear their other speculations, you would certainly say so.
*Streps.* Please, what was that?
*Schol.* *Chærephon* the *Sphettian* <note>A resident of the Attic deme of Sphettus</note> once asked him whether a gnat buzzed from its mouth or its tail.
*Streps.* And what did he say?
He said it has a straight, thin gut with a bladder at the end of it; when air is forced into the bladder, it makes a sound as it breaks out.
*Streps.* Then I see that a gnat's tail is a trumpet! How blessed is this anatomist of gnats! Surely he can hide himself from blind justice, since he knows these dark, internal pathways so well.

# SOCRATES.

Why should we praise *Thales* <note>A pre-Socratic philosopher often cited as the first Greek philosopher</note> any longer?
Come, open your Thinking-shop <note>Phrontisterium: A place for meditation or a 'thinking-shop'</note> for me,
And let me see this *Socrates* quickly.
<margin>* The school is revealed; the scholars are in various positions; *Socrates* is hanging in a basket.</margin>
I long to learn; open the door— Oh, Hercules!
What strange beasts do we have here!
*Schol.* Why are you surprised?
Who do you think they look like?
*Streps.* Like the poor
<margin>* Suidas <note>Referring to the Suda, a 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia that likely annotated this passage</note></margin>
Spartan <note>Lacedaemonian</note> captives taken at *Pylus* <note>A reference to the Battle of Pylos (425 BC) where many Spartans were captured</note>.
Why do they look so intently at the ground?
*Schol.* These men seek out things that belong to the Earth!
*Streps.* Oh, they are looking for leeks! Do not trouble yourselves any longer, friends,
For I know better where to find good, large ones.
*Schol.* Come, let us go inside.
*Streps.* Let us stay a while and talk with them.
*Schol.* No, no; they cannot endure being in the open air for long.
*Streps.* What is this, for heaven's sake, tell me?
*Schol.* This is Astronomy.
*Streps.* And this?
*Schol.* Geometry.
*Streps.* But what is it good for?
*Schol.* To measure land.
*Streps.* What, farmland or pasture?
*Schol.* No, the whole Earth.
*Streps.* A fine joke indeed!
That would be a massive help to farmers.
*Schol.* Here is the whole world, and this is *Athens*.
*Streps.* How?
I can hardly believe that. What has become of the judges?
Where are the *Cicynians*, my fellow countrymen?
*Schol.* Here is *Euboea*; see how far it is stretched out.
*Streps.* Yes, almost stretched to pieces between us
And *Pericles*; and where is *Sparta*?
*Schol.* Here.
*Streps.* It is too near us! Why, with all your skill,
Do you not help to push it further away?
*Schol.* It is not possible.
*Streps.* No? You will regret it then.
But what man is that hanging over there in the basket?
*Schol.* That is he.
*Streps.* He? What "he"?
*Schol.* *Socrates*.
*Streps.* How, *Socrates*?
Call him.
*Schol.* Call him yourself; I am not at leisure.

# SOCRATES.

### Scene 3.

### Strepsiades, Socrates.

*Streps.* Hey, Socrates!
<margin>* These are the words of Silenus (whom Socrates resembled in his ugliness) in Pindar's work. Scholiast <note>An ancient commentator on a classic text</note>.</margin>
*Socr.* Why do you call me, a mortal?
*Streps.* First, I would gladly know what you are doing up there?
*Socr.* I walk in the air and gaze upon the sun.
*Streps.* Why do you view the gods from a basket, and not from the ground?
*Socr.* I could not elevate my thoughts to contemplate these mysteries unless my intellect were suspended in this way, where my delicate thoughts melt into the air, which they resemble. If I stood upon the ground, I would find nothing, no matter how strictly I searched up and down; for the magnetic force of the Earth would draw the moisture from my brain, just as we see happens with watercress <term>nose-smart</term>.
*Streps.* How? How is that? Does the brain draw the moisture out of watercress? Come down, sweet *Socrates*, and quickly teach me the knowledge for which I came.
*Socr.* What did you come for?
*Streps.* To learn the art of speaking. I am being torn to pieces by debts and high interest, tossed up and down, and forced to pawn all my goods.
*Socr.* How did you run into such debt?
*Streps.* I have been eaten into this waste <note>referring to financial ruin</note> by horses; and I would learn from you your other language—the one that teaches men how to pay nothing. For that, by all the gods, I will give you whatever you ask!
*Socr.* "By all what gods?" We do not recognize the gods the city worships here.
*Streps.* How do you swear then? By copper coins, like the Byzantines?
*Socr.* Do you wish to be skilled in divine affairs?
*Streps.* By *Jove* <note>Jupiter/Zeus</note> (if any such being exists), I would!
*Socr.* Then you must become acquainted with the *Clouds*, our venerable goddesses.
*Streps.* With all my heart.
*Socr.* Then sit down upon this couch.
*Streps.* Very well.
*Socr.* Now take this garland.
*Streps.* Why a garland? Alas, *Socrates*!

<meta>This page continues the conversation between Socrates and Strepsiades as the Chorus of Clouds begins to reveal itself. Socrates is explaining the divine nature of the Clouds and their role in intellectual life.</meta>

<header>SOCRATES.</header>

<margin>
<note>original Greek: μυστοδόκος δόμος — the house that receives the initiated.</note> At Eleusis in Attica, the mysteries of Ceres <note>Demeter</note> were celebrated, to which only Athenians were admitted, not strangers; if anyone revealed them to an uninitiated person, they were both put to death. > "The far-renowned Cecropian <note>Athenian</note> plain,
> "Where shines the * Eleusinian Temple <note>Fane</note>,
> "Where the most secluded dwellings are,
> "Statues and temples of the gods;
> "Where altars blaze with incense, and where
> "The holy days last all the year;
> "Where the lively Graces every spring,
> "And youths with virgins, dance and sing."

*Streps.* Tell me, good *Socrates*, what things are these that speak so finely? Are they ladies?
*Socr.* No, they are *Clouds*, the deities of idle men. From them we derive our logic, discourse, and reason, our high whimsies <note>Capriccio's</note>, and elaborate fancies.
*Streps.* My soul, I thought, leaped while they were speaking, and now it wants to dispute subtly about smoke, sharply refuting one opinion with another. Oh, how I long to see them once more!
*Socr.* Look over there toward *Parnes*; see how gently they glide to the earth.
*Streps.* Where? Show me.
*Socr.* See, in shoals <note>crowds</note> they creep into the caverns of the mountain.
*Streps.* What are these things? I cannot see them yet.
*Socr.* Look there at the entrance.
*Streps.* I still barely see them.
*Socr.* Either you see them now, or you are blind.
*Streps.* By *Jove*, I do see them! Great Clouds, for you fill everything!
*Socr.* Did you not know these deities before?
*Streps.* Not I; I thought they were only mists and vapors.
*Socr.* Then you did not know those who support the Sophists.
*Streps.* If these are Clouds, how is it that they look like women? For clouds do not have such a shape.
*Socr.* No? What shape do they have then?
*Streps.* I don't know exactly; they look like flying fleeces, but by *Jove*, they look nothing like women—these have noses!

<margin>
* The Socratic method of arguing by asking questions.
</margin>
*Socr.* * Answer what I ask you.
*Streps.* Ask me quickly.
*Socr.* Did you ever see a cloud shaped like a Centaur, a leopard, a bull, or a wolf?
*Streps.* I have; what of it?
*Socr.* The Clouds can take whatever form they please. When they see a hairy man with curls like *Clitus*, they mock his madness by taking the shape of a Centaur.
*Streps.* And when they see someone who defrauds or plunders the state, like *Simon*, what do they do then?

<header>SOCRATES.</header>

*Socr.* They do resemble him and turn into ravenous wolves. This was the reason that yesterday, when they 
<margin>* A coward.
* Effeminately dressed.</margin> beheld *Cleonymus*, they fled like deer; and seeing *Clisthenes*, they have now turned into women.
*Streps.* Great **Queens**, if you ever design to speak to mortals, make me acquainted with your rumbling voice.
*Chorus.* "All hail, old man, who hunts for wisdom,
"And you, the priest of subtle trifles, say:
"What would you have with us? For to none but you
"Of all the heavenly Sophists would we stoop so low;
"Except for *Prodicus*, to him because he is grave and wise,
"And to you, because you walk upright, your eyes
"Rolling on every side, your look severe,
"And because you go barefoot and endure many miseries."
*Streps.* Good heavens, what voice is this? How strange and stately.
*Socr.* These are our **Goddesses**; the rest are mere toys.
*Streps.* Is Olympian **Jove** <note>Jupiter/Zeus</note> no **Deity** then?
*Socr.* What *Jove*? There is no such thing; it is mere fancy.
*Streps.* How? Whence then proceeds all rain?
<margin><note>original Greek: Τιὸσ ὐετὸσ — Zeus Hyetios, or Zeus the Rainer.</note> Jupiter was the particular Deity of rain; hence he was surnamed the **Rainer**.</margin> *Socr.* Only from these clouds. Did you ever see a shower without them? Take the **Clouds** away, and heaven must rain fair weather.
*Streps.* By *Phoebus*, you have cleared it well. Until now, I thought *Jove* made water through a sieve. But whence comes thunder? When I am sick, that frightens me. These thunder as they tumble up and down. How can that be?
<margin>Deriding Socrates as if he were ignorant of natural philosophy.</margin> *Socr.* When they are full of water and driven upon one another by their own weight, they roar and break.
*Streps.* But who is it that drives them? Is not that *Jove*?
*Socr.* No, an ethereal whirlwind.
*Streps.* A whirlwind? Hmm! I knew not that until now. But whence comes lightning then—that glittering fire which terrifies and burns us? *Jupiter* usually darts this down on perjured men.
*Socr.* And how (you phlegmatic, dull *Saturn* <note>meaning a slow, old-fashioned man</note>), if it is darted on the perjured, do *Sinon*, *Theorus*, and *Cleonymus* escape it? No, he strikes his own temple, or the **Sunian Promontory**, or sturdy oaks. Did the oak ever forswear itself?
*Streps.* I know not; that which you say seems like reason. But what then is lightning?

<meta>catchword: Socr.</meta>

<summary>Socrates dismisses the existence of Zeus, explaining to a skeptical Strepsiades that rain, thunder, and lightning are natural phenomena caused by the Clouds and "ethereal whirlwinds" rather than divine judgment. He mocks the idea that lightning strikes the perjured by pointing out that many known scoundrels are never hit, while innocent trees and temples are.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the conversation between Socrates and Strepsiades. Socrates is explaining his "scientific" theories on natural phenomena like thunder, while Strepsiades expresses his desire to learn the art of sophistry to escape his mounting debts.</meta>

<header>SOCRATES.</header>

*Socr.* When the winds are trapped tightly, they swell the clouds like bladders, and at last break out with violence and horrid noises; and by friction <note>original: "contrition," meaning the grinding or rubbing together of the clouds</note> kindle one another. But you, who seek wisdom among us, how happy you will be above all Grecians if you understand well, and remember, and can endure much, and will never be tired of standing or walking, nor have any feeling of the cold, nor care for dining, and can refrain from wine, from athletics, and all other trifles.

*Streps.* Oh, for a steadfast soul untiring despite cares, a frugal, self-denying one that can feast upon a dish of herbs—you could never find anyone better suited; I am a mere **anvil**.

*Socr.* Do you believe in no Gods but those we teach: the Chaos, the Clouds, and the Tongue? Only these three.

*Streps.* I will not even speak of any other, much less bestow an offering on their altars.

*Chor.* <gloss>c</gloss> Speak boldly then; say what is your request,
"For if you honor us, you shall be blessed."

*Streps.* Great Queens, I petition for a small matter: that I may out-talk all the Greeks by a hundred furlongs.

*Chor.* "To you alone this gift we will allow;
"None shall speak such powerful sentences as you."

*Streps.* I do not care for powerful sentences, but subtle ones to cheat my creditors.

*Chor.* "It is not much you ask, and you shall obtain it;
"Learn from our ministers and you shall gain it."

*Streps.* I shall, relying on your promises, forced by poverty, the *Koppatia* <note>Referring to the expensive breed of horses marked with the letter 'Koppa,' which caused his debts</note> and an unlucky marriage. Now let them use me as they please: beat me, starve me, burn, freeze, or flay me, as long as I escape my debts. I do not care if men call me shameless, smooth-tongued, audacious, petulant, abominable, a forger of words and lies, a contentious <term>barretor</term> <note>a person who habitually instigates groundless lawsuits</note>, or an old, winding, bragging, testy, crafty fox.

*Socr.* Spoken like a man of courage! If you learn from me, your fame shall spread as wide as the heavens.

*Streps.* What shall I do?

*Socr.* You shall spend all your time with me; a life the happiest in the world.

*Streps.* I long to see that day.

*Socr.* Your door shall always be crowded with clients who will come to you for counsel and to discuss cases worth the wealth of kingdoms, to your heart's desire.

*Chorus.* "**Test this old man; first see if he is fit;**

<margin>As the scholars of Socrates did, especially Xenophon and Plato.</margin>

*Streps.* **I would find witnesses to the act**

*Streps.* **I did not come here to steal anything.**

--- Page 254 ---
<meta>This page continues the scene where Socrates attempts to evaluate Strepsiades' intellect. Having found the old man's memory and character lacking in the previous page, Socrates now attempts to teach him the basics of formal education, such as poetic meter and grammar, with equally frustrating results.</meta>

<header>SOCRATES.</header>

# Act 2.

->*Socrates, Strepsiades.*<-

*Socr.* By Chaos and this air I breathe, I never met anyone as stupid as this fellow; he is so clownish and forgetful that he forgets simple things even faster than he learns them. I will call him out. What, ho! *Strepsiades*! Come out and bring your bed with you.
*Str.* The fleas will hardly let me bring myself.
*So.* Quick, put it down there, and pay attention to what I say to you.
*Str.* I am ready.
*So.* What do you most want to learn: poetic measures, verse, or rhythm?
*Str.* By all means, measures; for I was recently cheated of two pecks <note>A dry measure of about two gallons.</note> by a grain dealer.
*So.* That is not what I am asking. I want to know which you consider the finest measure: the *trimeter* or the *tetrameter*? <note>Poetic meters consisting of three or four metrical feet.</note>
*Str.* In my opinion, the finest measure is a bushel.
*So.* You are talking nonsense.
*Str.* What will you bet that your *tetrameter* does not hold a bushel?
*So.* Go away! How dull and blockheaded you are. But perhaps you will be more capable at *rhythm*.
*Str.* How can rhythms help me with my grain?
*So.* First, they will make you pleasant company. Then you shall know which rhythm suits an *anapest* and which suits *dactyls*.
*Str.* *Dactyls*? I certainly know what those are.
*So.* Well, what is a *dactyl*?
*Str.* What else but this finger here? It has been a *dactyl* <note>original: "Dactyle." A pun on the Greek word "daktylos," which means both a metrical foot and a finger.</note> ever since I was a child.
*So.* You are a useless dunce.
*Str.* I do not care for learning these technicalities.
*So.* What do you want, then?
*Str.* That—that unjust and cheating *sophistry*. <note>Deceptive reasoning used to win arguments, regardless of the truth.</note>
*So.* But there are things that must be learned before you come to that. For instance, which creatures are *masculine*?
*Str.* I surely know that, or I would be truly mad: a ram, a bull, a goat, a dog, and a pigeon.

<summary>Socrates attempts to teach Strepsiades the basics of poetic meter and grammar, but the old man consistently confuses intellectual concepts with physical objects and grain measurements.</summary>
<keywords>Socrates, Strepsiades, education, poetic meter, dactyl, tetrameter, sophistry, grain measures, comedy, Aristophanes</keywords>

--- Page 255 ---
<meta>This page continues the comedic dialogue between Socrates and Strepsiades. Having already failed to grasp the concepts of poetic meter and rhythm, Strepsiades is now being tutored by Socrates on the grammatical gender of nouns and names, leading to a series of linguistic puns and insults regarding cowardly Athenian citizens.</meta>

<header>SOCRATES.</header>
<sig>L l l</sig>

<margin>* Ridiculing Socrates as ignorant of grammar.</margin>
*Socr.* See how you err, calling both the male and female a pigeon.

*Str.* Right, by Neptune! How should I say it then?

*Socr.* Call this one a cock-pigeon, and that one a hen.

*Str.* A pigeon, cock and hen! Ha! By this air, for this single lesson, I will fill your *meal-trough <term>Cardopus: a large vessel used for kneading dough.</term> with meal.

<margin>* A meal-trough. In Greek, the word has a masculine ending but takes a feminine article.</margin>
*Socr.* Again you are wrong; you call it *Cardopus*, but it is *haec Cardopus* <note>original: "hæc Cardopus"; Socrates uses the Latin feminine demonstrative "haec" to point out that despite its masculine-looking ending, the Greek word for "kneading-trough" is grammatically feminine.</note>, and therefore henceforth call it *Cardopa*. Next, it is proper that you know which names are masculine and which are feminine.

*Str.* I certainly know which are feminine, I am sure of it.

*Socr.* Let us hear them.

*Str.* *Philina*, *Cletagora*, *Demetria*, and *Lysistba*.

*Socr.* And which are masculine?

*Str.* A great many: *Philoxenus*, *Milesias*, and *Amynias*.

*Socr.* You are mistaken.

*Str.* Are these not masculine in your view?

<margin>* Effeminate cowards.</margin>
*Socr.* By no means. If you saw *Amynias*, how would you address him?

*Str.* *Amynia*, ho! <note>By dropping the 's' at the end of the name, Strepsiades uses a feminine vocative ending to mock Amynias's lack of manliness.</note>

*Socr.* What, you would make a woman of him?

*Str.* And with good reason; he has thrown away his weapons and will not fight. But for what purpose am I learning these common trifles?

*Socr.* They are not so common. But come, lie down.

*Str.* What must I do?

*Socr.* Consider for yourself the business that concerns you.

*Str.* Not in this bed, I thank you! If I must lie down, I will meditate on the ground.

*Socr.* But there is no other room.

*Str.* Wretch that I am! How I shall be tormented by these fleas!

*Socr.* Now, think deeply into your affairs; try every turn and winding, every shift. If you get stuck on anything, give it up and move to another thought—but be sure you do not fall asleep.

*Str.* Oh! Oh!

*Socr.* What is the matter now?

*Str.* I am being killed by these bloodsuckers, these *Corinthians* <note>A pun on "koris," the Greek word for "flea," and the Corinthians, who were currently at war with Athens.</note>.

<summary>Socrates attempts to teach Strepsiades the proper grammatical genders of nouns and names, leading to jokes about effeminate men. The scene ends with Socrates forcing Strepsiades into a flea-ridden bed to meditate on his legal troubles.</summary>
<keywords>Socrates, Strepsiades, grammar, gender of nouns, Amynias, cowards, philosophical meditation, fleas, Corinthians pun</keywords>

--- Page 256 ---
<meta>This page continues the comedic interaction between Socrates and Strepsiades. Following their discussion of grammar and gendered nouns, Socrates is now attempting to force Strepsiades into a state of deep, philosophical meditation—interrupted by the physical reality of bedbugs—to find a clever way to escape his debts.</meta>

<header>SOCRATES.</header>

*Socr.* Do not torment yourself.

*Str.* How can I help it
when I have no money left, no color in my face,
hardly any life, no shoes, and have grown almost into a ghost
from staying awake?

*Socr.* Now, what are you thinking about? Nothing?

*Str.* Yes,
by Neptune.

*Socr.* What?

*Str.* I'm wondering if the fleas
will leave any piece of me left or not.

*Socr.* Curse you!

*Str.* You could have spared your curse; I'm dead already.

<margin>* Socrates argues in Plato's *Phaedrus* that external objects should not distract him, which Aristophanes mocks here.</margin>

*Socr.* Fie, fie! You must not be so sensitive. Cover
your face and study to find some subtle trick.

*Str.* I wish I could learn how to trick these wicked fleas.

*Socr.* Let's see, what is he doing? What, asleep? Have you thought
of anything yet?

*Str.* What would you have me think about?

*Socr.* What do you want to learn?

*Str.* I've told you that already
a thousand times: I want to learn how to pay no interest <term>use-money: interest on a loan.</term>.

*Socr.* Come then, cover yourself, and refine
your thoughts; break your business down into its smallest parts.

*Str.* Alas!

*Socr.* Lie still; and if you get stuck on anything,
skip it for a while and come back to it later.

*Str.* Hey, my dear Socrates!

*Socr.* What is it, old man?

*Str.* I have found the thing that will do it.

*Socr.* How so?

*Str.* First tell me
where I might meet with a Thessalian witch;
for I would steal the moon one of these nights,
and having caught her, lock her in a chest
as carefully as I would keep a mirror.

*Socr.* What will you gain by that?

*Str.* What? If the moon
never rises again, I won't be bound to pay any interest.

*Socr.* How so?

*Str.* Because interest, you know, is paid by the month.

*Socr.* That's good, but I will propose another problem.
Suppose that you were legally bound
to pay five talents <term>Talent: A very large unit of currency in ancient Greece.</term>; could you erase the record <term>rase figures: to erase or scrape away the writing of a debt from a wax tablet.</term>?

*Str.* I don't know, but I'll try.

*Socr.* You must not limit
your thoughts so narrowly within yourself—

<meta>catchword: But</meta>

<summary>Strepsiades hilariously fails at philosophical meditation, proposing instead a plan to steal the moon to stop the passage of months and thereby end his interest payments. Socrates attempts to move him toward a more practical (if still dishonest) legal trick involving the erasure of debt records.</summary>
<keywords>Socrates, Strepsiades, The Clouds, Aristophanes, interest, debt, talents, Thessalian witch, moon, comedy, philosophy</keywords>
