<meta>This page continues the comedic dialogue between Socrates and Strepsiades, following the previous page where Socrates instructed Strepsiades to meditate on a solution to his debts.</meta>

<header>SOCRATES.</header>

But like a beetle tied to a thread,
Let them play and flutter in the air.
*Str.* I have it! I have it! The most excellent way to cancel
A deed, as you will admit when you have heard it.
*So.* What is it?
*Str.* Did you never see at any grocer's
A clear, transparent stone which they use
To start fires?
*So.* You mean a magnifying glass. <note>original: "burning-glasse"; a lens used to concentrate sunlight to start a fire.</note>
*Str.* The very same.
*So.* What would you do with it?
*Str.* While the clerk is writing the deed—do you see?—
I will stand next to him with my magnifying glass 
Toward the sun, and I’ll burn away every letter. <note>Legal documents were often written on wax tablets; the heat would melt the wax and erase the text.</note>
*So.* Wisely said, by all the Graces!
*Str.* How I long
To cancel a debt of fifty pounds that way.
*So.* That is well. Now tell me, if your opponent
Sues you, and you are likely to be defeated
For lack of witnesses, how will you avoid
His lawsuit?
*Str.* Most easily.
*So.* In what way?
*Str.* Before
It comes to judgment, I would hang myself.
*So.* Pshaw! <term>Push: an interjection of contempt, similar to "pshaw" or "pish."</term> You're talking nonsense.
*Str.* Yes, by Jove! There is no one
Who will prosecute a lawsuit against the dead.
*So.* Go away, you're acting like a fool; I’ll teach you no more.
*Str.* Why, dear *Socrates*,
Why?
*So.* You forget as fast as you can learn.
Tell me the first thing you were taught today.
*Str.* The first? Wait, let me see... the first thing, you say?
What do you call that thing we use to hold our flour?
Wretch that I am, I have forgotten it!
*So.* See? Don't you deserve, 
In your forgetfulness, to be punished like a dunce?
*Str.* Alas, what shall I do? For if I do not learn
The language of trickery, I am completely ruined.
Good Clouds, advise me on what course I should take.
*Cho.* "If you have a clever son at home,
" You had better send him here in your place.
*Str.* I have a son, and he is clever, too;
But he will not learn, which only adds to my misery.
*Cho.* And will you allow that?
*Str.* For a person of such promise—

<meta>catchword: His</meta>

<summary>Strepsiades proposes using a magnifying glass to erase his debts and suggests suicide as a legal defense, leading an exasperated Socrates to quit teaching him. On the advice of the Clouds, Strepsiades decides to send his clever but reluctant son to learn in his stead.</summary>

<header>S O C R A T E S :</header>

His mother is a woman of great spirit;
I’ll try once more. If he refuses, I’ll make
No more fuss but turn him out of doors.
Stay just a while; I will be back quickly.

## Act 3.

->Strepsiades, Phidippides, Socrates.<-

*Str.* Now, by the Clouds, you're staying here no longer!
Get out, and go feed in *Megacles’s* stable.
*Ph.* Alas, what madness has possessed you, Father?
By *Jove*, I think you are out of your mind.
*Str.* See, see, he swears by *Jove*! Aren’t you crazy,
At your age, to believe there is a *Jove*?
*Ph.* Is the truth to be mocked?
*Str.* Well, I see
You are still a child and believe old wives' tales.
But come, I will tell you something that shall make you
A man—as long as you are sure to tell it to nobody.
*Ph.* Fine; what is it?
*Str.* You swore just now by *Jove*.
*Ph.* I did.
*Str.* See how good it is to learn;
There is no such thing as *Jove*.
*Ph.* What then?
*Str.* A whirlwind
Has blown *Jove* quite away and now rules all the Heavens.
*Ph.* What nonsense is this?
*Str.* It is the serious truth, son.
*Ph.* Who tells you so?
<margin>* As if he should say "the Atheist"; for the Melians were infamous for atheism because of Diagoras, who professed it. Socrates was a student of Aristagoras, a Melian. See chapter 3.</margin>
*Str.* Our *Socrates* the * Melian,
And *Chaerephon*, who trace the footsteps of fleas. <note>A reference to a scene in *The Clouds* where the philosophers measure the distance a flea can jump.</note>
*Ph.* What? Have you reached such a height of madness
As to believe such gloomy dreamers?
*Str.* Watch your words! Do not defame men of such deep wisdom
And subtle spirits. These men live sparingly;
They never spend money on a barber,
Oils, or baths—whereas you waste my wealth
As freely as if I were already dead.
Come then, and be their student in my place.
*Ph.* What good can be learned from people like them?
*Str.* Everything that is considered wisdom, boy!
And first, to know yourself, and what a dunce
You are—how stupid, crude, and forgetful.
But stay a little; cover your face for a moment.

<meta>catchword: Ph.</meta>

<summary>Strepsiades tries to convince his son Phidippides to join Socrates' school, arguing that traditional gods like Jove have been replaced by scientific forces like the "whirlwind." He praises the philosophers' frugality while insulting his son's vanity and intellect.</summary>
<keywords>Strepsiades, Phidippides, Socrates, Jove, Atheism, The Clouds, Whirlwind, Chaerephon, Melian, philosophy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the comedic scene from Aristophanes' *The Clouds*, where the elderly Strepsiades attempts to enroll his skeptical son, Phidippides, in Socrates’ school (the "Thinkery") to learn how to argue his way out of debts.</meta>

<header>SOCRATES.</header>

*Ph.* Alas, my father is mad! What shall I do? Accuse him in court of insanity, or order a coffin for him? For he talks as nonsensically as if he were at death's door. <note>original: "drawing on"</note>
*Str.* Come on now. Let’s see—what is that?
*Ph.* A pigeon.
*Str.* Good; and that?
*Ph.* A pigeon.
*Str.* Both the same? Ridiculous! Take care you do not make such mistakes hereafter. This one you must call a cock-pigeon, and that one a hen-pigeon.
*Ph.* A hen? Is this the wonderful learning, Father, you acquired since your admission among these earthworms?
*Str.* This and a great deal more; but being old, I soon forget what I am taught.
*Ph.* I think it was a lack of memory that made you lose your cloak.
*Str.* No, it is hung up upon the arts and sciences.
*Ph.* And where are your shoes?
*Str.* Lost for the common good, like *Pericles*. <note>A reference to the Athenian statesman Pericles, who accounted for missing public funds by stating they were spent on "necessary expenses" (likely bribes).</note> But let us be gone; and see that you learn to obey me, even if it means doing wrong to everyone else. Remember that I bought you a little cart to play with when you were only six years old.
*Ph.* Alas, you will be the first one to regret this.
*Str.* Do not worry about that; do as I bid you.
*Ho.* <note>Likely an OCR error for Strepsiades (Str.) or a stage direction for "Householder."</note> *Socrates*, I have brought my son at last, though much against his will.
*So.* Yes, that is because he is rude, untaught, a child of ignorance, and unacquainted with our hungry baskets. <note>In the play, Socrates is famously depicted suspended in a basket to contemplate the sky.</note>
*Ph.* Go hang yourself in one of them!
*Str.* What impudence! Do you talk this way to your Master?
*Socr.* "Go hang"—with what a natural grace that was pronounced! How do you think he should ever learn to overthrow a nimble adversary, or win a Judge's heart with rhetoric?
*Str.* Fear not, just teach him; he is clever by nature. For when he was but a little child, he would build houses for you, make leather coaches and ships, and cut frogs out of apple peels. What is your opinion then? Do you not think he is capable of learning both arguments? <note>original: "languages"; referring to the "Better" and "Worse" arguments in the play's central debate.</note> Or if not both, be sure he learns the worse one.
*Socr.* Well, we shall try what may be done with him.
*Str.* Farewell; and remember that in everything I say that is just, you must learn to contradict me.

### Act 3

### Scene 1. Strepsiades.

<margin>
* The Greeks reckoned backward from the twentieth day of the month; see the *Life of Solon*, 6b4.
</margin>

The* fifth, the fourth, the third, the second—hmm.
The most abhorred and dreadful day is at hand,
The "Old and New"; <note>The *henē kai nea*, the last day of the lunar month in the Attic calendar, when debts and interests were legally due.</note> everyone I owe money to
Threatens to sue me and vows my utter ruin.
Yet I ask for nothing but what is just and reasonable:
"My friend, be patient with me until another time."
But they all answer me that words are no payment;
They revile me and swear they will sue me on my bonds.
And let them! What do I care, as long as my Phidippides
Has learned the art of cheating? I shall know shortly;
I only need to knock at the school. Hello! Son!

### Scene 2. Strepsiades, Socrates.

**Socrates:** God save you, Strepsiades.
**Strepsiades:** And you as well.
<margin>
* Socrates sometimes accepted such gifts, though not money. See chapter [X].
</margin>
* First, take this bag of meal, for it is proper
That we pay our respects to our reverend Master.
Now tell me, has my son mastered the art
For which I placed him with you?
**Socrates:** Yes, perfectly.
**Strepsiades:** Thanks be to Deceit, the Queen who governs all things!
**Socrates:** Now you may defeat all your adversaries.
**Strepsiades:** Even if a witness swears that I have borrowed money?
**Socrates:** Yes, even if a thousand swear it.
**Strepsiades:** Io! Io! <note>original: "Iö. Iö." — an ancient Greek exclamation of triumph or joy.</note>
Triumph, my boys! Woe to you money-lenders!
You and your bonds and your compound interest <term>use on use</term> can go hang yourselves now!
You will trouble me no more. Oh, what a son
I have, who fences with a two-edged tongue!
The pillar and guardian of my house, the terror of my enemies!
Come forth quickly and meet my joyful embrace;
Come forth and hear your father!
**Socrates:** See, here is the man.
**Strepsiades:** Oh, my dear boy!
**Socrates:** Go, and take him with you.

<summary>Strepsiades anxiously counts the days until his debts are due but rejoices when Socrates confirms that Phidippides has mastered the art of deceptive rhetoric. Confident that they can now defeat any legal claim through trickery, Strepsiades welcomes his son back as the "guardian" of his estate.</summary>

--- Page 261 ---
<meta>This page continues from Act 3, Scene 2, where Socrates has finished instructing Phidippides in the "Unjust Argument." Strepsiades has just arrived at the school to collect his son, eager to see if he has learned how to legally evade the family's debts.</meta>

<header>SOCRATES.</header>

### Scene 3.

### Strepsiades, Phidippides.

**Strepsiades:** Hurrah, my son! Oh, how I rejoice to see your changed complexion! You look now, I think, as if you were inspired by the spirit of contradiction. I read contrary questions in your very face; your very eyes, I think, say, "How? How is that?" You can now persuade the world that you are the one being wronged, even when you are the one doing the wrong. I see it,
<margin>* <note>original: "Δειν' και τετανδις"</note> "Formidable and strained." Manuscript commentary.</margin>
I plainly see it; a true Attic mien. <term>mien</term> <note>The original text uses "mine," an archaic spelling for a person’s look or manner, particularly their facial expression.</note> Now let it be your study to recover the fortune of the man whom you have almost ruined.

**Phidippides:** Why, what is it that you fear?

**Strepsiades:** The "old and the new" day. <note>The *henē kai nea*, the last day of the lunar month when debts were legally due.</note>

**Phidippides:** Can one and the same day be both old and new?

**Strepsiades:** I know that; I am certain my creditors have agreed to sue me on that day.

**Phidippides:** They will lose by it if they do, for it is impossible to make two days out of one.

**Strepsiades:** How is it impossible?

**Phidippides:** Just as it is impossible for a woman to be both old and young at the same time.

**Strepsiades:** But the law has determined it so.

**Phidippides:** But those men do not understand what the law means.

**Strepsiades:** Why, what is the meaning of it?
<margin>* See the *Life of Solon*, Chapter [X].</margin>
**Phidippides:** Ancient Solon was naturally a friend to the people.

**Strepsiades:** What does that have to do with this?

**Phidippides:** He appointed two days: the last day of the old month for the summons <term>citation</term>, and the first of the new month for the actual payment of the money.

**Strepsiades:** But why the last day for the summons?

**Phidippides:** So that the debtor, having one day of warning, might escape and avoid the trouble of the next day.

**Strepsiades:** Why do the magistrates then take all the court fees <term>forfeits</term> upon the "old and new" day?

**Phidippides:** They are hungry, and they want to taste their meat before they should actually sit down to the meal.

**Strepsiades:** [To the audience] We are the fools who sit still and do nothing, while those of us who are wise and quick have finished the business. You blocks, you stones, you sheep, you empty bubbles! Let me congratulate this son of mine.

<summary>Strepsiades celebrates Phidippides' newfound ability to use sophistry to manipulate the law. Phidippides argues that the "old and new" day of the Athenian calendar is logically two separate days, meaning creditors cannot legally demand payment on it.</summary>

<keywords>Strepsiades, Phidippides, Solon, Athenian law, debt, logic, sophistry, creditors, Attic mien</keywords>

<header>SOCRATES</header>

I'll celebrate myself and my good fortune in a song.
> "Now Strepsiades, you are blessed,
> "Among the most discreet, the best,
> "What a son you have! Now all
> "My envious neighbors may say,
> "When they hear that he alone
> "Has my creditors overthrown."
But come, my boy; now you shall feast with me.

### Section 4. Pasias, Strepsiades, Witness.

**Pasias:** And must a man be cheated of his own property like this? It is better to take any course of action than to suffer this. You must assist me in this business, neighbor, so that I may call my debtor to account. There is one friend turned into an enemy; yet I will not shame my country; before I do it, I will give him warning.
->**Strepsiades**<-
**Strepsiades:** Well now, what would you have?

**Pasias:** The "old and new" day has come. <note>The deadline for debt repayment.</note>

**Strepsiades:** Bear witness, friend: he named two days. What sum is it you demand?

**Pasias:** Twelve pounds you borrowed with interest, back when you bought your son a racehorse.

**Strepsiades:** A racehorse? You know I never cared for them in my life.

**Pasias:** And you swore by Jove and all the gods to pay it.

**Strepsiades:** By Jove? That was then, before my son had learned the "all-convincing" speech.

**Pasias:** You will not deny it.

**Strepsiades:** What have I gained but that, for all the cost of this learning?

**Pasias:** Do you dare deny it, if I should put you under oath and make you call the gods to witness it?

**Strepsiades:** Which gods do you mean?

**Pasias:** Jove, Mercury, and Neptune.

**Strepsiades:** By Jove? Yes, I will swear to that; I'll bet you three pence on it.

**Pasias:** Curse you for this impudence!

**Strepsiades:** If you were rubbed with salt, it would make your wits sharper. <note>Strepsiades suggests Pasias is like a piece of meat that needs curing because he is "stale" or dull-witted.</note>

**Pasias:** Are you laughing at me?

**Strepsiades:** You would take up six bushels. <note>Likely an insult regarding Pasias's size or the amount of salt needed to "cure" his stupidity.</note>

**Pasias:** So help me Jupiter and all the gods, I will get even with you for this mockery.

**Strepsiades:** I am extremely amused by your gods; and this Jupiter you swear by—they are excellent entertainment for an educated man.

<summary>A creditor named Pasias arrives to collect a debt from Strepsiades, who uses his son's new sophistic training to mock the creditor and deny the validity of his religious oaths.</summary>
<keywords>Strepsiades, Pasias, debt collection, sophistry, Jove, Jupiter, oaths, legal loopholes, Greek comedy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues from the previous scene where Pasias, a creditor, confronts Strepsiades for a debt. Strepsiades has just finished mocking Pasias’s attempt to swear by the gods, claiming that the gods are no longer "legal tender" in his new philosophical worldview.</meta>

<header>SOCRATES.</header>

*Pasias:* Well, you will one day answer for these words.
But tell me whether I shall have my money
Or not; give me my answer, and I’m gone.
*Strepsiades:* Stay but a little; I will answer shortly,
And plainly.
*Pasias:* Surely he’s gone to fetch the money.
*Strepsiades:* Where is the man who comes to ask me for money?
Tell me, what is this?
*Pasias:* That which it is: a *Cardopus* <term>the masculine form for a kneading-trough</term>.
*Strepsiades:* You ask for money, and yet you are such a dunce?
As long as I live, I’ll never pay a penny to a man
Who calls a *Cardopa* <term>the grammatically correct feminine form</term> a *Cardopus*.
*Pasias:* You will not pay then?
*Strepsiades:* Not as far as I know.
Since you won't stay any longer, please, go about your business.
*Pasias:* Yes, I’ll be gone; but in the meantime, know
I’ll have my money, as sure as I live this day.
*Strepsiades:* You may chance to go without it; yet I’m sorry
You should be punished so for a mistake—
For saying *Cardopus* instead of *Cardopa*.

->Scene 8.<-

->*Amynias, Strepsiades, Witness.*<-

<margin>* These were Xenocles, Xenotimus, and Demotimus: tragic poets and actors.</margin>
*Amynias:* Oh, oh, alas!
*Strepsiades:* Who is that who keeps up such a bawling?
Who are you? One of *Carcinus's sons? <note>Carcinus was a tragedian often mocked in comedy; his sons were said to be dwarf-like or bad dancers.</note>
*Amynias:* It is I, the unhappy one!
*Strepsiades:* Keep it to yourself.
*Amynias:* Unlucky chance, oh cruel destiny,
To ruin my chariot and all my horses at once!
<margin>* This and the following line are taken from Xenocles the tragedian, which is the reason for Strepsiades's answer.</margin>

*Strepsiades:* What harm did *Tlepolemus* <note>A character in a play by Xenocles</note> ever do to you?
*Amynias:* Do not mock me, but rather tell your son
To pay me the money which he had from me,
For I was never in greater need of it.
*Strepsiades:* What money, man?
*Amynias:* That which he borrowed from me.
*Strepsiades:* Then I perceive you’re in a sad condition.
*Amynias:* I had a nasty fall while driving my horses.
*Strepsiades:* You are only joking; it was more like driving an ass.
*Amynias:* I do not joke when I demand my money.
*Strepsiades:* Upon my word, you are not in your right mind.
*Amynias:* How so?
*Strepsiades:* Your brain, I think, is troubled.
Either pay me—

<meta>This page continues the scene where Strepsiades, having adopted the sophistry taught at the Thinkery, uses absurd logic to avoid paying his debts. He previously dismissed Pasias by mocking his grammar; now he confronts another creditor, Amynias.</meta>

<header>SOCRATES:</header>

*Amynias:* Pay me my money immediately, or I will cause you trouble.
*Strepsiades:* Tell me, does Jove create and send down rain, or does the sun draw it up from the sea?
*Amynias:* I neither know nor care.
*Strepsiades:* What? Are you fit to receive money, yet so ignorant of these sublime and subtle mysteries?
*Amynias:* Well, if you cannot give me the principal amount, at least pay me the interest.
*Strepsiades:* Interest? What kind of creature is that?
*Amynias:* What else but the increase of money by months and days, as time passes?
*Strepsiades:* Very well. And do you think the sea is fuller now than it was at first?
*Amynias:* No, not a drop; it is not right that it should be.
*Strepsiades:* By your own admission, the sea hasn't grown at all; then with what conscience can you demand that your money should increase? Get away from my door! Someone fetch me a whip!
*Witness:* Well, I will bear witness for him.
<margin>* The name of the horse which he bought from him.</margin> *Strepsiades:* Why don't you go? Will you move, *Samphoras*?
*Amynias:* Is this not outrageous?
*Strepsiades:* Will you be gone? Or shall I lead you by a chain and make you perform tricks? If you stay just a little longer, I will send you and your cart and horses packing!
*Chorus:* "Now observe what it means to apply
"One's studies to an evil end.
"This old man, who is intent
"On cheating his creditors,
"Has foolishly thwarted himself,
"And will soon find out to his cost,
"That in this false art, his son
"Has attained perfection:
"A cunning way to refute justice,
"So that at last, the father will wish him mute."

# Scene 1.

->*Strepsiades, Phidippides.*<-

*Streps.* **O**h Neighbors, Kinsmen, Countrymen, help, help!
I am beaten all over: oh, my head, my back!
You strike your father, you rogue!
*Phid.* I do indeed, father.
*Streps.* Look, look, he even admits it.
*Phid.* I certainly do.
*Streps.* Thief, villain, <term>parricide</term> <note>One who kills or attacks a parent</note>.
*Phid.* More, I beg of you;
I am very much pleased by these pretty titles.
*Streps.* Rascal!
*Phid.* Please, shower me even more with these "roses."
*Streps.* Do you beat your father?
*Phid.* Yes, by Jove <note>original: "Jove"; the Roman name for Zeus, king of the gods</note>, and rightly so.
*Streps.* Oh, you rogue! What justice can there be in that?
*Phid.* I will prove it through logic.
*Streps.* Through logic?
*Phid.* Very easily. In which reasoning
Shall I debate you?
*Streps.* Reasoning?
*Phid.* Yes, the Superior
Or the Inferior? <note>Referring to the two types of arguments Phidippides learned from the Sophists: the "Just" and "Unjust" arguments</note>
*Streps.* I have raised you well indeed
If you can prove this to be good: that any son
May beat his father.
*Phid.* You will admit as much
If I prove it so well that you cannot answer it.
*Streps.* Well, I will hear for once what you have to say.

# Scene 2.

->*Chorus, Strepsiades, Phidippides.*<-

*Cho.* "Old man, it is very important for you to refute
"Your son, whose confidence seems to suit
"A just cause; how did this dispute begin?"
*Str.* I shall tell the story from the beginning. As soon
As we had dined, I took a lute and asked him
To sing "The Sheep-shearing" by Simonides <note>A famous Greek lyric poet known for traditional songs</note>.
He told me it was an old-fashioned and ugly custom

<header>SOCRATES.</header>

To sing at dinner like a miller's wife.
*Phi.* And was this not enough to deserve
<margin>* At noon. Virgil, Eclogues 2. <note>Refers to the time of day when cicadas—referred to here as grasshoppers—chirp most loudly.</note></margin>
A beating, when you would make men chirp like * grasshoppers?
*Str.* That is exactly what he said inside; and he added that
Simonides was an unpleasant poet.
I must confess I could hardly tolerate him;
But then I told him to take a myrtle branch,
And perform some piece by Aeschylus. "That Aeschylus,"
He says, "is the most absurd of all poets,
The harshest, most disorderly, and full of bombast <term>bombast</term> <note>Pretentious, inflated, or overblown language</note>."
Do you not think my heart pounded at such language?
Yet I restrained myself. Then I said, "Recite
A learned speech from some modern wit."
He immediately repeats a passage from *Euripides*—
A tedious, long oration about how a brother
(Good Heavens!) violated his sister’s bed.
At this, I confess, I could no longer contain myself,
But scolded him sharply. We began to argue—
Words followed words—until at last he turned to blows,
To striking, to pulling, to tearing at me.
*Phi.* And was it not just?
You, who would speak ill of *Euripides*,
The wisest of all poets.
*Str. Wisest?* Ah!
What have I said? I shall be beaten again.
*Ph. By Jove* <note>original: "Iove"; the Roman name for Zeus, king of the gods</note>, and you deserve it.
*Str.* How, "deserve it"?
You ungrateful wretch! Have I not raised you,
Fed you, and supported you since you were a little child,
Supplying all your needs? How then can I deserve it?

> *Chor.* "Now I believe every youthful breast
> "Is possessed with expectation,
> "That if the glory of the day
> "Is carried away from the plaintiff <note>Strepsiades, who is bringing the complaint against his son</note>,
> "By this example they may all
> "Fall heavily upon the old men;
> "What you have done with the utmost art,
> "It is now your part to justify."

*Phid.* How sweet it is to study clever new things,
And to despise all fundamental laws!
When I applied my mind to horse-racing <term>horse-coursing</term> <note>The practice of hunting or racing with horses</note>
I could not speak three words without faltering;
Now, since I gave it up, I am acquainted
With weighty sentences and subtle reasons,
Able to prove I ought to beat my father.
*Str.* No, go back to racing! For I would rather
Support your horses than be beaten like this.

<summary>Strepsiades recounts how an argument over classical versus modern poetry led to his son, Phidippides, physically attacking him. Phidippides uses his new sophistic education to argue that his father's outdated tastes justify the beating.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the debate between Strepsiades and his son Phidippides. Having been physically assaulted by his son, Strepsiades is now being subjected to a sophistical, "logical" justification for the violence, which Phidippides learned at Socrates's "Thinkery."</meta>

<header>->SOCRATES.<-</header>
<sig>Ooo</sig>

*Phidippides:* I will begin where you interrupted me, and first I will ask: did you not beat me when I was a child?
*Strepsiades:* But that was out of love.
*Phidippides:* That is quite right. Tell me then, should I not repay your love with equal love? If being beaten is a sign of being loved, why should I suffer blows <term>stripes</term> while you receive none? I was born as free by nature as you were; nor is it right that sons should be punished <term>chastized</term> while their parents are not.
*Strepsiades:* Why?
*Phidippides:* You cite the law that allows all children to be beaten. To which I answer: old men are children twice over <note>A common Greek proverb: "dis paides hoi gerontes," meaning that the elderly return to a state of dependency and mental simplicity similar to childhood.</note>, and therefore, when they do wrong, they ought to be punished just as much as we are.
*Strepsiades:* But there is no law that says parents should be punished.
*Phidippides:* Was the man who made that law not a human being like you and me? He created a law which all the old men followed; why may I not just as well establish another, and have all the young men follow my advice? However, all the beatings given before this law was made must be forgiven without any dispute. Besides, notice how roosters and other creatures fight with their fathers <term>sires</term>, yet they do not differ from us at all, except that they do not make laws.
*Strepsiades:* Why then, if you want to imitate the roosters, do you not eat on a dunghill and sleep in a hen-roost?
*Phidippides:* It is not the same thing; our *Socrates* does not approve of going that far.
*Strepsiades:* Then do not approve of their fighting either; in this, you argue against yourself.
*Phidippides:* How so?
*Strepsiades:* Because the authority I exercise over you will be your own whenever you have a son.
*Phidippides:* But if I never have any, then I shall never have authority, and you will go to the grave mocking me.
*Strepsiades:* That makes too much sense.
*Phidippides:* Now hear another argument.
*Strepsiades:* I am ruined.
*Phidippides:* And then perhaps you will accept the blows I gave you

*Strepsiades:* Not half so bad!
*Phidippides:* I’ll beat my mother, too.
*Strepsiades:* What? What are you saying? This is even worse than the other!
*Phidippides:* What if I prove, using the second argument <note>The "Worse Argument" or "Unjust Discourse" taught by Socrates</note>, that I ought to do it?
*Strepsiades:* If you do that, you will have nothing left to do but prove that you, your "wife" Socrates <note>A derogatory reference to Socrates’s perceived lack of traditional masculinity or the domestic nature of the "Thinkery"</note>, and that "wiser" argument can all go hang yourselves together! *Strepsiades:* Alas, this is a misfortune, but a just one. I should not have sought ways to avoid paying back the money I borrowed. Come then, my son, let us take revenge on that wicked Socrates and Chaerephon <note>Socrates’s companion and assistant</note>, who have deceived us both.
*Phidippides:* I will not wrong my teachers.
*Strepsiades:* Show respect for Heavenly Jove!
*Phidippides:* "Heavenly Jove"? See how you rave now, father! There is no such thing as Jove.
*Strepsiades:* There is!
*Phidippides:* <margin>Act 3</margin> A whirlwind <term>Vortex</term> has blown Jove completely away and now rules everything.
*Strepsiades:* No, son, he has not been expelled. I was just a fool to worship a clay idol <term>fictile deity</term> in his place <note>Strepsiades refers to a 'dinos,' a type of ceramic jar, which he mistook for the god 'Vortex'</note>.
*Phidippides:* Well, if you insist on being mad, be mad by yourself.

## Scene 3
### Strepsiades.

*Strepsiades:* How mad I was to trust in Socrates and cast off all our Gods! Good Mercury, do not be displeased or punish me, but forgive me. I took such pains and studied only to talk nonsense. Tell me, what should I do with these fellows?

<meta>This page begins the biography of Xenophon, following the transition from the previous discussion of Socrates and his portrayal in contemporary literature.</meta>

# XENOPHON.

## CHAPTER I.

->*Xenophon: His Origin, Parentage, and Following of Socrates.*<-

<margin>Laertius</margin>
Xenophon was an Athenian, the son of Gryllus, of the Erchiean Tribe. The time of his birth is nowhere expressly recorded. Stesiclides affirms he died in the first year of the 105th Olympiad. <margin>* Lucian, *On Longevity*</margin> Lucian states that he lived ninety years; from this it is evident that he was born at or before the first year of the 82nd Olympiad. If the learned Casaubon <note>Isaac Casaubon (1559–1614), a renowned classical scholar.</note> had observed this, he would not have <margin>* Athenaeus, *Animadversions* §. 12.</margin> altered the text of Athenaeus based on the supposition that Xenophon was only ten years old in the fourth year of the 89th Olympiad (the setting of his *Symposium*), whereas he was actually no less than thirty-six years of age at that time. Laertius says he flourished <term>reached his prime</term> in the fourth year of the 94th Olympiad (though Suidas reads the 98th). Or, according to others, he flourished in the 89th Olympiad along with the rest of the Socratic philosophers, of whom he became one by the following accident.

<margin>* Laertius</margin>
Meeting Socrates in a narrow lane, the philosopher stopped him with his staff and asked him where all kinds of food <term>meat: here meaning food or provisions in general</term> were sold. Socrates gave a serious answer and then asked him where it was that men were made good and virtuous. While Xenophon paused to think, Socrates said, "Follow me then, and learn." From that time forward, he became a disciple of Socrates.

During the great war between the Lacedæmonians <note>Spartans</note> and Athenians, called the Peloponnesian War (the natural boldness of his spirit perhaps being excited by the example of his master, Socrates), he was personally engaged in the fight at Delium in the first year of the 89th Olympiad, in which the <margin>* Strabo, Book 9. Laertius.</margin> Boeotians defeated the Athenians. In this defeat, Xenophon was unhorsed and thrown down during the flight; Socrates (whose own horse had also been killed under him, and was fighting on foot) took Xenophon upon his shoulders and carried him many furlongs until the enemy gave up the pursuit. This was the first trial of his military profession, which he afterward resumed on another occasion.

<summary>This page introduces Xenophon’s Athenian origins, discusses the chronological debates regarding his birth and lifespan, and recounts his first meeting with Socrates and his early military service at the Battle of Delium.</summary>
<keywords>Xenophon, Socrates, Gryllus, Erchiean Tribe, Peloponnesian War, Battle of Delium, Isaac Casaubon, Diogenes Laertius, Olympiads</keywords>

--- Page 272 ---
<meta>This page describes how Xenophon came to join the expedition of Cyrus the Younger against his brother Artaxerxes, detailing the political context and the advice Xenophon received from Socrates and the Delphian Oracle.</meta>

# XENOPHON.

### CHAPTER 2.

->On the occasion of his following Cyrus into Asia.<-

<margin>
* Xenophon, *The Expedition of Cyrus*, Book 1.
* *Chronological Canon on the Greek Eras*, page 113.
* Xenophon, Book 3.
* Xenophon, Book 3.
* Chion, *Epistle to Mithridates*.
</margin>

Artaxerxes succeeded his father Darius to the Kingdom of Persia. His younger brother, Cyrus, had been sent for from his province in Lydia during his father’s illness. (This refers to the first Artaxerxes recorded by the Arundel Marbles <note>The Parian Chronicle, part of the Arundel Marbles collection of Greek inscriptions.</note> in the second year of the 93rd Olympiad, which a learned scholar confused with the latter Artaxerxes six years later). Cyrus was imprisoned by his brother upon the accusation of Tissaphernes, but he was released through the intervention of his mother, Parysatis. 

Upon returning to his government, Cyrus used every secret means to strengthen his position. The Ionian cities had been handed over to Tissaphernes by Artaxerxes, but they all revolted to Cyrus, with the exception of Miletus. His excuses for raising forces were the garrisoning of those cities and his opposition to Tissaphernes. Clearchus also raised many troops for him in Chersonesus, under the pretext of warring against the Thracians. He also privately maintained an army in Thessaly under Aristippus, while Proxenus of Boeotia brought him forces supposedly to be used against the Pisidians. 

This Proxenus—who had been a student of Gorgias of Leontini <note>A famous Greek sophist and rhetorician.</note> and was a guest-friend of Xenophon—sent an invitation to Xenophon to join Cyrus, assuring him that he would be held in higher esteem by the prince than he was in his own country. Xenophon consulted with Socrates about this letter. Socrates, fearing that if Xenophon sided with Cyrus the Athenians would be displeased with him (since Cyrus had previously aided the Spartans <note>Lacedemonians</note> against them), advised him to seek the counsel of the Delphian Oracle. 

Xenophon went there and asked Apollo to which of the gods he should offer his vows and sacrifices to ensure the success of his intended journey. The Oracle answered that he should sacrifice to those gods to whom such offerings were due. Returning to Athens, he shared this response with Socrates, who blamed him because he had not asked whether it was better to go or stay, but instead—having already decided to go—had only asked how he might best complete his journey. "Nevertheless," Socrates said, "since you phrased your request that way, you must do as the gods command." 

After sacrificing according to the Oracle’s directions, Xenophon set sail. At Sardis, he found Proxenus and Cyrus ready for their expedition into Asia. He was immediately introduced to Cyrus, and both the prince and Proxenus earnestly pleaded with him to stay. He remained with Cyrus, not as an officer, but as a volunteer. In this capacity, he did nothing unbecoming of a soldier; consequently, he was among those whom Cyrus most esteemed.

<margin>
* Xenophon, Book 1.
</margin>
Cyrus, having drawn all his forces together, marched up and

<meta>The text continues on the next page with the word "gave."</meta>

<summary>Xenophon joins the expedition of Cyrus the Younger as a volunteer after consulting the Delphian Oracle and Socrates. The text explains the political tensions between Cyrus and his brother Artaxerxes that led to the recruitment of Greek forces.</summary>
<keywords>Xenophon, Cyrus the Younger, Artaxerxes, Socrates, Delphian Oracle, Proxenus, Tissaphernes, Persian Empire, Anabasis, Peloponnesian War context</keywords>

--- Page 273 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Xenophon, detailing the outbreak of the conflict between Cyrus the Younger and his brother Artaxerxes, leading up to the Battle of Cunaxa and the subsequent capture of the Greek generals.</meta>

<margin>
* Diogenes Laertius, *Lives of the Philosophers*.
* Xenophon, *The Expedition of Cyrus*, Book 2.
* Diogenes Laertius.
</margin>

Cyrus gave battle to *Artaxerxes* at the start of the fourth year of the ninety-fourth Olympiad (when *Xenenetus* was Archon) at *Cunaxa*, which is five hundred stadia <note>Approximately 57 miles or 92 kilometers.</note> from *Babylon*, near the *Euphrates* River. While he was inspecting both armies, he told *Xenophon*, who had ridden up to him, that the sacrifices were auspicious. *Xenophon* then gave him the Greeks' watchword: *Jupiter the Preserver* <note>original: "Zeus Soter"</note>. 

The Greeks were victorious against *Tissaphernes*, but *Cyrus*, through his excessive boldness, was killed while attacking the King. The Greeks, believing themselves to be masters of the battlefield and assuming *Cyrus* was still alive, returned to their camp only to find it plundered by the enemy. * The next day, the King sent *Phalinus* to them to demand they surrender their arms. *Xenophon* answered: "We have nothing left but our arms and our bravery. As long as we keep our arms, we may use our bravery; if we surrender them, we are no longer masters of ourselves. It would be an act of folly," he said, "to surrender the only things we have left, since by keeping them we might perhaps make ourselves masters of what you possess." *Phalinus* smiled and replied: "Young man, you look and speak like a philosopher, but rest assured that your bravery will not overcome the King's power." *Clearchus* gave this answer on behalf of the whole army: "If we are considered friends, it will be better for the King that we remain armed; if we are enemies, it is better for ourselves."

*Tissaphernes*, having made a truce with *Clearchus*, treacherously took him and four other commanders—*Proxenus*, *Menon* (with whom *Xenophon* had a personal feud), *Agias*, and *Socrates* <note>Socrates of Achaia, one of the Greek generals, not the philosopher.</note>—along with twenty captains and two hundred common soldiers into his custody. Delivering them to the King, they were all beheaded. The Greeks were then summoned to lay down their arms under the pretext that *Clearchus* had been executed for a treasonous plot discovered by *Menon* and *Proxenus*, who were said to be highly rewarded. *Xenophon* demanded that those two be sent back, as they were the most fit to advise the Greeks, being friends to both sides. The Persians, unable to provide an answer, departed.

### CHAPTER III.

->How he led the retreat of the Greek army.<-

<margin>
* Xenophon, *The Expedition of Cyrus*, Book 3.
</margin>

* The Greeks, finding themselves in such a desperate situation, were in despair of ever seeing their homeland again. *Xenophon* called together the officers who had served under *Proxenus* and told them: "You must not expect any mercy from the King. He showed none even to the body of his dead brother, having fastened his head and hand to a gallows. You must resolve to place your safety in your own weapons." *Apollonides*, a Boeotian, argued that there was no means of safety except by seeking the King’s favor...

<summary>Following the death of Cyrus at the Battle of Cunaxa, the Greek generals are treacherously captured and executed by the Persians, leaving Xenophon to rally the demoralized troops for their retreat.</summary>
<keywords>Battle of Cunaxa, Cyrus the Younger, Artaxerxes, Tissaphernes, Clearchus, execution of generals, retreat of the ten thousand, Greek mercenary bravery</keywords>

--- Page 274 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Xenophon during the retreat of the Ten Thousand. Following the capture and execution of the Greek generals by the Persians, the army is left in a state of despair and must elect new leaders to guide them back to Greece.</meta>

<header># X E N O P H O N.</header>

...favor, and began to assess the dangers surrounding them. *Xenophon* answered, "When, after the death of *Cyrus*, they marched up to the King's armies, I worked for a truce; but when our captains went to him unarmed, he betrayed <note>original: "abused"</note> them. Therefore, *Apolionides* deserves to be dismissed as a disgrace to his nation." *Agasthias* replied (words which *Laertius* ascribes to *Xenophon*) that he was an insignificant fellow whose ears were pierced like the slaves of *Lydia*. So they turned him out. Having called together all the commanders, *Xenophon* advised them to choose new ones to replace those who were lost; he himself was elected in the place of *Proxenus*. Thereupon, he put on his finest clothes, as they were most fitting either
<margin>
* Aelian, *Various History*, 3. 24.
</margin>
for death or victory. For *his greatest delight was in beautiful, polished armor, affirming that if he conquered, he deserved such equipment; if he were conquered and died in the field, they would decently reflect his status and were the most fitting burial ornaments for a valiant man.* His shield was from *Argos*, his breastplate from *Attica*, his helmet from *Boeotia*, and his horse from *Epidaurus*; by which *Aelian* argued the elegance of the man, in choosing things that were beautiful and deeming himself worthy of them.

Thus adorned, he made a speech to the army, advising them (using the example of their late commanders) not to trust the enemy. He suggested that, to facilitate their return, they should burn their wagons <term>carriages</term> and tents. This advice was put into execution. *Cheirisophus*, a *Lacedaemonian* <note>Spartan</note>, had charge of the vanguard, while *Xenophon* led the rear. He was chosen (says [ ]) *Chio* as much for his courage as for his wisdom, being excellent in both. Between these two there grew such a great friendship that, during the entire time of the retreat, they only ever had one disagreement. Their march was directed toward the headwaters of those great rivers that lay in their path, so that they might cross them where they were fordable. Having crossed the River *Zapatas* <note>OCR: "Zathe"; likely the Great Zab</note>, *Mithridates* came up to them and harassed the rear with projectiles. Because the Greeks were unable to retaliate, *Xenophon* provided two hundred slings and, finding fifty horses fit for service among the baggage train, he mounted men upon them. Having thus thwarted the second attack of *Mithridates*, they marched to *Larissa*, located on the *Tigris*, and then to *Mespila*. During their march from there, *Tissaphernes* overtook them with a great army, but he was twice defeated. Consequently (as the safest course), he seized a mountain under which they were forced to pass. *Xenophon*, with a party, gained the top of that mountain by another route, despite great difficulty and trouble. To encourage his soldiers, when one of them named *Soteridas* grumbled that *Xenophon* was on horseback while he himself marched on foot, burdened by the weight of his shield, *Xenophon* heard him and dismounted. He took the man's shield from him and, pushing him out of his rank, marched on—notwith-

<meta>The catchword "standing" indicates the sentence continues: "notwithstanding the weight..." on the next page.</meta>

<summary>Xenophon is elected as a general of the Ten Thousand and begins organizing their famous retreat, demonstrating leadership through his personal conduct and tactical innovations against Persian harassment.</summary>
<keywords>Xenophon, Ten Thousand, Anabasis, Cheirisophus, Tissaphernes, Mithridates, Greek retreat, ancient warfare, leadership</keywords>

--- Page 275 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of the retreat of the Ten Thousand under Xenophon's leadership, detailing the arduous journey through hostile mountain territories toward the Black Sea.</meta>

<header># XENOPHON.</header>

...standing, he also had a cavalry breastplate <term>cuirass</term>) on in his place. But the soldiers beat and insulted *Soteridas* until they forced him to take back his shield and return to his position. When they had reached the summit, the enemy, having been outmaneuvered, fled and set fire to the villages.

<margin>* *Xenophon, Book 4.*</margin>
The Greeks (blocked by the River *Tigris*) marched over the mountains into the country of the *Carduchi*, a people hostile to the *Persians*, who were rugged and warlike. From them, the Greeks encountered such opposition that during seven days' march through their country, they were forced into constant combat and suffered more hardships than the *Persians* had ever inflicted upon them. Fording the River *Kentrites*, which forms the border of that country, they passed into *Armenia*. There, after putting to flight several troops of cavalry raised by the King’s governors <term>deputies</term> in those regions to block their passage, they marched without further trouble to the headwaters of the *Tigris*, which they crossed. From there, they reached the River *Teleboas* <note>OCR: Teleloa</note> in western *Armenia*, where they were plagued by heavy snow and lost many men to the extreme cold. They eventually reached the River *Phasis*, near which lived the *Phasiani*, the *Taochi* <note>OCR: Tacchi</note>, and the *Chalybes*. The *Taochi*, whose land they entered first, had moved all their supplies into fortified strongholds, reducing the Greeks to great want. With much effort, they finally captured one of these forts, where they took enough livestock to sustain them through the territory of the *Chalybes*—a fierce nation from whom they received nothing but blows. From there, they marched to the River *Harpasus* and then to the *Scythini*, where the ruler of *Gymnias*, a town in those parts, led them through enemy territory (which he commanded them to burn as they went) to Mount *Theches*. From its peak, they could see the sea, to the great joy of the soldiers. Passing peacefully through the land of the *Macrones* to the *Colchian* Mountains and defeating the *Colchians* who opposed them, they arrived at *Trapezus* <note>modern: Trabzon</note>, a city on the Black <term>Euxine</term> Sea where there was a Greek colony. There, they offered sacrifices and celebrated athletic games.

<margin>* *Xenophon, Book 5.*</margin>
They sent *Cheirisophus* to *Anaxibius*, the *Spartan* <term>Lacedaemonian</term> Admiral (with whom he was a close friend), to obtain ships for their transportation home. While they waited for his return, they sustained themselves by raiding the *Colchians* and *Drilae* <note>OCR: Drylans</note>. But when he did not return and their supplies began to fail, *Xenophon* persuaded the neighboring cities to clear a land passage for them. They traveled to *Cerasus*, a Greek city, where they held a muster and found only eight thousand six hundred men remaining of the ten thousand who had originally gone up with *Cyrus*; the rest had been consumed by enemies, snow, and sickness. They divided the money that had been made by the sale of captives, setting aside a tenth as an offering to *Apollo* and *Diana*. *Xenophon* kept his portion to be dedicated at *Delphi* and *Ephesus*. From *Cerasus*, they passed through the country of the *Mossynoeci* <note>OCR: Mosyneci</note>, a primitive <term>barbarous</term> people divided into factions; the stronger faction rejected their friend-

<meta>catchword: friend-</meta>

# XENOPHON.

friendship, they joined with the weaker party, whom they left as masters of all. Then they marched to the *Chalybes*, and from there to the *Tibarenes*, passing quietly through their country to *Cotyora*, a Greek town and a colony of the *Sinopeans*. Thus far the army marched on foot. The distance from the place where they fought with *Artaxerxes* to *Cotyora* was one hundred twenty-two encampments, six hundred twenty <term>parasangs</term> <note>A Persian unit of distance, roughly 3.5 miles.</note>, and ten thousand eight hundred twenty <term>furlongs</term> <note>A unit of distance equal to one-eighth of a mile.</note>; the journey took eight months.

Those of *Cotyora* refused to provide them with a market or hospitality for their sick, so the Greeks entered the town by force and took provisions—partly from *Paphlagonia* and partly from the territory of the *Cotyorites*. Consequently, the city of *Sinope*, to which *Cotyora* paid tribute, sent ambassadors to them. They complained of this treatment and threatened to join with *Corylas* and the Paphlagonians against the Greeks. To this, *Xenophon* answered that they did not fear to go to war against both if necessary; however, if they chose, they could gain the friendship of *Corylas* and the Paphlagonians just as easily as the *Sinopeans* could. Upon hearing this answer, the ambassadors became calm and promised them full friendship from the State of *Sinope*. They also promised to assist them with ships for the whole army, as it was impossible to travel by land because of the rivers *Thermodon*, *Halys*, *Iris*, and *Parthenius*.

*Xenophon* had planned to establish a colony there, but his intention was revealed by *Silanus*, a soothsayer. Consequently, the people of *Sinope* and *Heraclea* sent word to the Greeks, promising them not only a sufficient fleet but also secretly asking *Timasion*, a Greek commander, to promise the army a large sum of money to transport them to *Troas*. Xenophon (who only desired the common good) persuaded the army to accept these offers and to mutually agree not to abandon one another until they were all in safety. Although those of *Heraclea* sent the ships, they did not send the money. Because of this, *Timasion* and other commanders—fearing the soldiers’ reaction—asked *Xenophon* if the army could go to *Phasis* instead. He refused, but this led to a suspicion that he was plotting to deceive the army and bring them back to *Phasis*. However, he successfully cleared his name. Here, a general inquiry was made into all offenses committed since the death of *Cyrus*, and the offenders were punished. Some accused *Xenophon* of beating them, but all these instances were proven to be for just causes: one was for attempting to bury his sick companion alive, some were for abandoning their ranks, and others were for lying on the ground in the snow or lingering behind. Thus, all matters were quietly settled.

XENOPHON.

# CHAPTER IV.

->*The End of the Retreat.*<-

<margin>Xenophon, Book 6</margin>
As soon as their fleet was ready, the Greeks set sail for Harmene <note>Original: "Harmond"</note>, the port of Sinope. There, Chirisophus met them with some galleys sent by Anaxibius, who promised them pay as soon as they reached Greece. The army, eager for a general, entreated Xenophon with extraordinary displays of affection to accept the supreme command. Xenophon refused, either because he was dissuaded by unfavorable sacrifices <note>Xenophon frequently consulted the gods through animal sacrifice before making leadership decisions.</note> or because he was unwilling to offend the Lacedaemonians <note>Spartans</note> by sidelining Chirisophus. Chirisophus was subsequently chosen, but he was deposed shortly after for refusing to extort a large sum of money from Heraclea, a Greek city and their ally. 

When Xenophon also refused to be employed in that extortion, the army became divided. The soldiers chose ten captains from among their own ranks. Two thousand one hundred men remained with Chirisophus, while Xenophon led two thousand infantry and forty horsemen. Chirisophus went by land to meet Cleander, the governor of Byzantium, at the mouth of the River Calpas, leaving what ships he had to Xenophon. Xenophon landed on the borders of Thrace and the territory of Heraclea and marched quietly through the center of the land.

The mutineers, landing at Calpas, surprised and plundered the surrounding country. However, the Thracians rose up against them, destroyed two regiments, and besieged the hill where the rest of the mutineers were encamped. Xenophon, being informed along the way of the desperate condition of these Greeks, went directly to the place, setting fire to all flammable materials as he went. The enemy, fearing they would be attacked in the night, slipped away, as did the Greeks. Xenophon overtook them on the road to the port of Calpas, where they embraced him with great joy. 

Arriving at the harbor, the army issued a decree that it should be a capital offense for any man to propose dividing the army again, and that they should continue their journey in their original formation. Since Chirisophus had died, the former commanders were restored to their positions, and they substituted Neon in his place. When Neon went out with two thousand men to pillage the country, he was defeated by Pharnabazus, a lieutenant of the King of Persia, and lost five hundred men. The rest were rescued by Xenophon. 

Encouraged by Xenophon, the army marched through a large forest, defeating Pharnabazus, who had opposed their passage there. Cleander came to meet them, and after expressing much kindness to Xenophon and establishing a bond of hospitality <term>guest-friendship</term> with him, he departed. The army then marched through Bithynia to Chrysopolis in the territories of Chalcedon. 

<margin>Xenophon, Book 7</margin>
From there, Anaxibius, the Lacedaemonian admiral, transported them to Byzantium, where he had promised they should...

and calmed the riot, a fact specifically confirmed by Chio, an eyewitness. In this way, they were persuaded to leave the city peacefully. Once they had departed, Xenophon, wishing to return home, took his leave of the army and returned to Byzantium with Cleander. After Anaxibius was removed from his post as Admiral and subsequently insulted by Pharnabazus (at whose urging he had treated the Greeks so harshly), he asked Xenophon to return to the army to lead them to Perinthus, from where they were to be transported back into Asia. The army welcomed Xenophon with great joy. However, when they arrived at Perinthus, Aristarchus the governor would not allow them to be transported. Seuthes, King of Thrace, had invited them to help him against Medocus, the usurper of his kingdom. He offered a large sum of money to every soldier and his own daughter in marriage to Xenophon. Since they did not know where else to spend the winter, they went to join him. At supper, everyone (following custom) drank a toast to the King and gave him a gift. Xenophon, who sat next to him, stood up, took the cup, and told him,

> "that he gave the King himself and all his companions to be his faithful
> "friends and ready servants in the recovery of his kingdom."
> In this, their assistance was of great benefit to Seuthes. The army

lacked pay, so Xenophon criticized Heraclides for failing to arrange it. Heraclides then tried to turn Seuthes against him by bringing the other commanders to the King and advising them to say that, if necessary, they could lead the army on missions just as well as Xenophon. However, they all protested to Seuthes, declaring they would not serve at all without him. So, Seuthes sent for Xenophon as well, and after agreeing to proceed, they marched toward the country of the Melinophagi <term>Millet-eaters</term> as far as Salmydessus. After conquering those places, they returned to the plains of Selybria. There, they were met by Charminus the Lacedaemonian <term>Spartan</term> and Polinicus, who had been sent by Thibron <note>Original: "Thymbro"</note>. They informed the Greeks that the Spartans had planned a war against Tissaphernes and that the command had been given to Thibron, who requested this army of Greeks to assist them, promising them good pay. Seuthes willingly handed the army over to them. They sent Xenophon to demand their back pay, which he obtained only after much persistence. From there, they sailed to Lampsacus, where Euclides, a soothsayer and acquaintance of Xenophon, asked him how much gold he had brought back. Xenophon declared that he did not even have enough to pay for his journey home and had been forced to sell his horse and other personal belongings. The next day they marched to Ophrynium, where Bito and Euclides arrived to pay the army. Being friends of Xenophon, they restored to him his horse (refusing to accept any money for its redemption)

Then, marching forward by way of Adramyttium and Kertonium, near Atarna, to the plain of Caicus, they reached Pergamum, a city of Lydia. Here Hellas—the wife of Gongylus the Eretrian and mother of Gorgion and Gongylus—entertained Xenophon. Based on her information, Xenophon surprised Asidates, a wealthy Persian, capturing him along with his wife, children, and all his property. Upon returning to Pergamum, the Lacedaemonian <note>Spartan</note> captains and soldiers agreed to give him an extraordinary share of horses, oxen, and other spoils. Then Thibron arrived and, taking command of the army, joined it with the rest of the Greek forces, with which he waged war against Tissaphernes.

<margin>* This was perhaps a common name for the kings of that country, just as Ptolemy was for those of Egypt; see the chapter on Thales.</margin>
The governors of the King's provinces through which they passed were as follows: Artimas of Lydia; Artacamas of Phrygia; Mithridates of Lycaonia and Cappadocia; Syennesis of Cilicia; Dernes of Phoenicia and Arabia; Belesys of Syria and Assyria; Roparas of Babylonia; and Arbacas of Media. The Phasiani and Hesperitae (under Teribazus), the Carduchi, Chalybes, Chaldeans, Macrones, Colchi, Mossynoeci, Coeti, and Tibareni are free nations. Paphlagonia is governed by Corylas, the Bithynians by Pharnabazus, and the European Thracians by Seuthes. The total distance of the expedition (the "Ascent" and "Descent") consisted of 215 stages, 1,150 parasangs <note>A Persian unit of distance, roughly 3.5 miles.</note>, and 34,255 furlongs <note>Equivalent to Greek stadia.</note>. The time taken for the entire journey was one year and three months.

### CHAPTER V.

->**His Service to Agesilaus and His Banishment**<-

<margin>* Diogenes Laertius.</margin>
After this expedition, the defeat in Pontus, and the breach of promise by Seuthes, King of the Odrysians, Xenophon went into Asia with Agesilaus, King of the Lacedaemonians. He delivered the soldiers of Cyrus to Agesilaus for a sum of money, and it is acknowledged that he was loved by him infinitely. Emilius Probus <note>Also known as Cornelius Nepos.</note> says that he conversed intimately with him, while Cicero states that Xenophon instructed him. Plutarch affirms that, on Xenophon's advice, Agesilaus sent his sons to be educated at Sparta to learn that most excellent art: how to obey and how to command. Agesilaus crossed into Asia in the first year of the 96th Olympiad <note>396 BC.</note>. He waged war successfully against the Persians, but the following year he was called home by the Lacedaemonians to help his country, which had been invaded by the Thebans and their allies. The Persians had corrupted these allies to draw the war away from their own territory. As Xenophon was returning with Agesilaus from Asia to Boeotia, he realized the danger of the war they were entering. When he arrived at Ephesus, he left behind half of the gold <margin>* See Chapter 3 above.</margin> which he had reserved as an offering from his share of the money which the

The army was divided at Cerasus during their return from the expedition of Cyrus. Xenophon left his share of the spoils with Megabyzus, the priest of Diana <note>Artemis</note>, with the request that if he survived the dangers of the war, the money should be returned to him. If he died, it was to be consecrated to Diana, either by being made into an image dedicated to the goddess or used in whatever way Megabyzus thought would be most acceptable to her. The other half of the spoils he sent as an offering to the Athenian treasury at Delphi, * inscribing on it both his own name and that of Proxenus, his predecessor in the command of that regiment. Agesilaus, upon his return, laid waste to Boeotia and defeated the Thebans and their allies in a great battle at Coronea, * which is described in detail by Xenophon, who was present at the battle.

<margin>* Pausanias</margin>
<margin>* Laertius</margin>
During Xenophon's absence from his own country, the Athenians proclaimed a decree of banishment against him. This was because he took part against the King of Persia, who was their friend, and followed Cyrus, who had assisted the Lacedaemonians <note>Spartans</note> against Athens * by providing their general, Lysander, with money for a navy. * Ister says he was banished by a decree of Eubulus and later recalled by the same man. Laertius claims he was banished for "Laconism" <term>admiration for or imitation of Spartan culture and politics</term> upon his joining Agesilaus. Some place this decree in the third year of the 96th Olympiad, but the author of the history of Cyrus’s expedition implies that it happened before his first return from Asia, stating that before handing the army over to Thibron, Xenophon—ignorant of this decree—had intended to go home.

### CHAP. VI.

->How he lived at Scilluns and at Corinth<-

<margin>Laertius, Pausanias, Eliacs.</margin>
<margin>* Suidas.</margin>
<margin>† Laertius.</margin>
To reward him for suffering in their cause, the Lacedaemonians <note>Spartans</note> supported him at public expense. After purchasing Scilluns from the Eleans, they built a town there and granted a fine house and land to Xenophon. Consequently, he left Agesilaus and moved there, taking with him his wife, Philesia, and his two sons, Diodorus and Gryllus, who were nicknamed the "Dioscuri" <note>A reference to the mythological twin sons of Zeus, Castor and Pollux</note>. * While there, Pelopidas the Spartan sent him captives from Dardanus as a gift to serve as slaves, for Xenophon to dispose of as he pleased.

<margin>Xenophon, On the Expedition of Cyrus, Book 5.</margin>
<margin>Laertius.</margin>
<margin>* Strabo, Book 8.</margin>
Scilluns was located near Olympia, famous for the celebration of the Olympic Games. When Megabyzus came to see the Games, he returned to Xenophon the money that had been left in his custody. With this money, * and by the advice of the Oracle, Xenophon purchased a portion of land and consecrated it to Diana in a place designated by Apollo. Through this land ran the River Selinus, sharing its name with the river at Ephesus that runs by Diana’s Temple. The river was well-stocked with shellfish and other fish, and the land was filled with all kinds of wild animals for hunting.

It also details his religious devotion, including the construction of a sanctuary to Diana using funds saved from his military campaigns.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the biography of Xenophon, detailing his religious devotion and his later years in Scillus and Corinth during the conflicts between the Greek city-states.</meta>

<header>X E N O P H O N.</header>

He also built a temple, and afterwards, using the consecrated money, he offered the tithes of the land's produce to Diana <note>Artemis</note>. All the citizens and neighbors, both men and women, were invited to a feast. There, provided by the goddess's allowance, they were served bread, wine, and meat from animals that were either taken from the consecrated ground and sacrificed, or killed in hunting by the sons of Xenophon and other citizens. These hunts were prepared for the feast on the sacred ground, and from Pholoe <note>OCR: Phaloe</note> they took wild boars, goats, and stags. The place lies on the road between Lacedaemonia <note>Sparta</note> and Olympia, twenty stadia <term>an ancient Greek unit of distance, approximately 600 feet</term> from the Temple of Olympian Jupiter <note>Zeus</note>. 

Within the sacred ground were woods and hills filled with enough trees to support pigs, goats, and sheep, which also provided plentiful pasture for the pack animals of the merchants attending the feast. Around the temple was a grove of various fruit trees. The temple itself was a small-scale imitation of the great temple at Ephesus, featuring a cypress statue here that resembled the gold one there. A pillar near the temple bore this inscription: <margin>* Diogenes Laertius</margin> <margin>* Socratic Epistles, 18</margin>

-> **GROUND SACRED TO DIANA. LET THE OWNER PAY A TITHE OF HIS YEARLY INCREASE AND USE THE SURPLUS TO MAINTAIN THE TEMPLE. IF HE NEGLECTS THIS, THE GODDESS WILL SEE TO IT HERSELF.** <-

* During this retirement, Xenophon employed his time in hunting and writing histories. He invited his friends there—including, among others, Phaedo and Aristippus—who were greatly delighted with the location, the buildings, and the trees planted by the owner's own hand.

<margin>* Diogenes Laertius</margin>
* At length, a war broke out between the Eleans and the Lacedaemonians. The Eleans invaded Scillus with a great army and, before the Lacedaemonians could come to their relief, they seized Xenophon’s house and lands. His sons, along with a few servants, escaped privately to Lepreum. Xenophon went first to Elis, then to Lepreum to join his sons, and finally with them to Corinth, where he took a house and lived for the rest of his life. 

During this time, the Argives, Arcadians, and Thebans joined forces against the Lacedaemonians and had almost defeated them. At that point, the Athenians passed a public decree (<margin>* Greek History, final book</margin> * mentioned by Xenophon) to come to their aid. Xenophon sent his sons on the expedition to Athens to fight for the Lacedaemonians; for, as Diocles affirms, they had been educated at Sparta in the discipline of that city.

This enmity ended in a great battle at Mantinea in the second year of the 104th Olympiad. Diodorus, without performing any memorable feat, escaped safely and later had a son named after his brother. Gryllus was positioned opposite the Theban cavalry. Although the Thebans had gained the advantage through the bravery of their general, Epaminondas, a resolute company of Spartan cavalry broke in upon him,

<margin>
Diogenes Laertius; Aelian, *Various History*, 3.3; Saint Jerome, *Letter to Nepotian on the Death of Heliodorus*; Stobaeus, *Sermon* 106.

* Diogenes Laertius, citing Aristotle.
</margin>
One of his sons was Gryllus, who slew Epaminondas with his own hand, as Pausanias affirms was attested both by the Athenians and the Thebans. Pausanias adds that he had seen at Athens a picture of the battle at Mantinea confirming the same, and that at Mantinea a pillar was erected with a statue of Gryllus on horseback. In this noble action, Gryllus lost his life. The news of his death reached Xenophon at Corinth while he was sacrificing, crowned with a garland. As soon as he heard his son was slain, he took off his garland and laid it aside. Then, asking in what manner he died, it was answered that he died fighting bravely in the midst of his enemies, and after having slain many of them, he fell at last himself. Upon hearing this, Xenophon took up his garland again, placed it upon his head, and proceeded with the sacrifice without shedding a single tear, only saying,
> *I knew that I had fathered a mortal;*
and calling the gods to whom he sacrificed to witness that the virtue of his son gave him more contentment than his death gave him sorrow. Innumerable were the epitaphs and praises written about Gryllus to please Xenophon, from which it may be gathered how greatly he was esteemed.

<margin>* Athenaeus, *The Deipnosophists*, Book 10.</margin>
That he made a visit to Dionysius, the Tyrant of Sicily (though at what time is uncertain), is implied by Athenaeus, who relates that while at a feast of the Tyrant's, being compelled by the cup-bearer to drink, he called the Tyrant by name: 
> “What is the matter, Dionysius?” he said. “Your cook, though excellent in his art, does not force us to eat against our inclination; why then do you force us to drink?”

## CHAPTER VII.

### His Death, Person, and Virtues.

<margin>
* Diogenes Laertius.
* Lucian, *On Longevity* <note>original: "De longæv."</note>.
</margin>
Xenophon, being full of years (which according to Lucian exceeded ninety), died at Corinth in the first year of the hundred and fifth Olympiad <note>360 BC</note>, when Callidemus (or Callimedes) was Archon, at the time when Philip, son of Amyntas, began his reign in Macedonia. He had a noble, modest look and long, thick hair; he was handsome beyond expression (to use the words of Laertius). He was adroit in everything, particularly devoted to horses and hunting, and skillful in military tactics, as his writings attest. He was devout, a great lover of sacrifices, and skillful in interpreting them. He was an exact imitator of Socrates and temperate in his habits, as appears from his saying:
<margin>
* Athenaeus, *The Deipnosophists*, 4.
* Diogenes Laertius.
</margin>
> *It is pleasant to eat herbs when hungry, and to drink water when thirsty.* 
He was so candid and honorable that when he might have stolen the writings of Thucydides, which lay concealed, he chose instead to publish them with honor <note>Xenophon is credited with publishing Thucydides' *History of the Peloponnesian War* and completing it with his own *Hellenica*</note>.

<margin>* Eunapius, *Introduction* <note>original: "Proœm."</note>.</margin>
In short, he was a person perfect in every way, as well for action as for contemplation. Xenophon (says Eunapius) was the only man among all the philosophers who adorned philosophy with his words and his deeds.

# XENOPHON

and actions; he describes moral virtue in his discourses and writings. In his actions he was exceptional; regarding his conduct, he was a most excellent General. Alexander would not have been great if Xenophon had not said that even the incidental <term>original: "perfunctory," meaning minor or passing</term> actions of valiant people ought to be recorded.

<margin>* Laertius.</margin>
He was the first who committed the disputes of his master, Socrates, to writing, and did so with great fidelity, not inserting digressions of his own as Plato did. For that reason, as 
<margin>* Book 14.</margin>
Aulus Gellius observes, Xenophon accused him of falsehood. That there was a great hostility between these two is affirmed by the same author, who, as proof of it, alleges that neither of them names the other in any of their writings. 
<margin>* Concerning Greek Historians, Book 1.</margin>
Vossius only observes that Xenophon mentions Plato once in his *Memorabilia* <note>original: "ἀπομνημονεύματα" (apomnēmoneumata)</note>, a fact overlooked by Aulus Gellius. This hostility is further acknowledged by 
<margin>* Book 3.</margin>
<margin>* The Deipnosophists, 11.</margin>
Athenaeus and Laertius, and confirmed by the Epistle of Xenophon to Aeschines, wherein he condemns Plato. He argues that, not being satisfied with the doctrine of Socrates, Plato went to the Pythagoreans in Italy and to the Egyptian priests—evidence of a mind not faithful to Socrates. That he was at odds with Aristippus as well is evidenced by his writings.

<margin>* Laertius, Life of Aristippus.</margin>
Laertius has two epigrams concerning him, the first upon his going with Cyrus into Upper Asia:

> ->*Great Xenophon at once made two ascents,*<-
> ->*To Asia in person, and to Heaven by fame:*<-
> ->*His style and action (lasting monuments)*<-
> ->*Lay to Socratic wisdom equal claim.*<-

The other upon his banishment:

> ->*The Cecropians <note>Athenians</note>, noble Xenophon,*<-
> ->*Banished you from their land because you aided Cyrus;*<-
> ->*But strangers proved far kinder than your own:*<-
> ->*What Athens owed you was by Corinth paid.*<-

Laertius reckons seven men named Xenophon: this one is the first; the second an Athenian, brother to Nicostratus, author of the *Theses*—among many other things, he wrote the lives of Pelopidas and Epaminondas; the third, a physician of Cos; the fourth, writer of the *History of Hannibal*; the fifth, writer of *Fabulous Monsters*; the sixth, a sculptor of Paros; the seventh, an old Comic Poet. Suidas reckons three more: one of Antioch, the second of Ephesus, the third of Cyprus. These were historians, or rather writers of romances; that of the first called *Babylonica*, the second *Ephesiaca* in ten books, and the third *Cypriaca*—the story of Cinyras, Myrrha, and Adonis.

---

## CHAP. VIII.

->*His writings.*<-

<margin>* Brutus & On the Orator, book 2.
* The Orator.
* Cicero, The Orator.
* Life of Sophocles.</margin>
Dionysius of Halicarnassus says that *Xenophon* was a studious emulator of *Herodotus*, both in his vocabulary and language. His style (according to *Cicero*) is *soft* and *sweet* <note>original: "melle dulcior" (sweeter than honey)</note>, far different from the noise of Orators in the Forum. In his voice, *the Muses seem to speak*, for which reason he was nicknamed the *Attic Muse*; or according to others, the *Attic Bee*, a title formerly conferred on *Sophocles*. His style and manner of writing are discussed at length by *Aristides Adrianensis* in a specific treatise, which is mistakenly attributed to *Hermogenes*.

<margin>* Cicero, Tusculan Disputations 3.
* On Old Age.</margin>
The books of *Xenophon* (which *Scipio Africanus* always kept at hand, and *Cicero* advises reading as very profitable in many things) numbered forty, according to *Laertius*. Different people categorize them differently, but these are the general titles:

<margin>* Book 15.</margin>
*Cyropaedia* <note>original: "Κύρου παιδεία"</note>; the *Life and Discipline of Cyrus* (as *Cicero* translates it) in eight books. It was written *not according to historical truth* (though *Diodorus Siculus* seems to treat it as such) *but as the model of a just empire* <note>original: "non ad historiae fidem... sed ad effigiem iusti imperii"</note>.
<margin>* Gratiarum actio.</margin>
Because of this, *Ausonius* says, *in relating the virtues of Cyrus, he has provided a wish rather than a history, describing not what he was, but what he ought to have been.*

<margin>* Annals.</margin>
*Anabasis* <note>original: "Κύρου ἀνάβασις"</note>; the *Expedition of Cyrus the Younger into Asia*, in seven books. As *Laertius* observes, each of these has a prologue, while the work as a whole has none. *Masius* suspects that *Xenophon* was not the author of this book; the Bishop of *Armagh* <note>James Ussher</note> ascribes it to *Themistogenes*, though it is recognized as *Xenophon's* by *Plutarch*, *Cicero*, *Dionysius of Halicarnassus*, *Hermogenes*, *Laertius*, *Athenaeus*, and others.

*Hellenika* <note>original: "Ἑλληνικὰ"</note>; the *Greek History*, in seven books, continuing where *Thucydides* left off. The same was done by *Theopompus*, but he only went as far as sixteen years, while *Xenophon* covered forty-eight.

*Agesilaus*, of which piece *Cicero* says, *that although Agesilaus would not allow his picture or statue to be made, this book alone surpasses all pictures or images in his praise.*

<margin>* Laertius.</margin>
The *Constitution of the Lacedaemonians*, and the *Constitution of the Athenians*, which *Demetrius* denies were written by *Xenophon*.

The *Apology of Socrates*, and the *Memorabilia of Socrates*, which is perhaps that history of philosophers mentioned by *Suidas*.

<margin>* On Duties 2.</margin>
*Oeconomicus* <note>Economics</note>, the last book of the memorable discourses, with which *Cicero* was so delighted that in his younger years he translated it.

*Symposium*, set in the fourth year of the eighty-ninth <term>Olympiad</term>

*Hiero, or, On Kingship.*
*The Accounts of Revenues.*
*On Horses.*
*On Horsemanship.*

Besides these works which still survive, *Xenophon* seems to have written other things; *Valerius Maximus* and *Pliny* both cite his *Periplus* <term>a manuscript detailing a coastal voyage or circumnavigation</term>.

There is a treatise titled *Equivocations* attributed to *Xenophon's* name, but it was forged and forced upon the world by *Annius* <note>Giovanni Nanni, also known as Annius of Viterbo, was a 15th-century friar notorious for publishing forged "ancient" texts</note>.
