<page-num>110</page-num>
<header>XENOPHON.</header>

# XENOPHON'S EPISTLES.

->* To Aeschines.<-

### Epistle 1.

<margin>* Stobaeus, *Sermones*</margin>
<margin>* Referring to Plato, who added much of his own to the discourses of Socrates and traveled to Egypt, Italy, and Sicily.</margin>
While meeting with *Hermogenes*, among other things I asked him what philosophy you followed. He answered that it was the same as that of *Socrates*. I admired you for this inclination when you lived in *Athens*, and I continue to admire you now for your constancy, which surpasses that of other students of wisdom. To me, the greatest proof of your virtue is your devotion to that man—if we may indeed call the life of *Socrates* "mortal." Everyone knows that there are divine beings over us; we worship them as beings who exceed us in power. What they are is neither easy to discover nor lawful to investigate. It is not the place of servants to examine the nature and actions of their masters; their duty is only to obey them. More importantly, those who busy themselves with things belonging to humanity deserve more admiration; conversely, more trouble comes to those who seek glory in vain and inappropriate subjects.

For when, *Aeschines*, did anyone ever hear *Socrates* discourse about the heavens, or advise his scholars to pursue mathematical demonstrations? We know he understood music no further than what the ear perceives. But he was always discussing something excellent with his friends: what constitutes fortitude, justice, and other virtues. These he called the proper concerns of mankind; other things, he said, men could not achieve, or else they were akin to fables—the kind of ridiculous things taught by arrogant professors of wisdom. Nor did he only teach this; his practice matched his teaching. I have written about this at length elsewhere, and I hope you will find it pleasant to read, even though you are already familiar with it. 

Let those who are not satisfied with what *Socrates* delivered give up based on this conviction, or else confine themselves to what is probable. While he lived, the Deity testified to his wisdom; after he died, his murderers could find no atonement through repentance. But these "extraordinary" persons <note>referring to Plato and his followers</note> are obsessed with *Egypt* and the vast learning of *Pythagoras*. This unnecessary study proves their inconstancy toward *Socrates*, as does their love of tyrants and their preference for the luxury of a Sicilian table over a frugal life.

<summary>Xenophon writes to Aeschines, praising his loyalty to the practical and moral teachings of Socrates while criticizing others—specifically Plato—for straying into abstract sciences and foreign luxuries.</summary>
<keywords>Xenophon, Aeschines, Socrates, Hermogenes, Plato, Pythagoras, moral philosophy, Athens, Socratic method, virtue</keywords>

--- Page 287 ---
<meta>This page continues the collection of Xenophon's epistles, moving from the letter to Aeschines on page 110 to letters addressed to Crito, Sotira, and Lamprocles regarding wealth, death, and virtue.</meta>

<header>XENOPHON.</header>

## To Crito.
### Epistle 2.

<margin>Stobaeus, *Sermones* 201.</margin>
*Socrates* often told us that those who provide much wealth for their children but neglect to improve them through virtue are like those who feed their horses well but never train them in <term>horsemanship</term><note>The original uses "the manage," referring to the manège or the art of training and handling horses.</note>. By this means, their horses are in better physical condition but are worse for actual service; the merit of a horse consists not in being fat, but in being useful in war. In the same way, those who purchase lands for their children but neglect their personal development are in error. Their possessions will be of great value, but the children themselves will be worth nothing, whereas the owner ought to be more honorable than his estate. Whosoever, therefore, raises his son well gives him much, even if he leaves him little. It is the mind that makes a person great or small. To the good, whatever they have seems sufficient; to the unrefined, it seems too little. You leave your children no more than necessity requires, which they—being well-educated—will consider plentiful. The ignorant, though free from present trouble, have no less fear for the future.

---

## To Sotira.
### Epistle 3.

<margin>* Stobaeus, *Sermones* 891.</margin>
Death, in my opinion, is neither good nor evil, but simply the end of life. It is not the same for everyone, for as people are stronger or weaker from birth, their lifespans are unequal. Sometimes death is hastened by good or evil causes. And again: <margin>* Stobaeus, *Sermones* 278.</margin> It is not fitting to grieve so much over death, knowing that birth is the beginning of man's pilgrimage and death is the end. He died as all men must (no matter how unwillingly); but to die well is the mark of a willing and well-educated person. Happy was *Gryllus* <note>Gryllus was Xenophon’s son, who died heroically at the Battle of Mantinea.</note>, and happy is anyone else who chooses the most virtuous life rather than the longest, even though his life (as it pleased God) was short.

---

## To Lamprocles.
### Epistle 4.

<margin>Stobaeus.</margin>
You must first accept the excellent assertion of *Socrates*: that *riches are to be measured by their use*. He did not call large possessions "riches," but only as much as is necessary. He advised us not to be deceived in judging this; those who have enough he called truly rich, while he called the rest poor—suffering from an incurable poverty of the mind, rather than of their estate.

<sig>Ttt</sig>
<meta>catchword: Epistle</meta>

<meta>This page continues the collection of Xenophon's epistles, following his letters to Crito, Sotira, and Lamprocles concerning virtue and the nature of death.</meta>

<header>XENOPHON.</header>

### Epistle 5.

<margin>
* Socratic Epistle 18.
* <note>Leo Allatius, a 17th-century scholar, organized these letters differently.</note>
</margin>

**T**hose who write in praise of my son **Gryllus** <note>Gryllus was Xenophon's son who died heroically at the Battle of Mantinea in 362 BC.</note> did as they ought; and you likewise do well in writing to us of the actions of **Socrates**. We ought not only to endeavor to be good ourselves, but also to praise him who lived chastely, piously, and justly, and to blame fortune and those who plotted against him—who before long will receive their punishment. The Lacedaemonians <note>Spartans.</note> are much incensed by it (for the ill news has reached here already) and they reproach our people, saying they are mad once again for being persuaded to put to death the man whom the **Pythia** <note>The Priestess of the Oracle at Delphi.</note> declared the wisest of men.

If any of **Socrates'** friends lack the things I have sent, give me notice and I will help them, for it is just and honest to do so. You do well in keeping **Aeschines** with you, as you mentioned in your letter. I have a plan to collect the sayings and actions of **Socrates**, which will be his best <term>apology</term> <note>A formal defense or justification of one's conduct.</note>, both now and for the future—not in the court where the Athenians are judges, but for all who consider the virtue of the man. If we did not write this freely, it would be a sin against friendship and the truth. 

Just now, a work of **Plato’s** on that subject came into my hands, wherein the name of **Socrates** is used, and some of his discourses are included which are not unpleasant. * But we must admit that we never heard, nor can we commit to writing, anything of that specific kind; for we are not poets as he is, even though he renounces poetry. For amidst his entertainments with beautiful people, he affirmed that there was no poem of his in existence, but only those of a **Socrates** made young and handsome. Farewell to you both, who are dearest to me.

---

### Epistle 6.

<margin>
* Socratic Epistle 21.
</margin>

**I**ntending to celebrate the feast of **Diana** <note>Artemis.</note>, to whom we have built a temple, we sent to invite you here. If all of you could come, it would be for the best; otherwise, if you send such people as you can conveniently spare to assist at our sacrifice, you will do us a favor. **Aristippus** was here, and before him **Phaedo**, who were both much pleased with the situation and the structure of the place, but above all with the plantation which I have made with my own hands. The place is well-stocked with animals suitable for hunting, which the goddess enjoys. Let us rejoice and give thanks to her, for she preserved me from the King of the Barbarians <note>Artaxerxes II, the Persian King.</note>, and afterwards in **Pontus** and **Thrace** from even greater evils, even when we thought we were out of the enemy's reach. Though you may not come, I am still obliged to write to you. I have composed some memorials of **Socrates**; when they are per-

<meta>catchword: per-</meta>

<summary>Xenophon discusses the legacy of Socrates, his intention to write the "Memorabilia" as a defense of his teacher, and his lifestyle at his estate in Scillus where he built a temple to Diana.</summary>

<header>XENOPHON</header>

...perfect, you shall have them. *Aristippus* and *Phædo* did not disapprove of them. Greet *Simon* the leather-dresser in my name, and commend him for continuing Socratic discourses; he has not been diverted from philosophy by poverty or his trade, unlike some others who refuse to know or admire such discourses and their effects.

---

<margin>* Socratic Epistle 19.</margin>
### *Epistle* 7.

Come to us, dear friend, for we have now finished the Temple of *Diana* <note>The Temple of Artemis at Scillus, which Xenophon built on his estate.</note>, a magnificent structure. The grounds are planted with trees and consecrated, and what remains of the estate will be sufficient to maintain us. For as *Socrates* said: if circumstances are not fit for us, we will fit ourselves to them. I am writing to *Gryllus*, my son and your friend, to provide for your needs; I wrote to *Gryllus* because you have expressed such kindness toward him since he was a little one.

---

### To Xantippe.

<margin>* Socratic Epistle 21.</margin>
### *Epistle* 8.

I delivered six measures of meal, eight <term>drachmas</term> <note>An ancient Greek silver coin.</note>, and a new garment to *Euphron* of *Megara* for your use this winter. Accept them and know that *Euclid* and *Terpsion* are exceedingly good, honest persons, who are very affectionate toward you and *Socrates*. If your sons desire to come to me, do not hinder them, for the journey to *Megara* is neither long nor difficult. 

Please stop weeping; it may do you harm, but it cannot help. Remember what *Socrates* said, and follow his practice and precepts. In grieving, you will only wrong yourself and your children. They are the children of *Socrates*, and we are obligated not only to maintain them but to preserve ourselves for their sakes. Otherwise, if you or I, or any other who ought to look after his children after the death of *Socrates*, should fail, they might lack a guardian to maintain and protect them. I study to live for them, which you will not do unless you take care of yourself.

<margin>* The reason is manifest from Xenophon and Laertius, who describe him as such: Xenophon calls him <note>original: "μανίωδης" (maniodes)</note> "frenzied" or "mad" (Apology of Socrates) in all things except for his affection for Socrates. Leo Allatius is much perplexed concerning this title.</margin>

Grief is one of those things which are opposite to life, for the living are prejudiced by it. *Apollodorus*, nicknamed the *Soft*, <margin>*</margin> and *Dion* praise you for refusing to accept anything from anyone, claiming that you are rich. That is well done; for as long as I and your other friends are able to maintain you, you shall need no one else. Be of good courage, *Xantippe*; lose nothing of the spirit of *Socrates*, knowing how great that man was. Think upon his life rather than his death—though to those who truly consider it, even his death will appear noble and excellent. Farewell.

<keywords>Xenophon, Socrates, Xantippe, Temple of Diana, Scillus, Gryllus, Megara, Apollodorus, Socratic Epistles, grief, friendship</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the epistles of Xenophon and transitions to the section on Aeschines. It features a letter from Xenophon to Cebes and Simmias regarding his literary caution and his reverence for the memory of Socrates.</meta>

<header>XENOPHON.</header>

### To Cebes and Simmias
## Epistle 9.

<margin>
Socratic Epistle 28.
</margin>
It is commonly said that nothing is richer than a poor man; I find this to be true in myself. Though I do not have much, I seem to possess a great deal as long as you, my friends, take care of me. It is generous of you to provide for me whenever I write. Regarding my *Commentaries* <note>Likely referring to the *Memorabilia* or his other Socratic writings.</note>, there are none that I am comfortable having others see in my absence, as I declared in your presence at the house where *Euclid* stayed. I know, dear friends, that once a piece of writing is shared with many people, it cannot be taken back. *Plato*, though he is not there, is greatly admired throughout *Italy* and *Sicily* for his treatises; however, we cannot be convinced that they deserve such study. I am not only careful about losing the honor due to learning, but I am also protective of *Socrates*, fearing his virtue might be harmed by my poor account of it. I believe it is the same thing to slander someone as it is to fail to praise those we write about to the fullest extent. This is my fear (*Cebes* and *Simmias*) for now, until my judgment is better informed. Farewell.

---

## AESCHINES.

<summary>In this final epistle, Xenophon expresses his reliance on his friends' generosity and his hesitation to publish his writings for fear of failing to adequately represent the virtue of Socrates. He also expresses skepticism toward the popularity of Plato's works in Italy and Sicily.</summary>

<keywords>Xenophon, Cebes, Simmias, Socrates, Plato, Euclid, Socratic Epistles, literary reputation, virtue, Italy, Sicily</keywords>

--- Page 291 ---
<meta>This page begins the biography of Aeschines, transitioning from the preceding section on Xenophon. It details Aeschines' humble origins, his devotion to Socrates, and his eventual journey to the court of Dionysius in Sicily.</meta>

# AESCHINES.

## CHAPTER I.

### *His life.*

<margin>Diogenes Laertius calls him a "stuffer of Lucanian sausages" <note>original: "Laert. Lucanicarum fartoris."</note></margin>
AESCHINES was the son of *Charinus*, the sausage-maker <note>original: ὁ ἀλλαντοποιὸς</note>, or, as *Plato* and others say, the son of *Lysanias*. He was an *Athenian* of the *Sphettian* Tribe. He was very industrious from his childhood. Addressing himself to *Socrates*, he said to him, **"I am poor, and have nothing to give you but myself."** **"Do you not know,"** answered *Socrates*, **"that you have made me a rich present?"** He was the most diligent of all his students and never left his side; because of this, *Socrates* said that only he, the sausage-maker <note>original: ὁ ἀλλαντοποιὸς</note>, truly valued him. He was not liked by *Plato* or *Aristippus*. *Idomeneus* says it was Aeschines who advised *Socrates* to escape from prison, an act which *Plato* attributes to *Crito*. Being very poor, *Socrates* told him to take some of his dialogues and sell them for money. When *Aristippus* read them at *Megara*, he suspected their origin and mocked him, saying, **"How did you come by these stolen works?"** <term>Plagiary</term> <note>Used here to mean plagiarized or stolen writings.</note>

<margin>Plutarch, *On the Control of Anger* <note>original: "Plut. de ira cohib."</note></margin>
On another occasion, when *Aristippus* had a falling out with him, someone asked what had become of their friendship. He answered, "It is asleep, but I will wake it." Meeting *Aeschines*, he said, **"Do I seem so insignificant and unfortunate to you that I do not even deserve to be corrected?"** **"It is no wonder,"** answered *Aeschines*, **"if your nature, which exceeds mine in everything, is the first to find out what is appropriate."**

<margin>Diogenes Laertius; Philostratus, *Life of Apollonius*; and from him Suidas <note>original: "Laert. Philostrat. vit. Apollon; and from him Suidas."</note></margin>
Driven by poverty, he went to Sicily to visit *Dionysius* the **Tyrant** at the time when *Plato* and *Aristippus* were there. Since *Plato* was out of favor with the **Tyrant**, he took the opportunity to get back into his good graces by introducing *Aeschines*.

<margin>Plutarch, *How to Tell a Flatterer from a Friend* <note>original: "Plutarch. de adulat. & amici discrim."</note></margin>
He requested that he might be allowed to speak with him, which the Tyrant granted, assuming he would say something in his own defense. As soon as he came into his presence, he began like this: **"If you knew of anyone, Dionysius, who came with a hostile intent to do you harm, even if he failed in the attempt, would you allow him to leave unpunished?"** **"Not at all,"** answered *Dionysius*, **"for not only the evil actions but also the designs of enemies deserve to be punished."** **"Then,"** *Plato* replied, **"if any man should come here with the intent to do you a service, and you did not give him leave, should you neglect and despise him?"** *Dionysius* asked whom he meant. **"Aeschines,"** he said, **"a person of as much integrity as any of Socrates's friends,"**

<sig>Uuu</sig>
<meta>catchword: able</meta>

<summary>This page introduces Aeschines of Sphettus, a devoted but impoverished follower of Socrates who allegedly authored Socratic dialogues. It describes his rivalry with other philosophers and his arrival at the court of Dionysius the Tyrant in Sicily under Plato's sponsorship.</summary>
<keywords>Aeschines, Socrates, Plato, Aristippus, Dionysius the Tyrant, Sicily, Socratic dialogues, Sphettian Tribe, Charinus, Lysanias</keywords>

--- Page 292 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Aeschines the Socratic, detailing his time at the court of Dionysius in Sicily, his return to Athens, and the catalog of his written works.</meta>

<header>AESCHINES.</header>

"...able to reform those with whom he converses, but who is neglected despite having undertaken a great sea voyage to come here and discuss philosophy with you." *Dionysius* was so pleased by this that he embraced *Plato*, admiring his candor and greatness of spirit, and entertained *Aeschines* bountifully and magnificently. 

Thus says *Plutarch*; but *Laertius* says that when *Aeschines* arrived there, he was despised by *Plato* and recommended by *Aristippus*. The latter account is confirmed by the *Socratic Epistles*. He shared some dialogues with *Dionysius* and was rewarded by him, living with him until *Dionysius* was deposed and *Dion* took over the kingdoms. 

He then returned to *Athens*, where he did not dare to profess his philosophy because the reputations of *Plato* and *Aristippus* were so great. He
<margin>Diogenes Laertius; Hesychius Illustris</margin>
taught and took money privately. At last, he applied himself to writing orations for the forum, in which *Timon* says he was very persuasive. *Lysias* wrote one oration in response to him, titled <term>On Slander</term> <note>original: "περὶ συκοφαντίας"</note>, or according to *Athenaeus*, <term>On the Debts of Aeschines the Socratic</term> <note>original: "περὶ Αἰσχίνου τοῦ Σωκρατικοῦ χρείας"</note>. In this, he slanders him for many things which are not at all probable—such as patronizing an unjust cause and borrowing without intending to pay it back—as well as for pretending to sell ointments contrary to the laws of *Solon* and the precepts of *Socrates*, and for harming *Hermaeus*, his wife, and his children; see *Athenaeus*. 

*Aristotle*, nicknamed <term>the Fish</term> <note>original: "ἰχθύς"</note>, was his intimate friend. *Laertius* counts eight people of this name: the first, this *Aeschines* the philosopher; the second wrote on rhetoric; the third was an orator and contemporary of *Demosthenes*; the fourth was an Arcadian and a student of *Isocrates*; the fifth was from *Mytilene*, nicknamed <term>the Scourge of Orators</term> <note>original: "Rhetoromastix"</note>; the sixth was a Neapolitan Academic philosopher and disciple of *Melanthius* of Rhodes; the seventh was a Milesian who wrote on politics and was a friend of *Cicero*; and the eighth was a sculptor.

### CHAP. II.
->His writings.<-

<margin>Laertius; Athenaeus</margin>
He wrote dialogues, orations, and epistles. *Athenaeus* affirms that through the first of these, he gained a great reputation for temperance, humanity, and integrity. *Menedemus* accuses him of claiming ownership of many dialogues of *Socrates* which he obtained from *Xanthippe*. *Panaetius* believes them to be his own, not forgeries. 

Those which express the Socratic style, says *Laertius*, are seven in number. The first is *Miltiades*, written in a simpler style. The second is *Callias*, for which he is blamed by *Athenaeus* for treating of the enmity between *Callias* and his father, and for deriding *Anaxagoras* and *Prodicus* through their students *Theramenes*, *Philoxenus*, and *Ariphrades*. The third is *Rhinon*. The fourth is *Aspasia*, cited by *Athenaeus* and *Harpocration*.
<margin>Book 14; Book 5</margin>
The fifth is *Alcibiades*, also cited by *Athenaeus*. The sixth is *Axiochus*, in which *Athenaeus* says he slanders *Alcibiades* as being addicted to wine and women; a detail which is not found in that...

<meta>catchword: Axiochus</meta>

<header>AESCHINES.</header>

<margin>* Lib. 5, on which see Casaubon, chapter 20.</margin>
The *Axiochus*, which survives among the spurious Platonic dialogues, argues (contrary to the opinion of *Vossius*) that it is not the same work. The last, *Telauges*, the purpose of which was a satirical mockery of the vices of that person, as appears from *Demetrius Phalereus* and *Athenaeus*.

<margin>* Laertius</margin>
* There were seven other dialogues, called <term>headless</term> <note>original: "ἀκέφαλοι"</note>, which were attributed to *Aeschines*. They were very loosely written and did not sufficiently reflect Socratic rigor; because of this, *Pisistratus* the *Athenian* denied they were his. *Perseus* says the majority were written by *Pasiphon* of *Eretria* and falsely mixed in with the dialogues of *Aeschines*. Their names (according to *Suidas*) were *Phaedo*, *Polyaenus*, *Draco*, *Eryxias* (perhaps the one that still exists), *On Virtue*, *Erasistratus*, and *The Scythians*.

<margin>* Laertius</margin>
<margin>* Epistle to Julia Augusta</margin>
* His orations provided full testimony of his mastery of rhetoric. To confirm this, *Laertius* cites the one written in defense of the father of *Phaecus* the general, and those in which he primarily imitated *Gorgias* of *Leontini*. Of these, *Philostratus* mentions the one concerning *Thargelia*.

Regarding his *Epistles*, *Laertius* mentions one addressed to *Dionysius* the Tyrant as still existing in his time. Another appears under his name among the *Socratic Epistles*, which reads as follows:

<margin>* Epistle 23.</margin>
> As soon as I arrived at Syracuse, Aristippus met me in the forum, and taking me by the hand, led me immediately to Dionysius. He said to him, "Dionysius, if a man should come here to instill folly in you, would he not be aiming to harm you?" When Dionysius agreed, Aristippus continued, "What then would you do to him?" Dionysius answered, "The worst I could." "But if someone," Aristippus said, "should come to improve your wisdom, would he not be aiming for your benefit?" When Dionysius acknowledged this, Aristippus said, "Behold then Aeschines, one of Socrates’s disciples, has come here to instruct you. He aims for your benefit; therefore, bestow on him the rewards you admit are due to such a person." I interrupted him, saying, "Dionysius, Aristippus shows an admirable friendship in this introduction, but we possess only as much wisdom as keeps us from misleading those with whom we speak." Dionysius was pleased by this, commended Aristippus, and promised to provide what he had admitted was due to me. He heard our *Alcibiades* and seemed delighted with it, requesting that if we had any other dialogues, we should send them to him—which we promised to do. Therefore, dear friends, we intend to be with you shortly. While I read, Plato was present (which I almost forgot to tell you) and whispered something on my behalf privately to Dionysius because of Aristippus. As soon as Aristippus had left, Plato told me that he never spoke freely when that man (naming Aristippus) was present; but as for what he said to Dionysius concerning you, I refer you to him. The next day, in the garden, Dionysius confirmed his words about me with many playful remarks (for they were nothing more). I advised Aristippus and Plato to stop their rivalry because of their public reputations; for we shall look most ridiculous if our actions do not match our profession.

<header>AESCHINES.</header>

## CRITO.

<margin>* Suidas</margin>
**C**RITO was an Athenian student of Socrates, whom he loved so dearly that he never allowed him to lack necessities; more has already been said about this in the life of Socrates. <margin>* Xenophon</margin> Being much troubled and sued by those who had received no injury from him, but who took advantage of his quiet disposition—as he would sooner part with money for no reason than go to law—Socrates advised him to hire someone of the same busy, troublesome temperament to keep the others away. In following this counsel, Crito chose Archidemus, an excellent but poor lawyer. Being indebted to Crito for his gifts and kindness, Archidemus eagerly prosecuted all those who molested not only Crito but any of his friends. Crito wrote seventeen dialogues comprised in one volume, listed by Laertius as follows: *That the good are not made so by learning; On having most; What is expedient, or the Politician; On honesty; On wickedness; On security; On Law; On Divinity; On arts; On conversation; On wisdom; Protagoras, or the Political Man; On letters; On Poetry; On what is honest; On learning; On knowing or science: what it is to know.* He also wrote an *Apology for Socrates*.

<margin>* Suidas</margin>
He had four sons: Critobulus, Hermogenes, Epigenes, and Ctesippus, all of whom were students of Socrates and have been mentioned previously.

<margin>* Laertius</margin>
Suidas identifies three more people of this name: one wrote Getic stories; the second was from Pieria, a city in Macedonia; and the third was from Naxos; both of the latter were historians.

---

## SIMON.

<margin>* Laertius</margin>
**S**IMON was an Athenian and a leather-dresser. When Socrates came to his shop and held disputes there, Simon committed to writing all that he remembered of them; because of this, his dialogues were called <term>leather dialogues</term> <note>original: "σκυτικοὶ"</note>. There were thirty-three dialogues in all, collected in one volume: *On Gods; On the good; On the honest; What is honest; On the just (the first and second); On virtue (that it cannot be taught); On fortitude (the first, second, and third); On law; On the people; On honor; On Poetry; On health; On love; On Philosophy; On knowledge; On music; On Poetry; What is honest; On doctrine; On disputation; On judgment; On that which is; On number; On diligence; On labor; On avarice; On boasting; On honesty (or according to others, On Counsel); On discourse; On expedients; On doing ill.* He is reported to be the first who used the Socratic style of discourse. When Pericles promised that if Simon would come to live with him, he would lack nothing, Simon answered that he would not sell his freedom of speech. Among the Socratic Epistles, there is one still extant under his name, written as an answer to Aristippus.

<summary>This page provides biographical sketches and bibliographies for Crito and Simon, two devoted followers of Socrates, detailing their personal loyalty to their teacher and listing the numerous Socratic dialogues they authored.</summary>

<keywords>Crito, Simon the leather-dresser, Socrates, Archidemus, Athenian philosophy, Socratic dialogues, Archidemus, Pericles, Diogenes Laertius</keywords>

--- Page 295 ---
<meta>This page concludes the biography and correspondence of Simon the leather-dresser and provides brief biographical and bibliographical entries for other original followers of Socrates: Glauco, Simmias, and Cebes.</meta>

<header>GLAUCO, &c.</header>

<margin>* Perhaps <note>original: "χλευάζεν"</note> mock rather than <note>original: "θαυμάζεν"</note> admire.</margin>
<margin>* <note>original: "παιδείαν"</note> education, which Allatius translates as <note>original: "institutionem"</note> instruction.</margin>
> I hear that you mock our learning to Dionysius; I confess I am a leather-dresser, and I work at that trade to the reproof of indiscreet persons who think to follow Socrates yet live luxuriously. As for your children, Antisthenes will correct them, to whom you write scoffing at my manner of life; but enough of this mirth; only be mindful of me and of thirst <note>In Socratic philosophy, "thirst" often refers to the discipline of self-denial and physical endurance.</note>; these things contribute much to the wife.

### GLAUCO.

<margin>* Laertius</margin>
GLAUCO was an Athenian. He wrote nine dialogues, comprised in one book: *Phidylus, Euripides, Amyntichus, Euthias, Lysithides, Aristophanes, Cephalus, Anaxiphemus,* and *Menexenus*. There are thirty-two more falsely attributed to him.

### SIMMIAS.

<margin>* Laertius</margin>
SIMMIAS was a Theban. He wrote twenty-three dialogues in one book: *On Wisdom, On Reasoning, On Music, On Verse, On Fortitude, On Philosophy, On Truth, On Letters, On Doctrine, On Art, On Conduct, On Decency, On Choice and Avoidance, On Friendship, On Knowledge, On the Soul, On Right Living, On Possibility, On Wealth, On Life, What is Honorable, On Industry,* and *On Love.*

### CEBES.

<margin>* Laertius</margin>
CEBES was from Thebes, a philosopher and a disciple of Socrates. He wrote three dialogues: *The Tablet* (which is still in existence), *The Seventh*, and *Phrynichus*. He also wrote a narrative of the underworld and other works. These are the philosophers who did not divide the teachings of their master into various sects; in this respect, they may more properly be called "Socratic," though the others also assume that title.

### Errata.

**M**any errors were missed during printing because of the author’s absence, such as:
Page 1, line 15: "from which it is passed down to us."
Page 2, line 4: "a very great anachronism."
Line 9: "Phoenicians" <note>original: "φοίνικε"</note>.
Page 4: "his time in travel; He went to Crete."
Page 28, line 18: "for his birth."
Page 29, line 9: "my wave-washed grove."
Page 39, line 3: "Judicature" <term>the administration of justice</term>; line 23: "a silent, unexpressed consent."
Page 41, line 6: "with Minors."
Page 63, line 4: "the checkered cloister."

# New and Excellent Romances, Printed for **HUMPHREY MOSELEY**, at the Prince's Arms in *St. Paul's Churchyard*.

*Cassandra*, the famous romance; the complete work in five parts, written in *French* and now elegantly rendered into *English* by a **Person of Quality**. Folio <term>a large book format made by folding sheets of paper once</term>.

**Ibrahim**, or the **Illustrious Pasha** <note>original: "Bassa"</note>, an excellent new romance; the complete work in four parts, written in *French* by *Monsieur de Scudéry* <note>Though attributed here to Georges de Scudéry, these romances were primarily written by his sister, Madeleine de Scudéry</note>, and now **Englished** <term>translated into English</term> by *Henry Cogan*, Gentleman. Folio.

*Artamenes*, or the **Grand Cyrus**, an excellent new romance written by that famous wit of *France*, *Monsieur de Scudéry*, Governor of *Notre-Dame*, and now **Englished** by *F. G.*, Esquire. Folio.

The **Continuation** of *Artamenes*, or the **Grand Cyrus**, that excellent new romance, being the third and fourth parts; written by that famous wit of *France*, *Monsieur de Scudéry*, Governor of *Notre-Dame*, and now **Englished** by *F. G.*, Esquire. Folio.

The **third volume** of *Artamenes*, or the **Grand Cyrus**, that excellent new romance, being the fifth and sixth parts; written by that famous wit of *France*, *Monsieur de Scudéry*, Governor of *Notre-Dame*, and now **Englished** by *F. G.*, Esquire. Folio. **1654**.

The **fourth volume** of *Artamenes*, or the **Grand Cyrus**, that excellent new romance, being the seventh and eighth parts; written by that famous wit of *France*, *Monsieur de Scudéry*, Governor of *Notre-Dame*, and now **Englished** by *F. G.*, Esquire. Folio. In press.

The **fifth volume** of *Artamenes*, or the **Grand Cyrus**, that excellent new romance, being the ninth and tenth parts; written by that famous wit of *France*, *Monsieur de Scudéry*, Governor of *Notre-Dame*, and now **Englished** by *F. G.*, Esquire. Folio. In press.

The History of *Polexander*, a romance, **Englished** by *William Brown*, Gentleman. Folio.

The History of the Banished Virgin, a romance, translated by *J. H.*, Esquire. Folio.

*Cassandra*, the famous romance; the first three books written in *French* and elegantly rendered into **English** by the Right Honourable the Lord *George Digby*. Octavo <term>a book format made by folding sheets of paper three times to form eight leaves</term>.

The History of *Philoxipes* and *Polycrite*, taken out of *Artamenes*, or the **Grand Cyrus**, made **English** by an honourable person. Octavo.

The History of **Don Fenise**, a new romance written in **Spanish** by *Francisco de las Coveras*, treating of the various effects of love and fortune; **Englished** by a **Person of Honour**. Octavo.

*Aurora*, *Ismenia*, and the Prince, with *Oronia* the **Cyprian** Virgin, translated by *Thomas Stanley*, Esquire; the second edition, corrected and amended. Octavo.

**Cleopatra**, a new romance written in **French** by the famous author of *Cassandra*, and now **Englished** by a gentleman of the Inner Temple <note>One of the four professional associations for barristers in London</note>. Octavo.

*La Stratonica*, or the **Unfortunate Queen**, a new romance written in **Italian** and **Englished** by *J. B.*, Gentleman. Quarto <term>a book format made by folding sheets of paper twice to form four leaves</term>.

**Choice Novels** and **Amorous Tales**, written by the most refined wits of *Italy*, newly translated into **English** by a **Person of Quality**. Octavo.

*Nissena*, an excellent new romance written originally in **Italian** and now **Englished** by an honourable person. Octavo.

*Dianea*, an excellent new romance written in **Italian** by *Giovanni Francesco Loredano*, a noble **Venetian**, in four books; translated into **English** by Sir *Aston Cockaine*. Octavo.

# New and Choice Histories, Printed for **HUMPHREY MOSELEY**, at the Prince's Arms in *St. Paul's Churchyard*.

Historical Accounts of the United Provinces of *Flanders*, written in Italian by Cardinal *Bentivoglio*, and now rendered into English by the Right Honorable *Henry*, Earl of *Monmouth*. Folio <term>a large book format made by folding sheets of paper once</term>.

The History of the Wars of *Flanders*, written in Italian by that learned and famous Cardinal *Bentivoglio*; translated into English by the Right Honorable *Henry*, Earl of *Monmouth*. The entire work is illustrated with a map of the 17 Provinces and over 20 figures of the chief personages mentioned in this history. Folio. 1654.

The History of the Wars of the Emperor *Justinian* with the *Persians*, *Goths*, and *Vandals*, written in Greek by *Procopius* of *Caesarea* in eight books; translated into English by Sir *Henry Holcroft*, Knight. In this work, the city of *Rome* was taken and retaken more than eight times when it was the seat of the Empire. Folio.

*De Bello Belgico* <note>original: "On the Belgian War"</note>, the History of the *Low Country* Wars, written in Latin by *Famianus Strada* and translated into English by Sir *Robert Stapylton*; illustrated with various figures. Folio.

The History of Life and Death, or the prolongation of life, written by *Francis*, Lord *Verulam*, Viscount *St. Alban*. 12mo <term>duodecimo; a small book format</term>.

Mr. *Howell’s* History of *Louis* XIII, King of *France*, including the life of his Cardinal *de Richelieu*. Folio.

---

## Books newly Printed this Term for me, **HUMPHREY MOSELEY**.

A German Diet, or the Balance of *Europe*, in which the power and weakness, glory and reproach, virtues and vices, plenty and wants, advantages and defects, and the antiquity and modern state of all the kingdoms and nations of Christendom are impartially weighed by *James Howell*, Esq. Folio.

*Curia Politiae* <note>original: "The Court of Politics"</note>, or the apologies of several princes, justifying their most eminent actions to the world through the strength of reason and the most exact rules of politics; written in French by the accurate pen of *Monsieur de Scudéry*, Governor of *Notre-Dame*, and now faithfully rendered into English with the figures of many emperors and kings. Folio.

*Parthenopeia*, or the History of the most Renowned Kingdom of *Naples*, with the dominions annexed to it and the lives of all its kings.
The first part is by that famous antiquary *Scipio Mazzella*, translated into English by Mr. *Samson Lennard*, Herald of Arms.
The second part was compiled by *James Howell*, Esq., who, besides adding some supplements to the first part, continues the thread of the story to these present times, 1654. It is illustrated with the figures of the kings and the coats of arms of all the provinces.
